Plato: a book and simulator for clay target shooting

Welcome to clay target shooting. Here you will learn how this sport works, why you have to shoot ahead of a moving target, and how the Plato simulator can help you.

Safety: this book and the simulator are for learning. They do not replace in-person training, the rules of the shooting ground, the help of an instructor or a gunsmith, or the legal requirements in force.

Chapter 01

What clay target shooting is, and why this project exists

Welcome to the world of clay target shooting. If you've never tried it, you might be surprised how quickly you can start enjoying it. Watching the clay leave the trap, following it with your eyes and breaking it in the air is exciting from day one. But hitting it isn't just about aiming and firing. You need to see where it's going, follow its movement and lead the shot just the right amount. That learning comes little by little, with practice, safety and good advice.

This guide wants to go with you on that journey. We'll start with the basics: what clay target shooting is, how it's organised and what disciplines exist. Then we'll look at why a shot sometimes falls behind, above or below the clay. We'll also look at how to recognise each mistake. The Plato simulator will let you watch these movements calmly, repeat them and understand them better before taking what you've learned to the shooting ground. It doesn't replace an instructor or real experience. But it can help you get to both with more curiosity, confidence and enthusiasm to learn.

1.1

Introduction to clay target shooting

We'll start with the essentials: what clay target shooting is, and why a moving target calls for a different way of aiming. Then we'll look at how the Plato simulator can help you, and what things you can only learn on a shooting ground, under supervision.

What changes when the target stops standing still? This first unit introduces the clay, the throwing machine, and the coordination needed to solve a moving encounter.

Clay target shooting brings together several shotgun sports. A machine throws a disc for the shooter to try to break in flight. The reference ISSF clay measures 110 mm in diameter, is between 25 and 26 mm high, and weighs about 105 g. A hit counts when, after a valid shot, a visible fragment breaks off.

The difficulty is that the clay doesn't wait. While the shooter sees it, decides and moves the shotgun, the clay keeps moving. The pellets are moving too, and spreading out. That's why it isn't enough to aim at where the clay is: you need to find the point where both movements will meet.

Experience helps you coordinate eyes, body and gun. You don't need extraordinary eyesight. Experts stand out mainly because they organise the task better and keep a steadier movement.

1.2

Brief history

Clay target shooting is about hitting a moving target, not aiming at a still image. To understand how it became a sport with rules, we'll now look at its first targets and machines.

How did the old trials with live birds turn into a sport of artificial targets, calibrated machines and shared rules? To understand it, you have to follow both the objects and the institutions.

In the 19th century there were competitions with live birds. Words like “pichón” and trap come from that period. Modern practice uses artificial targets. FITASC stopped organising trials with live animals in 2003.

Before today's clay, glass targets and other designs were tried. In 1882, George Ligowsky patented a concave, fragile disc designed to spin, fly and break. The patent doesn't prove he was the sole inventor, but it does show that shape, spin and breakage were already being studied.

The great leap forward was joining the target with repeatable machines, measured grounds and common rules. The organisation now known as the ISSF was founded in 1907, and FITASC adopted its current name in 1938. Shotgun shooting at flying targets appeared around the 1900 Olympics, although those events weren't the same as modern Trap. Today's sport is the result of a technical and regulatory evolution.

1.3

Clay target shooting as an organised sport

History shows that the modern clay isn't just a disc: it's part of a system of machines, measurements and common rules. Next, we'll look at which institutions organise and maintain that system.

What role do an international federation, the Spanish federation, a regional one and a club each play? Telling these levels apart helps you avoid confusing rules, licence, facility and day-to-day activity.

Clay target shooting doesn't have a single rulebook for all its variants. The ISSF regulates Olympic events and other international disciplines. FITASC keeps its own rules for Universal Trench, Sporting and other disciplines. Before checking a rule, you need to know which discipline you're shooting.

In Spain, the Real Federación Española de Tiro Olímpico publishes the rules for its area. The regional federations organise the activity in each territory and connect clubs and athletes with the national structure.

The club applies the rules in daily life: access, opening hours, supervision and use of the grounds. It can offer training courses, practice sessions, competitions and social rounds. These aren't the same thing. The paperwork, the scoring and the rules change, but safety and authorisations are always compulsory.

A personal note. My own experience at La Bastida, Toledo, showed me that the ground itself can be a way in. Getting to know the facilities, talking to people at the federation and looking for training helps you make sense of the first steps. This is a regional example, not a national procedure. Each region, club, licence and application process can add its own requirements. Chapter 2 will retrace that journey with the specific administrative and federation rules.
1.4

Main families of disciplines

Federations, regional bodies and clubs have different roles, but together they organise this sport. With this structure clear, we can now look at the main disciplines without confusing them.

Why aren't Trap, Skeet, Sporting, Universal Trench and Robot equivalent names? Each family organises the origin, uncertainty and direction of the targets differently.

In Trap disciplines, the clays are launched from in front of the shooter and move away. Olympic Trap uses groups of machines and ISSF schemes. Universal Trench works with five machines and FITASC rules. They look alike at first glance, but the parameters and the pace differ.

In Skeet, the clays are launched from two side houses and cross known flight paths. In Sporting and Compak Sporting, several machines present very different flights: crossers, incomers, outgoers and other paths.

Double Trap launches two targets at once and is no longer part of the current Olympic programme. Robot-type machines can follow a rulebook or a local set-up. That's why “robot” describes a machine, not a universal discipline.

1.5

Why it's hard

In each discipline, the point where the clay is launched and the information available to the shooter both change. All of them require working out the encounter, which is why hitting the target is harder than it looks.

How can a shot that arrives late still look correct? The difficulty becomes clear once you look at the whole chain: perception, decision, movement and flight, while the clay never stops moving.

Between the clay's launch and the impact, several things happen: the shooter spots it, reads its direction, decides, moves the shotgun and fires. After that, the pellets need time to arrive. Throughout that whole sequence, the clay keeps moving.

The basic idea can be summed up like this: lead = clay speed × time to encounter. This is a simplification, because the clay loses speed, rises and falls, and the pellets also slow down and spread out. Even so, it explains why the shot has to be aimed at a future position.

A miss doesn't leave a mark like a target face does, either. The shooter might have stayed behind or under, slowed the gun down, or lifted their head. Before correcting anything, you need to find out which of those steps went wrong.

1.6

The need to observe and repeat

A miss can come from the eyes, the timing, the movement or the geometry, and two similar misses can need opposite corrections. That's why it pays to observe methodically and repeat each drill with one specific question in mind.

When does repeating teach you something, and when does it just burn another cartridge? Repetition teaches you something when it keeps the scenario the same, sets out a hypothesis, and changes just one variable.

In a round, the next clay can change machine, angle, height or station. That's part of the sport, but it makes it hard to know why you missed. Repetition helps when it keeps the same scenario and changes only one decision.

The method is simple: observe, set out a hypothesis, repeat and compare. For example, if we think the movement started late, we repeat the launch and change only that moment. Repeating isn't blindly trying again.

Training repetition shouldn't be confused with a “no bird” in competition. Nor does it produce a recipe that works for every flight. Once you understand a relationship, you need to go back to varying situations and check whether the lesson still holds.

A personal note. I found it frustrating to move through the rotation without knowing why I'd missed. That's where the idea came from: using repetition of the same clay to compare decisions, whenever the ground and its supervision allow it.
1.7

Birth of the Plato simulator

Repeating helps you learn when you keep the same situation and change only one variable. Being able to pause, compare and try again is the foundation of the Plato simulator.

How does a brief correction on the ground turn into a scene you can observe and discuss? Plato exists to make that translation visible, without replacing real experience.

Plato was created to turn brief corrections, like “you're behind” or “you're staying under”, into a scene you could watch calmly. The simulator shows the clay's trajectory, the meeting point, the pattern and the correction for each shot.

The app runs on a single HTML page and shows the view from the shooter's eye. It includes Universal Trench, Olympic Trap and Robot, five stations, slow motion, wind, repetition and different pattern spreads. Its value lies in observing and comparing, not in copying a distance off the screen and taking it straight to the ground.

Plato doesn't teach you how to handle a gun, and it doesn't reproduce weight, recoil, noise or competitive pressure. Nor does it grant authorisations or replace an instructor. Real practice has to take place at an authorised shooting ground, with proper supervision.

A personal note. This is the motivation behind building the project. It explains its origin, but it isn't an official history of the sport or evidence of effectiveness.
1.8

Project objectives

Plato lets you calmly observe what happens in a fraction of a second. It doesn't replace what has to be learned with a real gun and supervision, so let's now look at what it can genuinely be used for.

What should the simulator help you understand, and which promises should it avoid making? Its goals form a cycle of observation, comparison, decision and responsible validation.

The first goal is to explain lead: aiming the shot at the zone where the clay and the pellets can meet. That zone changes with direction, speed, distance and the moment the shot is taken. There's no single lead that works for every clay.

The simulator also lets you compare trajectories and repeat a launch without changing the other parameters. That way you can study the effect of the station, the wind, the speed or the pattern spread separately.

The pellet cloud is shown in a simplified way. It helps you understand spread and margin, but it doesn't replace a real pattern test with the actual shotgun and cartridge. The final goal is to make a decision, observe the result and revise the hypothesis.

1.9

How to use this manual

The project shows physical relationships and shooting decisions, but it doesn't certify equipment or guarantee results on the ground. Knowing what it offers will help us decide how to read this book.

Do you need to read everything in order? The book offers different paths for getting started, prioritising safety, practising with Plato, or reviewing the technical model.

This book can be read from start to finish, or used to answer one specific question. If you're starting out, carry on to the chapters on getting started, safety, the ground and equipment. If you already shoot, you can go straight to misses, geometry or the disciplines.

To practise with Plato, it helps to follow a short cycle: read an idea, open the simulator, change one variable and observe the result. Then hide the hints and try to repeat the decision. One clear question teaches you more than many changes at once.

The technical part explains units, formulas and the limits of the model. In real practice, the law, the current rules, the ground's instructions and the safety officer always take precedence.

Reading method. Take just one useful question to the ground each session. The manual aims to sharpen your attention. It doesn't aim to fill your head with instructions all at once.

What we have learned

  • The clay and the pattern have to meet at the same place and at the same moment.
  • Each discipline changes the machines, the stations and the trajectories.
  • Spotting a miss is difficult because the clay doesn't leave a mark the way a target face does.
  • Plato lets you repeat and compare, but it doesn't replace training or real practice.

Expert reading. Every representation should state which part of reality it covers and which source it rests on. The simulator explains relationships, but it doesn't certify a ground or a gun.

Responsible access, paperwork and first steps

How to get started in Spain

To get started in clay target shooting, you don't need to buy a shotgun. The first step is to get to know a ground, get some guidance, and understand what role the club, the federation and the Guardia Civil each play. This chapter sets out those steps so that a beginner knows who to ask, and doesn't spend money before it's time.

Procedures, prices, schedules and insurance can change. This information was last checked on 2 August 2026, but it's always worth confirming the current details with the Intervención de Armas y Explosivos, the regional federation and the ground you've chosen.

2.1

The initial problem

We now know what clay target shooting is, where it comes from, and how Plato can help. Before we talk about technique, we'll look at how to get started in Spain with proper guidance, safety and legal backing.

At the start, words like club, licence, federation and ground tend to get mixed up. They don't mean the same thing, and none of them fully replaces the others.

A club organises its own activity and decides how members or visitors gain access. The federation handles the sporting relationship and the competitions within its area. The Guardia Civil enforces firearms law. Finally, each ground has its own rules for access, safety, insurance and equipment.

It's also worth telling apart the gun's category and the type of licence. The category describes the gun; the licence authorises owning and using it under the conditions set out. As a general guide, here are the best-known relationships:

  • Category 1: pistols and revolvers. Licence B is granted restrictively; certain competition guns can be documented under licence F.
  • Category 2: includes rifled long guns for big-game hunting, normally associated with licence D.
  • Category 3: includes certain long guns for sport shooting, shotguns and other smooth-bore long guns. For an ordinary clay target shotgun, the usual reference is licence E.
  • Licence F: covers guns classed as competition guns and is tied to the shooter's sporting accreditation. It isn't the automatic next step for just any clay target shotgun.
  • Licences A and C: apply to professional cases defined by the Firearms Regulations, and aren't part of a beginner's normal path.

This guide is only a general pointer. The exact classification of the gun and the licence you need must be confirmed with the Intervención before buying anything. Being federated doesn't document a shotgun, and holding licence E doesn't automatically let you into just any ground, either.

A personal note. When you're starting out, everything can seem to depend on everything else: the club asks for a federation licence, the federation points you back to the club, and you still need the firearms licence. The simplest way through is to talk to a ground that welcomes beginners first, while checking with the Intervención de Armas in parallel.
2.2

Recommended first step

The first decision shouldn't be to buy anything, but to find someone who can guide and supervise your first steps. The normal thing to do is approach a club, an authorised ground or the regional federation.

The most practical way to get started is to contact a regional federation, a club or a ground that works with beginners.

A club doesn't grant firearms licences, but it does know the daily activity: which disciplines are available, opening hours, courses, visitor access and permitted equipment. RFEDETO publishes a directory of regional federations that helps you find clubs and grounds. It's worth using official contacts and checking that they're up to date.

The first message can be very simple: “I'm a beginner, I don't have a shotgun or a firearms licence, and I'd like to find out about clay target shooting.” After that, you just need to ask:

  • whether you can visit the ground before shooting;
  • whether there's an introductory course or taster day;
  • what documents and insurance are required;
  • who provides the gun and the ammunition;
  • what protection you need to wear and how much the activity costs.

The second contact is administrative. The Intervención de Armas for your home address explains how to apply for licence E, the fee, the documents and the tests. The ground guides you; the Guardia Civil decides.

A practical tip. Starting with the ground or the federation isn't a mandatory order set by law. It's simply a convenient way to get to know the sport while you sort out the administrative side.
2.3

Club, federation and administration

Talking to the right people beforehand avoids risks, expense and false expectations. To make sense of their answers, we need to tell apart the roles of the club, the federation and the Administration.

Each body answers a different question: where to practise, how to take part in the sport, and what firearms law allows.

The club organises training sessions, membership fees, courses and member access. The regional federation processes sporting licences and organises the activity in its community. The Intervención de Armas y Explosivos receives applications, checks the paperwork for guns and enforces the Firearms Regulations. Neither the club nor the federation can grant licence E.

Intervenciones de Armas offices are spread across Spain. You don't need to hunt for one at random: for licence E, the one that applies is the office for the applicant's home address. The official prior-appointment system lets you choose the province, the procedure and an available office. The Guardia Civil states that this service operates across all 52 provinces. Think of the map as a provincial network of offices, not a single national headquarters.

The prior-appointment system helps cut down waiting times, although the Guardia Civil notes that it's optional. Before travelling, it's worth checking the office, the opening hours and the paperwork. If there's any doubt about which office applies, either the appointment system itself or the provincial Comandancia can point you in the right direction.

The federation licence and its insurance also have limits. The compulsory sports insurance covers certain risks under the conditions set by law and by the policy, but you shouldn't assume that any card covers any activity or ground. It's worth asking for the certificate, the assistance phone number and the exclusions.

A simple rule. Ask the club about access and how things work. Ask the federation about the sporting licence, competitions and insurance. Ask the Intervención about licence E, the exam, the gun guide, storage and firearm paperwork.
2.4

Applicable firearms licence

The club, the federation and the Administration work together, but each has its own functions and authorisations. This distinction matters for understanding the firearms licence for a clay target shotgun.

To buy and document an ordinary clay target shotgun, the usual reference is licence E.

Licence E covers the guns set out in the Firearms Regulations, including category 3.2 shotguns. Only one licence E can be issued per person. It allows you to document up to six shotguns or six rifled long guns for sport shooting, with a combined maximum of 12. Its ordinary period of validity is five years. From the age of 67, it requires the three-yearly renewal endorsement set out by law.

The application is submitted to the Intervención that covers your home address, either in person or online if you have an electronic signature. The usual requirements include identification, the medical and psychological fitness certificate, proof of engagement in hunting or the sport, the fee, and, for a first-time application, the certificate of competence. The current list should be checked when you start the process.

There's no single national date for the exam. Each province sets its own schedule. Your application entitles you to three sittings. Once you've passed the theory, you have six months to pass the practical.

The theory test has 20 questions and lasts 20 minutes. You need at least 16 correct answers. The official syllabus covers seven topics, and the Guardia Civil publishes sample question sets and answers. The most useful approach is to study topic by topic, do timed mock tests, and review your mistakes.

The iPhone and iPad app Licencia de Armas España offers questions by chapter, mock exams, a review of your mistakes and statistics. It can be handy for practice. However, its listing shows a version published in 2018, and mentions a syllabus updated in 2020. It should be used as support, not as an official source or a guarantee of passing. Where there's a difference, the syllabus, question sets and answers published by the Guardia Civil take precedence.

In the practical test with a shotgun, loading, aiming, firing and unloading are all assessed. You fire two shots standing, unsupported, at a fixed 50 cm target face set 25 metres away. It isn't a round of clays: what's assessed is safe handling and the minimum result required.

Licence E and licence F. Licence E is the usual reference for an ordinary clay target shotgun. Licence F covers guns classed as competition weapons and depends on a sporting authorisation. Before buying a specific gun, confirm its classification with the Intervención.
2.5

Federation licence

Licence E is the usual route for acquiring and documenting an ordinary clay target shotgun, through its own administrative process. The federation licence belongs to the sporting side of things and serves a different purpose.

The federation licence identifies the athlete within the sport. On its own, it doesn't authorise owning a shotgun.

The federation licence certifies the shooter's relationship with a federation for a specific period. It can be used for training, competitions, federation services, and to demonstrate sporting engagement in certain procedures. Its scope depends on the territory and the discipline.

It can be arranged directly, through a club, or via a federation platform. It can also be regional, or need an extra accreditation for a national competition. That's why you need to check validity, scope, discipline and insurance, not just hold a card.

It's worth keeping a record of the licence number, the issuing federation, the dates, the associated club if there is one, the policy and the assistance phone number. A taster session that happens before you sign up may have its own cover arranged by the organiser; ask about it.

A useful comparison. The federation licence is your sporting ID. Licence E is the usual administrative authorisation for the shotgun. They're related, but they aren't interchangeable.
2.6

Introductory course

Joining a federation lets you take part in the sporting activity and benefit from its cover, but on its own it doesn't authorise you to acquire or keep a gun. Now that we've separated these two issues, we can see an introductory course as training, not just paperwork.

A good course starts with safety and handling. Breaking clays comes later.

You need to tell apart the competence certification tied to a first licence E and a club or federation's sporting course. They can complement each other, but an internal diploma doesn't replace the official tests.

Before booking, it's worth asking in writing who runs the course, where it takes place, how many students each instructor handles, and what insurance is in place. You should also clarify who provides the gun and the ammunition, how many shots are included, and what protective equipment is required.

The first part should teach you to check the state of the gun, keep the muzzle pointed in a safe direction, keep your finger off the trigger, and obey any stop command. Students need to know how to open, unload and hand over the gun before working on stance, gun mount or lead.

After that can come a balanced stance, placing the stock, watching the clay, and a few simple throws. Progress is measured first by safe, repeatable behaviour, not by the number of clays broken.

Compare more than just the price. A low ratio, clear objectives and real supervision time are usually worth more than a promise to fire lots of cartridges.
2.7

First visit to a ground

A responsible course prioritises the state of the gun, the commands and the ability to stop, not the number of hits. In your first visit to a ground, we'll see how these rules are applied by everyone together.

The first visit is for getting to know the place, listening to the commands and confirming the conditions before shooting.

Before you go, confirm the day, the time, the contact person, the paperwork, the cost, and whether you can come in as an observer. A competition, maintenance work or the weather can change the opening hours.

At reception, you follow the instructions of the person in charge. They may ask for identification, your booking, your federation licence, or, if you're bringing your own gun, your licence and ownership permit. Eye and ear protection goes on before entering the designated area.

No one should touch someone else's gun, or go into a restricted area just because other users are doing it. It's also worth asking about which cartridges are allowed, collecting spent cases, and how waste is handled. Watching a full round is already a first lesson. If something isn't clear, there's no obligation to shoot.

What to observe. Commands should be clear, access to the line should be controlled, and everyone should know the stop procedure. A good appearance doesn't replace these checks.
2.8

Practical walkthrough example

It's better to leave your first visit knowing who's guiding you, what the conditions are, and what the next step is, than to improvise a shot. An example will now help us put the enquiries, the training and the paperwork in order.

The exact order can vary, but a simple route stops you mixing up paperwork and purchases.

  1. Find a ground or club that takes beginners, and ask for a visit.
  2. Confirm access, insurance, training, equipment and cost.
  3. Check with the Intervención de Armas for your home address about licence E.
  4. Arrange the federation licence if the activity requires it.
  5. Complete the relevant training and tests.
  6. Practise several times under supervision before buying your own equipment.
Personal note. In my case, getting close to the La Bastida area and talking to the federation helped me make sense of information that was scattered. This example doesn't set the current conditions of that ground, nor does it serve as a general procedure for the whole of Spain.

The important thing isn't to do it quickly. The important thing is knowing which body confirms each step. Opening hours, fees and admission can change; the firearms licence always depends on the administrative decision.

2.9

What a beginner shouldn't buy yet

The worked example sets out procedures that can seem confusing, even though timeframes and requirements vary by territory. It also shows why it's worth putting off purchases until you can choose with good judgement.

Putting off some purchases stops you spending money on equipment that might not suit your body, the discipline or the ground.

The first purchase worth putting off is the shotgun. Stock fit, length, stance and eye dominance all matter a great deal. An excellent model can still suit a particular person badly.

It's also not worth buying large amounts of cartridges purely for their price or advertised speed. The gun determines the compatible gauge and chamber. The ground and the discipline can limit the load, the pellet size or the pellet material. Chokes also need to suit both the gun and the use it's put to.

Modifications to the stock, the recoil pad, the weight or the trigger should be made by competent people. A technique error can look like a fault in the gun. That's why it's better to watch several sessions before modifying anything.

A competition vest, coloured lenses, a cart and other accessories can wait. The one reasonable exception is having good eye and ear protection, confirmed with the instructor or the ground.

It isn't a ban. It's a way of buying with more information. Legally acquiring the gun also follows its own licence and documentation procedure.
2.10

Checklist to get started

Trying things out, getting measured and asking for advice before buying stops you getting used to unsuitable equipment, or to a discipline you don't know yet. Now let's bring these decisions together into a final preparation checklist.

Before a first practice session, the place, the person in charge, the paperwork, the insurance and the equipment should all be clear.

  • Booking, activity and the person responsible confirmed.
  • Administrative requirements checked for your actual goal.
  • Club, federation licence and insurance understood separately.
  • Instructor, gun, ammunition and supervision identified.
  • Eye and ear protection available before stepping onto the line.
  • A realistic budget, without buying a shotgun blindly just yet.
  • If there's any safety doubt at all, don't handle the gun or shoot.

For a first visit, comfortable clothing, sturdy footwear and the required protection are usually enough. Don't bring a gun or ammunition on your own initiative. The prices for signing up, training, the fee, the medical and psychological certificate and the sessions should be checked on the date you pay.

One unchecked critical box means you stop. If the safe direction, the person in charge, the gun's documentation or the insurance cover aren't clear, practice must be postponed.

What we have learned

Getting off to a good start means following a simple order: get to know a ground, separate the sporting side from the administrative side, get training, and practise before buying. The club explains access; the federation, the sporting activity; and the Guardia Civil, the licence and the gun's paperwork.

  • The federation licence doesn't replace licence E.
  • Administrative competence certification and a clay shooting course aren't the same thing.
  • Opening hours, prices, insurance and equipment are confirmed with the ground.
  • The first worthwhile investment is good guidance, supervision and protection.

Chapter 03

Safety before technique

Safety isn't paperwork. It's the necessary condition for learning and sharing a ground without putting anyone at risk. The law requires you to keep control of the gun at all times. On the ground, that obligation turns into clear actions: safe direction, loading only when it's your turn, an open gun outside of firing, and obeying the person in charge.

Knowing the rules by heart isn't enough. Noise, being rushed or a mistake can cause a lapse. This chapter sets out a simple sequence that needs to be practised under supervision.

3.1

Fundamental principle

Getting off to a good start isn't about accumulating equipment, but about knowing who's supervising you, which documents you need, and how to act at each stage. With these steps clear, we reach the most important rule: safety.

What rule protects us when we can't be sure whether there's a cartridge inside? Treating every gun as if it were loaded, keeping the barrel pointed in a safe direction, and keeping your finger off the trigger form a single discipline. They aren't three separate tips you can trade off against each other.

The first rule is to treat every shotgun with the same care, even if we believe it's unloaded. The muzzle must always point towards a safe area, and your finger stays off the trigger until the moment you fire. Doubt never justifies careless handling.

The Firearms Regulations require you to keep control of the gun and act with due care. The mechanical safety catch is a help, not a guarantee. Each shooter is responsible for their own shotgun, but anyone who spots a danger must alert the referee or the person in charge.

Personal note. I've seen impact marks on shelter roofs. That observation shows what can happen when direction control is lost. It doesn't tell you the cause of each hole. The lesson you can actually verify is simpler: a roof is never part of the target zone, and the barrel must not point towards it.
Expert reading. Safety is organised in layers: controlled direction, visible unloading, finger off, a set procedure and supervision. No layer allows you to remove the others.
3.2

Safe zones and no-go zones

Always treating a gun as if it were loaded protects us even when we slip up. To apply this rule, we need to know which directions and areas are safe.

Which way is it safe to point the barrel? It isn't enough to say “forwards”. You need to recognise the firing line, the authorised zone for flight and fall, the machines, the waiting area, and anywhere occupied by people or property.

The firing line separates the waiting area from the stations and from the zone the clays fly and fall through. If there's a person in front of the stations, guns stay open and unloaded.

A safe direction isn't just somewhere that currently looks empty. You also need to think about what's behind it, and which surfaces might deflect a shot. The ground's signage and commands take priority, because every facility is different.

Exercise before you start. Before your first round, ask the person in charge to point out the line, the waiting area, the gun racks, access to the machines and the limits of the shooting sector. Repeat the map in your own words. “In front” stops being ambiguous once you can name every zone.
Stated variation. This plate uses five stations for consistency with the trap disciplines covered in the book. It doesn't define universal regulation dimensions, nor does it replace the authorised layout of a specific ground.
3.3

Gun open except when shooting

Safe direction depends on the state of the whole ground, not on what the shooter thinks they're about to do. That's why the gun must stay open except during the authorised moment of firing.

Why does an open shotgun communicate better than saying “it's empty”? Because it lets its state be checked from a distance. It also makes it harder for it to fire while waiting, rotating stations, or leaving the station.

On an over-and-under shotgun, opening it fully leaves the chambers visible. That state lets anyone check from outside that the gun can't fire. Outside your turn, the shotgun must be open and unloaded.

It stays that way while waiting, rotating stations, and leaving the station. It's only closed during your turn, with the barrels pointed towards the target zone. To hand it to someone else, it's also opened and the chambers are shown.

Custom and rule. Some clubs specify a particular way to carry an open shotgun. Others require you to place it in a gun rack. Follow local instructions as long as they keep the gun unloaded, open and free from sweeping the barrel across anyone. Don't turn a learned custom into a universal rule.
Expert check. “Open action” and “unloaded gun” are different properties. The first makes it easier to check the second. Neither removes the duty to control the barrel.
3.4

Safe sequence before firing

An open shotgun lets the whole group see its state and share the same safety information. Now let's set out the checks needed before closing it and firing.

When does waiting stop and shooting begin? Only when the station, the turn, an open line, a clear zone and authorisation to load all coincide. The cartridge must never come before those conditions.

Before stepping onto the station, the gun is open and unloaded. Check that it's your turn, that the previous shooter has left, and that no one is in front. Once at the station, load only the permitted amount.

The shotgun is closed with the barrels towards the target zone. Your finger stays off the trigger until the gun is mounted and the shot is imminent. The referee's commands take priority: at STOP you stop, and at UNLOAD you unload.

Control phrase. Before closing the gun, run through three mental checks: “my turn, area clear, facing forward”. This doesn't replace the referee's commands, but it helps stop the action getting ahead of the situation.
Regulatory definition. Under ISSF rules, a gun can be considered loaded from the moment a cartridge, even an empty one, touches it. That's why you don't rest ammunition on the gun while waiting.
3.5

Safe sequence after firing

Before starting, the station, the turn, an open line and authorisation to load must all coincide. After the shot, another series of steps leaves the gun in a state that everyone can check again.

When can the shooter turn around or leave the station? Only after keeping the gun pointed forward, opening it fully, removing the cartridges, and confirming that both chambers are empty.

The attempt doesn't end when you break or miss the clay. It ends when the shotgun is open, unloaded and checkable again. It's opened fully with the barrels pointed forward.

When opening an over-and-under, you need to control the ejection of the spent cases. They're then collected or placed where the ground indicates. You only turn round or change station with the gun open and empty.

Personal note. The advice I was given — “open without turning round” — matches the regulations I checked. What's universal is the order of steps. The exact way of picking up cases or waiting for the next shooter can vary by discipline and ground.
Final state. A safe system needs an unambiguous exit condition: attempt finished, chambers empty, action open and station clear. Rotating before that mixes two states and makes supervision harder.
3.6

Misfire or jam

Opening, unloading and checking the chambers closes the cycle without relying on assumptions. If the gun doesn't respond as it should, you need to follow the special protocol for a malfunction.

What should someone do if they pull the trigger and what's expected doesn't happen? Stay still, keep the muzzle pointed towards the flight zone, don't open or touch any mechanisms, and alert the referee or the person in charge.

A click, an odd recoil, or a jammed action can have different causes. The initial response is the same: keep the barrels pointed towards the safe zone, take your finger off the trigger, and alert someone.

You don't open it suddenly, you don't fire again, and you don't look down the barrel. The referee decides what happens with the clay, and the person in charge or a gunsmith determines what to do with the gun. If the cause isn't clear, the shotgun is withdrawn from the round.

Rule to start with. If you don't understand what's gone wrong, don't try to fix it yourself. Keeping the gun pointed in a safe direction and asking for help is competent behaviour, not a sign of clumsiness.
Distinction. “Faulty cartridge” and “gun malfunction” are conclusions you reach afterwards. The immediate response is the same either way. Decisions about a re-shoot, repair or withdrawal belong to the referee, the person in charge and, where relevant, the gunsmith.
3.7

Typical risks for beginners

When something goes wrong, it's safer to hold your direction and wait for instructions than to hunt for the cause under pressure. Many of a beginner's risks appear when they try to do too many things at once.

Why can someone who knows the rules still break them? Because attention, hands and eyes all get overloaded. The risk shows up in small steps: asking a question, closing the gun, adding a cartridge, or trying to fix something.

Many errors appear when switching from one action to another. A beginner might turn round to speak, close the gun too soon, or touch the trigger while lifting the gun. The routine must always be the same: barrels forward, finger off, open and check before talking or moving.

You also need to load only what's permitted, and make sure the roof, the sides or people don't come within the barrels' path. Only one voice should give safety commands; technical corrections wait until the gun is open.

Personal note. The holes I've seen in roofs are a powerful visual warning. They don't prove who fired or how. I present them as a sign that automatic habits can fail and that transitions need to be practised.
Human factor. Risk concentrates whenever the state changes: waiting to your turn, shooting to unloading, a misfire to inspection. Standardising these transitions reduces mental load without relying on memory under stress.
3.8

Eye protection

Getting your equipment ready, knowing the commands, and listening to a single person in charge cuts down many errors before you even step onto the line. The first physical protection against fragments and particles is your eyewear.

Do tinted glasses count as protection? Only if, as well as helping you see, they're proper shatterproof eye protection. They also need to cover well and be kept undamaged.

On the ground, fragments, dust or cartridge cases can reach the eye. That's why you need impact-resistant glasses with good coverage and fit. Ordinary sunglasses aren't shooting protection.

The lens colour can improve contrast, but there's no single tint that's perfect for every background. Protective eyewear must allow side vision, stay in place during the gun mount, and be compatible with your ear protection. A damaged lens should be replaced.

Practical rule. Put your glasses on before you approach the line and keep them on while there's activity. A clay or a case doesn't wait for your turn.
Limit. This section doesn't certify any particular model. Check the marking, the manufacturer's documentation and the ground's requirements. The European PPE Regulation sets out design and conformity obligations — it doesn't mean that any sports glasses will do.
3.9

Hearing protection

Glasses need to protect well, fit the face, and be comfortable with the rest of your equipment; the lens colour matters less. The ground also produces very loud noise, so good hearing protection is needed too.

Why protect yourself before the first shot, if a round doesn't take long? Because a gunshot is impulsive noise. It can cause immediate damage, and it adds up with every exposure after that.

A shot produces impulsive noise capable of causing immediate damage. Protection goes on before entering the active zone. Don't wait for the first shot to find out whether the noise bothers you.

Earplugs depend on being inserted correctly. Earmuffs can lose their seal because of glasses, hair or a cap. For impulsive noise, double protection may be recommended. Even so, the shooter still needs to be able to hear the referee's commands.

Personal note. Under a metal sheet roof, I've perceived the shot as harsher. It's a physically plausible impression, consistent with reverberation, but it would need to be measured to quantify it.

Earplugs and earmuffs each have different advantages. Earplugs depend a lot on size and how they're inserted. Earmuffs are easy to check at a glance, but a thick pair of glasses arms, hair or a cap can break the seal. Level-dependent electronic systems let you hear conversation while limiting loud sounds. They shouldn't be confused with consumer headphones with no protective rating. NIOSH recommends double protection, earmuffs over earplugs, for impulsive noise. It also notes that real-world fit can perform worse than the figure on the packaging.

Warning sign. Ringing, a blocked feeling or muffled hearing after shooting point to inadequate protection. They justify stopping and consulting a healthcare professional. They aren't a normal part of it that you should just accept.
Scope. The occupational limit of 85 dBA over eight hours doesn't, on its own, describe a gunshot. Impulse noise needs to be assessed by peak level, duration, repetition, environment and effective attenuation.
3.10

Body safety

A single exposure can damage your hearing, and the damage can build up without us noticing it at the time. As well as protecting eyes and ears, we need to position our body correctly to shoot safely and control recoil.

When does pain stop being a technical nuisance and become a safety warning? When it changes your gun mount, your balance, your attention, or your ability to control the gun.

The recoil pad needs to sit firmly and repeatably against the shoulder. If the stock forces you to stretch your neck, or strikes your cheek, there may be a technique or fit problem. It's checked with an instructor, and always with the gun open and unloaded.

Sturdy footwear, staying hydrated, suitable clothing and breaks all help you keep control. Pain, dizziness, shaking or losing focus are reasons to stop. There's no number of rounds that's safe for everyone.

Personal note. Where I shoot, I've heard “caramelo” used as an informal name for swelling or a lump on the face after repeated impacts. The term isn't a medical diagnosis. Persistent pain, swelling or dizziness call for stopping and seeking medical attention.
Stop criterion. None of these sources give a universal number of rounds for beginners. The useful limit is functional: once your ability to maintain direction, stay open, keep your balance or hold your attention starts to drop, the session is over.
3.11

Beginners and supervision

A stable stance protects the shoulder and the face, and helps you keep your balance without clamping the shotgun rigidly against the body. A beginner should learn this positioning with a qualified person supervising them.

When does someone know enough to train alone? Not when they can recite the rules. They know it when they carry them out consistently, recognise their own limits, and follow the club's specific procedure without help.

Reading the rules isn't enough. On the ground there's noise, turns and the unexpected. An instructor can see if the muzzle drifts off line, if a finger goes to the trigger too soon, or if the gun is opened badly.

Corrections are explained before loading, or afterwards, with the shotgun open and empty. There's no number of clays that grants independence. Independence comes when the safe sequence is maintained without help, including when there's a stop command or an incident.

Personal note. I think it's sensible to start with someone alongside you, but informal experience doesn't always amount to qualification. It's worth turning to an instructor, coach or person recognised by the ground, and avoiding several advisers giving instructions at once.
Assessment. Independence is context-specific. Being safe with an over-and-under on a familiar ground doesn't demonstrate competence with a semi-automatic, a different discipline or a temporary ground.
3.12

Safety in the simulator

Good supervision isn't just watching: it simplifies the tasks, controls the ammunition, and stops the action when a doubt appears. The simulator can reinforce these ideas, but it also needs to be used responsibly.

Can a screen teach real safety? It can organise concepts and questions. But it can't certify handling, assess a gun, or grant authorisation to use it.

Plato helps you understand trajectories and decisions. It doesn't reproduce weight, closing the action, recoil, noise, people around you, or unexpected commands. Hitting the target in the simulator doesn't prove someone knows how to handle a shotgun.

In Spain, owning and using guns requires the relevant authorisations and a permitted facility. The app doesn't issue licences, and it doesn't replace the tests or in-person training. It's there to help you bring better questions to your instructor, not to practise real handling at home.

Bridging it correctly. Use Plato before or between training sessions to form a hypothesis. Don't use the result to argue against a safety order, to diagnose a gun, or to practise with ammunition outside an authorised ground.
Stated simplification. The code in `app.html` models inputs and visual physics, not a certification system. A risk not appearing on screen doesn't mean it doesn't exist on a real ground.

What we have learned

  • The muzzle stays pointed in a safe direction, and the shotgun stays open outside your turn.
  • Before firing, you load and close the gun pointing forward. Afterwards, you open it, empty it, and only then turn round.
  • If something goes wrong, hold your position and alert the person in charge.
  • Protection, attention and supervision are all compulsory. The simulator doesn't replace any of them.

Expert reading. Safety works through visible states and multiple barriers. If the rule, the gun or the local procedure raises a doubt, it's clarified before loading.

Chapter 04

What a shooting ground is like, and how a round unfolds

A clay target shooting ground brings together access points, stations, machines, waiting areas and people with different roles. Understanding that order helps you settle in calmly, and know when to wait, shoot or change station. This chapter explains how a round works, and what can vary depending on the discipline or the club. The measurements and procedures cited correspond to sources checked on 2 August 2026.

4.1

Overview of a shooting ground

Safety sets limits that will be present throughout this book, and no technique can override them. Now we'll walk through the shooting ground, get to know its zones, and see how a group completes a round.

The ground is easier to understand starting from the entrance: reception, waiting area, stations, trap house and flight zone. Each zone has a function and limits.

The public zone includes the entrance and the office. Next comes the waiting area, where the assigned ground is confirmed. The shooting line is reserved for shooters, the referee and authorised staff. Ahead of that are the trap house, the flight zone and the pellet fall zone. Nobody enters those zones while the ground is in use.

The Weapons Regulation requires the facility to have a safety zone. Spectators must stay behind the shooters, and the machines must be protected. Each ground applies these rules through its own site plan, signage and access control.

Services vary from club to club. There may be a gun shop, an instructor, a café or gun hire, but none of these are compulsory. On an actual visit, the booking and staff instructions take priority.

Specific example. Someone arrives before their turn. They identify themselves, confirm “layout C, 11:00” and wait behind the boundary. They go in when the person in charge calls them, even if they can see free stations.
Personal comment. On a first visit, it helps to ask what's in front of and behind the line. In front is the shooting zone. Behind are the waiting area and the permitted routes.
Technical note. A ground is divided into four spaces: public zone, controlled zone, shooting line and safety zone. The official plan for the facility always takes priority over a teaching diagram.
4.2

Several layouts at the same facility

A layout includes zones for moving around, waiting, shooting and receiving falling clays; it isn't just a shooting line. When there are several layouts, their sectors must be coordinated and must not overlap.

A facility can have several layouts. Each one has its own schedule, discipline, group and person in charge.

Layouts are usually identified with letters or numbers. This reference is used to organise bookings, results and access. It also stops the flight paths of nearby layouts interfering with each other.

Several disciplines can coexist within the same facility. Olympic Trap uses fifteen machines and Universal Trench uses five. Both have five stations, but their clay programmes are different. Before entering, you need to confirm the layout and discipline.

Personal comment. A club's arrangement can change due to scheduling, maintenance or competitions. It's worth checking availability before travelling there.
Specific example. The board shows “A: Olympic Trap, competition; B: Olympic Trap, maintenance; C: Universal Trench, 10:00; D: Universal Trench, 11:00”. Even though C looks empty at 10:45, the group booked for D doesn't go in there. Confirm your letter and wait for the call.

To begin with, it helps to treat each layout as an independent room: you enter at the assigned place, time and group. Two similar lines can use different rules and schemes.

Technical note. The separation between layouts is physical, administrative and human. The booking only covers the time slot. Safety and control are the responsibility of the facility's staff.
4.3

Parts of a trap shooting ground

In a shared facility, you must respect the limits and instructions of each layout, even if one seems to be idle. In a trap layout, the setup is divided into stations, trap house, machines and fall zone.

Five stations form a straight line. The trap house is 15 m ahead, and the flight sector continues beyond it.

The five stations are numbered from left to right and form a single line. The trap house is 15 m ahead of that line. The waiting area is behind the shooters, never between the stations and the trap house.

In Universal Trench there are five machines aligned inside the trap house, one facing each part of the shooting line. The trap house is protected and partly underground. The clays come out from beneath its cover and continue towards the ground.

Beyond the trap house is the flight sector. It shouldn't be confused with the whole safety zone of the ground. The regulations limit valid flight paths, and the Weapons Regulation protects a larger space around the facility.

In Olympic Trap there are also five stations in line, but the trap house contains fifteen machines: three per station. This difference changes where the clays originate and shouldn't be hidden in a generic diagram.

The same flight path looks different from each station. A clay may look like a side angle from station 1 and almost head-on from station 3. It's the shooter's position that changes, not the clay's flight.

Specific example. In Universal Trench, a target released from the machine furthest to the right can move away to the left of the ground. The person at station 1 sees it crossing a large part of their field of view. From station 5, the same starting point is closer and the apparent angle changes. The machine number and the station number are not interchangeable.

The basic idea is easy to remember: waiting behind, five stations in line, trap house ahead and flight beyond. The infographic explains spatial relationships, but it doesn't replace the approved plan of an actual ground.

Technical note. You need to keep the structure of the trap house, the machine settings and the view from each station separate. These are three related but different problems.
4.4

Group organisation

Knowing each part helps you understand the distances, the lines and the responsibilities, even when the machines are hidden beneath the trap house. This space is also where the group of shooters needs to organise itself.

A typical squad brings together up to six shooters. Five occupy the stations and the sixth waits behind the first. One person alone must direct the sequence.

The best-known arrangement brings together six people: five on the line and one waiting. There can also be smaller groups, especially in teaching or training.

In competition, the referee is in charge. In practice, the person appointed by the club is in charge. They start the round, resolve questions and can stop it. The coach teaches, but several voices at once only create confusion.

In some unofficial competitions, the shooters themselves help with refereeing. This is a local practice, not a universal rule. One person can call the scores and two others can record them separately. The double record makes it possible to compare results if a doubt arises. The organisers decide who carries out these roles and how an error is corrected.

The best-equipped grounds may use electronic scoreboards. These show the score and, in some cases, the shot time. This information helps the referee and the shooter, but it doesn't replace a ruling under the regulations when there's an incident.

Specific example. Ana is squad member number 6 and starts off waiting. She watches the shot from station 5. She goes to station 1 when it's her turn. Before that, she checks visually that the previous person has left. If she isn't sure whether to move forward, she stays in the marked area and asks the person in charge. She doesn't improvise between stations.
Personal comment. Before the first round, it's enough to explain where each person waits, how a stop is announced and who keeps count. Asking a question should be easy.

The group works like a wheel: five active positions and one waiting. Each clay belongs to one shooter, but the pace depends on everyone.

Technical note. It helps to distinguish role, position and turn. “Shooter 3” can identify a person on the sheet. “Station 3” identifies a physical square. “Third turn” describes a moment in the sequence. Using a single number for all three creates a lot of confusion when explaining a rotation.
4.5

Rotation between stations

The group must keep to an order: each person has a turn, a station and duties that the others can see. The rotation keeps that order while the shooter's point of view changes.

The rotation moves from station 1 to 5. Then you go back behind to the waiting position at station 1. You never cross in front.

The rotation lets you shoot from five different viewpoints. From station 1 you move to 2, and so on up to 5. From there you return behind, without crossing active stations or passing in front of the trap house.

The shooter waits their turn to move forward. On leaving station 5, they carry the gun open and unloaded. If the person in charge calls a halt, the whole rotation stops.

The common mistake is moving because the next station looks free. The right signal isn't “there's room”, but “it's my turn to move forward”.

Specific example. Luis has just taken his shot at station 3. Station 4 is empty for a moment, but the person who was there still needs to make room at station 5. Luis waits for the next shot to finish. He moves forward once the wheel has shifted. From station 5, he returns behind the line with the gun open and unloaded.

In a full squad, the shooters start out spread across the stations. The referee and the scoresheet set the order. Saying everyone starts at station 1 is only useful for explaining an individual exercise.

The correct rotation ends up becoming automatic: wait, open, unload and move along the set route. Fluency comes with practice.

Technical note. Each shot frees up a station. An incident stops the whole wheel. That's why moving ahead of time affects the whole group.
4.6

Number of clays and shots

Rotating isn't simply walking around the ground: you need to carry the gun in a safe state and respect the turn at the new position. The number of clays and shots sets the pace and the limit of the round.

A typical official round contains 25 clays. Clay, shot and cartridge are different quantities.

In ISSF Trap and Universal Trench, a round consists of 25 targets. Each shooter receives five from each station. The distribution of flight paths changes depending on the discipline.

In the ordinary rounds, up to two shots at a clay are allowed. If it's broken with the first, there's no second shot. In the finals and final tie-breaks of ISSF Trap, only one is allowed. That's why 25 clays doesn't always mean 25 cartridges.

Each clay earns one result: hit or lost. Breaking it with the second shot still counts as a hit when that shot is allowed. The scoreboard doesn't add a point per shot.

Specific example. Marta completes a round of 25 clays. She breaks 18 with the first shot, 3 with the second, and misses 4. Her score is 21/25. She has fired 32 shots: 25 first shots plus 7 second shots on the clays not hit the first time. None of these figures can replace another.

A training session can use fewer clays or repeat a flight path. It shouldn't be compared without explanation to an official round. For learning, it's sometimes better to shoot less and review each attempt.

Personal comment. When you're starting out, the score tells you how often you hit, but not why. Noting the flight path and whether a second shot was needed gives you more information.
Technical note. With two shots allowed, the theoretical maximum is 50 cartridges. Actual use is 25 plus the number of second shots fired.
4.7

How to call for a target

The round organises cartridge use, scoring and second-shot options according to the discipline. Each attempt begins when the shooter calls for the target and the release mechanism is activated.

The call activates the release system. The call must be short and clear.

When it's their turn, the shooter gets ready and uses a call that activates the system: pull, go, “los” or another one accepted by the organisers. What matters is that it's clear, short and consistent. It helps to keep a similar duration, volume and tone for each clay.

The release can be activated with a button or with microphones. The voice system listens for the call and orders the clay to be released. It doesn't recognise a word the way a voice assistant does: it responds to the sound. That's why a particular voice may not trigger a microphone well even when others do.

If the system doesn't detect a call, the shooter keeps the gun under control and waits. They don't call several times in a row or decide on their own. Under the referee's direction, they can try a small change in vowel sound or tone so the microphone picks up the call better. The Canterbury manual also notes that some voices need a slight adjustment.

Before the first round of each day, FITASC allows a test of the gun, or fogueo. It's done one shooter at a time, at their station, following the squad order and only when the referee gives the order. The barrels point towards the ground and above the trap house, within the safe zone.

This test stops the first scoring shot arriving completely cold. It also lets you check the sequence of gun mount, safety and shot before calling for the first target. It doesn't replace checking the state of the gun before each turn.

At some grounds it's customary to start that warm-up with the sixth shooter, or whoever is waiting. This is a local arrangement. It shouldn't be presented as a universal rule: the valid order is whatever the referee indicates according to the regulations and how the ground operates.

Specific example. Elena says “pull” once and no target appears. She stays at the station and looks at the person in charge. He confirms that the microphone did not pick up her voice. He resets the sequence and tells her when to call again. The absence of a clay does not turn into two overlapping calls.
Personal comment. Practising the same call several times, without releasing any clays, can reduce tension. This should be done with the coach's permission.

The simulator represents the call, a delay and the flight. It doesn't reproduce the acoustics, the microphone or the faults of an actual ground.

Technical note. The delay brings together detection, processing, selection and mechanical response. Without technical data, it's not possible to know which phase caused a delay.
4.8

Valid clay, broken clay and no-bird

Calling for the target is a clear command that should trigger the release under the expected conditions. From that moment, the referee and the regulations decide whether the target is valid, broken or void.

“Broken” can mean that the clay came out faulty or that it was hit. The moment it breaks changes the ruling.

A valid clay comes out whole and follows the expected flight path. If the shot knocks off a visible fragment, it counts as a hit. A cloud of dust without a fragment isn't enough. The decision belongs to the referee.

If the clay comes out already broken, it's declared void and repeated according to the discipline. ISSF uses “no target” and FITASC uses “no bird”. It doesn't count as a miss even if the shooter has fired.

It can also be voided for an irregular flight path, a release from the wrong group, or two clays released together when only one should have come out. The exact causes vary between regulations.

Specific example. A clay is released intact. After the first shot, orange dust appears, but the clay keeps flying and no visible piece breaks off. If the permitted second shot also fails to break off a fragment, the result is a miss. Dust alone does not turn the target into a hit.

To avoid confusion, it helps to say “hit by the shot” or “broken on release”. If there's any doubt, listen for the ruling and carry on. The explanation can wait for the break.

Technical note. First you check whether the clay was valid. Only then do you decide whether it was a hit or a miss.
4.9

Deliberate repetition during training

Not every clay we see counts, and not every break is credited to the shooter; it depends on how it came out and what happened afterwards. In training, a clay can be repeated for a different purpose, as long as it's recorded as a repeat.

Repeating during training is useful for comparing attempts. Everything stays the same and only one thing changes.

A regulation repeat happens when the referee declares a clay void. A training repeat is different: the coach and the shooter agree to watch a flight path again to study a problem.

It helps to ask a simple question, such as “am I starting the movement late?”. The station, machine and preparation stay the same. Then a single decision is changed. If everything is changed at once, the result teaches you little.

Personal comment. Repeating can uncover an error that a varied round hides. Afterwards, you need to go back to different clays to check whether the improvement holds.
Specific example. From station 2, Clara is given the same agreed flight path twice. The first time, she notes “eyes fixed on the trap house; sharp reaction”. The second time, she keeps everything the same except for a slightly wider point of focus, as suggested by her coach. Afterwards, she goes back to the rotation and checks whether the improvement also shows up with an unannounced clay.

Only the person in charge can authorise this practice. You don't enter the trap house, you don't touch the thrower, and you don't interrupt another squad.

The simulator's “Repeat target” function keeps the previous target without adding a clay or changing station. It's useful for comparing perception and timing, but it doesn't reproduce all the conditions of an actual ground.

Technical note. The minimum method is: attempt A, a single change, attempt B, and a follow-up test with varied flight paths.
4.10

Day-to-day running of the club

Repeating to learn keeps the same parameters and allows decisions to be compared, while a regulation repeat responds to specific causes. Both must respect the turns and the club's safety rules.

Before shooting, you need to confirm documentation, payment, turn and ground. The specific system depends on each club.

The club may ask for identification, a licence and other documentation. It also checks whether the person is a member or a visitor, charges for the service and assigns a time slot. These tasks vary depending on the facility.

Some clubs use prepaid cards. These are inserted into a reader and deduct a round or the clays thrown. The card may be personal and won't necessarily work at another ground. If the facility offers several disciplines, it's easy to accidentally activate the wrong layout or programme. Before inserting the card, check the discipline, layout, price and balance.

Specific example. A visitor presents the requested documents, pays for a round and is given “layout C, 10:00”. Before heading out, they check that the letter and time match the receipt. If the counter deducts clays without a release, they do not open the machine. They note the time and let the person in charge know.

Staff load clays, check microphones, maintain the machines and organise the turns. A fault is reported to the person in charge. The user never enters the trap house or handles the system.

Personal comment. One question avoids a lot of mistakes: “What do I need to show, where do I wait, and who lets me through?”
Technical note. A balance issue is resolved at the office. A breakdown, with the technical supervisor. The decision on a clay is up to the referee.
4.11

Behaviour and etiquette

Bookings, maintenance, the people in charge and the internal rules make each round possible before the first target is called for. All this work needs respect and good manners, both on and off the line.

Good manners protect concentration and safety. Silence, order and a clear authority keep the round moving at a steady pace.

Talking behind a station, crossing a boundary, or giving advice during preparation can distract the shooter and mask a call. Technical help should wait for the break.

With an over-and-under shotgun, it helps to open the gun in a controlled way. With the barrels pointing towards a safe area, the free hand can hold the ejected cases so they don't fly off. They're then placed carefully in the container provided. They shouldn't be thrown down hard. On grounds with acoustic activation, a case hitting a metal bucket can end up triggering the next shooter's clay. This precaution depends on the equipment and local rules.

FITASC requires the use of case containers where they're available. Material isn't collected between active shooters or during a halt. Visitors stay outside the shooting area, and everyone follows the instructions of the person in charge.

Specific example. A companion wants to photograph their friend from a side angle. They stay behind the barrier and ask before the round starts. The person in charge points out a permitted spot and a moment with no action taking place. The photography adapts to the ground. The ground doesn't get reorganised around the photography.

During learning, it helps to listen to a single instruction. The coach corrects the technique and the referee decides the result, when the roles are kept separate.

Personal comment. Camaraderie doesn't mean you have to accept advice. Saying hello, asking questions and respecting silence all help you learn without pressure.
Technical note. Good behaviour reduces acoustic, visual and decision-making noise. If an action adds a distraction, it should wait.

What we have learned

A ground separates access, waiting, shooting line, trap house and safety zone. The five stations are 15 m from the trap house. Universal Trench uses five machines and Olympic Trap fifteen. The squad rotates in order, and a sixth person waits behind station 1.

A typical round has 25 clays. Before starting, card, discipline, ground and turn are confirmed. During the round, the person in charge is respected, the target is called for clearly, and the gun and cases are handled with control. The regulations set the framework, and each club organises its day-to-day application.

Chapter 05

Shotguns, stocks, chokes, cartridges and kit

Choosing equipment for clay target shooting isn't about buying the most expensive shotgun, the tightest choke and the fastest cartridge. It's about making the person, the discipline, the gun, the ammunition and the ground work together. If one piece doesn't fit, the beginner ends up adapting their body to the tool. If the piece is well understood, the technique shows through more clearly and the equipment doesn't hide the problem.

This chapter was reviewed on 2 August 2026. It explains international rules and documents available as of that date. Even so, it doesn't replace the gun's manual, the competition regulations, the club's rules or the work of a qualified gunsmith.

5.1

Main types of shotgun

We now know how the ground is organised, how the group rotates, and what happens from calling for the target to the end of the action. Now we'll get to know the equipment, always linked to the environment and the person using it.

To start with, it helps to separate two ideas that often get mixed up. One is the mechanism: how the gun opens, loads and fires. It can be a break-action with two over-and-under barrels, a break-action with side-by-side barrels, or a semi-automatic with a single barrel. The other idea is the use and the set-up: hunting, sporting, trap or another discipline. An over-and-under can be a hunting gun or a competition gun. There are also semi-automatics built for clays. That's why the outward shape isn't enough to tell whether a gun suits a person.

The over-and-under shows the chambers when it opens. The chamber is the housing the cartridge goes into. It also allows two different barrels and two different chokes, with no magazine. The semi-automatic reloads after each shot by gas or by inertia, and can make the recoil feel less pronounced. In exchange, it requires you to check the chamber, the receiver and the magazine. The side-by-side places the two tubes horizontally and remains very present in hunting and traditional gunmaking. FITASC allows all actions in Universal Trench except slide (pump) actions, including semi-automatics, provided their ejection doesn't disturb others. That rule is a reminder that “common” and “permitted” don't mean the same thing.

In practice, the choice starts with the use. Trap looks for stability, a repeatable gun mount and an image designed for clays moving away. Sporting requires you to deal with varied releases, heights and directions. A light field shotgun can be comfortable to carry and quick to move. But, if everything else is equal, less weight also increases the speed of free recoil. A competition gun tends to add weight, locking mechanisms built for heavy use, balance options and more specialised stocks. These are design trends, not guarantees that a specific shotgun will fit.

Before comparing models, you need to set a minimum baseline: calibre and chamber compatible with the discipline, a known mechanism, a completed inspection, and safe practice opening and unloading it. Only after that does it make sense to compare impressions. A gun that breaks more clays in five attempts may just have had easier flight paths. A gun that allows twenty identical gun mounts gives a more useful clue for learning.

Specific example. Ana tries a sporting over-and-under, a trap gun and a semi-automatic shotgun with the same instructor. She doesn't just count broken clays. She notes whether she mounts the gun at the same point every time, whether she sees the clay over the rib, whether the gun tires her arms, and whether she can show at a glance that it's unloaded. That information helps more with a first choice than a brand or a price.
5.2

Over-and-under shotgun

There's no single clay shotgun: each mechanism changes the loading, the balance and the way the second shot is taken. We'll start with the over-and-under, because it lets you clearly see the state of its two barrels.

In an over-and-under, the two barrels sit one above the other and pivot on the action, which is the central part of the gun. When you move the top lever, the barrels swing open. Then you can see both chambers and load or remove a cartridge in each. This quick check explains part of its use on the ground: open and empty, it shows its state to the shooter, the referee and the other squad members. Even so, it must always be checked, keeping the gun pointed in a safe direction.

The two shots can use barrels with different chokes. The choke is the narrowing at the muzzle, or a tube that does that job. A selector, if there is one, decides which barrel fires first. The exact set-up is in the manual. When you open the gun, the extractors lift the cases so they can be removed by hand. Automatic ejectors can throw them clear if they've been fired. Some models let you disable or change that function, but it shouldn't be assumed. The practical advantage is being able to control two cartridges and two chambers. The cost is taking the recoil without the cycling of a semi-automatic, and looking after the lubrication and wear of the moving parts.

For a beginner, “two barrels” doesn't always mean “two triggers”. Many modern over-and-unders have a single trigger and a mechanism that switches to the second barrel. Some competition guns have a removable trigger group. Even so, the so-called cassette isn't a universal part, and it doesn't appear on every model. It shouldn't be taken out on the ground as a matter of routine, either. Any disassembly is done according to the manual, with the gun unloaded and in a suitable place.

When trying out an over-and-under, it's worth looking at more than just the opening. You need to see whether the top lever moves without losing control, whether the forend gets hot or slips, whether the ejectors send the cases where they should, and whether the selector is clear beyond doubt. A new action may feel stiff. But sudden hardness, an incomplete lock-up or unusual marks call for inspection, not more force.

Specific example. At the station, Luis only loads when it's his turn. He closes the gun with the barrels pointing towards the flight zone and fires. Without turning round, he opens it, checks both chambers and leaves the cases according to the ground's rules. If one hasn't fired, he keeps the gun pointed in the same direction and calls the referee. Opening the gun on his own initiative during a misfire can go against the discipline's procedure.
5.3

Semi-automatic shotgun

The over-and-under has two barrels and opens in a very visible way, which is common in many clay disciplines. Comparing it with the semi-automatic will show the difference between a sporting preference and a mechanical function.

A semi-automatic fires once each time the trigger is pulled. It uses part of the energy from the shot to extract the case, eject it, cock the mechanism and load the next cartridge. It isn't automatic: it doesn't keep firing if you hold the trigger down. The most common systems work by gas or by inertia.
