In both cases, several areas need checking: the chamber, the ejection port, the carrier and the tubular magazine.

Its main appeal is practical. The gun's cycle and, in some models, the damping systems can distribute the force differently and reduce the felt recoil. SAAMI notes that the action, the fit and the way the gun is held all influence that sensation, even though the physical impulse is still there. There's also a possible nuisance for others: the case is ejected sideways. FITASC allows semi-automatics in Universal Trench provided the ejection doesn't disturb other shooters, and requires cases to be picked up before leaving the station.

Saying that in Spain a semi-automatic “holds three cartridges” needs context. Some hunting rules limit the magazine to two cartridges. With one in the chamber, that allows three shots without reloading. The Weapons Regulation also sets separate general limits for semi-automatic long guns. On a ground, the discipline is what governs, and only the permitted cartridges are loaded when it's your turn. A hunting rule shouldn't be turned into a universal sporting capacity.

The choice between gas and inertia isn't settled by a stock phrase, either. The gas system taps off part of those gases and feeds them to a piston. It usually needs that area cleaned and checked. The inertia system uses the relative movement of masses and has different maintenance needs and a different sensitivity to assembly. Compatibility with light loads, and unloading, are checked in the model's manual and with authorised ammunition. Cartridges are never loaded for testing outside the station.

Worked example. Marta finishes two shots and leaves the action open. The instructor asks for a full demonstration: finger off the trigger, muzzle pointed in a safe direction, ejection port open, chamber visible, lifter and magazine checked. She then fits a chamber flag if the club requires one. The routine is a little slower at first. That is exactly why it needs to be learnt properly.
5.4

Side-by-side shotgun

The semi-automatic makes part of the reloading easier, but requires you to know the magazine, the chamber, the bolt and the ejection. The side-by-side adds another historical and visual reference to this basic comparison.

The side-by-side is a break-action shotgun with two barrels placed horizontally. Like the over-and-under, it opens, shows the chambers, and allows two shots. What changes, though, is the picture in front of the eye, the width of the assembly, and the balance between hands, action and weight. It can have one or two triggers, extractors or ejectors, and fixed or interchangeable chokes, depending on the era and the model.

It's associated with hunting and traditional gunmaking by history, not because it's outdated. Purdey still makes side-by-sides based on an action developed in the nineteenth century, and Beretta keeps specific manuals for its 480 series. In many modern clay disciplines you see fewer of them than over-and-unders, but FITASC Universal Trench allows this mechanism. There may be fewer of them due to commercial supply, ergonomics, more specialised stocks and ribs, or competitive habit. There's no technical rule that stops you breaking a clay just because the barrels sit side by side.

An old side-by-side calls for more care, and not because of its style. Before using modern ammunition, calibre and chamber markings, locking mechanisms, welds, bores, chokes and proof testing should all be checked. “Old” doesn't mean unsafe. “Nice-looking” doesn't mean fit for use, either. Compatibility is set by the gun's identification and a professional assessment, not by a photo or a family memory.

On mounting the gun, a beginner may notice more width near the muzzle and a different grip, especially with straight or English-style stocks. You shouldn't try to centre each barrel separately. The eye lines up with the plane the gun was designed for, and the combined pattern is checked on paper. If the two barrels centre incompatibly, the fix is a technical one. It isn't changing your aim between shots.

The double trigger, if there is one, lets you choose directly which barrel fires first. A single trigger may have a selector or a fixed sequence. This difference means you need to know the model, but it doesn't change the basic rule: every check is done with the action open, the chambers visible, and following the manual.

Personal note. I've seen side-by-sides at local shoots. That's an observation about cultural variety, not a measured national frequency. If someone brings an inherited one, the useful conversation isn't whether it's out of fashion. It's what its chamber is marked for, what cartridge it allows, how its safety works, and when a gunsmith last checked it.
5.5

Hunting shotguns and clay shotguns

The side-by-side keeps its usefulness and its tradition, even though today it isn't the most common choice on every ground. More important than the position of the barrels is telling apart a hunting shotgun from one set up for clays.

“For hunting” and “for clays” indicate design priorities, not closed borders. In hunting, what may matter is walking for hours, reacting to an irregular opportunity, and carrying a lighter gun. On a ground, dozens or hundreds of shots are repeated. The gun is mounted almost the same way over and over, and it's valued for keeping its balance, lock-up and comfort through intense sessions. This difference helps explain why a competition shotgun tends to be heavier, adjustable and more expensive. But it doesn't prove that a field gun can't work for starting out.

Weight shows the trade-off well. With the same ammunition, more mass reduces the speed and energy of free recoil. SAAMI notes, though, that the recoil you feel also depends on the action, the recoil pad, the length, the drop, the angle of the recoil pad — called pitch — and the way you hold the gun. More mass stabilises the movement and can smooth out the session. It can also tire small arms and make changing direction less agile. Less mass makes the gun easier to carry and bring up, but increases control errors and transmits a different impulse.

The stock also changes the task. The stock is the part that rests against the shoulder, the face and the hand. Browning explains that the eye acts as the rear sight. It also explains that a straighter stock, with less drop, is used on trap and skeet guns for targets that tend to rise. A clay shotgun may add an adjustable comb, a raised rib, weights and long barrels. A sporting gun looks for stability without losing the ability to handle varied targets. No geometry makes up for a painful or poorly repeatable gun mount.

Cost should be looked at over the equipment's whole life. A cheap option that doesn't allow spares, adjustment or servicing can end up expensive. An over-specified competition platform can be unnecessary for someone who still doesn't know which discipline they prefer. On top of the budget, you need to add inspection, a case, chokes, tools and possible stock changes. Trying out the club's equipment lets you wait until you have your own criteria.

Worked example. Diego starts out with a hunting shotgun checked over and lent by the club. After two rounds he notices cheek slap and has to duck to keep sight of the clay. Before buying a trap gun, the instructor checks length of pull, drop, eye dominance and point of impact. Together they see that a temporary adjustment and a more repeatable gun mount solve part of the problem. The rest points towards a better-informed purchase.
Personal note. In one morning I've gone past a hundred shots. I mention it to show how intense a single session can be, not as an average or a figure to aim for.
5.6

Trap and sporting

Weight, stock, barrels and balance depend on the intended use; a well-known brand doesn't guarantee the gun will suit the shooter. Even within clay shooting, Trap and Sporting have different needs.

A trap gun is set up for a fairly specific family of clays. They come out ahead, move away and often rise. A sporting gun has to handle targets that come in, go out, cross, drop, roll or change distance a great deal. That leads to different trends. Trap favours stability, a higher stock and a raised point of impact. Sporting looks for a more neutral picture and controlled switching between different flight paths. These are optimisations, not bans on cross-use.

Catalogues show these differences without giving a single recipe. Trap tends to look for more stability; sporting, more ease in changing flight path. Barrel length has an influence, but it doesn't classify the gun on its own. The stock, the rib, the weight and its distribution also count.

The regulations also change. FITASC Universal Trench allows up to 28 g of lead and defines five machines and schemes. Sporting regulations organise varied presentations. In its events, ISSF controls a maximum nominal load of 24 g with a control tolerance. To choose cartridge, choke and barrel order, you need to read the current edition of the discipline's rules. It isn't enough to apply “trap equals 24 g” to anything that has a trap.

Someone who switches between disciplines can use a single shotgun and change the set-up, use sets of barrels, or have dedicated guns. Each option costs learning. If eye height, weight or swing speed change, the body's reference point changes too. For a beginner, it's usually more useful to keep one set-up over several sessions and note which limitation appears before adding another variable.

Worked example. Sara uses a sporting gun with a mid-height rib on Universal Trench and feels comfortable, but it blocks some high clays. She doesn't buy a trap gun straight away. First she tests at the pattern plate to see where her gun shoots and works on her gun mount. Then she compares a higher stock. She decides on the basis of a repeated pattern and comfort, not because the commercial name says so.
5.7

Parts of a shotgun

Trap calls for different movements and ways of presenting the gun from Sporting, so the equipment should be chosen according to the discipline. Before making adjustments, it helps to know the parts of the shotgun.

Learning the parts isn't about memorising a diagram. It's useful for reporting an issue without touching what you don't understand. From back to front come the recoil pad, the stock, the comb and the grip. Next come the action, the top lever, the safety catch, the selector if there is one, the trigger guard and the trigger. Ahead are the forend, the chambers, the barrels, the rib, the bead, the muzzle and the chokes. On a semi-automatic, the bolt, the ejection port, the carrier, the magazine and the release controls are added.

The chamber holds the cartridge. It isn't the same as the barrel. The extractor lifts the case and the ejector can throw it clear. The action joins the lock-up and the firing mechanism on a break-action gun. Some competition models have a removable trigger group, but calling every shotgun's group a cassette is a mistake. Chokes are the fixed constriction or the interchangeable tube at the muzzle. The rib is the top plane that guides peripheral vision, not a telescopic sight.

The names vary between Spain, Latin America and each manufacturer's own translations: carrillera or lomo, banda or solista, báscula or receptor. What matters is matching each word to the real part in the manual. During a malfunction, it's enough to say “the action won't open” or “the case is stuck in the chamber”. There's no need to diagnose a broken ejector. That diagnosis is for the person in charge or the gunsmith.

There are also parts that look alike but don't do the same thing. The recoil pad transmits the support to the shoulder. The heel and the toe are its ends. The forend is for gripping, and it completes the lock-up of the break-action assembly. The trigger guard protects the trigger. The muzzle is the end of the barrel, and never a handle. Naming them correctly helps give short, clear safety instructions.

A personal record with a photo and the manufacturer's terms stops the wrong names being carried from one gun to another. It also makes it possible to point out a fault to the person in charge without inventing words or handling the gun to show it off.

Worked example. Before the first practice session, the instructor hands over an open, unloaded over-and-under. The student points out the chambers, muzzle, safety, trigger and top lever without operating anything. Then they repeat the identification on a semi-automatic with the bolt locked back. This shows why “it's open” is demonstrated differently on each mechanism.
5.8

The stock and the body

Knowing the names of the barrels, the action, the trigger, the safety catch, the forend and the stock lets you follow clear instructions. The stock is the part that directly connects the gun to the body.

The stock positions the shoulder, the face and, above all, the eye. Since the shotgun doesn't usually have a rear sight, the eye takes on that role. That's why length, drop, cast and the angle of the recoil pad matter. Two people of the same height may need different measurements.

Length runs from the trigger to the recoil pad. If there's too much, it makes the gun mount harder; if there's too little, it cramps the posture. Drop sets the height of the eye, cast centres it sideways, and the angle of the recoil pad spreads the support. No single measurement should be adjusted on its own.

Pain in the shoulder or cheekbone isn't part of learning. It can point to poor support, an unsuitable stock, tension or too many rounds. If it persists, you need to stop and find the cause.

Personal note. Where I shoot, I've heard that soreness or swelling on the face called a “caramelo” (literally “sweet”). It's a field term, not a clinical one.

Your dominant hand isn't enough on its own to choose the side or the fit of the stock. Your supporting shoulder and dominant eye matter too. The simple dominant-eye test is explained in 6.10, Looking at the barrel instead of the clay. If the dominant eye and the shooting shoulder don't match, an instructor should assess the posture and any possible visual aids before modifying the gun.

At some grounds there's a practice for checking from the front whether the eye, the rib and the bead line up. This is an inherently dangerous manoeuvre, because a person stands in front of the muzzles. It must never be improvised or carried out between shooters. It can only be directed by a competent instructor or the person in charge of the facility, with the shotgun open and completely unloaded, the chambers checked both physically and visually, and all ammunition removed from the area. Safer alternatives that don't put anyone in front of the barrels should always be preferred. This observation can reveal a misalignment, but it doesn't replace several gun mounts or a patterning test.

Winter clothing changes the effective length and the support. That's why the fit should be checked in normal conditions, without chasing tiny differences every week.

Worked example. Pablo repeats five unloaded gun mounts. The instructor sees him tilt his head, corrects his posture first, and only then adjusts the comb. Finally they check the result on a pattern plate.
5.9

Adjustable comb

A suitable stock always places the eye, shoulder and hands in a similar way; an unsuitable one forces you to correct every gun mount. An adjustable comb changes the height of the eye relative to the rib.

The adjustable comb moves up and down and, usually, sideways too. It's used to position the eye comfortably and repeatably relative to the rib. Raising it usually raises the point of impact; moving it sideways centres the eye without tilting the head.

Seeing more of the rib can help keep a rising clay in view, instead of blocking it with the barrel. But the comb doesn't “raise the pellets” on its own. It changes the relationship between eye and gun, and the shooter reorients the whole assembly. An adjustable rib can genuinely change the point of impact mechanically on some models. Stock and rib can give similar results on the pattern plate by different geometric routes.

Personal note. In my case, seeing more rib made it obvious that I could keep the rising clay in view instead of blocking it with the barrel.

The comb shouldn't be moved after every miss. A low shot can also come from lifting your head, stopping the gun, or misreading the flight path. You start from a recorded position, make small changes, and check the point of impact.

With the gun unloaded and without firing, a simple reference is sometimes used on the ground: placing two one-euro coins, one on top of the other, at the back of the rib. If, on mounting the gun, the bead is still visible above them, there's visible rib gain. This is only a rough guide; it doesn't replace professional fitting or a patterning plate. A high set-up lets you see more of the clay and aim slightly below it. That picture can require some adjustment when going back to a hunting shotgun, with which many shooters cover more of the clay.

The posts must stay firm and within the manual's limits. The starting position is marked so it can be recovered. After every change, unloaded gun mounts are practised and checked to make sure nothing moves.

Worked example. Elena loses the clay when she covers it with the barrel. After several low patterns with a stable gun mount, the instructor raises the comb slightly, notes the change and repeats the test. If it improves without causing discomfort, she keeps the adjustment for several sessions.
5.10

Fully adjustable stocks

Raising the comb can change where the pattern hits relative to what we see when aiming, but it has to be measured and tested. Stocks with more adjustment offer more possibilities, though they also make it easier to lose a stable reference.

A fully adjustable stock lets you adjust more than just the comb. It can change length of pull, comb height and side position, rotation, pitch, toe-out and the position of the recoil pad. Some systems add damping and balancing weights. Its reasonable promise isn't to hit the clay on its own. It's to let different people position their eye, face and shoulder without carving a new stock for every test.

There are three routes. A manufacturer can build turrets and weights into wood, as B-Fast does. It can also offer a modular structure, like the TSK options cited by Beretta. Or a stockmaker can measure, cut, add mechanisms, or make one to measure. Browning documents adjustable comb, recoil pad and length on trap models. A Spanish patent describes a fully adjustable stock for personalised fitting. That shows there's local development, though a patent doesn't prove the availability, quality or suitability of any specific product.

Personal note. I knew of “LB” as a Spanish example, but in this review I haven't found a clear, public primary catalogue that lets me identify a manufacturer, model and specifications under that name. So I'm keeping it as a personal reference, not a documented recommendation. What the book can state is that stock-fitting workshops and stockmakers do operate in Spain. But any choice you make needs verifiable trade identity, a budget, a warranty and authorised work.

A made-to-measure wooden stock and an adjustable metal structure solve problems in different ways. The first can spread the contact continuously and give less temptation to change it. The second makes it easier to test, repeat measurements, or adapt to clothing and bodily changes. They also change the look, weight and stiffness. The decision should include how the adjustments will be kept in place during transport and use.

Added weight at the back shifts the balance. It can also change how quickly the gun starts or stops moving. That's why a fit that's comfortable for the body still needs to be tested in motion. A stock that positions the eye well can change the feel of the swing if it changes the mass distribution a lot.

Stops, scales and screws must let you return to a reference point. Without a record, adjustment turns into a string of sensations that can't be compared.

Worked example. A shooter changes length of pull, comb and butt plate on the same day and hits two more clays. He doesn't know what helped. A traceable adjustment starts with measurements and photos. First it fixes the length to allow a free mount. Then it adapts the fit of the butt plate, centres the eye, and only then checks the height. Every step is recorded and can be undone.
5.11

The rib

Having more adjustment options doesn't mean the shotgun ends up better: every change needs a reason, a measurement and a way back. The rib lets the eye see part of that geometric relationship.

The rib is the long surface sitting above the barrels. In Spanish it has two names, banda and solista, depending on the region and the maker. It gives a visual plane, reduces glare when it's well finished, and holds the bead. It can be flat, stepped, raised, ventilated or adjustable. Its look affects what the shooter perceives. But in clay shooting, the main focus stays on the clay. The rib and the bead are noticed peripherally, to keep the alignment.

Staring fixedly at the bead turns the task of intercepting a clay into static aiming. The clay loses sharpness, the swing becomes deliberate, and it tends to stop. But completely ignoring the rib doesn't fix an irregular gun mount either. During preparation, you can check that eye, rib and beads are aligned. When the clay comes out, the gaze goes to the clay and the gun follows from peripheral vision.

A raised rib can allow a more upright stance and leave more space below the clay. An adjustable one can change the point of impact on models designed for that. Beretta documents a front-and-rear B-Fast mechanism. Krieghoff explains that lowering its rib relative to the barrel raises the point of impact on certain models. So the intuitive rule “raising the rib always raises the shot” doesn't hold. It depends on the pivot point and the manual.

The mid-bead, if there is one, is mainly used to check that the eye isn't off-centre. It doesn't create a sighting line like on a rifle. If the two beads seem to come apart on mounting, the cause may be the height of the face or the tilt of the gun. This check is done before calling for the target. During the flight, trying to line up a geometric shape again takes attention away from the movement.

The width, the matte finish and the ventilation change the contrast and the feeling of heat. They don't replace a stock that positions the eye well. A rib that feels comfortable at the gunsmith's should be tried out in the actual light and stance of the ground.

Worked example. Javier swaps a white bead for an orange fibre-optic sight because he can see it more clearly. In his first training session he starts watching it and misses behind. The instructor doesn't remove the fibre sight. He has Javier do three unloaded gun mounts to confirm alignment, then asks him to focus on the leading edge of the clay. The accessory stays as a reference point, not a target.
5.12

Chokes

The rib helps position the eye relative to the gun, although during the shot your gaze should stay on the clay. The choke doesn't change that direction; it changes the shape and density of the pattern, which we'll look at now.

The choke is a narrowing at the muzzle. It can be fixed or interchangeable. It doesn't add accuracy or power: it changes how the cloud of pellets opens up. Generally, more constriction keeps more density at distance; a wider opening spreads the pattern sooner.

Many specialised trap shotguns, especially traditional models and set-ups, have fixed chokes. Other modern trap shotguns use interchangeable tubes. You shouldn't assume a set-up from the name of the discipline: check the barrel markings and the model's manual. A fixed choke should only be modified by a qualified gunsmith.

The names Cylinder, Modified or Full are families, not universal measurements. Stars and notches should be checked against the manufacturer's table. A tube that fits may not be rated for the material or load you've chosen. A threaded barrel is never fired without a choke tube fitted.

An over-and-under can use a different choke in each barrel. The first is usually more open and the second tighter, because the clay is moving away, though this doesn't apply to every discipline. A wide pattern doesn't fix a big error. The actual combination is only known by patterning both barrels.

The tube must be clean, undamaged and properly seated. It's checked with the gun unloaded. If it's loose, protrudes or shows damage, stop using it until a competent person checks it.

Extended chokes are easier to identify, but they're still parts under pressure. Their brand doesn't guarantee compatibility.

Worked example. Using the same shotgun and cartridges, an instructor compares several patterns for each choke. He looks at centring, density and evenness. A single cartridge doesn't represent normal variation.
5.13

Cartridges

The choke doesn't move the centre of the shot: it changes how the pellets are distributed as distance increases. That distribution also depends on the cartridge used.

A shotgun cartridge brings together the case, the head, the primer, the powder, the wad and the pellets. On the box, you need to check the calibre, length, load, pellet size and material. You also need to read any indication of velocity and use. Calibre 12 isn't enough on its own. The chamber length marked on the barrel limits which fired case length is allowed. Browning's manual warns that using a length greater than the chamber can dangerously raise pressure.

Grams indicate pellet mass, not powder or total “power”. ISSF controls a maximum nominal load of 24.0 g with a test tolerance of 0.5 g, and a maximum diameter under its rules. FITASC Universal 2025 allows up to 28 g of lead with a 2% tolerance and regular pellets of up to 2.5 mm plus tolerance. RIO sells 24 g and 28 g options in calibre 12. These figures belong to specific products and disciplines. The club can impose additional materials or limits.

A 28 g load contains more pellet mass than a 24 g load of the same size and material. But the exact figure requires measuring diameter, density and manufacturing variation. More mass or more velocity tends to increase the impulse and the free recoil. SAAMI also includes the wad and the gases in the calculation. The advertised velocity depends on a measurement method. It doesn't guarantee that two cartridges will give the same flight time, pattern or feel in a specific shotgun.

The batch and how it's been kept matter. Deformed cases, damp, corrosion or cartridges of doubtful origin are set aside following the manufacturer's and the ground's instructions. Calibres aren't mixed in your pocket. A smaller cartridge can slip dangerously into a larger barrel and become stuck as an obstruction. The inspection is done before loading. Ammunition is never repaired or reloaded during a round.

The original box keeps the batch number and specifications. That information is needed if an anomaly appears or pattern results are compared. Keeping batches separate stops you blaming the choke or the technique for a variation in ammunition.

Worked example. Before a competition, Nuria doesn't switch from 24 to 28 g on the strength of informal advice. She reads the rulebook and the entry notice, checks the chamber and the manual, confirms the permitted pellet size, and tests the pattern and recoil with time to spare. If she trains for two disciplines, she logs each cartridge by batch so she doesn't put a change in ammunition down to the gun.
5.14

Personal equipment

The load, the pellet size and the muzzle velocity influence the number of pellets, the recoil, the spread and the energy they retain. Personal equipment completes the preparation and protects the shooter.

Personal equipment starts with two protections, not the vest: hearing and eyes. The ISSF 2026 guide requires adequate hearing protection near the line and impact-resistant glasses for athletes, referees and officials. FITASC Universal requires hearing and eye protection at its event. Tinted lenses can improve contrast in certain light. But they don't replace impact resistance, a good fit, peripheral vision, or a mount that doesn't shift when you bring the gun up.

The noise of a gun is impulsive. It can cause cumulative or immediate damage. NIOSH recommends double protection, plugs and muffs, for gun noise in its occupational guidance. On a specific outdoor ground, the choice should take into account measurement, frequency, fit and rules. Thick glasses can break the seal of the muffs. Electronic protectors make conversation easier in some situations. But ISSF bans athletes and coaches from using devices with sound amplification or reception on the competition ground, except for authorised exceptions.

The vest gives an even surface at the shoulder and accessible pockets. It shouldn't carry loose cartridges mixed with other objects. A cloth helps dry your hands or remove outside moisture, but it doesn't authorise cleaning a loaded gun. A cap and glasses help with the light. Stable footwear stops slips. Clothing should let you turn your torso and mount the gun without stiff folds. Gloves can improve grip or warmth if they keep your feel and don't get in the way of the trigger guard and controls.

A full bag carries what you need to stay organised, not a portable workshop: documentation, spare protection, glasses in a case, a cloth, water and the gun's choke tool. Medication, heat and fatigue can affect control and attention. Equipment doesn't fix them. If your glasses steam up or a hearing protector stops you hearing the referee, you step out of the sequence and sort it out before carrying on.

Every item should be placed without encroaching on the neighbouring station or forcing you to leave the gun propped up in some improvised way. The order of the vest should also stay the same from round to round.

Worked example. On a covered shooting ground one morning, Álvaro notices his glasses' arm is pushing his earmuffs open. Before shooting, he switches to a compatible model and checks his ear plugs are properly fitted. He carries 25 cartridges in one pocket and spent cases in another, his cloth clipped to his vest, and his phone on silent. Being organised cuts down on fiddling while others are shooting.
5.15

Basic maintenance

Glasses, hearing protection, footwear and clothing need to work together, not just be separate accessories. To keep everything safe, both the gun and the equipment need to be checked, cleaned and maintained.

Maintenance starts before cleaning: gun unloaded, ammunition out of the area, a safe direction, and the manual open at the right model. Barrels and chambers are checked without looking down the muzzle. The condition and tightness of the chokes, the lock-up, the forend and any visible damage are also checked. If there's an obstruction, deformation, crack, loose part or odd behaviour, you don't try “just to see if it fires”. It's put aside and a gunsmith is consulted.

After a day's shooting, an over-and-under usually needs disassembly to the authorised level, cleaning of the bores and chambers, removal of plastic or lead residue, drying and a light film of protection. The break-action surfaces get the grease specified by the manufacturer. Chokes are removed with the gun unloaded. The thread and housing are cleaned, lightly lubricated and reinstalled with their tool. Beretta warns that cleaning or firing a threaded barrel without a tube fitted can dirty, damage or block the thread.

More oil doesn't mean more care. It can trap dirt, get into the wood or the mechanisms, and affect how the gun works. A semi-automatic adds maintenance of the bolt, the magazine and the gas or inertia system according to the manual. Instructions for one action aren't applied to another. Before reassembling, you check that no patch is left in the bore. Internal repairs and adjustments are for the manufacturer, an authorised service centre or the gunsmith.

The final inspection isn't just about the metal shining. You check that the bore is clear, that surfaces are dry where the manual doesn't call for lubrication, and that no part or tool is missing. Wood, polymers and finishes get compatible products. A solvent suited to barrel deposits can damage varnish, adhesives or the recoil pad. The gun is stored once its moisture has settled. It's never left damp inside a closed case. Before the next outing, an unloaded functional check is repeated, because transport and storage can also loosen parts or reveal corrosion.

Chapter closing map. Maintaining a gun well means first recognising what you're inspecting. Go back to 5.7, Parts of a shotgun, to locate the barrels, action, lock-up, forend and other essential parts. The review in 5.8, The stock and the body, and the adjustments in 5.9 and 5.10, are a reminder that cleaning doesn't authorise changing measurements or replace a professional fitting. Finally, 5.12, Chokes, and 5.13, Cartridges, connect constriction, the actual gun-and-ammunition combination, and patterning distance. Maintenance keeps that combination in safe condition, but only a test on the pattern plate lets you check its pattern.

Worked example. After a wet session, Carmen unloads and transports her shotgun according to the rules. At home, she separates the ammunition, cleans from the chamber outwards, dries the metal, checks the chokes and notes that the action is opening stiffly. She doesn't force the top lever or file any surfaces. She stores the gun where no one else can access it and arranges a check-up before the next round.

What we have learned

The shotgun is a system, not a category of prestige. The mechanism determines how it loads, unloads and shows its state. Specialising in trap or sporting changes the priorities for balance, stock, rib and pattern. The right choice starts with a supervised trial, not with a catalogue.

  • The eye acts as the rear reference: the stock and the comb must position it the same way every time, without pain.
  • The rib, the comb and the point of impact are related, but they aren't the same thing and don't follow one universal rule.
  • The choke and the cartridge produce a pattern that belongs to that combination; only an actual patterning test shows the centre, density and variation.
  • Glasses and hearing protection are part of shooting, and maintenance ends with safe, legal storage.

Expert reading. The simulator can isolate rib gain, velocity and spread to teach cause-and-effect relationships. It doesn't reproduce felt recoil, moment of inertia, deformation of the cloud, the mechanical cycle or fitting. That boundary needs to be clear when the reader carries a digital observation over to an actual gun.

Chapter 06

Why a beginner misses the clay

A miss doesn't have a single cause. The pattern — that is, the cloud of pellets — can arrive late, low or off the flight path. The eye can stop tracking the clay. The head can change the alignment. The gun can slow down even though the shooter thinks they're still moving it. From the station, several causes feel almost the same: the clay stays whole and there's little information left to work out what happened.

This chapter turns twelve common phrases heard on the ground into questions that can be observed. It isn't meant to diagnose from a distance or replace the instructor. It separates what's backed by studies and manuals, what depends on the discipline or on each person, and personal comments marked as such. The goal isn't to pile up corrections. The goal is to pick a single hypothesis and test it safely.

6.1

First instinct deceives

The shotgun, stock, choke, cartridge and protective gear form a set that needs to be fitted and maintained properly. Now that we know the equipment, we can look at why a shot misses even when it looks right from the outside.

The first question comes up straight away: if the bead looks aligned with the clay, why doesn't it break? Because that image is of the present moment. The meeting with the pattern will happen an instant later.

This first intuition comes from tasks where the result seems immediate. We point at an icon on a screen, touch a ball at rest, or aim at a fixed mark. In those cases, the position we see still holds good by the time the action finishes. A clay is different. It's a moving target, and it doesn't wait for the shooter to finish looking, deciding and moving. By the time the pellets reach the target's distance, the clay is no longer where it was when it started.

This doesn't mean the shooter has to work out a formula as the clay comes out. It means picturing where the meeting point will actually happen. Research on visuomotor interception, which studies how the body intercepts moving objects, describes exactly that: actions that aim to coincide with a moving object even though there are delays in vision and in movement. The nervous system works with recent information plus prediction. If you react only to the current position, you arrive late when the target is moving fast.

There are also two separate time spans that shouldn't be mixed up. One belongs to the person: detecting, recognising and organising the movement. The other belongs to the shot: leaving the barrel and covering the distance. The second one doesn't disappear just because the cartridge is fast. Ballistic calculators call the time it takes the projectile to arrive the time of flight. They also define lead as the correction needed for a moving target. In a real shotgun, the pellet cloud and its loss of velocity complicate the detail, but the basic idea stays the same: during that interval, the clay keeps moving.

Example: a clay crosses from left to right. The beginner picks it up visually, brings the barrel to its edge and fires just as the two appear to overlap. If the centre of the pattern then crosses the flight line, it may meet the trail, not the clay. A useful correction isn't simply telling them to “see more gap”. It might be starting the movement earlier, keeping a separation learned with the instructor, or continuing the swing — that is, the follow-through movement — through the shot.

Personal note. Watching beginners, I've often thought of a video game: they put the reference point right on the clay and expect an instant response. It's a comparison to explain the idea, not a universal measurement. You can explore this in the simulator by repeating the same trajectory: one attempt aimed at the current position and another aimed at a future zone.
Technical reading. In the simplest version, the future point depends on the target's speed and the total time to the meeting point. That time includes the human part before the shot and the pellets' flight afterwards. Even so, it's best not to add them up as universal constants: they change with the task, the distance, the trajectory, the shooter and the cartridge. The infographic represents the relationship, not a certified scale.
6.2

Delays add up

Aiming at the spot where we see the clay ignores the fact that the clay is moving and that the shooter needs time to respond. To understand this delay, we'll break down all the time spans that add up.

There's no single delay called “reaction”. The sequence includes detecting, deciding, moving, actuating, firing and waiting for the pellets to fly. Meanwhile, the clay keeps going.

The eye doesn't hand over a complete trajectory the moment an orange blur appears. First you have to pick the clay out from the background. Then you have to read its direction and apparent speed, and decide how to start the movement. Then the body accelerates an object with weight, the hand completes the intended action and the shot leaves the gun. Finally, the pellets cover the distance. Calling all of this “reaction speed” hides where the problem might actually be.

Experimental research on interception shows that vision-guided corrections arrive with latency — that is, with a delay. A 2026 study found approximately 114 milliseconds to respond to position jumps in two manual on-screen tasks. That figure shouldn't be carried straight over to a shotgun: the people, the movement, the setting and the task were all different. What it does confirm is a more modest and solid idea: new visual information doesn't change the action instantaneously.

Nor should these steps be pictured as a rigid queue that never overlaps. The gaze can keep updating the trajectory while the gun is moving. The shooter can correct during the swing. Experience helps anticipate regularities. Reviews of visuomotor control describe a mix of prediction and ongoing adjustment. That's why an expert doesn't just have “faster reflexes”: they read the important information sooner and move the gun more steadily.

Example: two shooters miss the same crosser behind. The first spotted the clay late because they were looking too close to the trap. The second saw it in time, passed it, but stopped the gun when pulling the trigger. The result may look similar, but the correction isn't. The first shooter is helped by reviewing their gaze zone. The second, by maintaining continuity. Telling both of them “more lead” can hide the cause and add tension.

For a beginner, the chain should work as a practical checklist. When did the clay come into clear focus? Did the movement start smoothly or in a rush? Was the gap still growing at the moment of firing? Did the barrel keep moving afterwards? There's no need to answer all of this during a round. It's enough for instructor and pupil to pick one observable step and compare it across several similar repetitions.

Specific exercise. Repeat the same clay in the simulator and split your observation into two rounds. In the first, pay attention only to when you recognise the direction. In the second, pay attention only to whether the movement continues after the shot. Don't change speed, trajectory and reference point all at once.
Limit of the model. A drawn timeline doesn't prove how long each phase takes for a given person. Measuring that would need synchronised eye tracking, gun movement, trigger signal and trajectory. The infographic shows cause and order: every process takes time, and the clay keeps moving. It doesn't offer regulatory or clinical timings.
6.3

Falling behind the clay

Seeing the clay, deciding, moving the gun and waiting for the pellets to arrive all take time, while the clay keeps moving. The most common result is that the pattern ends up behind.

“You're behind” tells you where the centre of the shot passes relative to the clay. It doesn't by itself show why it happened. Separating result from cause stops you applying the same fix to different misses.

On the ground, being behind means that the useful part of the pattern crosses the zone the clay has already passed through. The clearest case is a side crosser: the clay moves sideways and the cloud is left in its trail. On a near-straight-away, the phrase is harder to read, because the error can show up as height and depth instead. “Behind” always has to be read against the actual trajectory, not against the edge of the screen or the horizon.

You often hear that this is the most common beginner's mistake. The claim matches the experience of many instructors, but we haven't found population-level data that would give it a universal prevalence. There is concrete evidence that higher-level shooters show steadier gun movement and an earlier, longer final gaze than sub-elite shooters. That finding supports working on visual stability and gun movement. It doesn't support claiming that every single miss was behind.

The same result can be reached by several routes. The shooter might detect the clay late, start from a poorly chosen hold point, accelerate suddenly, try to measure the gap by looking at the barrel, or stop the gun when firing. They might also misread the speed or the distance. The faster and more sideways the clay looks, or the longer the time of flight, the more the required separation shows up. But “adding gap” without sorting out the movement can produce a miss in front on the next attempt.

Example: a clay crosses from right to left. The pupil manages to get past the leading edge, hesitates to check the gap, and pulls the trigger once the gun has already lost angular speed. The mental picture before the shot was correct, but the meeting point wasn't. During the hesitation, the clay moved on and the barrel slowed down. The useful cue might be “keep your eyes on the clay and follow through the shot”, not necessarily “aim a metre further ahead”.

The analysis should rest on patterns, not a single result. If several similar crossers miss behind and the footage or the instructor spot a stop, a strong hypothesis emerges. If some end up in front and others behind, it's worth reviewing gun mount, gaze and rhythm before settling on a lead figure. Fragments are also misleading: breaking the trailing edge might suggest a centre that's behind, but the uneven distribution of the pattern means you can't turn every break into an exact measurement.

Single-variable test. Keep the same trajectory and ask the instructor to watch only whether the barrel keeps moving. If the movement doesn't stop but the pattern still lands behind, then change the meeting point. Safety and ground procedure take priority over the exercise.
Expert reading. “Behind” is a relative classification in the meeting plane. Attributing a cause would need, at minimum, the gun's angular position, its angular velocity before and after the shot, the clay's trajectory and an estimate of the pattern. The simulator can show the geometry; it shouldn't present a psychological cause as if it had been measured.
6.4

Shooting under the clay

Being behind describes where the shot landed, but it can stem from a late start, a stop, or too little lead. If the shot lands low, we need to look separately at the vertical axis.

On a rising clay, “low” can describe where the pattern passes. It doesn't automatically identify whether the cause was the eyes, the body, the gun or the timing.

Being low means the useful centre of the pattern passes below the clay at the meeting point. The definition sounds simple, but it isn't always easy to judge from the station. A clay that's rising and moving away changes apparent size, passes across different backgrounds, and can look like it's climbing less once it starts losing vertical speed. The shooter has to track that climb without forgetting the sideways movement or completely blocking the reference.

One possible cause is reading the flight path as flatter than it is. Another is stopping the gun's rise when firing. The relationship between the eye and the rib can also shift. The rib is the top surface of the barrels that the eye uses as a reference. If the cheek loses its repeatable contact, or if the stock puts the eye at a different height, the direction the shooter thinks they're giving doesn't match the actual line. Manufacturer manuals confirm that comb height and cast are used to keep the dominant eye consistently aligned with the plane of the rib.

That doesn't turn stock adjustment into an instant cure. Browning, for example, documents trap guns whose point of impact sits above the point of aim to keep the clay visible. It also documents models with adjustable rib or comb. These are facts about specific guns, not a universal recommendation. The point of impact should be checked with a pattern test. Any modification must be made with the gun unloaded, following safe procedure and with competent help.

Example: the pupil breaks side clays but keeps going low on a very steep straight-away. Before raising the comb, the instructor watches several attempts. They see the cheek lifting off at the end and the barrel stopping its rise right as the shot goes off. The equipment wasn't the main variable after all. Work focuses on a comfortable mount, visual tracking and follow-through. Afterwards, if the trend continues with a repeatable gun mount, the point of impact of the gun-and-shooter combination is checked.

You also have to avoid over-correcting. After hearing “low”, a beginner might suddenly jerk the barrels up and lose the clay, or force their head into an awkward position. A useful correction is small in scale and comes with something to watch for: keeping the clay visible, keeping the cheek in the same place, and finishing the movement above the previous line. If pain shows up, the test stops. An uncomfortable position is never made routine just to land one isolated hit.

Checking sequence. First confirm with the instructor that the trajectory is genuinely rising at the point of the shot. Then check for vertical continuity and cheek contact. Only with a repeatable gun mount does it make sense to check the point of impact and consider adjusting the gun.
Separating variables. The line of sight, the barrels' axis and the pattern's statistical centre are three different references. Raising the eye by adjusting the comb can change how they relate, but the result depends on the specific gun and must be verified by patterning. The infographic simplifies the pattern as a centred cloud; a real one is irregular.
6.5

Covering the clay

A low shot can stem from misreading the trajectory, from the gun mount, or from the relationship between the eye and the stock. Correcting it by blocking the clay can also create a visibility problem.

Leading doesn't always mean losing the clay behind the barrels. “Blocking it” describes one specific picture. It depends on the trajectory, the technique, and the relationship between eye, stock and rib.

From the shooter's viewpoint, blocking the clay means part of the gun comes between the eye and the clay until it hides it. This can happen on a rising clay when the barrel line passes above the image of the clay. It can also happen with a shooting method that deliberately crosses its trajectory. The difficulty starts when the shooter runs out of visual information. They can no longer read direction, apparent speed or continuity properly.

“Putting the barrel ahead” shouldn't be confused with “looking at the barrel”. In shotgun shooting, the gun can sit ahead in peripheral vision while central attention stays on the clay. The NSCA distinguishes visual connection with the clay from consciously checking the gap. When the shooter looks at the near reference to measure it, visual tracking is interrupted and the movement can slow down.

The picture also depends on the equipment. The height of the eye above the rib and the shotgun's point of impact determine how much barrel is visible and where the centre of the pattern sits relative to the apparent reference. Some trap models are designed or regulated to shoot above the point of aim, making it easier to keep the clay visible. That's manufacturer data for specific setups. It doesn't mean every beginner should raise their stock, or that there's an ideal ratio for everyone.

Personal note. With a shotgun that forces you to cover a rising clay, I've felt that the first shot can wipe out the reference point and make the second one harder. Actual visibility changes with the trajectory, the method, the gun mount and the fit. So it isn't a universal rule. It does help to ask one specific question: after the first shot, did your eye stay connected to the clay, or did you have to find it again?

Example: on a straight-away, the pupil raises the barrel until the clay disappears and fires from memory. If they miss, they lift their head to find it again, and the second attempt starts from a different alignment. Before touching the gun, the instructor checks the gun mount, the point of impact and the method chosen. They might teach a reference that keeps the clay in view, or confirm that the setup needs a professional evaluation. The correction is verified with a pattern test, not by feel.

Field question. After the first shot, try to describe just this: “I kept seeing the clay” or “I had to find it again”. That answer is more useful than immediately assuming you needed more height. Don't adjust an adjustable comb mid-round without unloading the gun and following the manufacturer's and the ground's procedure.
Optics and geometry. Occlusion depends on the angle formed by the barrels and the rib as seen from the eye, not just the straight-line distance to the clay. A small change in eye height alters the image and its relationship to the line of the shot. Only a patterning test establishes the true point of impact of the whole system.
6.6

Lifting the head

Sometimes the clay gets blocked because the eye and gun are set too flat, not because it's a technique recommended for everyone. If we lift our head off the stock to see it again, that also changes the direction of the shot.

The urge to check whether you've hit the clay can lift the cheek off the stock at the worst possible moment. The movement changes the eye's alignment and also breaks the shot's visual continuity.

Lifting the head breaks the eye's position relative to the rib. On a shotgun with no rear sight, the eye acts as the rear reference. If the cheek lifts off the stock, the shooter stops looking from the point the gun was fitted for.

The movement can start before the shot out of fear of blocking the clay. It can happen at the same instant, through anticipation. Or it can come afterwards, from the rush to check whether the clay broke. All three variants look alike from the outside, but they affect different phases. The last one is the most deceptive: the shooter thinks they've already finished, even though the follow-through keeps the alignment going and, if there's a second shot, keeps the visual reference needed to correct it.

Causer and colleagues' research didn't specifically study “lifting the head”. It did find that, in trap, skeet and double trap, an earlier and longer final gaze was associated with higher level and with successful attempts. The reasonable conclusion is to favour a stable visual connection. It isn't a claim that any long gaze guarantees a hit. Gun movement, trajectory and pattern still play a part.

Example: the first shot doesn't break the clay. The pupil lifts their cheek to look over the barrels, rushes to put it back down and takes the second shot from a different height. Even though they “aim” at the same spot, the eye-rib relationship has changed twice. The instructor can ask them to finish the movement while keeping comfortable contact, and let the result come into view on its own, instead of hunting for it with their head.

“Keeping the cheek down” doesn't mean crushing it against the stock or putting up with pain either. Enough pressure is whatever gives stable contact without excessive tension. If a correct gun mount forces an uncomfortable position, the problem may be one of fit, not of willpower. A good diagnosis separates habit, anticipation and gun geometry before repeating rounds with the same discomfort.

Observable sign. Ask the instructor to watch the distance between cheekbone and comb from the moment you call for the target until the end of the follow-through. The student should concentrate on the clay, not try to watch their own cheek while shooting.
Point of impact. Raising the eye changes the visual alignment relative to the rib. The size of the change in the pattern depends on the gun's geometry and how much the head has moved. It can't be worked out from the infographic. It must be checked under controlled conditions with the actual gun.
6.7

Inconsistent gun mount

Lifting the head breaks a reference that needs to stay stable while we move the gun and fire. A different gun mount on every attempt creates that same uncertainty even before calling for the target.

When the stock, cheek and body settle into a different position on every attempt, the results look random even though the clay is almost the same each time.

Mounting the gun means resetting feet, body, shoulder, stock, cheek, hands and gaze. If the recoil pad or the cheek land in a different place, the gun's direction changes before the swing even starts. The shooter might blame the lead, when in fact they're starting from a different reference on every attempt.

Being consistent doesn't mean copying a posture from a photograph. Anatomy, handedness, mobility and discipline all change the solution. The CPSA manual and manufacturer documents present stock adjustment as a way to align the eye with the rib repeatably. The instructor's job is to find a safe, comfortable setup for that person, not to force them into some universal set of angles.

Foot position matters because it sets the range of movement, but it doesn't explain everything. An uncomfortable stance can make the torso hit its limit mid-crosser and the gun stall. A raised shoulder can change the recoil pad's contact. A cheek that settles late can change the picture right before the shot. That's why the assessment works through the chain from the ground up to the eye, and watches several repetitions.

Example: repeating the same clay, the pupil alternates between high and low misses with no clear pattern. The instructor stops correcting lead and just watches the mount. They notice that, on some attempts, the heel of the recoil pad sits outside the shoulder pocket and the head tilts to find the rib. Once a comfortable gun mount is stabilised, the misses spread less. Only then does it make sense to work on the meeting point.

Working outside a real round can help, but it has to keep the same safety culture. The CPSA describes mounting drills and alignment checks. Any practice with a gun must be done completely unloaded, in an authorised area and according to the club's rules, ideally with supervision when starting out. The simulator, for its part, doesn't reproduce weight, contact or anatomy. It can only teach the geometric consequence of moving the eye, not train the physical gun mount.

Useful record. Across three similar attempts, the instructor notes only four reference points: feet, shoulder pocket, cheek contact and eye position. If one changes, a lead correction still isn't added. The priority is a repeatable, pain-free foundation.
Variability. A scattered cloud of results can mix variability in the gun mount, in the swing and in the pattern itself. No single part can be identified from just one clay. The visual overlay of several gun mounts is a qualitative tool; a formal measurement would need spatial references and synchronisation.
6.8

Flinching and recoil anticipation

Without a gun mount we can repeat, any change in lead gets tangled up with movements of the eye, shoulder and stock. On top of that, fear of recoil can alter the movement right before firing.

Physical recoil happens after the shot has begun. Flinching appears when the body braces too early and changes the gun's orientation before the pattern has even left the barrel.

In everyday use, a flinch can include a sudden clench of the hand, closing the eyes, a tensed shoulder, or a jerk that throws the gun off line. Not all of these show up together, and they don't have a single cause. Expecting the noise and the impact, an uncomfortable fit, a load that feels unpleasant, the pressure to hit the clay, or fatigue can all play a part. Framing it purely as “fear” places blame and makes diagnosis harder.

The physics of recoil is well defined: the mass and velocity of the shot charge and the gases generate a push in the opposite direction, transmitted to the gun and the shooter. SAAMI publishes free recoil formulas for comparing setups under stated assumptions. An experimental study with sporting guns measured very brief impulses and forces that varied with ammunition and gun. This data shows that the physical stimulus is real. On its own, it doesn't predict how much discomfort any given person will feel.

Anticipation is a different matter. If the hand clenches, the shoulder pushes, or the eyes close before the shot is complete, the line can be thrown off. Research on recoil dynamics in trained shooters recognises anticipatory effects such as the flinch. Its findings shouldn't be applied directly to clay shooting beginners: the guns, the tasks and the experience levels are all different.

Example: the pupil tracks several clays well, and right before pulling the trigger, hunches their shoulder and drops the barrel. Afterwards they describe an unpleasant knock to the face. Firing more cartridges to “get used to it” can reinforce the expectation. The sensible response is to stop the round and review gun mount, fit, permitted load and hearing protection with the instructor and, if needed, with a gunsmith. Nobody improvises tests with tampered cartridges or carries out unauthorised exercises.

The instructor should watch the sequence: what changes before, during and after. Authorised footage can help distinguish prior tension from movement produced by the recoil itself. If the movement shows up at the end of a session, fatigue is one hypothesis. If it shows up from the very first shot, it's worth reviewing comfort, noise and experience. No infographic can diagnose pain or an injury.

Practical decision. When eye closure, pain or a contraction that compromises control appears, stop. Correction starts with safety and comfort, not with demanding another attempt. An instructor can scale down the task and a gunsmith can assess the fit; the simulator only lets you study the sequence without real recoil.
Two different signals. Calculated free recoil is an idealised property of the gun-and-cartridge system. Perceived recoil includes stock geometry, posture, contact, impulse duration, noise and expectations. Anticipation is a prior human response; it shouldn't be inferred from calculated energy alone.
6.9

Stopping the swing

Flinching shows that an anticipated bodily reaction can spoil an otherwise correct aim. Stopping the swing also breaks the movement at the most important moment.

The shot isn't the end of the movement. It's a moment within it. If the barrel loses speed before the meeting point, a crossing clay keeps moving and leaves the pattern in its trail.

The swing is the movement with which the body and gun track the apparent trajectory. On a crosser, the barrel needs to pick up direction and angular speed, hold the chosen relationship with the clay, and keep it through the shot. Pulling the trigger shouldn't act as a signal to brake. When it does, the picture the shooter remembers may have been correct an instant earlier, but it no longer represents where the pattern is going to be.

Saying that stopping “equals shooting behind” is a useful simplification for side crossers, not a law for every clay. If the clay is rising or going away, the error can mix with low and depth. Nor does every delayed cloud prove a stop: starting late or misjudging the meeting point produces a similar result. The cause has to be seen in the movement itself.

Causer and colleagues' research provides an objective reference: elite trap, skeet and double trap shooters showed slower, steadier barrel movement than sub-elite shooters, along with differences in gaze. “Slower” doesn't mean sluggish, nor that there's a single correct speed. It describes less agitation in the measured profile. The reasonable lesson is to look for smoothness and stability, not to chase the clay with jerky corrections.

Example: the pupil passes a clay from left to right, feels the gap is enough and, on firing, tenses up their hands. The barrel stays almost still while the clay keeps going. On the next attempt they get a single instruction: finish the arc at a safe reference point beyond where the shot is taken, without changing the gaze zone or exaggerating the lead. The instructor confirms from the outside whether the continuity genuinely improves.

The follow-through serves two purposes. Mechanically, it stops the intention to brake from showing up too early. Visually, it keeps information about the clay flowing, for a possible second shot and for understanding the result. It isn't about making a long, theatrical gesture after a hit. It's the natural consequence of an action that isn't cut short by anticipating the noise or the break.

Specific exercise. In a supervised repetition, choose a final reference point on the arc within the safe zone and think about reaching it. The instructor watches only the barrel speed around the shot. If there's continuity but the miss is still behind the clay, check the meeting point afterwards.
Kinematics. A stop is a drop in angular velocity, not the total absence of visible movement in a photograph. Measuring it would need a time series of the barrel. The simulator can show two comparative curves, but mouse movement doesn't reproduce mass, inertia or the body's coupling.
6.10

Looking at the barrel instead of the clay

The shotgun needs to keep moving through the shot so it doesn't end up pointing at a spot the clay has already passed. The movement loses its smoothness when we stop looking at the clay and start looking for the barrel.

The bead informs from the periphery. The clay needs the visual attention that lets you read direction and speed. This is where the mistake of shifting focus onto the gun gets diagnosed. Setting up the gaze positively before the target is released is covered in 8.11, Gaze zone.

Central vision supplies the detail needed to track the clay. Peripheral vision covers a wider field and keeps a reference on the gun. While the clay is in flight, your main attention should stay on the clay.

The mistake happens when the shooter tries to measure lead as a fixed distance. They look at the bead, check the gap, and go back to the clay. In that switch they lose information and can stall the gun. It's better to keep the clay sharp and notice the barrel peripherally.

Studies with shooters link a steadier final gaze with better results and a less agitated gun. They don't point to one exact spot valid for every discipline, but they do support the idea that gaze can be trained.

Before correcting the gaze, it's worth knowing your dominant eye. There's a simple guideline test for this. You form a small triangle with your hands and, with both eyes open, centre a distant object inside it. Without moving your hands, close one eye, then the other. The eye with which the object stays centred is usually the dominant one. The test should be repeated; it isn't a clinical diagnosis.

If a right-handed person has a dominant left eye, there's cross dominance. They may see two barrel references, or let the non-aiming eye take over when aiming. An instructor can look at several solutions: switching shoulder, adjusting technique, or fitting a small translucent patch or occluder on the lens of the eye not aligned with the gun. The patch blocks the bead reference, but keeps part of the peripheral vision. It should never be fitted by trial and error: it needs supervision and must comply with the discipline's rules.

Simple sign. If after the shot you remember the bead clearly, but not the clay, you may have focused on the gun. An instructor should confirm this.
Scope. A steady gaze doesn't mean keeping your eyes still. Its location and duration depend on the task, and eye dominance should be checked more than once.
6.11

Too much information

Looking at the barrel takes away information about the clay and tends to stiffen a movement that should be continuous. Getting too many instructions at once can cause the same kind of block.

Thinking isn't the enemy. The problem shows up when a person tries to apply too many new rules at once, in a skill that still demands a lot of working memory.

When starting out, almost everything demands attention: feet, shoulder, cheek, gaze, clay, lead, trigger, noise and result. Working memory keeps a limited amount of information active while the movement is being organised. If the instructor piles on explicit instructions with no order, the pupil may try to run through all of them during an action that lasts a very short time. The movement becomes late, choppy and hard to assess.

Motor learning research confirms that applying several instructions at once can depend on working memory capacity, especially in learners. Other studies compare an internal focus, aimed at body parts, with an external focus, aimed at the effect of the movement. External focus often improves learning and performance, although it isn't an absolute rule for every skill or every phase. Sometimes a correction about the body is necessary; the question is when, and how many at once.

Cutting the load doesn't mean teaching by guesswork or hiding the technique. The beginner needs a clear explanation outside the action and a short cue inside it. They can understand that the cheek sets the alignment, but when calling for the clay, attend only to “seeing its leading edge”. In another round, the focus is “finishing the swing”. The complexity stays in the learning programme; it isn't piled onto a single attempt.

Example: before a clay, three onlookers say at once “open your feet more”, “push your cheek down”, “look further out”, “lead more” and “don't stop”. The pupil tries to check every instruction and spots the clay late. Whoever's in charge needs to bring it back to a single voice, choose the priority, and leave the rest for later. If the gun mount is sound, the cue could simply be to keep the clay sharp. The observations get discussed afterwards.

Choosing a single variable also stops you changing equipment compulsively. Angle, cartridge speed, choke and stock height can all explain differences, but throwing them all into the first correction makes it impossible to know what actually produced the change. The simulator helps when it keeps the scenario fixed and changes only one variable. It does harm if it turns into a panel where the pupil changes everything after every miss.

Teaching rule. Before calling for the target, the student should be able to repeat the cue in a short sentence. After the attempt, only that variable is assessed. Other observations are noted for later batches, except for a safety issue, which is always corrected immediately.
Expert nuance. The advantage of an external focus has broad experimental support, but it depends on the task, experience and how it's framed. It doesn't justify banning all body awareness. In the cognitive stage, explicit information builds the model; during execution, it's best given sparingly so as not to overload online control.
6.12

Fatigue and too many rounds

A single correction we can check teaches more than a pile of instructions all at once. Tiredness adds to the confusion, so shooting more rounds doesn't always mean training better.

A session stops teaching once it becomes hard to hold attention, gun mount or control. At that point, the person starts repeating a worse version of the movement.

A beginner's fatigue doesn't only show up as general tiredness. It can reduce the stock's steady pressure, delay the start of the movement, increase tension in the hands, or make the gaze leave the clay early. Mental fatigue can also appear: keeping to a routine gets harder, impulsive corrections multiply, and each miss weighs more heavily than the last. These signs reduce the value of stacking up more attempts.

There isn't enough evidence to recommend the same maximum number of rounds for every beginner. The right load depends on the gun, the cartridge, the fit, the discipline, the breaks, physical condition and experience.

Personal note. I'd start with only a few strings: while the gun mount still isn't stable, going over a couple can bring on bruising or discomfort. This is cautious field guidance, not a rule or a medical dosage.

Recoil is a repeated stimulus that can be measured. Trials with sporting guns show that ammunition, gun mass, stock shape and reduction systems all change the forces transmitted. However, perception and the effect on each body can't be worked out from a formula. Pain in the shoulder or face shouldn't be assumed to be a necessary toll of adaptation. It's a signal to stop and review technique, fit and load with qualified staff.

The closest evidence on fatigue in shooting has to be treated with care. A study on expert biathletes increased physical load and observed changes in timing, but not a drop in accuracy under their protocol. It isn't clay shooting, and it isn't a beginner population, so it doesn't prove that one extra round worsens any specific percentage. It does remind us that fatigue, rhythm and performance don't follow a simple relationship, and that experience allows for compensation.

Personal note. I use “caramelo” (“sweet”) as an informal way of referring to a lump or soreness on the face after poor gun mounts. It isn't a diagnosis. If there's swelling, persistent pain or things get worse, stop practising and seek proper assessment; “pressing harder” or carrying on to toughen up is not advised.

Example: the first two rounds show a repeatable gun mount. In the next one, the recoil pad drops, the pupil lifts their head and starts anticipating. Even if they want to finish a box of cartridges, the session is now training variability and discomfort. Stopping, drinking water, noting what changed and coming back another day protects both the body and the learning.

Stopping criterion. Stop the string when pain appears, safe control decreases, a single cue can no longer be maintained, or the instructor sees repeated degradation. An outstanding score and leftover ammunition are not reasons to continue.
Limit of evidence. As of the time of review, we haven't found a trial that establishes a universal dose of strings for trap beginners. That's why the chapter uses functional signs and visually sets the personal recommendation apart. Any persistent symptom is outside the scope of the simulator and requires human assessment.

What we have learned

  • A miss is born from the relationship between the trajectory, the pattern and the shooter's actions. “Behind” or “low” describe the result, but on their own they don't explain the cause.
  • Gaze, gun mount and swing should be watched across several repetitions before changing the meeting point or the equipment.
  • One single cue gives more useful information than five corrections all at once.
  • Pain, loss of control and growing fatigue are reasons to stop, not proof of sporting commitment.

Expert reading. A reliable diagnosis would need to synchronise the clay's trajectory, the gun's movement, visual behaviour, the instant of the shot and the pattern's distribution. The simulator can isolate geometric relationships and repeat scenarios; it doesn't watch the real body, and it can't identify the cause of a miss on its own.

Chapter 07

Step-by-step learning and Shu-Ha-Ri

Learning clay shooting isn't about finding a secret phrase to break the next clay. At the start there are many new tasks all at once: acting safely, positioning the body, repeating the gun mount, seeing the clay, moving the gun and making sense of a result that vanishes into the air. Mounting the gun means bringing the shotgun to the shoulder and setting the cheek down repeatably. Progress begins when we cut that load down and every attempt has one clear question behind it.

This chapter uses Shu-Ha-Ri as its teaching thread. It's an idea from traditional Japanese arts. First you follow a taught form, then you analyse it and adapt it, and finally you act with autonomy without forgetting the basics. It isn't a scientific theory of motor learning — that is, of movement learning. Nor is it a federation classification. It's a map for organising practice. Here it's set against experimental research, experience on the ground, and what the Plato simulator can actually do.

7.1

Learning as in other sports

Misses are easier to understand once we split them into gaze, timing, movement, gun mount and fatigue. Now we'll look at how to correct them without trying to change everything at once.

A fast skill is built slowly. First a recognisable movement gets organised. Then it's repeated with useful feedback. Only afterwards do speed, variety and autonomy get asked for.

In tennis, swimming or music, doing a movement fast before you can repeat it tends to hide the error. The same happens with clays, with an added difficulty: the real action lasts a very short time, and the clay leaves no mark you can study afterwards. That's why the initial technique isn't trying to look like competition form. It's after a sequence the pupil and the instructor can both recognise: the same set-up, a comfortable gun mount, an available gaze and a continuous movement.

Repeating isn't copying without thinking. Every attempt shows the gap between what was expected and what happened. Motor learning research points to some improvements being fast and others slower. It doesn't say how many clays any one person needs, but it's a reminder that progress doesn't always move at the same pace.

The phrase “muscle memory” is a shortcut. Muscle doesn't learn on its own: the nervous system, perception and planning all take part. With practice, some parts of the movement demand less attention and free up more capacity for reading the clay.

A correction helps when it points to something observable. The instructor can see whether the head lifts or the barrel stops. A phrase like “finish the arc towards that reference point” is usually more useful than a list of movements. Safety and pain, on the other hand, get corrected immediately.

Across five simple repetitions, the goal might be keeping the cheek down and finishing the movement, not breaking five clays. The instructor watches a single variable. Speed comes once the movement is already stable.

Specific exercise. Before a batch, set a process criterion that can be seen from outside: “I keep my cheek down until I've finished” or “the barrel doesn't stop when I fire”. Count the attempts that meet the criterion, as well as the clays broken.
Expert insight. Performance during practice and learning are not the same thing. Learning is better assessed afterwards, through retention and transfer. Retention is keeping what you've learnt after some time has passed. Transfer is using it in a somewhat different situation. An immediate improvement caused by constant help can disappear once that help is withdrawn.
7.2

First stage: Shu

As in other sports, a complex movement is learned step by step before introducing changes and decisions of your own. The first stage, Shu, focuses on learning a common basic form.

In Shu, the beginner keeps to a simple form set out by a single qualified voice. This isn't blind obedience. It cuts down decisions so they can learn safety, reference points and rhythm without becoming overloaded.

The Japan Karate Association describes Shu as protecting, or faithfully following, the teachings and the fundamentals. In clay shooting, this doesn't mean there's a universal stance that suits every body. It means accepting, for a while, a coherent routine taught by an instructor who can see it as a whole. Switching advice after every miss makes it impossible to know what's actually being learned.

The routine starts before calling for the target. The feet are set in a similar way for the agreed clay. The stance should give stability and allow you to turn without locking up. The gun is mounted repeatably. Shoulder, cheek and hands need to allow control without pain. The stock, the anatomy and the discipline all change the fit.
