Personal note. I found it helpful to be told to bring my face forward. It's a common teaching image. It doesn't license forcing the neck or imposing the same geometry on everyone.

Once the position is set, the gaze takes over. The pupil needs to find the clay and keep it available, while the gun's reference stays in side vision. Causer's work with shotgun shooters links level and success to measurable differences in final gaze and gun stability. It doesn't give one exact gaze point for every clay. It does support the idea that vision can be trained, and shouldn't be left to chance.

The movement keeps going through the shot. “Hold firm without stiffening” names a simple balance: enough contact to control the gun, without turning arms and hands into a brake. The beginner doesn't yet need to work out every method of leading. Leading means aiming at the point where the clay and the shot will meet, not at the exact spot where the clay is seen at that instant. At the start, it's enough to recognise a basic relationship between clay, barrel and continuity, and repeat it across a few attentive attempts.

A case from the ground: the pupil gets five instructions before every clay, and every attempt looks different. In Shu, the instructor cuts the task down to a constant set-up and a single cue, for example “sharp clay, finish the movement”. After the round, the gun mount gets discussed. During the flight, nobody tries to run through feet, shoulder, cheek, hands, eyes and trigger.

Following a form doesn't get rid of the questions. It puts them in the right place. The instructor explains what's being aimed for and stops the pupil changing gun, cartridge and stance all at once. Safety and the ground's rules always come above the exercise.

Dry mounting can be practised at home to build fluency. You choose a fixed point and rehearse bringing the stock to the cheek and shoulder in a smooth movement. Slow sweeps left and right can follow, without touching the trigger. This should only be practised after personally checking the gun is unloaded, with no ammunition in the room, in a safe setting, and where local legislation and rules allow it. At the start, it's best to learn the routine with an instructor.

Shu routine. Do a small number of slow gun mounts towards a safe reference point. Aim for the point to land in the same place each time, without forcing the neck or shoulder. If pain appears, you're unsure about the state of the gun, or you lose control in any way, end the exercise.
Limit of the framework. Shu-Ha-Ri comes from Japanese traditions and here works as a metaphor for organising practice. It hasn't been validated as a psychometric scale for shooting. Nor does it establish when a person can practise independently.
7.3

What the beginner should achieve

Shu cuts down the decisions, so the gun mount and the movement can be repeated safely without changing every detail. At this stage, it's worth setting clear goals for the beginner.

The first real progress isn't a high score. It's practising safely, without pain, and repeatably. It's also seeing the clay, recognising a miss and trying again with one concrete decision.

Safety has to come first because it puts everything else in order. “Automatic” doesn't mean unconscious or infallible. It means that breaking the gun open at the right moment, keeping a safe direction and paying attention to whoever's in charge don't require a fresh decision every time. That fluency is only gained through in-person training and under the specific rules of the ground. A screen can't certify it.

The second criterion is the absence of pain. A firm contact can feel unfamiliar, but pain in the shoulder, cheek, neck or hand doesn't prove correct adaptation. It can point to a variable gun mount, an unsuitable stock, tension, load, fatigue, or a mix of several of these. The cause needs observation from an instructor, a gunsmith or a healthcare professional where appropriate. Carrying on shooting to “toughen up” can lock in anticipation and make control worse.

Repeatability comes next. If the eye ends up at a different height on every gun mount, the image of the rib and the direction of the shot both change too. The initial goal isn't to freeze the body still. It's to cut down big variations and recognise a comfortable position. A small ritual helps: set the feet, breathe, fix the gaze zone, mount the gun and call for the clay. The instructor checks from outside whether what feels the same to the pupil actually looks alike.

Seeing the clay through the shot is another milestone. The beginner who keeps hunting for the bead loses information about direction and speed. In the study by Causer, Bennett and colleagues, elite and sub-elite shooters showed differences in gaze behaviour and gun movement. In another trial, specific gaze training improved performance measures in international skeet athletes. These are results from specific groups, not a universal visual recipe. Even so, they justify training visual attention.

The first conscious leads don't have to be expressed in metres. It's enough to tell “current position” apart from “meeting point” and to recognise whether the centre of the shot ended up behind, in front, high or low. Understanding a miss doesn't mean self-diagnosing it, either. The pupil forms a hypothesis and checks it against the instructor across several comparable attempts.

Basic confidence comes from that control, not from hitting the clay by chance. A clay broken with a different gun mount and a lost gaze teaches less than a miss made according to the cue. Once a person can repeat the set-up, describe what they saw, and accept a correction without rebuilding their whole technique, they're ready to gradually increase the variety.

Exit checklist. Before complicating the trajectories, check five signs: supervised safe handling, no pain, a recognisable gun mount, the clay staying visible throughout the shot, and being able to explain a single hypothesis for the result.
Useful measurement. Consistency requires several observations. A single result mixes skill, pattern variation and chance. Pattern is the spread of pellets as the shot opens. Authorised video, gun-mount reference points and a short log can provide data, but they don't replace professional fitting or clinical assessment.
7.4

Second stage: Ha

At the start, progress is measured by actions we can repeat and by understanding what we're doing, not just by breaking clays. Once that base is solid, the Ha stage introduces changes with a purpose.

Ha begins once the basic form can withstand small changes. The shooter compares alternatives, understands why they work, and adapts the solution without dismantling the whole movement.

In the institutional Japanese explanation, Ha is breaking away, or detaching, in order to think and work things out for yourself. In clay shooting it doesn't mean leaving the instructor or changing technique on a whim. It means the pupil can now hold onto the fundamentals while exploring one difference: a more sideways crosser, a different height, a different hold point, or the interval leading to the second shot.

Constant practice and variable practice produce different effects. Shea and Morgan's classic experiment found that a blocked sequence made performance easier during acquisition. Blocked practice means repeating the same task several times in a row. Random practice, by contrast, which mixes tasks together, could favour retention and transfer. Later work shows the effect depends on the task and doesn't always show up the same way in applied settings. The sensible conclusion is a gradual one: repeating one clay helps build a reference; mixing clays afterwards checks whether that reference can adapt.

Ha also changes the question about lead. Instead of looking for one distance that fits everything, the shooter compares relationships: what happens when the sideways component increases? When is it worth starting from a different hold point? Why does the second shot need a fresh read? The instructor can set up two blocks with a single difference, and then interleave them. This stops the improvement coming purely from anticipating a fixed sequence.

The second shot deserves a task of its own. The clay is further away, its vertical speed has changed, and the shooter has just taken the noise and the recoil. If the head lifts to check the result, continuity is lost. Rather than “shooting twice, faster”, Ha aims to keep vision and organisation intact: first attempt, reading the remaining clay, correction, and second movement, always within the safety and the rules of the discipline.

Miss patterns show up when comparable rounds get logged. Three misses behind on right-to-left crossers is a clue. One on its own isn't a diagnosis. If high and low alternate across every clay, it may be worth reviewing the gun mount before the trajectory. Variation only helps tell causes apart when you know what actually changed.

Example: first a medium clay from station 3 is repeated until a stable picture is achieved. Then only the hold point is changed, keeping the clay and every other condition the same. Finally two clays are mixed without announcing their order. If performance drops once they're mixed, that isn't a failure. It reveals that the solution still depended on preparing the response in advance.

Ha progression. Practise A in a block, practise B in a block, then alternate A/B. Note whether your execution holds up once you no longer know which one is coming. Don't add wind, a different cartridge, a different choke and a different stance all at once. Choke is the narrowing at the muzzle that changes how the pattern opens.
Functional difficulty. The same launch doesn't pose the same challenge to everyone. Recent studies on variability show that “more variable” doesn't always mean “better”. A moderate amount of variability can outperform both complete consistency and excessive change.
7.5

Third stage: Ri

Ha lets you compare different solutions without losing the reference that helps make sense of each change. The autonomy of Ri arrives after making many such comparisons consciously.

Ri isn't shooting with no foundation. It's solving a new situation quickly because the fundamentals are already integrated. It's also knowing how to go back to them when the result gets worse.

The JKA describes Ri as detaching in order to establish a creation of your own. Applied to clay shooting, the shooter no longer needs to talk themselves through the whole sequence before every clay. They read direction and apparent speed, choose a response compatible with their technique, and carry it out. That fluency can feel intuitive, but it's built on thousands of comparisons between perception, movement and result.

Expert level doesn't get rid of attention. It changes where it's used. In Causer and colleagues' study, elite shooters showed an earlier, longer final gaze and a steadier gun than sub-elite shooters. That doesn't mean everyone has the same visible style. It means experience can organise information-gathering and gun control differently.

Adaptation shows up when the ground, the background, the light, the station or the discipline change. A reference learned on a comfortable clay can fail against a more sideways clay. Ri doesn't look for a new rule for every case. It recognises relationships: more sideways component, less useful time, a different hold zone, a different rhythm. The shooter keeps a technique of their own, but knows which part can be changed and which fundamentals aren't negotiable.

Competitive preparation adds pressure, scoring and consequences. An expert can suffer from over-control if they try to consciously direct every fragment of an automated skill. At the same time, 2026 research on motor sequences warns that automation doesn't mean a complete absence of attentional demand, even after a great deal of practice. That's why Ri isn't described as an “empty mind”, but as selective, flexible attention.

Correcting yourself mid-round takes care. After a miss, the shooter decides whether it was normal variation, a clear read, or a pattern starting to repeat. Changing stock, technique and hold point over a single clay can add more noise. A small, checkable correction — recovering the gaze zone, say — protects continuity. If the problem persists, it gets logged to work on outside competition.

Example: on a new ground, the first crossers look faster against a dark background. The autonomous shooter doesn't multiply the lead purely on feel. They watch one attempt, recover their set-up, and adjust a temporary or visual reference. If they can't identify the cause, they go back to a basic routine. Returning to Shu isn't a step backward. It's using the fundamentals as a diagnostic tool.

Ri decision. During a string, classify the correction as “now” or “later”. Only correct now if the sign is clear and small. Take technical, equipment or strategy changes that need several trials to training instead.
Autonomy, not omniscience. There's no test that turns someone into Ri once and for all. Expertise is specific to tasks and contexts. An advanced shooter can go back to a guided stage when changing discipline or rebuilding a movement.
7.6

Why “just shoot by feel” doesn't work at the start

Ri doesn't mean improvising, but making your own decisions about a technique that's already understood and tested. That's why it's usually too soon to ask a beginner to shoot purely by feel.

Expert intuition is compressed knowledge. Asking for it from someone who doesn't yet have any reference points shows them the final result without teaching them what information produced it.

When an experienced shooter says “don't think, just shoot”, they may honestly be describing what they feel. They no longer consciously work out foot position, or put the meeting point into metres. Their perceptual-motor system recognises regularities and produces a response. The mistake is assuming the beginner has those same regularities just from hearing the phrase.

A practised skill contains steps the expert has stopped noticing. Karni's work showed that experience produces specific, progressive changes. Dahm's recent study found that even heavily practised sequences could still carry attentional demands. Together, these results point to avoiding two extremes: believing everything must be controlled with words forever, or claiming that learning only starts once every explanation is dropped.

Describing a feeling isn't the same as teaching a technique, either. “The clay's slow”, “just watch it” or “let it come out” can be excellent cues for someone who shares the same base. For a novice they're ambiguous phrases. A teachable instruction identifies what to watch, what to do and how to assess it: “keep the clay sharp and let the barrel keep moving to this safe reference point; I'll watch whether it stops”.

Copying only the visible result can be misleading. Two experts may look like they're moving freely, but their stocks, anatomy, hold points and methods aren't identical. The beginner who imitates the outward speed without understanding the organisation behind it tends to arrive late, tense up, or lose the clay. The same goes for equipment: copying someone else's stock height doesn't copy the position of their eye.

Teaching has to translate expertise without overloading. Outside the actual attempt, the full model gets explained. Inside it, a short cue is used. Research by Wulf and colleagues found advantages for feedback focused on the effect of the movement in sporting tasks. Feedback means information fed back about what's just happened. But that doesn't license banning every instruction about the body. At an early stage, an explicit correction about safety or gun mount can be essential. The skill lies in choosing the right information for that particular attempt.

Example: a companion says “I just look at the background and it happens on its own”. The instructor breaks the phrase down: gaze zone before calling for the target, peripheral pickup, attention on the clay, and gun continuity. Now the pupil can practise one part and get evidence back. Over time, that sequence might compress again into the feeling of “it happens on its own” — but it's no longer borrowed magic.

Practical translation. When you get intuitive advice, ask: “what should I watch for?”, “what specific action changes?” and “how will we know if I did it?”. If it can't be answered, keep the phrase as a personal feeling, not a rule.
Tacit knowledge. Expert execution can outstrip the athlete's own verbal explanation. That's why an interview provides hypotheses, while video, gaze, kinematics and outcome provide complementary measures. Kinematics means the analysis of movement.
7.7

Repeating the same clay

Useful intuition comes from well-remembered experiences, not from working without method. Repeating the same clay helps build it through comparisons that are easy to recognise.

Repeating one clay keeps part of the problem fixed. That way you can compare height, lead and continuity. Afterwards, that crutch has to be taken away, and the solution tried out among varied clays.

In a normal round, a miss can be followed straight away by a different clay. Angle, height and apparent speed all change before the pupil has made sense of the first one. Deliberately repeating one trajectory cuts down that noise. This shouldn't be confused with re-throwing a no-bird under the rules. It's a training exercise, authorised and organised by whoever's responsible for the ground.

The first block is there to find a picture. The pupil watches where the clay appears, how it climbs, and what gap forms before the shot. The instructor doesn't change gun mount, hold point and lead all at once. If the pattern keeps landing low and the gun mount stays consistent, a vertical correction is tried. If the gun stops, continuity is worked on before adding distance.

Repetition allows the first and second shots to be compared. By the second, the clay has moved on and the situation is no longer the same, even though the original clay is. It's worth logging separately where the gaze is lost, whether the head lifts, and what correction gets tried. Two hits aren't two copies of each other. They form a small sequence of perception and decision.

Blocked practice usually improves performance quickly within the block, but that progress can depend on knowing what's coming next. Shea and Morgan showed the classic paradox: greater difficulty during random practice, but later advantages in retention or transfer. A 2023 study reproduced signs of contextual interference in implicit adaptation, although not every applied task responds the same way. That's why the exercise needs two necessary phases: stabilise, then vary.

Plato applies this idea in a checkable way. The “Repeat target” button clones the last clay, keeps the station and trajectory, and marks it so it doesn't count towards the round or change station. This lets you compare attempts without using up another scenario. The pattern keeps its own variation, so repeating the clay doesn't turn the result deterministic. That difference is a reminder that a cloud of pellets isn't a solid disc.

Example: a crosser is repeated six times. The first two attempts establish the reference. The next two change only the lead. The last ones are done without any visual aid. Afterwards, five varied clays are called for. If the correction only works on the memorised crosser, it hasn't transferred yet. The log needs to say what changed, not just how many broke.

2-2-2 protocol. Two attempts to observe, two with a single correction and two to confirm it without further instruction. Finish with variety. On a real ground, the number and procedure are decided by the instructor or the person in charge.
Experimental control. “Same clay” controls the initial trajectory, not every human variable or the actual spread of the cartridge. In Plato, the pattern seed also changes between shots; the function repeats the clay, not an identical replay.
7.8

Fewer rounds, higher quality

A controlled repetition keeps the same clay and changes a single decision, which separates learning from chance. Rather than fixing a large number of rounds in advance, it's better to stop once attention and quality start to drop.

There's no universal number of rounds for learning well. The useful dose ends once attention, gun mount, comfort or control can no longer sustain the intended task.

The load depends on supervision, the gun, the cartridge, the fit, the breaks, physical condition and each person's individual response. The most defensible general rule is a functional one: keep going while the execution stays safe, comfortable and attentive.

Personal note. When someone is starting out, I prefer few rounds and often suggest no more than a couple. That guideline may make sense in my context, but it isn't a rule or a universal prescription.

A quality session is split into blocks with a purpose. Before starting, one variable is chosen. During the block, redesigning the whole technique is avoided. At the end, there's a rest, and one sentence gets logged. The number of repetitions isn't decided to empty a box of cartridges, but to gather enough evidence without spoiling the movement. If after several attempts it's no longer clear what's being practised, another shot just adds volume, not necessarily learning.

Physical fatigue can harm more than just the immediate result. Branscheidt and colleagues induced muscular fatigue and observed a deterioration in motor learning that persisted over following days. It was a different experimental task, so it doesn't let us calculate a maximum number of clays. It does advise against treating fatigue as a neutral setting where trying harder is enough.

Breaks aren't a magic formula either. One study on distributed practice found advantages over massed practice in a motor sequence. Distributed practice means spreading the work out with rests; massed practice means piling it up with little rest. Other protocols have produced more mixed results. The practical decision is to use breaks to recover attention and assess quality, not to promise that any specific duration will consolidate every movement. Between sessions, sleep and time also play a part in stabilising motor memories, but the findings shift depending on the task.

The stopping criterion needs to be agreed beforehand. Pain, swelling, loss of a safe direction, a gun mount that's different every time, difficulty holding onto a single cue, or frustration that leads to shooting without observing, are all sufficient signs. Nobody trains through pain to produce “muscle memory”. The session is stopped, the fit is reviewed, and if the symptom persists, appropriate assessment is sought.

Example: a pupil plans two rounds. After the first, they note that the cheek stays down and the clay stays visible. Halfway through the second, they start repositioning the recoil pad after every attempt and notice discomfort. The plan doesn't force them to finish. The session closes with three notes: which clay was worked on, which sign appeared, and what the instructor needs to review. That information is worth more than a score gained with a deteriorating execution.

Session traffic light. Green: safe, comfortable and alert. Amber: variation increases or the instruction becomes hard to sustain; pause and reassess. Red: pain, loss of control or unsafe behaviour; stop and ask for help.
Honest dosing. We haven't found a trial that establishes optimal rounds and breaks for beginners in clay target shooting. The chapter cautiously extrapolates principles of motor learning and presents the personal recommendation as a situated observation.
7.9

Evolution of results

Training with quality demands attention, a goal, and a pause to make sense of what happened in each attempt. Results have to be watched with patience, because progress is never a steady climb.

A round of 25 clays is a small observation, not a sporting identity. Progress shows up better in trends, repeatable processes and transfer than in one isolated jump in score.

In the first rounds there may be very few hits. That doesn't prove a lack of ability. The person is still splitting their attention between safety, gun mount, vision and movement. Once one or two pieces fall into place, the score can climb quickly. A plateau appears later, because the remaining errors are less obvious, and consistency demands repeating the solution across more trajectories and under more pressure.

Personal note. I've observed a progression that goes from almost no clays to 5–6, then to 15–20 and, with much more effort, approaches 23–25. It's a narrative experience, not a universal scale. It doesn't establish weeks, number of sessions or probabilities. Age, prior coordination, frequency, coaching, discipline, shooting ground, equipment and health produce different paths.

Nor does 23–25 guarantee winning a competition. A high score in a round of 25 expresses a very demanding performance, but rankings, total number of targets, tie-breaks and finals depend on the rules and the event. The ISSF changed formats in 2026 and brought back shoot-offs for certain qualifying ties. This manual shouldn't turn a saying from the ground into a permanent competition rule.

Statistically, a single round carries a lot of uncertainty. If each clay is represented as a hit or a miss with a stable probability, the result follows a binomial distribution. This is a simplification, because clays aren't identical or independent. A binomial distribution means a model for counting hits and misses across a fixed number of attempts. NIST recommends suitable intervals for small proportions, instead of blindly trusting a single point estimate. In plain terms: an 18 today and a 16 tomorrow can come from the same underlying level.

To read the trend, it's worth logging at least discipline, ground, starting station, score and one process criterion. A moving average across several rounds smooths out some of the noise. Splitting results by clay type reveals more than a single total. Retention matters too: does the improvement hold after a few days? So does transfer: does it show up on another ground, or only on the repeated clay?

Example: someone goes from 8 to 15 in a session of easy targets and drops back to 10 on a more demanding ground. They haven't “lost five clays” as if that were some fixed property. The sample changed, and perhaps the difficulty did too. The useful move is to compare categories, keep the record, and ask what pattern holds. Confidence is built through trend and understanding, not by protecting an isolated best score.

Minimal log. Log every round and add one process sentence. Review groups of five or more comparable rounds before concluding that you've moved up or down a level. Keep clearly different conditions separate.
Statistical model. The binomial distribution helps visualise sample variation, but it simplifies trajectories with differing difficulty, dependence between attempts and changes in the shooter's state. It shouldn't be used to classify people or predict competition results without further data.
7.10

Role of the coach

Periods without improvement and temporary setbacks are part of learning, and a single score isn't enough to make sense of them. The coach helps read these signals and choose the next drill.

The coach turns private sensations into shared observations. They protect the learner's progression and decide which correction deserves attention now and which one can wait.

The shooter can't see themselves in profile while following the clay. They might feel the head stayed still when it actually lifted. They might also recall a correct lead just before the barrel stopped. The coach watches that sequence from outside: stance, gun mount, start, continuity and the reaction afterwards. Their value isn't in piling up comments, but in linking one signal to a checkable task.

Safety comes first. Incorrect handling, pain or loss of control are corrected immediately, even if they weren't the focus of that set. Learning comes next. If the student misses behind, lifts their head and shifts their feet, the coach doesn't need to correct all three during the next flight. They pick the variable that limits the task the most and leave the rest for later.

Feedback can refer to the outcome — where the shot ended up. It can also refer to the process — what the movement did. Both are useful if they answer the current question. Wulf's research on sporting tasks found advantages for cues focused on the effect. Winstein and Schmidt's classic experiment showed that reducing the frequency of knowledge of results could hurt immediate practice while improving retention. These aren't universal recipes, but they warn against commenting on every detail after every attempt.

A competent coach withdraws support gradually. At first they might demonstrate, set up references and give frequent feedback. Later they ask the student what they saw first, wait through several repetitions, or only step in when the error goes beyond an agreed margin. That way they can check whether the person is learning to detect and correct without depending on a constant voice.

They also choose the drills. Repeating the same clay is right for isolating one relationship. Varying the presentations tests transfer. Hiding a visual aid tests autonomy. Difficulty is matched to level. A round that's too easy can produce a score without information. One that's too hard produces misses that can't be told apart. Teaching moves between these two extremes.

Example: the student gets three contradictory diagnoses from bystanders. The coach stops the conversation, asks what they saw, watches two attempts without stepping in, and settles on “keep the clay sharp.” They then compare authorised video or the outcome. Once the gaze is stable, they review the gun mount. The sequence also teaches a social rule: during a set, there is only one voice in charge.

Feedback contract. Before the block, agree what the coach will observe and when they'll speak. After each attempt, the student responds first with one sentence. The coach confirms, corrects or waits; they don't open five new issues at once.
Dependence on help. A better result while the coach is guiding doesn't demonstrate retention. Assessing it requires later attempts without the same help and, if possible, a transfer situation.
7.11

Using the simulator at each stage

A good coach turns vague sensations into observations that are reliable, concrete and easy to check. The simulator can support this work differently at each of Shu, Ha and Ri.

Plato lets you see, repeat and vary part of the perceptual problem. Each stage uses these advantages differently, without confusing mouse and screen with gun, body and shooting ground.

In Shu, the simulator helps you learn the vocabulary before recoil, noise and the pressure of your turn are added. Slow motion starts at 0.35× and can be taken up to 1.00×. The orange flight path, the yellow lead area and the blue pattern can be shown or hidden. The student calls for a clay, watches where it is and where the meeting point is calculated, and uses “Repeat target” to keep the same presentation. This isn't meant to train safe handling: Plato has no weight, no real barrel and no people standing around.

A Shu routine can use three passes. First, slow motion with the flight path and lead aid. Second, the same clay with the flight path but without the yellow area. Third, hidden aids and a comparison afterwards. The code keeps track of up to two shot corrections and splits them along axes relative to the clay. That lets you talk about behind, ahead, high or low without turning a hit into the only signal.

Ha makes use of the controls. You keep the same clay and change a single variable: cartridge speed, first or second pattern spread, rib gain, wind or station. You then compare disciplines and schemes. You need to write down the hypothesis: “if I increase flight time, I expect more lead,” for example. Moving several sliders around until it works only teaches you to find a combination on the screen.

In Ri, you can work at 1.00×, hide the aids, switch between schemes and use Robot to work through less predictable presentations. This trains reading and decision-making within the model, not full competitive technique. The hand moves a mouse with no mass and no recoil; the eye watches a flat view with a limited field and scale. A good virtual result doesn't license speeding up your real progression, nor does it demonstrate safety.

Research on virtual environments shows the potential for transfer, but also specific findings and limits. One table-tennis trial found real improvements after virtual practice. Another study on non-specific visual training in volleyball improved cognitive measures, but was less effective for concrete sporting skills than contextual practice was. Plato should offer hypotheses about perception and geometry, not promise automatic transfer.

The right cycle ends on the shooting ground. You take a single question to your coach, test it under real rules, and bring the observation back to the simulator. That way, Shu-Ha-Ri never turns into three levels of a video game. It's a way of deciding how much help to show, how much variation to introduce, and when to check that the learning lives outside the screen.

Guided session. Choose one presentation, repeat it with the aids on, hide lead first and then trajectory, and change only one variable. Write a conclusion an instructor could check. Don't transfer stock or ammunition adjustments from the screen without real-world validation.
Verified behaviour. In the reviewed version, repeating clones the clay and doesn't add to the round; slow motion alters the time step; the aids are toggleable; the correction keeps two shots. The pattern varies and the model simplifies aerodynamics, the body and perception.

What we have learned

  • Shu-Ha-Ri is a useful metaphor for ordering guidance, variation and autonomy; it isn't a scientific or federation scale.
  • Beginners progress first in safety, comfort, reliable repetition, vision and understanding their misses.
  • Repetition builds a reference. Changing the situation and withdrawing the aid lets you check whether what was learned is retained and works in other cases.
  • A single score doesn't define your level, and the rungs of personal commentary are experience, not a forecast.
  • The coach observes and paces the help; Plato makes a hypothesis visible; the supervised shooting ground confirms whether it transfers.

Expert reading. This chapter separates immediate performance, retention and transfer. It proposes progressing from controlled practice towards functional variability, gradually reducing feedback, and recording process alongside outcome. It also always states the distance between laboratory evidence, field experience and simulator behaviour.

Chapter 08

The geometry of lead, explained for shooters

Lead is not a fixed distance. The clay keeps flying while the pellets are travelling. That's why the gun has to be aimed at the zone where the two will coincide. The useful question isn't just “how much gap do I leave?” but “what flight path do I see, and where will the meeting happen?”

You don't need to do maths on the station. It's enough to read position, direction and time; move without breaking the swing; and check a single idea with an instructor.

8.1

The main rule

Staged learning gives us a method the simulator lets you practise: observe, repeat, change things with judgement, and make your own decisions. With that foundation, we can study the visual heart of the problem: the geometry of lead.

A shotgun can't send its pellets into the past. If the clay is moving, aiming at the spot where it is the moment you decide to shoot usually leaves the pattern behind. You need to aim the shot at a future meeting zone.

Picture a photograph taken the instant you call for the clay. It shows the shooter, the clay and a line joining the eye to the clay. That photo tells you where the clay is now. But it doesn't say where it will be when the shot arrives. The clay keeps moving while we watch it, move the gun, pull the trigger and the pellets fly. That's why putting the aiming point on the present picture doesn't freeze the scene.

That correction is called lead, or forward allowance. It doesn't always mean leaving a big gap out front. On an almost incoming presentation, the clay seems to move little from side to side; the important correction may lie in the height. On a clay crossing side-on, the separation is easier to see. On a diagonal, two parts mix together: the horizontal and the vertical. The main rule is about time and space. What it looks like changes depending on where we're watching from.

Research on visuomotor interception studies actions where the body has to match up with a moving object. “Visuomotor” means sight and movement are both involved. Even though there are delays in seeing, deciding and moving, the body learns patterns. It doesn't solve every shot with a conscious formula; it predicts and corrects during the movement. That's why repeating a similar clay teaches you more than hunting for one universal figure. It's also why a correct picture can arrive too late if the swing — the gun's continued movement — slows down.

In the Plato simulator, the yellow aid shows a recommended orientation. The blue cloud shows the estimated pattern — the zone where the pellets would go. The code first calculates an approximate interception, then adjusts the angle using the same simplified physics it uses to diagnose the hit. That aid isn't a recipe for the shooting ground. The screen doesn't reproduce the gun's weight, the gun mount, human delay, or the full ballistics. But it does let you hide the hint, repeat the same flight, and check the relationship between the current position and the future one.

Specific exercise. With supervision, and without changing trajectory or cartridge, repeat a crossing clay in the simulator. Make one attempt taking the red dot to the current position. Make another taking the dot to the yellow zone without stopping the movement. Then describe what changed in the meeting. Don't stop at whether the clay broke.
Technical reading. As a first approximation, the clay's future position is its current position plus what it travels during the time of flight. A real model adds trajectory curvature, pellet velocity loss, spread and three-dimensional geometry. “More lead” is the visible result of those relationships. It isn't a constant of the shooter.
8.2

What the meeting point is

The main rule is to bring the pattern to the clay's future position, not to shoot where it used to be. That future position is called the meeting point.

The meeting point isn't the spot the shooter is looking at when the clay appears. It's the three-dimensional zone where the clay and a useful part of the pattern can be at the same time.

It helps to build the idea step by step. First we look at where the clay is now. Then we work out where it's heading — it can go left, go right, climb, drop and move away all at the same time. Next we think about how much further it will travel before the shot reaches it. That gives us the possible future spot. Finally, the gun is aimed so the useful centre of the pattern passes through that zone.

The word “point” simplifies a great deal. The clay has size. The cloud contains many pellets. And both occupy volume over a small interval. On the shooting ground, you're after a window of coincidence, not a sizeless mathematical intersection. What's more, not just any graze breaks the clay: pellet density, the energy left, the clay's orientation and the pattern's real spread all matter. Here we use the centre of both movements to explain the geometry, not to promise absolute certainty.

From the eye, depth flattens out. Two positions several metres apart can look close together on a screen or against the sky. That's why the gap we see doesn't map directly onto the real distance. A similar-looking visual gap can stand for different numbers of metres depending on how far away the clay is. The useful reference is learned through a specific presentation: apparent speed, line, size and the moment chosen to shoot.

The meeting point also changes as the gun moves. If the shooter takes longer, the clay is already somewhere else and the pellets have to cover a different distance. If they shoot sooner, the window is closer. If they wait, it may be further away, higher, or already dropping. The simulator recalculates the recommendation at every instant, looking for the shortest distance between the centre of the shot and the moving clay. This shows that the solution isn't a fixed mark in the landscape.

Ground case. A student points to a tree as “the spot where you have to shoot”. The reference can work for one clay and one specific moment. But it fails if the station, the speed or the break point changes. The instructor keeps the tree as an initial reference and then asks the student to read the edge and the line of the clay.
Geometry. Solving the interception requires finding a future time at which the clay's position and the pattern's position are close enough. Plato approximates this by testing the time of flight and then adjusting yaw and elevation. Yaw is the sideways turn of the gun. Elevation is the angle up or down. The result depends on its reduced model of gravity, wind and velocity loss. It isn't a certified ballistic solution.
8.3

Variables that change lead

The meeting point depends on place and moment, so there's no fixed mark that works for every clay. The conditions of the presentation and the shot explain why lead changes.

Lead changes with the clay, the perspective, the shot and the execution. To know what produced the result, it's best to change only one variable at a time.

The first family belongs to the clay. Its speed sets how far it moves in a given interval. But direction decides how much of that movement we see sideways. A fast clay moving away almost head-on may call for little visible lateral correction. A slower one that's fully crossing can show a bigger gap. Height and curve add a vertical part. A rising clay isn't at the same height by the time the pattern arrives.

The second family belongs to perspective. Distance and angle turn real metres into visual movement. Change station, and the same presentation can look more incoming or more crossing. As it moves away, the clay looks smaller and its speed within the field of view changes. That's why two similar presentations don't always call for the same movement.

The third family belongs to the shot. A higher nominal muzzle velocity tends to shorten flight time. “Nominal” means the speed given as a reference figure. But the pellets lose speed and don't fly as a single projectile. Cartridge, pellet size, choke, barrel and conditions all change the real pattern. Choke is the narrowing at the muzzle that changes how wide the pattern spreads. Plato takes an effective speed from the cartridge control and applies a simplified drag. It's there for comparing cases within the simulator, not for turning the panel into a manufacturer's table.

The fourth family belongs to execution. Spotting the clay late, starting the movement roughly, lifting the cheek off the stock, or slowing down as you pull the trigger all change the real orientation. This can happen even when the shooter knows the theoretical gap. “Reaction time” isn't a fixed figure added to the lead after the shot, either. It happens before the pellets leave and gets mixed in with seeing, deciding and moving. Technique organises that time. It doesn't remove it.

The hold point can also be set higher or lower depending on the station. “Higher” describes the gun's starting orientation, not that the shooter is standing on a platform. To compare, repeat the last clay and change only the cartridge, the station or that orientation. The conclusion should express a specific relationship, not a universal rule.

Specific exercise. Choose a moderate crossing shot, turn on slow motion and repeat three times. First round: baseline setup. Second round: change only the cartridge speed. Third round: restore the cartridge and change only the station. Note what moved in the yellow aid and what stayed stable.
Expert reading. Visible lateral lead relates mainly to transverse velocity and time of flight. Transverse velocity is the part of the movement that crosses our line of sight. The radial component changes distance and scale; it's the part that comes closer or moves away. The vertical component mixes initial velocity and gravity. Separating axes avoids treating “lead” as a single number valid in any direction.
8.4

Frontal target

Speed, direction, distance and flight time act together; changing one changes the solution even if the clay looks similar. We'll start with an incoming clay to get a clearer look at depth and height.

A clay moving away almost head-on is deceptive, because it seems to move little from side to side. Depth dominates the scene. Height and apparent size give the most useful clues.

On a lateral crossing, the eye sees a clear trail. On an incoming clay, most of the movement heads into the background. The clay seems to get smaller and move less across the screen, even though it's covering real distance. A beginner can read that picture as an almost still clay and put the centre of the pattern on its current position. The correction usually doesn't look like a big horizontal gap. It looks more like reading the line as it rises or falls, and choosing the right moment.

Early in the flight, many trap clays climb. If the gun has a flat point of impact and the shooter puts the barrel right on the clay, it can block their view of it exactly when they most need to see its height. The point of impact is the centre where the pattern lands relative to the aiming point. It should be checked on a pattern plate. The gun mount — how the shotgun is placed against the face and shoulder — should be checked by a competent person.

Personal note. I've experienced that visual loss with shotguns or setups that forced me to cover the clay. It's a field experience. It isn't a rule that applies to every gun.

Gravity gradually reduces the vertical part of the movement, and if the flight continues, it starts to drop. From the eye, the clay can seem to “stop” near the top. It doesn't have to stop in space; it simply climbs less and less while continuing to move away. If the shooter chases only the vertical speed, they tend to shoot late. A complete read keeps track of depth, size and direction together.

A practical reference is to keep the leading or top edge visible, depending on the technique being used, and to keep the gun moving through the shot. This shouldn't turn into checking the rib to see the outline. The barrel stays as peripheral information. In the simulator, a straight-away presentation lets you compare the orange position, the yellow recommendation and the blue centre. That way, a small lateral component isn't mistaken for no correction at all.

Ground case. A student breaks crossing shots but misses low on central presentations. The instructor doesn't just say “more lead” as a blanket fix. They check whether the student is covering the clay, confirm the gun mount and repeat a frontal presentation. The focus stays only on keeping the clay visible during the rise.
Perspective. Lateral angular velocity tends to be small when the flight vector points close to the line of sight. The flight vector is the direction of the clay's movement. The change in distance alters the apparent size and the time of flight. The projected height depends on the relationship between real elevation and depth. A 2D view can fall short of showing these changes.
8.5

Crossing target

On an incoming clay, there can seem to be little crossing, even though height and distance are changing fast. On a crossing clay, the lateral movement we usually associate with lead is easier to see.

A crossing clay shows lead in the easiest way to see. The clay travels across the field of view. The line of the shot has to reach a future lateral position without the gun stopping.

When the clay crosses from left to right, or the other way round, most of its speed shows up sideways. You can clearly see it moving. That's why it's easy to understand that shooting at its current centre leaves the pattern trailing behind. That clarity makes the crossing shot a good presentation for learning the concept. But it doesn't make it an easy task. The shooter has to pick up the line, match the gun's speed to the clay's, build the right separation, and keep the movement going.

“Close” and “far” aren't only about size. At a greater distance, the pellets need more time and the clay can move further before the meeting point. Even so, the angular separation the eye sees also depends on crossing speed and perspective. You can't assume a distant clay should always be seen with a bigger gap. That's why measuring in barrel lengths, centimetres of screen, or background trees gives fragile references outside one very specific case.

The shot point needs to leave room to read the clay and complete a comfortable movement. Wait too long, and the distance grows, which can bring on rushing. Shoot before recognising line and speed, and the lead becomes a guess. The CPSA places the break point within a sequence: look point, hold and pick-up. It doesn't present it as a mark separate from the rest of the movement.

Continuity is key, because pulling the trigger doesn't stop the clay. If the gun slows down at that instant, the orientation that looked right can be lost. Causer and colleagues' study found steadier barrel movement in elite shooters than in sub-elite ones, and in successful attempts. But it doesn't give a single speed figure. “Continuing” means keeping the movement organised, not giving it a yank after the shot.

Specific exercise. Repeat a medium crossing shot with the method your instructor chooses. In one set, watch only the point where the gun picks up the line. In another, watch whether the separation holds through the shot. Don't correct hold, feet, cartridge and gap all at once.
Transverse component. Over a short interval, the future lateral displacement grows with the velocity perpendicular to the line of sight and with the time to the meeting point. Radial velocity changes distance and scale, but it doesn't translate directly into lateral gap. The simulator calculates in 3D before projecting to the screen.
8.6

Diagonal target

The crossing shot shows clearly that firing at the clay's current spot usually leaves the pattern behind. On a diagonal flight path, that lateral lead has to be combined with a vertical correction.

A diagonal forces you to read two changes at once. The clay moves sideways and also changes height. Correcting only one part leaves the pattern behind, low, or both.

A diagonal clay moves away while crossing and climbing or dropping. The eye sees a slope. But that slope on screen isn't the flight's real incline. It depends on the station, the depth and the projection. If the shooter treats the clay as a horizontal crossing shot, they can leave enough lateral separation and still end up low. If they focus only on the climb, they may reach the right height once the clay has already moved off sideways.

The phrase “behind and low” comes up a lot in coaching. There's no survey that lets us say it always happens to beginners. But it is a reasonable hypothesis when three things come together: late detection, a tendency to aim at the current spot, and a rising flight path. It needs confirming by watching several similar attempts. A single unbroken clay doesn't show where the densest part of the pattern actually passed.

Visual references can simplify without falsifying. Instead of thinking “right, higher, further,” the student identifies the leading-edge line and moves along that slope towards a future zone. The axes are then analysed separately. Did the pattern trail behind the direction of flight? Did it fall below? Did it stray sideways off the line? The simulator offers correction windows that separate lead, sideways error and height.

The body also has to be able to travel through that diagonal. A stance set up only for the start can run out of turn by the end. A hold point that's too high can hide the presentation. These decisions belong to the instructor and the discipline. The geometry shows which way the orientation should evolve. It doesn't replace adjusting the body, safety, or individual assessment.

Ground case. On a diagonal presentation to the left, Laura gets two contradictory instructions: “higher” and “further ahead”. The instructor repeats the clay and asks for a single image: follow its leading edge along the slope. Afterwards, they use the debris and their observation of the swing to decide what to work on in the next set.
Breakdown. In the meeting plane, the error can be projected onto one axis aligned with forward motion and another with height. An axis is a reference direction for measurement. Plato builds local axes and shows the corrections separately. It's a diagnostic tool of the model. The real pattern cloud is irregular, and a partial break doesn't precisely pinpoint its centre.
8.7

Rising target

On a diagonal, it isn't enough to just say “in front,” because the solution changes along two axes at once during the flight. A rising clay lets us study vertical movement in more detail.

A rising clay demands you keep reading its height as it moves away. Near the top it can look slow. But the depth keeps changing, and the meeting point doesn't wait.

When it's launched, the clay has a positive vertical component: part of its movement is upward. Gravity and aerodynamics gradually change that movement until it reaches a high point and then falls. The simulator reproduces the arc with reduced gravity to suggest lift, without modelling the full aerodynamics of a disc. This is a teaching decision. It lets you compare heights, but it doesn't turn the drawn curve into a measurement of a real clay.

From the station, the climb blends with the clay moving away. When vertical speed drops, the clay can feel as if it's hanging in the air. Waiting for that picture increases the distance for a second shot and can change the pattern density. It's best to choose a break point where there's still time to see the line and move with control, depending on the discipline and the instructor.

Visibility matters. With a shotgun whose pattern centre matches the aiming point, the shooter may feel they need to cover a rising clay. Some trap models have a point of impact set above the line of sight to keep the clay visible. Browning documents this purpose for the Citori Plus. Its setting shouldn't be applied to another gun without checking it first. The centre is confirmed by patterning, with a repeatable gun mount and by following the manual.

Raising the comb can lift the eye and change where the gun is aimed relative to the picture. But it doesn't fix a head that lifts, an inconsistent mount, or a swing that stops. Nor should it be adjusted between attempts on a hunch. First you need to work out whether the pattern is genuinely, repeatedly low, or whether the execution is changing. Only after that does a competent gunsmith or instructor decide whether it's a fit problem or a point-of-impact problem.

Specific exercise. In the simulator, repeat a rising central presentation with rib gain at zero and then with a moderate value. Watch how the relationship between the red dot and the blue centre changes. Restore the control when you finish. The exercise explains a relationship; it doesn't prescribe a real-world adjustment.
Two separate systems. The clay's trajectory depends on its initial conditions and the forces acting on it. The impact point depends on the angular relationship between the eye, the sightline and the pattern's effective axis. Changing the second doesn't change the first. The model implements the gain as an elevation added to the shot.
8.8

Five trajectories compared

A rising clay forces you to work out a future height without losing sight of it or blocking it with the gun. By comparing several flight paths from the same reference point, we'll see which parts of the solution stay the same and which change.

Extreme lateral crossings usually call for more visible lead. Each presentation combines direction, height, depth and shot timing differently.

The first clay comes out fast to the left. The lateral crossing dominates, and the meeting point shifts in that direction. The second also goes left, but diagonally: it demands keeping both slope and separation at once.

The third is central and almost vertical. Height and the loss of apparent size matter more here. The fourth presents a medium crossing to the right. The fifth opens up much wider: the bigger the lateral component the shooter sees, the further the clay travels during the pellets' flight, and the greater the lead usually needs to be. The exact value still depends on distance, speed and station.

These descriptions don't assign metres. A different machine, another station or a different distance changes the projection. Even in a regulated discipline, the shooter doesn't know a single fixed presentation in advance. The sector allows variation. FITASC's rules show that flight paths are defined with schemes, angles, heights and distances. They also show that a round can bring together different presentations. The plate here sorts visual families; it doesn't replace the official scheme.

For every flight path, it's enough to ask: where does it appear? Where is it heading? Which part of the movement dominates, and where do I want to break it? Then you practise a single family. Repeating the same presentation lets you connect sensation and outcome; changing station shows how the apparent crossing changes.

Practical decision. Before a set, the student picks one of five families and says only two things out loud: “dominant direction” and “comfortable break zone”. After the attempt, they compare that reading with what they observed. They don't try to memorise five gaps at once.
Honest comparison. The arrows in an infographic encode direction and relationship, but they aren't enough to compare time of flight unless they share scale, speed and distance. That's why they're labelled as qualitative diagrams. The simulator's exact values belong to each generated clay.
8.9

Visual lead methods

The comparison confirms there's no lead distance that works for every case, because it depends on direction and time. Visual methods offer different ways of building that separation during the swing.

There are several coherent ways of building lead. They all share a focus on the clay and on continuity. What changes is where the barrel is when it picks up the clay, and how the separation is created.

In the method of passing the clay, often called swing through, the barrels start behind. They then match the line, travel through the clay and fire once the separation appears. Its advantage for learning is that it lets you feel the relative movement. Its difficulty is that a shooter in a hurry can suddenly accelerate, or slow down right when they spot the gap.

In maintained lead, the barrels are placed out in front and keep the separation while tracking the clay. This shortens the distance the barrel travels across the clay and can give a steady picture. But it demands judging the visual distance early. If the student looks at the barrel to measure it, they lose information about the clay. The separation is controlled with peripheral vision, keeping attention on the clay.

The method the CPSA calls its own picks up and tracks the clay to recognise its speed and line, then pulls ahead before firing. In shooting-ground talk it's usually called pull away. Other schools of teaching use interception, or insertion: the gun enters ahead at a predicted position and adjusts from there. The names change between schools. What matters is that coach and student are talking about the same sequence.

No method removes the need to keep the gun moving. Nor is there one that's optimal for every person, discipline and presentation. The CPSA manual particularly recommends its own method for sporting beginners. But this chapter also covers trap, and doesn't turn that recommendation into a universal law. An instructor may choose a different progression depending on the discipline, gun mount and flight path.

Mixing techniques within the same attempt creates contradictions. For example, starting out in front as in maintained lead, waiting for the clay, letting it pass, and then trying to swing through it forces you to rebuild the rhythm from scratch. During a learning stage, it's best to stick to one method until you can explain its phases and repeat it. Later on, an experienced shooter can adapt the solution without losing continuity.

Specific exercise. Outside a round and with a safe direction, the instructor demonstrates a trajectory unloaded, following the ground's protocol. The student says out loud “acquire, create separation, continue” for the chosen method. They don't practise all three in the same set.
Same end condition. The methods are different control strategies that can produce a valid orientation at the instant of the shot. The final geometry alone doesn't show which one was used. To analyse the technique you have to watch how the barrel and the gaze move over time, not a snapshot of the gap.
8.10

Hold point

Each method organises the approach, the lead and the continuation of the movement differently, but they're all after the same physical meeting point. The hold point marks where that journey begins.

The hold point is the starting orientation of an over-and-under shotgun before the presentation. It's described in the first person: from my eye, aligned behind the top rib and the front bead, I look towards marker clay S.

The specific reference given by the author for training is this: at stations 1 and 2, orient the over-and-under slightly above and to the left of marker clay S; at stations 3, 4 and 5, slightly above and to the right. It describes a training approach based on his own experience. It doesn't replace an instructor's guidance, nor does it serve as a universal rule for any shooter, discipline or shooting ground.

The neutral hold sets the starting orientation on marker clay S. From there, the shotgun has a direct route into whatever flight path appears, and it makes it easier to keep continuity if a second shot is needed. It doesn't remove the need to spot the clay, read its direction and adapt the movement.

A higher hold can shorten the first movement when the presentation matches what was expected. It also brings a risk: the barrels can cut across the flight line. If the first shot misses, you may have to find the clay again and rebuild the aim. By then it will be further away and may have started to drop. The problem grows for anyone used to a flat rib who covers the clay with the gun, because they can lose sight of it right when they need to prepare the second shot.

Nor is it a good idea to aim the gun so far out that you have to swing back onto the clay or hold tension. The right point depends on the station, the discipline, the possible presentation, the method, the stance and how well the over-and-under fits. In trap it's a compromise that has to let you respond to left, centre and right without guessing at one specific machine.

Look point, hold point and break point are not the same thing. The eyes wait to detect; the barrels start from an orientation; and the shot is completed further along. If the student fixes their gaze on the aiming point, they lose information about the background and the clay.

Stance is set up towards the break point to preserve body movement on both sides. An instructor has to adapt this decision to the shooter's mobility and handedness. The muzzle always stays within the authorised zone.

Plato includes a hold position and resets the gun mount when you call for or repeat a clay. It's an interface reference, not a prescription for the shooting ground. Its use lies in comparing, from the virtual eye, how the starting movement changes between a neutral hold and a slightly higher one.

Specific exercise. With an instructor, compare the neutral hold over S with the station reference: slightly up-left at 1-2 or up-right at 3-5. Watch the path of the first movement and, after a simulated miss, check whether you can keep the clay visible and build the second shot without having to search for it again.
Optimisation problem. The hold point reduces the gun's expected travel when there's uncertainty. But it can't minimise it for every trajectory at once. A reasonable choice balances visibility, angular range and physical capacity. The optimal values are personal and depend on context.
8.11

Gaze zone

A good hold point avoids unnecessary movement without getting so far ahead that it stops you reading the presentation. The look point completes the preparation and marks where to look for the clay first.

The eyes wait in a zone where the clay can be spotted early and against a clear background. Peripheral vision signals the presentation. Attention then shifts to the clay to read line and speed. This builds a pre-shot pick-up routine that avoids reverting to the close focus discussed in 6.10.

Looking straight at the machine concentrates attention on a small, close zone. The presentation can appear as a flash crossing the field of view before it's recognised. The CPSA manual suggests looking out in front of the trap, or towards the first place where the clay is clearly visible. In trap, with the gun pre-mounted, this separation also helps you avoid fixing on the barrels.

“Looking into the distance” doesn't mean deliberately defocusing until you lose the clay. Before the presentation, you keep a wide field and receptive attention. When movement appears, the periphery helps detect it. Attention then moves to the clay and picks up direction and speed. The gun stays as a peripheral reference. If the shooter keeps switching focus between the clay and the aiming point to measure the gap, the visual information arrives in fragments.

The specific evidence for shotgun shooting is modest but useful. Causer and colleagues recorded gaze and kinematics in 48 trap, skeet and double trap shooters. Kinematics means how the gun moves. Elite shooters showed an earlier onset and a longer relative duration of the final fixation. The pattern also distinguished successful attempts from misses. The study backs up visual and gun stability. It doesn't fix a universal gaze coordinate, nor does it prove that a beginner should copy one specific duration.

Keeping both eyes open usually widens binocular and peripheral information, but only “when it's appropriate.” Binocular means both eyes are involved. Dominance can sit in the eye over the rib, be cross-dominant, central or incomplete. The CPSA explains that a ghost image can lead the shooter to look at the aiming point and stop the gun. Closing one eye, occluding it, changing shoulder or altering the fit are individual decisions. They're assessed more than once with an instructor and, if there are visual concerns, with a healthcare professional.

Simple check. After the attempt, ask which part of the clay came into sharp focus and when it appeared. Clearly remembering the aiming point but not the line of the clay suggests a near focus. It's a hypothesis to observe, not a diagnosis of dominance.
Reach of “quiet eye”. It's an operational measure of final fixation or tracking on a relevant zone. It isn't an order to keep the eyes still. Acquisition combines eye movements, peripheral attention and tracking. Its organisation depends on the task and the uncertainty. The editorial distinction is deliberate: 6.10 identifies a frequent cause of failure; this section organises the visual hold point, detection and the shift of focus to the clay.
8.12

Stock fit and geometry

The gaze needs room to find and recognise the clay before guiding the gun; looking at the barrel reduces that information. The relationship between the stock and the eye can make this vision easier or harder.

On an over-and-under shotgun, the eye acts as the rear reference, aligned behind the top rib and the front bead. You aim along that rib; you never look through the stock.

A clay-shooting over-and-under isn't aimed like a rifle. Mounting it consistently leaves the eye as the rear reference, behind the top rib and the front bead. The orientation is built by looking along the top of the rib towards that bead, with your main attention on the clay. You never look through the stock: the stock only positions the face, and with it, the eye. If the eye ends up low, high or off to one side, the direction being presented changes, even if the front bead looks close to the clay.

Raising an adjustable comb lifts the cheek and, usually, the eye too, relative to the top rib. In the over-and-under models documented by Browning, the comb allows height and cast to be varied to line the eye up with the plane of the rib. Cast is the sideways offset of the stock. The Citori Plus also offers an adjustable rib that changes the point of impact. These mechanisms shouldn't be generalised: every over-and-under has its own range of adjustment and procedure, and is adjusted unloaded, following its manual.

A shotgun that shoots high places more of the pattern above the aiming point and can keep a rising clay visible. A flatter set-up centres the pattern near the pointed line. Expressions like 50/50 or 70/30 describe a distribution observed at one distance and with one patterning method. They aren't an exact property that stays the same with any cartridge, choke, gun mount or distance.

Technique and mechanics are easily confused with each other. If the face lifts off on the first shot, the eye moves, and the second shot fires from a different geometry. Raising the comb doesn't fix that habit. If several carefully repeated patterns show a stable centre away from the reference point, then there's a case for reviewing the fit. The order is: check the gun mount, measure the pattern, make a small change, and measure again with competent help.

Plato represents rib gain as a ballistic lift in the shot without changing the visual width of the rib. This is a declared abstraction. It lets you observe how a higher centre changes the yellow reference, but it doesn't reproduce anatomy, barrel convergence, or the specific mechanism of any given stock.

Practical decision. Before adjusting any screws, the shooter takes several supervised mounts and several patterns as set out in the manual. If the centre varies from one to the next, the focus is on repeatability. If it stays off-centre, the initial adjustment is recorded and an instructor, gunsmith or manufacturer is consulted.
Angular chain. On an over-and-under, the stock positions the eye; the eye sits behind the top rib; the rib leads to the front bead; and that alignment relates to the pattern's axis. A small change near the eye grows with distance, but it doesn't let you predict centimetres without knowing the gun and testing it in a real trial.
8.13

First and second shot

The stock doesn't change the clay's flight path, but it does relate what the eye sees to the centre of the pattern. This relationship matters especially when comparing the first and second shots.

On an over-and-under, both shots are aimed from the eye along the top rib and the front bead. The second doesn't repeat the first: the clay is further away and at a different stage of its flight.

After a first miss, the clay keeps going. The extra distance lengthens the pattern's journey; the clay may be near the top of its arc or may have started dropping. The shooter keeps their eye behind the top rib of the over-and-under and aims by the front bead, but updates the meeting point. Repeating the first aim exactly doesn't guarantee the second shot.

The pattern can also be different. Many clay-shooting over-and-unders pair a more open first barrel with a tighter second one to keep density up at greater distance. This is a common set-up, not a universal rule: it depends on the gun, the discipline and the regulations. A tighter choke doesn't correct direction; it changes the spread. Cartridge, barrel and choke should all be checked on the pattern plate. Plato's two controls are a teaching comparison, not a test of a real over-and-under.

The most visible problem happens when the shooter lifts their head to check the result, or loses the clay behind the barrels. When they come back, the eye is no longer in the same reference position behind the top rib. Keeping the cheek down, attention on the clay and the gun moving preserves information for correcting. This doesn't mean firing twice automatically: the rules, the state of the clay and safety decide when a second shot is appropriate.

The correction should be based on the evidence available. If the first shot chipped a visible fragment off the back, that could suggest a meeting point that was too far behind. But a partial break isn't a precise instrument. If there was no sign at all, the shooter shouldn't invent a large compensation. An instructor can observe continuity, head height and direction. In the simulator, the two windows separately freeze the centre of the pattern and its error components.

This final section pulls the chapter together. The rule is still coincidence in time and space, but every instant builds a new meeting point. Comparing the five clays teaches that there's no single gap. The sequence of look, hold, appearance and tracking lets you reach that meeting point in order. The second shot shows why the geometry has to stay alive until the action is over.

Specific exercise. In Plato, use a repeated flight path and keep both corrections visible. Fire the first shot deliberately late and build the second without raising your point of view. Compare the distance, height and pattern for each window. On a real shooting ground, this exercise requires supervision and strict compliance with discipline and safety rules.
Model and limit. The code represents an over-and-under with two shots and applies the same base effective velocity to both. It changes the choke opening and evaluates each cloud against its corresponding future position. It doesn't reproduce the real geometry of the top rib and front bead, the barrel switch, recoil, mechanical recovery or the specific deformation of the cartridge.

What we have learned

  • Lead is a future coincidence between clay and pattern. It's not a fixed distance.
  • Direction, speed, height, distance, cartridge, perspective and gun movement all change the solution.
  • Incoming, crossing, diagonal and rising clays each split the lateral, vertical and depth components differently.
  • Visual method, hold point and look point form a single sequence. They should be learned with one consistent coaching voice.
  • Stock and point of impact change the gun's geometry. They're only adjusted after checking the gun mount and the pattern.
  • The second shot requires updating your read without lifting your head or losing continuity.

Expert reading. A rigorous validation has to solve the meeting point in three dimensions. It then has to project the solution from the eye and state the simplifications made for the clay and the cloud of pellets. Comparing only 2D positions can produce a visual aid that looks convincing but is physically wrong.

Chapter 09 · Optional technical interlude

The engineering version: the physical model

A simulator doesn't copy the whole of reality. Plato calculates the movement of the clay and the pellets in three dimensions, in metres and seconds, but it simplifies the aerodynamics, the cartridge and the pattern.

This chapter explains those decisions and compares them with regulations and technical studies. The figures on screen belong to the model: they don't certify a gun, a cartridge or a shooting ground.

9.1

Scope of the model

This geometric explanation lets us distinguish the current position, the meeting point, the height and the lead in both shots. Now we'll turn these relationships into a clear, checkable mathematical model.

The first question isn't whether the picture looks real. What matters is knowing what conclusions the model lets you draw. Plato usefully represents distances and the passage of time. It doesn't reproduce the full physics of the air, and it doesn't certify a gun, a cartridge or a shooting ground.

Plato calculates two main movements. The first is the clay's. It starts from a launch position and is given a velocity sideways, upwards and towards the back. It then falls with reduced acceleration and can be pushed off course by crosswind. The second movement is the shot. It starts from the virtual eye and opens out into 306 pellet directions. The pellets lose speed with distance and drop slightly. The program tracks each one of them while the clay is moving. To decide whether there's a hit, it checks whether a pellet passes close enough and still carries enough energy.

The program uses familiar physical units. Position is expressed in metres, time in seconds, cartridge velocity in metres per second, and energy in joules. It also uses angles and a wind value. Using a real unit, though, doesn't mean the value has been calibrated against field tests. The clay uses only 42% of gravity, to visually mimic the effect of lift. The pellet starts out at 78% of the chosen value. Drag, mass, fall and the energy needed to break are design values. You can check them in the code, but they don't come from a full test of the gun, cartridge and clay working together.

Full studies of a disc's flight take into account its position, tilt, velocity, spin, lift and air resistance. Plato doesn't solve that whole system. Nor does it represent the cartridge wad, chamber pressure, the deformation of each pellet, collisions between pellets, air density, temperature, or the disc's physical orientation. The spin you see on screen helps you recognise the clay, but it doesn't change its flight. The screen does offer numerical data: angle, height at 10 m, range, flight time, lead and impact energy. Other elements, such as the coloured ellipses and the fragments, are visual aids.

Example: as cartridge velocity increases, the yellow aid usually moves closer to the clay because travel time goes down. That comparison holds within the model. It doesn't let you conclude that two specific commercial cartridges will behave in exactly that way, because the nominal figure is measured under a protocol and the real cloud decelerates differently. The right way to use it is to form a qualitative hypothesis and then test it against documentation and real-world patterning.

Practical decision. Before interpreting a screen, classify the observation: “comparative relationship within the simulator”, “regulatory value” or “real measurement”. If you don't know which column it falls into, don't use it to adjust a gun.
Expert layer. The model is kinematic, because it describes motion, and stochastic, because it includes randomness. Some ballistic measurements are simplified. The 3D geometry and the passage of time are consistent within the program. However, its coefficients don't come from a published experimental calibration.
