Its validity is internal and educational; it isn't a certified measurement standard.
9.2

Coordinate system

A model is useful when it explains what it represents, what it simplifies, and what it can't check on its own. To be able to review it, we first need to fix a common coordinate system.

Calculating the flight needs a fixed reference; explaining the miss needs another one centred on the clay. Plato uses x for the sideways axis, y for height and z for depth.

The basic reference is the shooter's virtual eye. Its starting position is (0, 1.7, 0): zero sideways, 1.7 m in height and zero depth. The horizontal and depth origin therefore coincides with the centre station and the eye's plane; the ground sits at y = 0. The front of the trap house is at z = 15 m, and the clay's visible release point at y = 0.16 m. The machine is drawn below and slightly behind that opening.

That fixed frame is used to move every object around the shooting ground. When studying a shot, the origin is shifted to the clay's centre: Δx = xPellet − xClay, Δy = yPellet − yClay and Δz = zPellet − zClay. So Δz < 0 places the pattern closer to the shooter, Δz = 0 puts it on the same depth plane, and Δz > 0 means it has passed further away. Sharing depth isn't enough to break the clay: sideways position and height must also match, within the impact volume.

The positive x axis points to the shooter's right; a positive horizontal angle produces velocity towards that side. The positive y axis points up. The positive z axis moves away from the eye. In Universal Trench, stations are 2.5 m apart and the visible release stays at the centre; in Olympic Trap, stations are 3.15 m apart and the release reference lines up with the active station. When you change station, the code changes eye.x — it doesn't teleport the whole shooting ground.

The sight is defined with two angles. Yaw shows the turn to left or right. Pitch shows whether the sight rises or falls. The program converts those angles into a unit vector — that is, a direction whose length is one. It can also do the reverse operation and recover the angles. The camera then carries the points of the 3D scene onto the 2D screen, using a vertical field of view of 48 degrees. Zoom only changes this way of showing the scene. It doesn't shorten the trap house, doesn't speed up the clay, and doesn't change the meeting point.

Example: from station 1, the eye has a negative x relative to the centre. A clay launched from the centre no longer looks the same direction as it does from station 3. The physical trajectory can be identical, yet its apparent angle changes. That's a geometric reason to practise different stations without putting every difference down to the machine.

Specific exercise. Select a discipline, call for a clay, and note the station, angle and height. Repeat the same clay and check that it keeps its state. Then change station and call for a new one: compare the perspective, not the hits.
Expert layer. The internal units are metres, seconds and radians. The controls display angles in degrees. The projection limits the sight line to the edges of the canvas, so the screen can't represent any angle without limit. The shot originates at eye, which represents the eye. It doesn't originate at a muzzle with length and parallax — that is, with the difference in position between the eye and the barrel.
9.3

Simulation time

Well-defined axes let us place the shooter, the machines, the clay, the wind and the shot without depending on how they look on screen. Next we'll use those axes to define how time passes in the simulation.

Not every frame lasts exactly the same length. That's why the program measures the real time between one image and the next. It caps intervals that are too large, then applies the slow-motion factor before advancing the physics.

The browser hands requestAnimationFrame a high-resolution timestamp. Plato stores the previous one, subtracts the two, and divides by a thousand to get seconds. That interval, called realDt here, is clamped between 0 and 0.05 s. That way, a brief browser stall doesn't make the clay jump several metres at once when it resumes. Then simDt = realDt × slow is calculated. With the initial value of 0.35, one second of clock time advances 0.35 s of flight.

Position isn't calculated by adding a small displacement on every frame. The internal clock sim.t advances with simDt. The trajectory functions recalculate position from the time elapsed since launch. That way, the main parabola depends less on how many frames the computer shows. Fragments and the duration of visual aids are updated differently, using realDt. That's why a correction can stay visible for several real seconds even while the flight is in slow motion.

Slow motion allows factors from 0.15 to 1.00. There's no physical pause button: even the minimum value keeps things moving. “Reset” returns to the waiting state and clears the round, shots and fragments; “Repeat target” restarts the clock with a copy of the last clay. The launch delay, configurable between 0 and 0.35 s, occupies its own phase in which the clay isn't visible yet and firing is locked.

Browser performance can affect smoothness and responsiveness. Web documentation notes that requestAnimationFrame usually follows the screen's refresh rate and is normally paused in hidden tabs. Plato correctly uses the time it receives, so a fast screen doesn't automatically speed up the physics. However, the 50 ms clamp discards some of the time after a heavy load: it improves visual continuity, but it makes the simulated clock fall behind the real one.

Specific example. Watch the same clay at 0.35x to recognise the meeting point, then at 1.00x to judge the rhythm. Don't compare screen times if a test ran with the tab hidden or the device under load.
Verified limit. The model depends on measured time. It doesn't use accumulated fixed intervals or calculate intermediate states between frames. Impact searches do use fixed internal intervals of 1.2 ms and 0.7 ms. Those calculations are separate from the loop that draws the screen.
9.4

Clay launch

Separating physics time from playback speed stops slow motion from changing the result instead of just showing it more slowly. With a stable clock, we can calculate the clay's launch.

Each table doesn't just store an arbitrarily chosen initial velocity. It stores the angle, the height at 10 m, and the range. From that data, the program works out the horizontal and vertical velocities needed for the parabola to satisfy both measurements.

Every visible clay starts from an opening located 15 m from the eye and 0.16 m above the model's ground. The active table supplies a horizontal angle, a height measured 10 m after launch, and a fall distance. The function solveClayLaunch treats height as a parabola relative to the horizontal distance covered, and works out its slope and curvature to satisfy the control points exactly. From that it obtains horizontal velocity, vertical velocity and an approximate flight time.

The horizontal angle turns that speed into two components: vx = sin(angle) × vh and vz = cos(angle) × vh. The vertical component already comes from the resolved slope. The velocity shown by the compliance badge is the magnitude of all three components, converted to km/h by multiplying by 3.6. In Universal Trench, one of the scheme's five rows is picked at random; in Olympic Trap, one of the three machines linked to the station.

FITASC publishes ten schemes and defines, among other measurements, five machines, 15 m from the stations to the trap house, height control at 10 m, and range tolerance. ISSF publishes ten Trap tables for 15 machines. The documented review transcribes those official rows without touching them up to make the exercise easier or more impressive. The “meets official table” badge means the parabola reconstructs the selected row within the programmed tolerance; it doesn't mean a federation has certified the application, or that the browser has measured a real shooting ground.

Robot uses a different selection. A machine draws rows from circular, pseudo-random or weighted sequences. The “Force” control multiplies the range. “Height” adds 0.085 m to the height at 10 m for each degree of the control. “Spread” multiplies the angle. Limits are then applied at the end to avoid extreme values that could cause calculation errors. The drawn machine is placed behind the opening and follows the launch direction, to avoid a visual kink.

Specific example. In Universal Trench, compare the target height and the calculated height on the badge. Then switch on wind: the check still shows the base validation without wind, because the initial constraint and the perturbed flight are different questions.
Expert layer. The program calculates a parabola that passes exactly through the two points defined by the height and the range. To do this, it uses an effective gravity. This way of defining the parameters is useful and stable. However, the resulting velocity isn't an independent regulatory figure and may differ from that of a real machine, where air resistance and lift also come into play.
9.5

Clay movement

The thrower gives the clay a position, a direction and an initial velocity according to the chosen scheme. From that moment on, the clay has to move continuously under the model's forces.

During flight, Plato keeps the sideways velocity and the depthwise velocity constant, except when wind is acting. Height follows a curve built with reduced gravity. The program doesn't calculate air resistance on the disc, or changes in its tilt.

Once visible, the clay's position is obtained directly from the flight time. In depth, z = z0 + vz·t. In height, y = y0 + vy·t − ½·9.81·0.42·t². Sideways, an acceleration proportional to the wind control is added to the initial motion: x = x0 + vx·t + ½·wind·0.28·t². Instantaneous velocity is the derivative of those expressions: the vertical component decreases linearly, and the sideways one changes over time if there's wind.

The clay doesn't lose speed as it moves towards the back. Nor does the program calculate a drag force opposing the motion. It doesn't calculate lift that depends on the disc's angle, or forces that change its orientation. DLR research shows that a full aerodynamic model can include position, velocity, tilt, spin, gravity, air density, lift and drag. Plato replaces that whole system with a constant effective gravity. The 0.42 factor makes the disc appear to stay in the air longer, but it isn't a coefficient that holds for every real case.

The rotation you perceive in the disc's drawing isn't a physical variable either. The ellipse is drawn with a constant shape and colour; the slight turn of certain aids depends on the launch angle, to make them easier to read. There's no angular velocity of the clay that affects stability. That's why the model can't study how a tilted disc rises, falls on its edge, or changes its drag.

Flight ends when the centre reaches y = 0.12 m, leaves a useful visual window, or goes past 120 m of depth. On touching the ground, a visual break is triggered and, if it wasn't a repeat, the round moves on as a miss. The orange trajectory is clipped at ground level, so as not to suggest underground positions. That ending is an interface rule, not a simulation of a bounce or of material fragmentation.

Specific example. Call for a clay with no wind and repeat it with the same clay; then change the wind and call for a new one. Watch the lateral curve, but don't read the value as reproducing a specific weather day: the control is comparative.
What should be expanded. A more complete physical model would need forces that depend on velocity and orientation, disc parameters, attitude integration and calibration data. It would also need to validate measured trajectories, not just pass through two prescribed points.
9.6

Shot representation

The clay rises and moves away while the simplified gravity changes its vertical velocity and its trajectory. The shot needs a similar calculation in order to find it.

The shot starts at a specific moment and in a specific direction. It doesn't travel like a ray, or like a single bullet. Plato creates a central axis and a cloud of pellets. Each pellet can have its own spread, velocity and small delay.

The fire function only acts when there's a visible clay, shots remain, and the play hasn't ended. It takes the current time and converts the sight's yaw and pitch angles into a direction. It then adds the rib's vertical gain. The shot starts from the virtual eye, not from a muzzle with a defined length. This choice avoids having to calculate parallax — that is, the difference in position between the eye and the barrel. All firing lines originate from eye.

The effective velocity is set as 0.78 × cartridge velocity. With the initial control at 400 m/s, the calculation uses 312 m/s before introducing differences between pellets. The central axis is used to find the closest approach to the clay and to draw the likely centre. Around it, 306 directions are created. Only one in three is kept for the visual effect, but all of them take part in hit detection. The image is therefore a sample of a larger calculated cloud.

Each pellet advances according to its direction, its velocity factor and a short delay. Distance covered uses an exponential velocity loss — that is, a loss that follows a curve. Vertically, the full gravitational fall, ½·9.81·t², is subtracted from the moment that pellet starts moving. This is where it differs from the clay. The 0.42 factor only modifies the disc's effective gravity, to visually mimic its lift, without calculating its full aerodynamics. Current external ballistics studies track hundreds of pellets and calculate the air forces on them and their spacing. This confirms that a cloud represents the shot better than a single bullet. However, it doesn't by itself prove that Plato's coefficients are accurate.

The instant matters because the clay keeps moving while the cloud travels. To evaluate a pellet, the code checks the clay at shot time + pellet time. That avoids the mistake of comparing the whole cloud against a single frozen snapshot. The search lasts a maximum of 0.42 s, enough for the distances the design allows for, but it doesn't guarantee results for extreme shots outside that window.

Specific example. With slow motion, fire slightly behind and look at the frozen correction: the clay, the pattern centre and the hits belong to related moments, not to a single layer drawn at random.
Physical limit. The code doesn't represent ignition, pressure, the wad, exit from two separate barrels, choke deformation, or collisions between pellets. Energy is calculated per particle after the virtual muzzle. “First” and “second barrel” change the choke opening, not the origin or the internal ballistics.
9.7

Pattern

Calculating each pellet's trajectory lets us separate out the central direction, the spread, the fall and the arrival time. The set of all those trajectories forms the pattern we see.

Plato's pattern is a 3D cloud. Within a single shot it's always calculated the same way, but it changes with every new shot. Its radius grows with distance and with aperture. It doesn't aim to copy the exact pattern of any particular real shotgun.

The radius is calculated in metres as 0.22 + aperture×0.0045 + distance×(0.0035 + aperture×0.00003). Aperture starts out at 30 for the first shot and 20 for the second. A lower value produces a smaller radius and gets a tighter-choke label. The radius is converted into an angle using atan(radius/distance); that angle bounds the cone within which the directions are spread out.

The distribution isn't uniform, and it doesn't depend on chance alone. The first pellet occupies the central axis. The rest use the golden angle and a radius related to the square root of their order number. That first creates a regular coverage. Ripples, small clusters and variations controlled by the shot's seed are then added. The seed is the starting number that generates the pattern. The “Pattern variance” control decides how far the cloud departs from the regular template. Pellets in the outer zone can travel somewhat more slowly and set off with a delay of fractions of a millisecond. This forms a simplified shot string: the cloud has a certain length, and isn't a perfectly thin plane.

Published trials point to two cautions. Real patterns don't necessarily follow a Gaussian distribution, also known as a bell curve. Also, choke and distance change the pattern's area and density a great deal. A fuller 3D model can study the deformation, contact and forces that separate the pellets in more detail. Plato doesn't count hits within a regulation circle, or represent each pellet's material. The fixed mass of 0.083 g, the 306 samples, and the shape of the cloud are decisions the program makes on its own.

On screen, the blue ellipse is a projected estimate of the radius, not a paper pattern board. After firing, the correction panel maps each pellet onto a plane perpendicular to the shot's axis, using two axes: the clay's direction of travel and height. That view lets you tell apart the centre, the spread, and the pellet that broke it. It doesn't prove that a gap you see drawn will repeat with a real cartridge.

Specific exercise. Repeat one exit without changing the sight, and fire several times with variation at 0% and then at 100%. Compare the stability of the pattern, not the score: each repetition generates a new seed.
First and second shot. Only the choke opening selected for each shot number changes. The mass, nominal pellet count, drag, central velocity and barrel position don't change. This separation is there to teach density and distance, not to simulate two complete cartridges.
9.8

Chokes in the model

The pattern is a three-dimensional cloud with denser and sparser zones, not a uniform circle that guarantees a hit. Chokes change its aperture and must be applied separately to each shot.

On screen, choke isn't represented as a physical part of the gun. It's a control that changes the aperture, and so the radius of the pellet cone. The labels Full, 3/4, 1/2 and 1/4 serve as educational guidance within a scale of 0 to 100.

The “Pattern, shot 1” and “Pattern, shot 2” controls run through values from 0 to 100 in steps of five. The code shows Full up to 20, 3/4 up to 35, 1/2 up to 55, 1/4 up to 75, and “open” above that. The value goes straight into the radius formula. So 20 and 30 aren't millimetres of constriction, or percentages of pellets inside a circle; they're unitless internal parameters.

With the initial values, the first shot uses 30, labelled 3/4, and the second 20, Full. This reproduces the common idea of having a tighter pattern for a second shot that's usually further away. However, the simulator doesn't know about the choke selector, the choke's length, the bore, the wad or the cartridge. Nor does it check whether a specific combination really produces the order you'd expect.

Computational and experimental research confirms the general relationship worth teaching: choke affects spread, and its effect has to be read together with distance. But it also shows that the pattern emerges from more complex interactions, and varies from shot to shot. Manufacturer manuals require you to check the point of impact through real patterning. So the control can compare “more open” and “tighter” within Plato, but it can't choose a tube for competition, or diagnose a barrel.

After the first shot misses, the counter advances and the live drawing switches to the second control. If the clay breaks, there's no second shot. When you repeat the clay, it starts again with the first control. The switch is tied to the shot number, not to time, distance or a manual command. That makes it predictable for single-variable exercises.

Specific example. Keep the flight path and sight fixed. Compare values of 20 and 80 at a similar distance: the second pattern covers more space, but it may show lower local density. Don't conclude that “more open hits more”.
Limited correspondence. The function is monotonic. This means that, as the control value increases, the radius always increases for any positive distance. With a real shotgun, you need to fire several shots onto paper to study the centre, the percentage, the uniformity and the shot-to-shot changes. A single commercial label doesn't describe all of that behaviour.
9.9

Cartridge velocity

Choke changes the pattern's aperture, so the same error can be small or decisive depending on distance and barrel. Cartridge velocity also determines how long the cloud takes and with how much energy it reaches the clay.

The control shows a reference velocity between 360 and 430 m/s. However, the model starts the cloud at 78% of that value, and then reduces its velocity along an exponential curve. That's why reading only the figure on screen can lead you to the wrong conclusion.

If the user selects V, Plato uses v0 = 0.78V for the central pellet. Each peripheral direction can multiply it by a factor between 0.88 and 1.02, depending on variation. The loss with distance follows v(s) = v0·e−0.014s. Integrating that law gives the distance covered, s(t) = ln(1 + 0.014v0t)/0.014; inverting it lets you find the time for a given distance.

For example, 400 m/s in the interface starts out as 312 m/s on the axis before spread is applied. That's an explicit decision the program makes. It shouldn't be hidden behind phrases like “400 m/s cartridge”. SAAMI defines methods and conditions for measuring cartridge velocities, and explains that a cloud can trigger instruments differently depending on whether its leading edge or its dense zone is detected. The manufacturer's figure belongs to that protocol; Plato's velocity belongs to its formula. Comparing them requires knowing the distance to the sensor, the test barrel, the temperature and the detection criterion — data the interface doesn't ask for.

A higher velocity reduces the calculated time to reach the same distance. Since the clay keeps moving during that time, the meeting point usually ends up closer to its current position. It also reduces the fall over time, because the vertical term depends on t². Even so, direction, drag and the numerical search all interact: it's not a good idea to turn the difference into a fixed rule of centimetres without fixing the whole scenario.

The internal units are m/s. When the code shows the clay's initial velocity in km/h, it multiplies m/s by 3.6; to convert the other way, it divides by 3.6. NIST publishes the equivalent factor of 0.2777778. This conversion is exact within the model; what stays approximate is the physical value it's applied to.

Specific exercise. Repeat one flight path and compare 360 with 430 m/s, keeping the sight, choke and wind fixed. Watch how the lead changes. Then restore 400: the goal is to recognise the direction of the effect, not to choose ammunition.
Expert layer. The exponential loss of velocity per metre follows the law dv/ds = −kv. It doesn't use an aerodynamic coefficient calculated from Mach number, Reynolds number, shape and air density. It also stops the search at 0.42 s. So the solution is only studied within that time.
9.10

Rib gain or height adjustment

A higher initial velocity reduces flight time, but it doesn't eliminate air braking, or make every distance effective. Rib gain, meanwhile, creates a vertical difference between the sight and the shot's real centre.

Plato distinguishes between the red dot the user sees and the real direction of the shot's centre. Gain can raise that direction by up to 0.96 degrees. It doesn't move the camera, or make the shotgun's rib any wider.

The control runs from 0 to 100%. The code converts it linearly between 0 and 0.96 degrees. When firing, the direction is calculated as sight pitch + gain. That's why a user can keep the red dot on a reference point while the physical centre, drawn in light blue, travels somewhat above it. The “Flat” and “High” buttons set 0 and 65%, respectively.

The lead aid does the reverse operation. It first finds a physical direction that brings the shot's centre closer to the clay; it then subtracts the gain from the recommended pitch, to say where to place the sight. That way the difference between aiming and point of impact is preserved. The camera and the virtual gun don't rotate separately in depth, and the rib doesn't change width or perspective.

On a real shotgun, raising the comb changes the relationship between the eye and the rib's plane; an adjustable rib can shift the point of impact through its angle. Manufacturers state that this is checked by patterning, and that the adjustments depend on the model. Plato brings all of that together under a single vertical angle. It doesn't represent cast, drop, length of pull, buttplate pitch, cheek pressure, or repeatability of gun mount.

Example: a rising clay gets hidden when the reference sits directly on top of it. With gain, the pattern's centre can pass above the red dot and let you keep the disc in view. This explains a geometric relationship. It doesn't justify raising a real stock by trial and error: technique, fit, safety and pattern should be checked first, with competent help.

Practical decision. Use the control to compare 0% and 65% on the same visual problem. Take to the ground the question “where is my point of impact relative to my line of sight?”, not an order to adjust screws.
Approximate scale. The maximum of 0.96 degrees would shift the axis by about 0.67 m at 40 m if drop and perspective were ignored. That figure describes the program's angle. It doesn't automatically correspond to a 70/30 percentage, a comb height, or a click on a commercial rib.
9.11

Wind

Gain shifts the point of impact relative to what we see, but it doesn't widen the rib or the pattern. Wind adds an outside effect on the clay and the pellets.

The wind control makes the clay drift sideways. It doesn't represent the full real movement of the air. Nor does it act on the pellets. Even though the control is shown in m/s, the result isn't a weather forecast.

The slider allows values between −4 and +4 m/s. It appears in the sideways equation as ½·wind·0.28·t²; so the effective acceleration is 0.28·wind m/s². The sign chooses the side, and the drift grows with the square of time. An early clay barely drifts; one that's been flying longer builds up more displacement. The sign follows the program's x-axis convention, not a meteorological wind-direction notation. What's more, the value stays constant across the whole scene: there are no gusts, no gradient with height, and no zones sheltered by the trap house.

The control's name suggests an air speed. However, the formula converts that value directly into acceleration through a fixed coefficient. It doesn't use mass, area, air density, drag, or the disc's orientation. A real aerodynamic model calculates the forces from the air the clay receives. Those forces can change lift, drag and tilt. Plato offers a simple, easy-to-see change so you can compare situations.

The pellets don't get wind. Their positions only depend on direction, drag-affected distance and reduced vertical fall. As a result, turning on wind changes where the clay will be, while the cloud follows the same physics as in still air. That separation exaggerates the exercise's clarity, and can overstate the change in lead. The badge warns “baseline validation without wind”, because the height and range checks start from the original launch.

Example: with a clay travelling to the right, a positive wind increases its sideways component according to the code's convention. The yellow aid is recalculated with the modified instantaneous velocity. If the user fires using a reference learned without wind, they can end up behind or off line. The lesson worth keeping is that a change in flight forces a change in the solution. The exact amount shouldn't be carried over to a real shooting ground.

Specific exercise. Keep the discipline, scheme, cartridge and station fixed. Compare −4, 0 and +4 m/s across several clays and note only the direction of the correction. Restore zero before studying another variable.
Future extension. A physical wind model should enter as the air's relative velocity for both the disc and the spheres, with forces that depend on velocity and properties. It would need to be calibrated with data, and a decision made on whether to include gusts, spatial profile and orientation.
9.12

Impact detection

Wind acts over different flight times, so its effect points to a tendency, not a correction valid for every case. To decide the outcome, we need to check whether any trajectory actually reaches the clay.

The program doesn't award a hit just because two drawings overlap on screen. It compares each of the 306 pellets against the clay while both are moving. The pellet has to pass within the simplified radius of 5.5 cm and still carry enough energy.

For each direction, pelletHitTarget studies the time from zero to 0.42 s. In each interval, it compares the pellet's movement with the clay's, and looks for the moment they're closest. That way it doesn't need both to coincide exactly at one of the checked instants. The program keeps the smallest distance found. If it drops below 0.055 m, it calculates the energy at the estimated moment of contact.

Energy is calculated with E = ½mv², using a fixed mass of 0.000083 kg and the model's residual velocity. If the contact reaches at least 0.55 J, the pellet breaks the clay. If it enters the radius with less energy, it's logged as a “weak hit”. If none of the 306 pellets qualify, the shot misses even if the blue ellipse looks like it covers the clay. This introduces gaps, and stops the cloud being treated as a solid disc.

The 5.5 cm radius matches the geometric radius of a standard 11 cm diameter clay, but the volume is still simplified as a sphere around its centre. The disc's thin edge, its instantaneous orientation, the specific area struck, the minimum number of hits, and material strength aren't modelled. ISSF counts a clay as hit when at least one visible fragment breaks off; the sporting rule doesn't prescribe Plato's internal energy threshold.

There can be visual false positives and negatives. The 2D projection can overlap two objects that are separated in depth; the drawn ellipse is an estimate, and shows only a third of the pellets. Conversely, a pellet that isn't drawn can be the one that breaks the clay. The result text identifies its index and energy, but the figure shouldn't be read as a laboratory measurement.

Specific example. Place the blue centre near the edge of the clay and repeat. Some seeds will break it and others won't. The exercise shows the difference between “pattern centre” and “presence of a pellet”, not a calibrated probability.
Numerical resolution. At 300 m/s, a 0.7 ms interval covers about 21 cm before drag is applied. That jump could let the calculation skip past the clay without detecting contact. To avoid this, the revised version looks for the shortest distance within each interval. The test, which always produces the same result, confirms the centre, the edge at 55 mm, and the miss at 56 mm. It's still pending to represent the disc's orientation instead of treating it as a sphere.
9.13

Randomness

Continuous detection avoids missing hits that happen between two frames, and ties the break to distance and energy. Even with the same aim, pattern variation can give different results.

Plato uses randomness in three ways. The machine draw can't be repeated exactly. Robot's sequences can be repeated if they keep the same state. The pellet cloud stays stable within a single shot, but gets a new seed on the next one. The seed is the starting number used to generate the pattern.

In Universal Trench and Olympic Trap, Math.random() selects an allowed row. The ECMAScript standard requires it to return a number between 0 and 1 with an approximately uniform distribution, but leaves the strategy to the engine and offers no seed control. Because of that, a new session can't reconstruct the same machine sequence from a saved identifier. That computational randomness picks scenarios and points in the pattern; it doesn't prove that it reproduces the mechanical variability of a real machine, the deformation of the pellets, or human error.

Robot uses its own number generator, started with 24681357. It's called a linear congruential generator, and gets each new number through multiplications and additions with 32-bit integers. The “random” sequences use that number to pick a row. The weighted variant gives more chances to absolute angles of 40 degrees, or heights of 4.4 m. The circular variant advances in order. If the whole starting state were the same, Robot's sequence could be repeated. However, the Reset button doesn't return the seed or the index to their starting value. Resetting the round doesn't reset Robot's series.

The pattern gets an integer seed created with Math.random() every time you fire. From that seed, a function that always gives the same result generates small variations and clusters. All the calculations for the same shot use the same cloud. This is needed so the hit and the correction panel don't contradict each other. A later shot generates a different seed, even when the clay itself is repeated exactly.

The copy stored by “Repeat target” keeps the station, machine, position and initial velocity, so the clay repeats its trajectory under the current parameters. It doesn't freeze wind, cartridge velocity, choke, gain or the pattern's seed. If the user changes those controls, the repeat turns into a deliberate experiment; if they don't, pattern variation is still there. It's an exact repeat of the clay, and an approximate one of the shot.

Specific example. Repeat the same clay five times with variation at 45%. Note whether the result changes near the edge. Then set variation to 0%: the placement stabilises, although each shot still receives a seed.
Data pending to repeat a test. To reproduce a failure, you would need to save the machine seed, the Robot index, the shot seed and all the parameters. The code stores shotSeed in the frozen signal, but doesn't display it or allow it to be re-entered from the screen.
9.14

Parameters for each discipline

Controlled variation stops the pattern from always being identical, while a repeatable seed lets you study the same case. After that, each discipline adds its own limits and launch tables.

A discipline isn't just a change of appearance. It's a set of data and limits. This section explains which tables, machines, measurements and tolerances each discipline uses. How the on-screen selector works is explained afterwards, in 10.6, Discipline selection.

Universal Trench contains ten schemes of five rows. Each row stores machine, angle, height at 10 m and range between 60 and 75 m. The program draws any one of the five machines, draws five throwers 1.1 m apart, and keeps the visible release at the central marker clay. Stations are 2.5 m apart. These choices reflect the FITASC structure, including the 5 m range tolerance used in verification.

Olympic Trap contains ten tables of fifteen rows, transcribed from the second printing of the ISSF 2026 Rule Book. Each station is linked to three machines, and the draw only picks within that group. The target distance is 76 m with a tolerance of ±1 m; heights and angles come from each official row. 15 machines and five marker clays are drawn, with 3.15 m between stations. The automatic comparison protects row-by-row accuracy, even though the aerodynamics and the visual representation remain simplifications by the simulator.

Robot offers five sets built around a single machine. ABT CPSA 2026 and Trap1 FITASC 2025 keep their own regulated limits separately: 70 ±1 m for ABT and 50 ±2 m for Trap1, along with their sectors and heights. Circular fairground, extreme local and pseudo-random are explicitly non-regulation models. Force, height and spread all let you modify any of them; on a regulated profile, the badge switches from “Within limits” to “Outside limits” once the setup stops meeting them.

All the modes share cartridge velocity, two apertures, variation, wind, the rib, slow motion, the delay, and a maximum of two shots in the ordinary round. Load weight in grams isn't part of the physical state. The program doesn't tell 24 g apart from 28 g, or change the pellet count. It also always rotates through five stations and counts 25 clays. These similarities are decisions the simulator makes. They shouldn't hide exceptions in the rules, such as Trap finals with a single shot. What's reviewed here is the content of each configuration. Section 10.6 explains what the screen keeps, resets or shows when the user switches discipline.

Specific example. Compare Universal Trench and Olympic Trap without touching the controls. Read the machine, height and range from the badge. Then go into Extreme Robot and change only the lateral setting. The question is which set of parameters changed, not which discipline “is harder”.
Configuration review. Each row is tied to an edition, a set of units and a set of tolerances. The test compares it against an independent reference table. This tracking makes it possible to check that the data has been copied correctly. It doesn't replace trap-machine testing, field measurements, or federation approval.
9.15

Model validation

Official parameters belong to specific schemes and dates, and shouldn't be mixed with teaching profiles or informal shoots. To validate the model, you need to check those tables and the physical rules the program must always follow.

Plato has two internal checks. They're useful, but they have limits. The first compares the schemes and the launch geometry. The second reviews, with repeatable results, the collision, the energy, the chokes, the internal seed, slow motion, rib gain and the yellow guide. Neither replaces an external experimental test.

scripts/verify-schemes.js extracts the tables from app.html. It then compares them row by row against two independent reference tables: 50 rows from the ten Universal Trench schemes, and 150 rows from the ten ISSF tables. Next, it repeats the parabola calculation and checks the height and the range. In the run on 2 August 2026, it passed more than 200 launches. It showed model velocities between 68.0 and 115.8 km/h, and maximum numerical errors on the order of 10−14 m. It also checks the declared information and limits for ABT CPSA and Trap1 FITASC.

scripts/verify-ballistics.js extracts the ballistic functions and values from app.html. It then runs cases that must always give the same result. It checks hits at the centre and at the clay's physical edge. It also confirms that a case placed one millimetre outside is rejected. It reviews contact without enough energy, the remaining energy, the effective range and the fall. It compares the chokes for shot 1 and shot 2, both in radius and in trajectories. It checks that a cloud repeats with the same seed and changes with a different one. It also verifies that the physical state is the same at 0.35× and at 1.00× when comparing the same simulated time. Finally, it checks the rise from rib gain, the yellow guide, and the relationship between each shot and its frozen markers.

Both scripts carry out internal checks. verify-schemes.js can find badly copied data, incorrect information, or equations that don't match the independent regulatory tables. verify-ballistics.js can detect whether a change breaks the physical rules defined in the code. Passing these tests doesn't prove the coefficients reproduce a real shooting ground. Nor does it, on its own, check every screen interaction, every browser, responsive design, or accessibility.

External validation would need data measured outside the program itself: trajectories captured with calibrated cameras, chronography under protocol, paper patterns, and discrepancies logged by version. The DLR literature offers an example of 3D flight estimation compared against observed impact. ISSF and FITASC regulations provide constraints; they don't validate every coefficient. No software test suite would, on its own, turn Plato into a certifying body.

Minimum record. When reporting a bug, note the browser, mode, scheme, station, visible controls, screenshot, expected result and observed result. The interface doesn't expose or require a seed: to repeat the clay use “Repeat target”; exact reproduction of a pellet cloud is only possible if a technical diagnostic keeps its internal seed.
Checked result. verify-schemes.js and verify-ballistics.js finish with exit code zero on the reviewed copy. This means they haven't found any faults in the scripted checks. These are checks that can be repeated on the internal model, not external experimental tests. scripts/verify-manual.js only validates the integrated book that is about to be published. It checks its 204 subsections, the sources, the editorial closures, the summaries and the PNG images. It must be run again after any change to text or images.
9.16

Formulas explained

The automated tests check that the program is consistent and matches documented cases, but they don't certify a real shooting ground or a real cartridge. The final formulas let you review the relationships that produce these results.

Plato's formulas follow a simple sequence. First, a position and a velocity are set. Then time is allowed to pass. Finally, the meeting point is searched for, and it's checked whether any pellet passes close enough and still carries enough energy.

For the clay, the basic idea is position = starting position + velocity·time + ½·acceleration·time². In z there's no acceleration; in y the acceleration is −9.81×0.42; in x, wind×0.28 appears. The initial components come from the horizontal speed and the angle: sine splits it sideways, cosine forwards. The height at 10 m and the fall point fix the parabola before that breakdown.

For a pellet, the distance isn't v·t, because drag is introduced. With k = 0.014 m⁻¹, s(t)=ln(1+kv0t)/k and t(s)=(eks−1)/(kv0). Its position adds the direction vector, multiplied by that distance, to the eye, and subtracts ½·9.81·t² in height, using full gravity for that particle's own local flight time. The residual velocity is v=v0e−ks, and the energy E=½mv². The 0.42 coefficient from the earlier formula belongs to the clay, and doesn't take part in this fall.

The first estimate of the meeting point takes the clay's current position and its velocity at that moment. It works out how long the shot's centre would take to get there. It then moves the clay forward over that time, and repeats the calculation five times. Next, recommendedAimAt adjusts the yaw and pitch angles. It searches for the smallest distance through progressively smaller angular changes. It doesn't solve a single exact equation for the disc and the cloud. It starts from a first mathematical estimate and improves it through a numerical search that follows the model's rules.

The instantaneous distance between a pellet and the clay's centre is d = √((xp−xc)²+(yp−yc)²+(zp−zc)²). The integrator steps forward in 0.0007 s intervals, but the collision isn't decided only at those endpoints: within each interval, the instant of closest relative approach is calculated analytically. There's contact if that minimum distance is equal to or less than 0.055 m, the approximate physical radius of the modelled clay, and there's only a break if the relative energy also reaches 0.55 J. The conversions keep to SI: the controls use m, s, m/s, m/s², kg and J; the display converts the clay's velocity to km/h with the factor 3.6, and angles between radians and degrees with π/180.

Full example: a row supplies 30 degrees, 2 m at 10 m, and 70 m of range. The solution works out the velocities, the clock places the clay, the 400 cartridge turns into an effective 312 m/s, the aid estimates the future point, gain separates sight and axis, and 306 pellets look for contact. Every arrow in that chain corresponds to code; the absolute fidelity of its constants requires external data.

Reading for beginners. Don't memorise symbols. Keep hold of four questions: where does it start? Where is it heading? How much time passes? And what distance is left between the two? The equations are just a compact way of always answering them the same way.
Checking units. All the main sums combine quantities with compatible units. The factors 0.42, 0.28 and 0.78 have no unit. In that order, they affect the clay's effective gravity, the lateral shift from wind, and the shot's effective initial velocity. 0.014 should be read per metre so that the exponent has no units. The pellets' vertical acceleration is 9.81 m/s², with no other scaling factor.

What we have learned

The model is useful because it shares coordinates, units and time. The launch respects the table, the cloud calculates each pellet, and the hit compares position and energy. Even so, external measurements are still needed to calibrate its values.

  • Geometry works in three dimensions, and the camera only projects the result.
  • Slow motion scales the flight, while the visual duration of the analysis follows real time.
  • Choke, cartridge, rib and wind are teaching controls with limited real-world correspondence.
  • Repeat keeps the clay, but doesn't freeze the pattern's seed or any controls you've changed.
  • Two internal reviews check the schemes, the geometry, and the ballistic rules that must always give the same result. Validation through external tests remains future work.

Expert reading. The technical priority isn't adding more decimal places. What matters is being able to reconstruct and review each result. To do that, you have to separate out the regulatory data, the values chosen for the design, and real-world measurements. It's also worth expanding property-based and collision tests, logging seeds, and comparing the model against real trajectories, velocity measurements and patterns.

Chapter 10

How the simulator works, exactly

Plato lets you stop and review an action that, on the shooting ground, happens very fast. The sequence is simple: set up, call, track, fire and analyse.

The controls are there to compare one variable at a time. The application doesn't replace the shooting ground, and it doesn't teach safe gun handling, recoil, physical gun mount, or competitive pressure.

10.1

Access and requirements

The physical model lets us calculate the trajectory, flight time, spread, energy and contact, always within the limits stated. Now we'll leave the equations behind and learn how to use the screen that shows these calculations.

Opening Plato takes very little. This section explains what the browser needs, and what it means for the whole application to live inside a single web file.

The simplest way to use Plato is to open the application published on GitHub Pages. There's no program to install and no account to create. The browser downloads app.html, parses its HTML, CSS and JavaScript, and draws the scene in an area called canvas. The application doesn't query a server to calculate each clay. States, trajectories, shots and graphics are all calculated inside the browser itself. That's why the most comfortable environment is a current desktop or laptop computer with JavaScript enabled.

The design responds to different widths, but that isn't the same as a full mobile experience. Below 1040 pixels, the bar and the controls reorganise themselves, and below 560 pixels they drop into a single column. However, the main gesture is written for a mouse: moving aims, the right button calls for the clay, and the left one fires. There are no dedicated touch-event handlers. On a phone, you can open the page and tap the visible buttons, but fine tracking of the clay shouldn't be promised as equivalent to using a mouse.

The application can also be run from a copy of the repository. Since everything lives in a single file, opening app.html directly can work in modern browsers. Even so, a simple local server better reproduces the context of a website: from the project folder, you can use, for example, python3 -m http.server 8000 and visit http://localhost:8000/app.html. Once the files are downloaded, that local route doesn't need a connection for the physics or the drawing. The project's external links and the fonts, naturally, do need a network.

There's no closed list of certified browsers, or a certified minimum resolution, in the code. The sensible recommendation is to test an up-to-date browser with support for Canvas 2D, mouse events and requestAnimationFrame, and to have enough width to read the scene, the seven indicators and the parameters at the same time. If the clay looks small, the “Visual zoom” control enlarges the projection without changing distances or physics.

First access. Open the application in a window of at least 1280 × 720, leave the browser zoom at 100 %, keep “Slow motion” at 0.35x, and check that you can move the red dot before calling for your first clay.
Expert reading. The project doesn't need a build process or a calculation server, also called a backend, in app.html. It can calculate offline once the files are available. However, it isn't a progressive web app, it doesn't install a service worker to manage offline resources, and it doesn't guarantee that the public address stays cached.
10.2

Main screen

Plato can be opened on the web, or run locally with a modern browser, without installing a special server. The main screen brings together the scene, the state, the aids and the controls.

The screen brings together the shooting ground, the sight, several aids and the shot's data. This section explains how to tell each zone apart, without confusing them.

The top strip holds the immediate actions. On the left are “Call target”, “Repeat target”, “Fire”, “Reset” and the two station buttons. Next to them, “Flat” and “High” are quick shortcuts to two rib-gain values. Then come seven indicators: state, round, station, clay, lead, aim and result. Finally, three checkboxes let you show or hide the lead aid, the pattern and the trajectory.

The centre is the view from the shooter's eye, placed by the model at a height of 1.70 m. The grid floor and the horizon stay fixed; the mouse moves the shotgun's direction. Ahead are the five stations, the edge of the trap house, and the marker clay labelled “S”. If the translucent ground is switched on, you can also see the machines. When you call a clay, its orange disc appears. A dashed orange line shows the expected flight. The yellow area marks the ideal lead point. The blue area surrounds the pattern's likely centre at the spot you're aiming at.

The red dot is the sight. With rib gain, a light blue point joined by a line can appear above it: it's the physical direction of the shot's centre after applying the elevation, not a second sight. After firing, green marks a pellet that breaks the clay; the bottom-left panel keeps the correction for shots 1 and 2. The legend in the bottom right corner is a reminder of this visual grammar.

The bottom strip brings together the settings that stay the same between attempts. That's where you choose discipline, scheme, station, hold, zoom, slow motion, delay and cartridge velocity. It also shows the two pattern apertures, variance, analysis duration, wind, gain, the three Robot controls, and the “Generate notebook” button, which produces a printable PDF of the whole round with whatever settings are active at that moment, with a cover, an index, one sheet per clay, and a final page with the usage notice and the licence. The top bar reports on the current attempt. The bottom bar defines the conditions of the experiment. The badge in the centre shows the machine, the angle, the model's velocity, the height at 10 m, the range and the flight time. Its status can be “Matches official table”, “Within limits”, “Outside limits” or “Non-regulated model”.

Initial reading. Before shooting, locate just five references: the orange clay, the red sight, the yellow zone, the blue cloud and the dashed trajectory. Add the numbers once that visual reading no longer takes any effort.
Expert reading. The 3D scene is transformed so that it can be drawn on a 2D Canvas. The zoom changes the focal length used in that transformation. The sight changes its horizontal and vertical angles. Meanwhile, the shooting ground's point of view stays fixed to the shooter's station.
10.3

Calling for the target

The interface separates what happens on the field, the controls that change it, and the review of the result. The first action is to call for a clay.

Several steps happen between the call and the clay's appearance. The program checks the turn, chooses the release, applies the delay, and only then shows the flight.

You can call for a clay three ways: with the “Call target” button, with the right mouse button over the scene, or by pressing Enter. The code blocks a new call while a clay is in delay or being tracked, while a station change is still pending, or after all 25 clays have been used. That protection stops clays overlapping, or a correction that still belongs to the previous attempt from being disturbed.

On the first call of a round, the chosen starting station gets locked in. The application updates the eye's position, returns the sight to the selected hold, and checks the active scheme. In Universal Trench, it randomly picks one of the scheme's five rows. In Olympic Trap, it randomly takes one of the three machines linked to the station. In Robot, it uses the circular, pseudo-random or weighted sequence from the chosen configuration. It then converts the angle, the height at 10 m and the range into a three-dimensional initial velocity.

The state changes from “Waiting” to “Delay”. The “Release delay” value, between 0 and 0.35 s and defaulting to 0.03 s, represents the wait built into the call system or thrower. It doesn't include the human time needed to recognise and move: that time starts when the user sees the clay. Once the delay runs out, the state moves to “Tracking”, and the flight function starts returning visible positions.

Calling for a clay also clears shots, streaks and fragments from the previous scene, though it briefly keeps the frozen corrections until the new attempt replaces them. It resets the shot counter to zero, the result to a dash, and shows the “PULL” banner for 360 ms. The chosen clay is stored as the last clay, so “Repeat target” can rebuild it later.

This sequence is a piece of software logic. The button, the right click and Enter don't represent regulation calls, or a certified acoustic system. The delay is an observational variable, not the measured response of a real machine.

Exercise. Set the delay to 0.03 s, use a neutral hold, and call for five clays without shooting. Watch when the disc appears and how long you take to start tracking it. Then raise the delay to 0.30 s and see how an artificial wait can induce anticipation.
Model limit. The Universal mode uses Math.random() and doesn't show the seed, the initial number used to generate the draw. Robot uses an internal seed that does produce a calculated sequence. So “call again” doesn't guarantee the same launch. To compare the trajectory exactly, you need to use “Repeat target”.
10.4

Firing the shot

The call starts the small release delay and chooses a trajectory, but it doesn't move the aiming point. When you fire, the position the user chose turns into a cloud of pellets.

When you press to fire, the application creates a cloud of pellets, follows its path, and calculates the result. This section explains that sequence.

The user fires with the “Fire” button, the left mouse button over the scene, or the space bar. The action is only accepted when there's a visible target being tracked. You can't fire during the delay, after the clay has finished, while it's falling after two misses, or after using up the two available cartridges.

The first shot uses “Pattern, shot 1”, and the second, “Pattern, shot 2”. The starting values correspond to the labels 3/4 and Full, though the underlying slider is a teaching-scale aperture between 0 and 100. The indicated cartridge velocity is multiplied by 0.78 to get the effective velocity the model uses. Rib gain is added to the physical vertical angle without moving the red sight.

The simulator creates 306 pellet directions. It uses the golden angle to stop overly regular rings from forming. The “Pattern variance” control adds small gaps, clusters, delays and velocity differences. Each pellet loses speed along an exponential curve, and falls with the full acceleration of gravity. In each interval, the program looks for the moment when the pellet and the clay are closest. That stops a fast pellet passing through the clay between two checks without being detected. Contact uses a radius of 0.055 m. What's more, breaking the clay needs enough energy within a maximum window of 0.42 s.

Geometric contact isn't enough: the pellet has to keep at least 0.55 J, according to the internal threshold. If it does, the clay breaks, fragments appear, and the result shows centre or edge, pellet number and energy. If it touches without enough energy, “weak hit” is shown. If there's no contact, the miss for shot 1 or 2 is logged. After a first miss, you can still track the same clay; after the second, the state moves to falling until it ends.

The result belongs to the program, and doesn't replace the referee. The model doesn't rule on irregular clays, late releases, cartridge failures, gun malfunctions, or authorised re-throws. It only evaluates two shots, geometric contact and minimum energy.

Exercise. Repeat a clay and deliberately make the first shot fall behind it. Keep your movement going and use the second shot to correct. Compare the two thumbnails in the panel, not just the “broken” or “missed” text.
Scope of the figure. The 306 pellets, the mass of 0.000083 kg, the drag per metre and the 0.55 J threshold are decisions of the reviewed model, not a certification of a real cartridge. A pattern test depends on the specific gun, ammunition, choke and distance.
10.5

Shotgun movement

Each left click uses one barrel, and stores its own parameters, its pattern and its result. The mouse moves the aim to place the shot, while the scene stays fixed.

The mouse moves the red dot and represents the shotgun's direction. However, this movement alone doesn't teach the full gesture the body makes on the shooting ground.

Inside the canvas, the mouse position turns into two angles from the eye: sideways turn and elevation. The red dot is drawn exactly in that direction. Moving the cursor to the right increases the turn; raising it increases the vertical angle. The application hides the native cursor, so there aren't two visual references competing with each other.

The viewpoint doesn't turn freely with the sight. The horizon, the trap house and the ground keep their projection from the station; only the aiming line changes. This choice lets you compare the sight against a stable background. You can also fine-tune it with the arrow keys, which change the angles at an internal rate of 1.45 radians per second while held down.

The code holds a sensitivity of 0.0019 radians per pixel for the pointer-lock case, but the interface doesn't request that lock. In normal use, it takes the cursor's absolute position within the canvas. The visible limits are the edges of the canvas: the sight's projection is clipped to stay between zero and the available width or height. There's no sensitivity slider exposed to the user.

The mouse trains visual reading and decision-making, not muscle memory. On the shooting ground, weight, feet, torso, gun mount, noise and recoil all come into play. Moving the mouse well can show you understand the meeting point, but not that the gesture with a real shotgun is safe or repeatable.

Hold and zoom help separate two problems. “Hold” changes the starting direction the sight returns to; “Visual zoom” changes the projected scale, without physically bringing the eye or the target any closer. If you increase zoom, a small angular movement covers more pixels, and can feel more sensitive even though the clay's trajectory hasn't changed. To compare two tracking attempts, keep both values fixed and describe the error using the panel, not the apparent distance the cursor travelled.

Exercise. Track the clay without shooting and try to keep the red dot moving smoothly. Repeat with the yellow aid hidden. The goal isn't cursor speed, but avoiding sudden jumps once you recognise the direction.
Exact correspondence. aimFromScreen() uses the camera's focal length and trigonometric functions to convert pixels into the yaw and pitch angles. The zoom changes this visual conversion. That's why, when the scene is zoomed in, the same pixel movement represents a smaller angle.
10.6

Discipline selection

Moving the sight doesn't shift the whole field; it changes the shotgun's direction from the shooter's eye. The chosen discipline decides which machines and schemes can launch the clay.

Choosing Universal Trench, Olympic Trap or Robot changes the machines and the way releases are selected. The user also needs to check which settings stay active. The tables and physical limits for each option are explained in 9.14, Parameters for each discipline.

The selector holds exactly three disciplines: Universal Trench, Olympic Trap and Robot. There's no Skeet, Double Trap, Compak Sporting or Courses hidden as options. Changing discipline rebuilds the scheme selector, and changes the visible geometry of machines and marker clays. If the application is waiting, it also clears the previous review and returns the sight to the hold.

Universal Trench offers ten schemes with five machines. Each row defines machine number, horizontal angle, height at 10 m and fall distance; when you call, a row is drawn. Olympic Trap offers ten official tables of fifteen machines, grouped in threes in front of each station. Only one of the three linked to the active station enters the draw, and the target range is 76 m.

Robot offers five ready-made configurations, also called presets: “ABT CPSA 2026”, “Trap1 FITASC 2025”, “Circular fairground robot”, “Extreme local robot” and “Pseudo-random robot”. All of them use a single machine. The first two each follow their own regulated limits, and select releases within their sectors. The other three represent non-regulation popular-shooting models. Their sequences can be circular, weighted, or pseudo-random. The badge reports whether a regulated configuration is still within its limits after you move the controls.

The chosen configuration doesn't automatically change every laboratory control. Cartridge velocity, chokes, pattern variance, wind, slow motion, delay and gain stay as they were. The “Robot force”, “Robot height” and “Robot spread” controls only act in the Robot discipline, even though they stay visible in the other two. To compare disciplines rigorously, you also need to note down and restore these shared parameters.

The discipline names describe implemented sets, not a full competition. Plato's round keeps 25 targets and two cartridges as a shared framework, but it doesn't model round times, a squad's turns, referee calls, malfunctions, penalties, or every condition of a FITASC or ISSF event. What the verifier confirms is that the rows are consistent with the coded heights and ranges. A shooting ground's sporting compliance requires the current regulation, measurement and the involvement of the competent officials. This section deals with the usage flow and the visible states; it doesn't re-justify the regulatory figures worked out in 9.14.

Clean comparison. Use station 3, wind 0, cartridge 400 m/s and camera 0.35x. Call for five Universal clays and five Olympic clays without changing anything else. Describe the differences in launch before looking at the regulation label.
Verification. The script compares 50 Universal rows and 150 Olympic rows against independent reference regulation tables. It also checks height and range against the internal physics. It also reviews the limits and the information linked to ABT CPSA and Trap1 FITASC. It doesn't carry out an experimental test of a machine or prove that the set-up is approved by a federation.
10.7

Shooting stations

Universal Trench, Olympic Trap and Robot use the same engine, but have different tables, limits and regulatory references. The shooting station changes the viewpoint we watch the ground from.

The same scheme can look different from stations 1, 3 and 5. The reason is that the eye's position relative to the trap house changes, and with it, the clay's apparent angle.

The station can be chosen with a slider from 1 to 5, or with the “Station -” and “Station +” buttons. Before the round starts, the selected number becomes the starting station. When you call the first clay, the position gets locked in, and the round advances automatically after each target counted. If you start on 3, the sequence is 3, 4, 5, 1, 2, and it keeps going round until 25 are complete.

Changing station shifts the eye sideways. Universal Trench and Robot use an internal spacing of 2.5 m; Olympic Trap uses 3.15 m. The eye doesn't move forwards, or change height. From that new position, the directions towards the hold, the clay, the meeting point and the cloud are all recalculated. That's why a flight that looks almost head-on from the centre can pick up a sideways component from an end station.

In Universal Trench and Robot, the release marker clay stays at the centre of the trap house. In Olympic Trap there are five marker clays, and the active one lines up with the station. The fifteen machines are grouped in threes; the clay selection is limited to the group that matches the shooter. This relationship between station, marker clay and group is a functional difference, not just a change of decoration.

The side buttons change the station, and also carry out a full reset. The slider can only change the effective station while the round isn't locked in. “Repeat target” is the exception: it brings back the station stored with the release, and doesn't advance the rotation. That's the right tool for comparing references without the geometry changing halfway through the exercise.

A round of 25 produces five turns at each of the five stations, but the simulator doesn't coordinate with other shooters. The rotation is an individual counter. For example, if you start at station 4, the first five targets correspond to 4, 5, 1, 2 and 3; the sixth goes back to 4. This confirmed rule helps you anticipate the next viewpoint, though it doesn't, on its own, describe the running order of a real squad.

Exercise. Choose a Universal scheme, call for a clay at station 1 and repeat it twice. Reset, select station 5 and look for a launch from a similar family. Compare the apparent direction, not a distance memorised in pixels.
Nuance. The exact repetition keeps the target's initial position and speed. A comparison between stations can't be done with “Repeat target” without modifying the code, because that function deliberately restores the original station.
10.8

Clay speed

Rotating through the five stations changes the viewing angle without arbitrarily moving the ground or the marker clay. From any station, the clay's velocity remains a fundamental variable.

There's no control called “Clay velocity”. In Universal Trench and Olympic Trap, velocity is calculated from the height and the range of each release. In Robot, it can be changed indirectly.

Plato doesn't offer a general control called “Clay velocity”. In Universal Trench and Olympic Trap, the user chooses discipline and scheme; the code then takes the height at 10 m and the fall distance from a row. A function solves the parabola that satisfies both conditions under reduced gravity, and obtains the horizontal and vertical components. The initial velocity shown on the badge is the result of that calculation.

This matters because the visible number isn't an independent piece of data. It can't be kept fixed while everything else changes. Two rows can have a similar range and different heights. So they can need different initial velocities. This version's verifier checked 200 launches from Universal Trench and Olympic Trap. It got model velocities between 68.0 and 115.8 km/h. The numerical errors in height and range were practically nil, after comparing the rows against independent regulatory tables.

In Robot there is an indirect route. “Robot force” multiplies the nominal fall distance between 70% and 135%, then clamped to the 45 to 120 m range. Since the ballistic solution has to reach that new distance, the initial velocity changes. “Robot height” can also change it, by varying the height-at-10-m condition. The badge at the top lets you watch the result in km/h.

A faster release covers more ground during the same reaction time, and usually demands more exacting reading and movement. However, lead doesn't depend on km/h alone: angle relative to the eye, current distance, vertical component, cartridge and the moment of the shot all matter. The screen shows the model's initial velocity, not a radar measurement or the perceived angular velocity.

That's why “faster” might not look that way from every station. A clay moving away almost along the line of sight can have a lot of linear velocity and only shift a few pixels sideways; a slower one crossing hard can sweep across the screen fast. For a beginner, the badge's figure answers “how fast does the model release it”; the apparent movement answers “how fast does its direction change from my eye”. They're related quantities, but they aren't interchangeable.

Exercise. In ABT CPSA 2026, keep the height, lateral, cartridge and station fixed. Compare 80 %, 100 % and 120 % power. Note the model velocity, flight time, lead and “Within/Outside limits” status; don't change three variables at once.
Model. The trajectory uses g × 0.42 to visually represent the aerodynamic effect without solving the full dynamics of a real disc. So the internal relationship is consistent and verifiable, but it doesn't turn the calculated velocity into a test of a real trap machine.
10.9

Cartridge velocity

The clay's velocity changes the time available and the lead needed, always within the chosen profile. Cartridge velocity acts on the other side of that timing calculation.

The cartridge control can move from 360 to 430 m/s. Doing so changes how long the cloud takes to arrive. This section also explains which part of that behaviour is simplified.

The “Cartridge” slider accepts values between 360 and 430 m/s in steps of 5, defaulting to 400 m/s. That number is presented as a reference initial velocity. The engine doesn't launch the pellets at exactly that figure: it multiplies it by 0.78, so the default value produces an effective initial velocity of 312 m/s for the calculation.

From there, an exponential loss with distance is applied. The time needed to reach a given plane is calculated with the same model, and feeds both the yellow lead solution and the evaluation of each pellet. If you increase the configured velocity, travel time goes down, and the target covers less distance before the meeting point. The ideal point can move closer to the clay, though the visual difference depends a great deal on the angle and the distance.

The control doesn't choose a 24 g or 28 g load, pellet diameter, wad, powder, pressure or batch. The cloud always keeps 306 simulated particles and a fixed mass per pellet. So using 400 m/s doesn't mean you've reproduced a product advertised as 400 m/s. It's a comparison of one variable in the model, within a system that keeps every other decision constant.

To see it without confusion, it helps to repeat exactly the same clay. The user can fire once at 360 m/s, wait for it to finish, change only the cartridge, and repeat at 430 m/s. Watch the yellow region and the arrival time, and don't necessarily expect a big difference in the cloud's size, because aperture is governed by a different control.

A numerical example makes the internal scale clear: 360 m/s becomes 280.8 m/s effective, 400 becomes 312 m/s, and 430 becomes 335.4 m/s, before the loss with distance is applied. This doesn't mean the pellet keeps those velocities all the way to the clay. The isolated comparison asks how much the travel time changes within this drag law; it doesn't let you infer pressure, recoil, penetration or the suitability of a real load.

Exercise. Choose a crossing clay, repeat it three times and use 360, 400 and 430 m/s. Shoot from roughly the same red dot each time. Compare whether the centre falls behind or ahead, and write a one-sentence conclusion.
Warning. A manufacturer's nominal velocity depends on the test method and conditions, and the real velocity drops during flight. Plato's scale is for comparative sensitivity; it doesn't replace manufacturer data, test benches or validated ballistic tables.
10.10

Chokes

The cartridge determines how long the pattern takes and what energy it keeps on arrival, not just its release velocity. Chokes regulate the cloud's aperture on each shot.

Plato lets you choose one aperture for the first shot, and another for the second. A more open pattern covers more space, but it doesn't guarantee a hit, because it also spreads the pellets out more.

The controls are called “Pattern, shot 1” and “Pattern, shot 2”, because what the user sees is the resulting pattern. Each one runs along an internal scale from 0 to 100. The interface translates the ranges into labels: up to 20, Full; up to 35, 3/4; up to 55, 1/2; up to 75, 1/4; and above that, open. The starting values are 30 and 20 — that is, 3/4 for the first, and Full for the second.

The pattern's radius is calculated with a fixed term, another tied to the aperture value, and another that grows with distance. A high value produces a wider cone; a low one, narrower. The live blue cloud follows the control that matches the next cartridge. After the first shot misses, the display automatically switches to the second choke.

The simulation keeps 306 pellets in both cases. Opening spreads those directions over a wider cone and can create more gaps in a particular region. Closing concentrates them, but demands placing the centre more precisely. The result isn't decided by comparing the clay with a solid circle: individual pellets propagate, and only an intersection with enough energy breaks it.

The label is a teaching equivalence, not the regulatory measurement of a real choke. Plato doesn't know the bore diameter, the constriction in millimetres, the cartridge, the wad, the specific deformation, or the distribution measured on a pattern plate. Its purpose is to show the trade-off between coverage and density, especially between a close first shot and a more distant second one.

The label boundaries also deserve attention: a value of 20 still shows as Full and 21 already falls into 3/4; 35 is still 3/4 and 36 moves to 1/2. It's an interface step over a continuous formula, not a sudden physical jump. If two tests use 20 and 21, the label changes more than the geometry does. The expert should record the numerical value; the beginner can use the label to get their bearings without treating it as an absolute classification.

Exercise. Repeat a clay and shoot at the same point with Full choke and Open choke on the first shot. Turn on “Pattern variation” at 45 %. Don't just compare break or miss: look at the spread, gaps and the position of the red centre in the panel.
Expert reading. patternRadius() defines the geometry; pelletDirections() defines the occupancy. The scale doesn't contain pattern-percentage tables by choke and distance, so it shouldn't be used to choose real equipment.
10.11

Rib gain

Choosing different chokes for the first and second shot lets you compare different densities at different distances. Rib gain shifts the shot's real centre vertically relative to the sight.

The sight can end up below the shot's real centre. This separation aims to show, in a simple way, the effect of rib gain on the point of impact.

The “Rib gain” control runs from 0 to 100% in steps of 5. Internally, that percentage is converted into an angle between 0 and 0.96°. The “Flat” button sets it to zero; the “High” button fixes it at 65%, equivalent to about 0.62°. These are quick shortcuts, not two complete gun types.

The red sight still shows where the user places the reference. When there's gain, the engine adds the angle to the shot's physical pitch. On screen, a slightly higher light blue point appears, with a faint line between the two. The yellow solution compensates for that elevation: for the physical centre to reach the meeting point, the recommended visual reference may end up slightly lower.

On a rising clay, this separation helps explain why a setup that hits high can keep the target more visible above the rib. But the simulator doesn't draw an adjustable stock, nor does it calculate the shooter's anatomy. The control brings together, in a single gain, the teaching effect of the eye-rib-point of impact relationship; it doesn't diagnose how much a real comb should be adjusted.

The correct comparison keeps the clay, station, cartridge and movement unchanged. With zero gain, align the sight with the aid and see where the blue centre ends up. Repeat at 65% and watch how the visual aid asks for a different reference to produce a similar meeting point. If you also change the hold or the zoom, you mix in different perceptual causes.

As an order of magnitude, the “High” shortcut applies 0.624°. At 20 m, a constant angular separation of that size equates to roughly 22 cm vertically; the maximum of 0.96° works out to around 34 cm, using the tangent and without adding any other corrections. This is a geometric reading of the model, not a table of real point of impact. It helps explain why a small angular difference can become visible with distance.

“Rib gain” is, moreover, the name chosen by the interface; it shouldn't be treated as a universal standardised quantity. To report a test, it's best to record the percentage and the equivalent angle, together with distance and trajectory, rather than simply saying “high”.

Exercise. Use a rising clay in slow motion. Alternate between “Flat” and “High” over repeats of the same target and describe how much of the clay stays visible. Don't apply the adjustment to a real shotgun without an instructor or gunsmith.
Limit. The gain is added as a constant angle to every shot. A real shotgun combines stock geometry, eye alignment, convergence, gun-mount dynamics and pattern; the equivalence is approximate and deliberately simple.
10.12

Wind

Gain explains why the red point and the shot's real centre may not coincide, even though the clay's flight hasn't changed. The wind control lets you add a known environmental effect.

Wind in Plato pushes the clay to one side. It doesn't alter the whole scene, nor does it push the pellets. This section clarifies what changes and what stays the same.

The “Wind” slider runs from −4 to +4 m/s in steps of 0.2, with zero as the default. There's no separate on/off checkbox: a value of zero switches it off. The sign determines which side of the lateral axis the acceleration is applied to. It's worth checking on screen which way each sign moves the clay. The left and right the shooter sees also depend on the station and the flight angle.
