The code adds to the clay's lateral axis a term proportional to half the time squared. It also updates its lateral velocity consistently with that acceleration. The deviation grows during the flight: at first it may look small, and later it clearly separates the orange trajectory from the no-wind version. The yellow aid is recalculated using the modified position and velocity.

Wind doesn't act on the pellets. Their position depends on the shot's direction, velocity loss and vertical drop. They receive no lateral movement caused by the air. There are also no gusts, turbulence, changes in the disc's orientation, or variations over time. So the control serves to show that a target that drifts forces you to update the meeting point. It doesn't represent the full ballistics within the atmosphere.

When the value isn't zero, the badge at the top adds “base validation, no wind”. The warning is a reminder that the regulatory check refers to the base trajectory calculated from height and range, not to the disturbed flight being observed. A case can show “meets regulations” for its base launch while, at the same time, being distorted by an experimental wind.

The formula itself lets you gauge the simplification. With +4 m/s on the control and 2 seconds of flight, the added deviation is 2.24 m; with −4 it takes the opposite sign. Doubling the time quadruples that part of the displacement. The result illustrates accumulation and direction, but it doesn't claim that a real 4 m/s wind displaces any clay by exactly that amount: orientation, lift, spin, gusts and specific aerodynamics are all missing.

Because of that time dependence, comparing two captures taken at different instants is misleading. A valid observation starts from the same repeat, waits for the same moment in the flight, and changes only the sign or the intensity.

Exercise. Repeat a clay with wind at 0, −4 and +4 m/s. Don't shoot first: watch when the trajectories start to separate. Then aim using the same reference and check why a fixed correction stops working.
Implemented formula. The lateral position adds 0.5 × wind × 0.28 × t². The 0.28 factor is a scaling decision of the model. It doesn't come from a measurement of the specific clay's aerodynamic coefficient.
10.13

Slow motion

Changing the wind lets you compare its effect without touching the other variables, but it doesn't offer an exact correction for a real shooting ground. Slow motion helps you observe it without changing the physics of the calculation.

Slow motion reduces the flight's speed so it's easier to observe. Some aids still use real time. It's worth understanding this difference so as not to get used to a pace that doesn't exist on the ground.

The “Slow motion” control runs from 0.15x to 1.00x in steps of 0.05. The starting value is 0.35x. While it's below 1.00, a note appears at the bottom of the scene reminding you that the analysis is slowed down. On reaching 1.00x, the note is hidden and the physical clock advances at the pace of the time elapsed between frames.

The animation loop uses requestAnimationFrame. On each frame it measures the real time, caps it at 0.05 s to avoid large jumps, and multiplies it by the scale before updating the clay and the sight via the keyboard. At 0.35x, one real second contributes 0.35 s to the flight. The trajectory, gravity, wind and meeting point all use that same scaled time, so their internal relationships don't fall out of sync.

The duration of the visual aids uses a different clock. Shots, bursts, fragments and corrections disappear according to real time. The “Analysis” control decides how long they stay visible. Thanks to this separation, you can slow the flight down without turning a 4.8 s correction into a wait of almost 14 seconds. So slow motion and analysis duration are two separate controls.

For teaching purposes, 0.15x or 0.35x helps you distinguish the orange clay, the yellow aid and the blue pattern, and see that the meeting point happens after the shot. It isn't a definitive training speed. Once the sequence is understood, you need to move up progressively to 1.00x; otherwise you can hit the target by moving late thanks to a decision time that doesn't exist on the ground.

A flight of 2 s of simulated time takes about 5.7 s of real time at 0.35x and about 13.3 s at 0.15x. That calculation helps you avoid confusing the observed duration with the one shown on the badge. If “Analysis” is set to 4.8 s, its markers last 4.8 s of the real clock, not 4.8 simulated seconds. This lets the beginner study the meeting point, while the expert can recognise two deliberately different time bases.

Exercise. Watch the same clay at 0.20x, 0.50x and 1.00x. In the first two repeats, say out loud “clay, future point, shot”. In the last one, avoid saying it out loud and check whether you can recognise the relationship quickly.
Performance. requestAnimationFrame usually syncs the drawing with the screen and can be throttled in hidden tabs. The step limit cushions pauses, but a slow device can still change the perceived smoothness even though the update uses elapsed time.
10.14

Robot and extreme parameters

Reducing the playback speed shows more slowly a process that keeps its physical timings; at 1× the model's normal pace returns. Robot mode uses that same physics with either regulated or extreme parameters.

Robot builds its clays from a single machine and several profiles. After trying extreme values, it's important to know how to return to a moderate, understandable setup.

Robot uses a single virtual trap and five profiles. “ABT CPSA 2026” continuously generates outputs within ±30°, 1.5-3.5 m of height at 10 m and 70 ±1 m. “Trap1 FITASC 2025” works within ±22°, 1.7-2.7 m and 50 ±2 m. “Fairground round” cycles through nine rows; “extreme popular” weights the more lateral or higher outputs; and “pseudorandom” draws from seven cases. The first two represent regulated limits; the last three are popular models.

“Robot force” multiplies the nominal range between 70 and 135%. The result is capped at 45-120 m. “Robot height” adds 0.085 m to the height at 10 m for every degree of the control, from −12° to +18°, and then caps the value at 0.8-5.8 m. “Robot lateral” multiplies the row's angle between 50 and 140%, with a final limit of −62° to +62°.

There's no fourth “irregularity” control. Variety depends on the chosen configuration and on the system that orders the sequence. Robot's internal seed starts at 24681357. From there, a mathematical generator calculates the following numbers. A reset doesn't return the seed to its starting value. So resetting clears the round and the scene, but doesn't guarantee that the pseudorandom series starts again in exactly the same way.

Extremes are useful for breaking comfortable references: a clay can come out very lateral and low, or almost vertical and high. They should be read as experimental exercises. To restore a regulated base, select ABT CPSA or Trap1 FITASC and set force, height and lateral to 100%, 0° and 100%, with wind at zero. The band should show “Within limits”; if a control takes the setup out of range, it will show “Out of limits”.

The “irregularity” mentioned in the manifest doesn't correspond to an independent control and shouldn't be invented. In this version, it's observed as diversity between rows and as selection order. “Extreme popular” increases the probability of certain rows, while “fairground round” cycles through them; neither adds continuous noise to a trajectory that has already been launched. Force, height and lateral are applied before resolving the flight, and wind acts afterwards, during the animation.

Difficulty ladder. Start on ABT CPSA with 100/0/100. Then increase only the lateral setting to 120%, return it to 100%, raise the height to +8° and finish with the extreme preset. Watch for when the band switches from “Within limits” to “Out of limits” and note which change makes your usual reference stop working.
Actual reset. Changing preset doesn't reset the three sliders, or the wind, cartridge or camera either. The reset is manual. Regulation status is recalculated from the profile's limits, but it's still a check made by the model, not an external measurement of the shooting ground.
10.15

Repetition

Robot lets you compare recognised profiles with popular setups that don't follow a standard, and it identifies them clearly. The Repeat function keeps exactly the same output so you can try a different decision.

“Repeat target” keeps the output and the station. Some controls can be changed between attempts. “Reset” performs a different kind of clean-up. Here we explain precisely what each action keeps and what it clears.

After a normal call, the application saves a copy of the target: starting position, velocity vector, machine, station, discipline, Robot label, height and range. “Repeat target” clones that copy, restores its station and starts the flight again with the “REPEAT” label. It doesn't increase the round counter or the number of breaks, and it doesn't set up the next station.

The phrase “exactly the same clay” refers to the saved starting geometry. The visible delay is taken from the current value, because startTarget() reassigns it. Wind, slow motion, cartridge, chokes, variation, gain and analysis duration are also read again during the new run. This lets you keep the output while deliberately changing one variable, but it means the repeat stops being identical if those controls are changed.

The pellets don't repeat the same microscopic pattern either: each shot creates a new random seed. The cone and the variation can stay the same, but the specific gaps change. That difference is useful as a reminder that the pattern isn't a solid plate, though it does mean you can't attribute a single result to the sight's geometry without observing several repeats.

“Reset” serves a different purpose. It returns the application to waiting, clears the target, shots, fragments and corrections, sets round and breaks to zero, unlocks the station and takes the sight back to the hold. It keeps the control values, the last saved clay and Robot's seed. So it isn't a universal “factory settings” button.

A reasonable comparison needs more than two results. Run three repeats without changing anything to observe the pattern's natural variation. Then run another three changing a single variable. Note whether the result was a centre hit, an edge hit or a miss, along with the horizontal and vertical correction. If you only compare one hit with one miss, the randomness of the 306 directions can make you believe a variable was the cause. Repeat keeps the trajectory. Running several attempts helps separate that effect from the pattern's natural variation.

Before pressing “Reset”, also record the discipline, scheme and station. Even though the last target is still available, a new round with inherited controls isn't the same as the chapter's starting setup. Resetting the counter and restoring the experiment are different operations.

One-variable method. Call for a clay, repeat it twice without changing anything, then change only the choke on the first shot. If the result changes, compare several patterns before concluding that the choke was the cause.
State. Repeat is blocked during delay or tracking, and requires lastTarget to exist. It can be triggered after a fall, break or leaving the window, provided the previous scene has reached an allowed state.
10.16

Reading results

A repeat doesn't count as an official clay, nor does it move the shooter to a different station, because it keeps the same geometry for the trial. Afterwards, the results panels show corrections that are easy to read.

Knowing whether the clay broke isn't enough to improve. The panel lets you see whether the shot was high, low, ahead or behind, and turn that result into a correction for the next attempt.

The “Result” indicator gives the immediate summary. A break reports whether the pattern's centre landed in the centre or edge zone, which of the 306 pellets caused the contact, and with how much energy. A contact below 0.55 J is labelled “light touch”. A miss shows shot 1/2; once both are used up, it warns that the clay will keep falling.

The scene keeps more information. The orange disc shows the clay's position at the key moment. The red sight marks where the shot was fired. The light blue point represents the shot's physical centre, and the green marks the impact that breaks it. The blue and yellow outlines compare the real pattern with the ideal one. The numbers 1 and 2 let you tell the attempts apart when both remain visible.

The bottom panel shows each shot separately. It places the pellets on a plane perpendicular to the shot's direction, as if you were looking at the cloud head-on. The horizontal axis shows the error along the clay's movement. The vertical axis shows the height error. If the centre is behind, the instruction is “lead more”. If it's ahead, it says “lead less”. If it's above, “aim lower” appears, and if it's below, “aim higher”. When the error is close to the limit accepted by the program, it shows “lead OK” or “height OK”.

“Right” on screen shouldn't be confused with “ahead”. On a clay travelling left, forward and behind occupy visual sides opposite to a clay travelling right. The correction uses axes relative to the target's velocity. It's also worth distinguishing the pattern's centre from the pellet that breaks it: an edge break can coexist with a poorly placed centre.

Example: if the panel places the centre 0.18 m behind and 0.03 m high, the horizontal deviation exceeds 0.12 m and the vertical one stays within 0.08 m. The useful instruction is to lead more, not to aim lower and lead more at the same time. If an edge pellet still breaks it, the result is valid for that simulation, but the diagnosis still reveals a centre that was lagging behind. Separating the outcome from the quality of placement stops you learning from a lucky break.

A screenshot would only demonstrate this reading if it captured, all at once, the numbered shot, the markers and the correction thumbnail with content. A scene that has already finished, or an empty panel, doesn't prove where the shot ended up. In the absence of a current screenshot meeting those conditions, this unit relies on the behaviour confirmed by app.html and on the teaching plate, which explains the system without pretending to record an actual run.

Reading routine. After every shot, answer in this order: high or low? ahead or behind? centre or edge? would you keep the same movement? Choose only one correction for the repeat.
Thresholds. The text treats a lead error of less than 0.12 m, and a vertical error of less than 0.08 m, on the calculated plane as correct. These are interface thresholds of the model, not official sporting tolerances.
10.17

Guided scenarios

The correction shows the lead and height error relative to the pattern's centre, separately for the first and second shot. The guided scenarios use this information to practise a single variable.

The controls can be organised into eight exercises that gradually increase in difficulty. Repeating lets you compare results in a fairly stable way.

The application doesn't contain a wizard that loads “Scenario 1” with a button. The guided scenarios are built using disciplines, schemes, repeats and controls. The recipe should be understood as an editorial protocol compatible with the reviewed version, not as a hidden automatic feature.

For a first almost head-on clay, use Universal, station 3, slow motion 0.25x, and call for targets until you get an angle close to zero; repeat that clay. For a medium crossing shot, keep the scheme and look for around 20-30°. For a strong crossing shot, use a row close to 40-45°. In all three cases, keep the wind at zero and the cartridge at 400 m/s. Watch first with the yellow aid, then repeat without it.

For a rising shot, select an output with a height at 10 m close to 3.0-3.5 m and compare “Flat” with “High”. For cartridges, repeat a crosser and alternate between 360 and 430 m/s without touching the choke. For chokes, use the same clay and cartridge, and alternate between Full and open on the first shot; assess several clouds, because the pattern changes between shots.

The extreme Robot scenario starts at “ABT CPSA 2026” with 100/0/100 and moves on to the extreme popular preset before increasing the controls. The wind one uses a repeat with 0, −4 and +4 m/s. There's no direct control for the clay's speed, so beginners shouldn't be asked to “set 90 km/h”; instead, you observe the resulting model speed shown on the badge.

In every exercise the sequence is the same: set a question, call for or find an output, repeat, change one variable, read the panel, and restore. “Reset” restarts the round, but doesn't restore the sliders. That's why it's worth noting down the base setup: station 3, neutral hold, zoom 1.00x, slow motion 0.35x, delay 0.03 s, cartridge 400 m/s, wind 0 and gain 0.

Each scenario needs a criterion for knowing when it's been understood. In the head-on shot, you need to recognise the height without chasing pixels. In crossers, you need to tell lagging behind apart from leading ahead. In the rising shot, you need to explain the separation between the sight and the physical centre. In the cartridge exercise, speed and time are related. In the choke one, coverage and density are distinguished. In Robot, you need to know how to restore the base setup, and in wind, how to recognise the sign of the deviation. Completing a recipe without being able to explain that relationship doesn't mean you've mastered it.

Session decision. Choose only two scenarios at a time. Five minutes of frontal shots and ten of medium crossers teach you more than working through eight recipes without comparing results.
Option to repeat the test. The Universal and Olympic schemes pick rows with Math.random(). That's why you can't be sure which release will appear at the start. The repeatable part of the exercise begins once the target has been saved with “Repeat target”.
10.18

Built-in help

A guided scenario should pose a question, set the controls and compare results, not chase a score by chance. The built-in help explains each element briefly, but it doesn't replace this manual.

The application offers brief explanations of the controls and a more complete help panel. It also includes links to the manual, the technical documentation and other resources.

Every main button, indicator, selector and slider has a title attribute. When you hold the pointer over it, the browser shows a brief explanation: what the control does, what range it represents, or how the data should be read. It's contextual help, useful for recalling a function, but it doesn't replace the chapter, because it doesn't show relationships between several controls.

The “Quick help” link opens an overlay panel within the same page, using the #ayuda fragment. Its blocks explain disciplines, speed and trajectory, cartridge and pattern, lead, on-screen elements, and a basic sequence of use. It's closed with “Close”, which returns to the empty fragment. The panel doesn't explicitly pause the animation; it's best to open it when the simulator is waiting.

“Manual” opens index.html in a new tab. “Technical documentation” opens the same file at the #ingenieria anchor. The project page and the MIT licence panel are also linked. Links that open in another tab use target="_blank" and rel="noopener"; “Quick help” and “MIT Licence” stay in the current tab.

The public page works on desktop, and its CSS reorganises controls on narrow screens. The help can be read on mobile because the panel allows scrolling, but the application doesn't implement touch gestures for aiming and firing with the same ergonomics. The documentation should say “accessible for reference” rather than promise equivalent mobile training.

The pop-up texts use the browser's title attribute. They usually appear when the mouse stays still. They aren't a permanent aid and may show up differently with the keyboard, a screen reader or a touchscreen. When a doubt affects a result, you should prefer the visible panel and this manual. The habit of “trying until it seems to work” doesn't replace the description confirmed in the code.

In a local copy, “Manual” and “Technical documentation” resolve paths relative to the same directory. On GitHub Pages they point to the project's published site. Opening a new tab keeps the simulator's state in the original tab, but doesn't sync changes between the two. Quick help, on the other hand, is a layer of app.html itself: it doesn't download another application or create a separate session.

Practical use. If you forget what a colour means, open “Quick help” and check “On-screen elements”. To understand why it changes, open the Manual. To review the formulas, go to “Technical documentation”.
Maintenance. The links to the Manual and Documentation point to index.html. If the book's structure changes, those paths and anchors need to be checked along with the screenshots and the help text.
10.19

Screenshots explained

Brief help lets you carry on with the exercise and links to a fuller explanation when needed. The annotated screenshots show a complete session without depending on the live animation.

A real screenshot can demonstrate how a feature looks in a specific version. To avoid being misleading, it must state what it shows, when it was taken, and what its limitations are.

A useful screenshot doesn't try to explain everything at once. It must state which version it shows and number the area to be highlighted. It must also name the parameter, explain what it changes, point out what should be observed, propose an exercise, and state a limitation. If this information is missing, the image only serves as a visual reminder. If the screen changes, it can even become an incorrect instruction.

In the general screenshot, callout 1 corresponds to actions; 2, to indicators; 3, to on-scene aids; 4, to the correction panel; 5, to the legend; and 6, to parameters. The flow reads from top to bottom and loops back to the diagnosis: set up, call, track, shoot, analyse and repeat. The order stops you from moving controls without knowing what question you're investigating.

The flight screenshot proves that the orange clay, the dashed line, the yellow zone and the blue cloud can coexist. The Olympic one shows fifteen machines and five marker clays. The extreme Robot one contributes the “Non-regulatory” label. An image whose scene has already finished and whose panel is empty is not included as proof of the first shot: that state proves neither the shot nor its correction. Every image kept backs up a claim that can actually be seen.

Screenshots alone don't prove the physics. A circle can be well drawn and badly calculated. That's why they're checked against app.html and verify-schemes.js. Older images whose panel layout no longer matches the reviewed code shouldn't be used either. This chapter prioritises the PNGs in docs/screenshots that correspond to the current interface.

Active reading. Cover the bottom strip of a screenshot with your hand and try to explain the state using only the scene. Then uncover the controls and check which inferences were correct.
Documentation check. After every update, it's worth comparing the size, the control names, the visible states and the legend against the HTML structure, called the DOM, and against the functions that draw the scene. A screenshot shows what a version looked like on a given date. It isn't a permanent specification.

What we have learned

The sequence is set up, call, track, shoot, read and repeat. Discipline, station, wind and Robot change the geometry; cartridge, chokes and gain change the shot; slow motion and aids change the observation.

Work on a single variable, keep the output with “Repeat target”, and take a specific question to the ground for the instructor.

Chapter 11

Universal, Olympic, Robot and other disciplines

Changing discipline changes the problem. The output, the trajectories, the permitted load, the pace and the background all vary. This chapter separates the rulebook, the machine's capability, the simulator's representation and the on-ground experience.

The regulatory references come from ISSF 2026 and the FITASC rules in force that were consulted. A simulator setup may respect their limits, but it doesn't homologate a shooting ground. In competition, the authorised officials decide.

11.1

Why disciplines don't all feel the same

We're already familiar with the screen, the controls and how to read results, without confusing the guide, the pattern and the impact. Now we can compare Universal, Olympic and Robot to understand their differences.

A technique that works in Universal can give worse results in Olympic or with an oscillating machine. The reason is that the output, the trajectories, the pace and the way you see the clay all change.

The first difference lies underground. Universal Trench uses five machines in a line and an output reference above the central machine. Olympic Trap employs fifteen machines, split into five groups of three, one facing each station. Trap1, a FITASC discipline useful for understanding part of what clubs call “robot”, uses a single machine that continuously changes direction and elevation within defined limits. The shooter doesn't see that mechanism, but does notice where the clay appears and how long it takes their eyes to pick it up.

The second difference is regulatory. In Universal, the ten official schemes combine angles, heights measured at 10 metres and ranges between 60 and 75 m. In Olympic, the ten ISSF tables coordinate fifteen trajectories and fix 76 m with a tolerance of 1 metre. The ammunition shouldn't be confused either: FITASC allows up to 28 g of shot in Universal, while ISSF limits its events to 24.0 g with a 0.5 g tolerance. These are regulatory maximums, not automatic purchase recommendations or descriptions of every commercial cartridge.

The overall feel depends on several factors, not a single figure. A faster output means finding the clay sooner. A wider apparent angle demands more lateral movement. A high clay changes the shotgun's plane. A dark background or light coming from the front can delay detection. Pace matters too. Both rulebooks move the group from left to right and allow two shots in the ordinary round. However, in ISSF Olympic Trap finals only one is allowed. That's why simply saying “Olympic is faster” leaves out a good part of the problem.

In Plato, the difference is reproduced through tables and geometry, but it isn't certified. Universal picks one of five rows from the scheme and launches the target from the central marker clay; Olympic picks one of the three machines in the group corresponding to the station. In Robot, ABT and Trap1 generate continuous positions within their limits, while the popular profiles cycle through or draw from their own rows. The program resolves a visual parabola from height and range. That's why it's useful for comparing decisions, even though it doesn't measure a real trap or reproduce the disc's full aerodynamics.

Ground case. Before blaming the cartridge for a poor round in another discipline, describe three observable changes: the appearance point, the time to the first shot and the extent of the movement. Then do five repeats in Plato with the same station and the visual aids on.
Expert reading. It's worth distinguishing the clay's linear speed, measured along its path, from its angular speed, which shows how fast its direction changes in front of your eyes. A fast target moving almost straight away from you can cross less of the screen than a slower one moving sideways. Difficulty doesn't depend on km/h alone.
11.2

Universal Trench

The same physics produces very different experiences when the launch point, the uncertainty, the pace and the rules change. We'll start the comparison with the five machines and schemes of Universal Trench.

In Universal Trench, the placement of the machines and stations, the trajectories and their tolerances are all defined. A beginner should first learn where the clay appears and how it crosses the centre line.

Regulatory parameters. The 2025 FITASC rulebook defines five machines on firm, flat bases at the same level. Between clays loaded on adjacent machines there must be 1 to 1.25 m, with 1.10 m recommended. The five 1 m square stations sit 15 m from the trap house's front edge and 2.5 m apart between centres. A clay placed on the cover, above machine 3, marks the zero-degree output. This is shooting-ground geometry, not an approximate setting each shooter can decide for themselves.

FITASC sets out ten schemes. Each one assigns an angle, a height at 10 m and a flight distance to the five machines. The trajectories must stay within a 90-degree sector with its vertex at machine 3; machines 1 and 2 throw to the right and 4 and 5 to the left, crossing the centre line. The current tables show heights from 1.5 to 3.5 m and distances of 60, 65, 70 or 75 m. The general range tolerance is ±5 m, and the height-check tolerance is ±0.50 m.

A round has 25 clays. The sono-pull system, which detects the shooter's voice, and its distributor must guarantee that each person receives the five trajectories from each station, in random order. A squad can have up to six people. Five occupy the stations and the sixth waits to replace whoever is at station 1. After station 5, it goes back to station 1, with the gun open and unloaded. The shooter has 10 seconds to call for the clay after the previous one. The output must be immediate, and only the system's reaction time is allowed, which the rulebook estimates at roughly a tenth of a second.

Two shots can be taken at the same clay. The load must not exceed 28 g of lead shot, with the tolerance set by FITASC, and the maximum nominal diameter is 2.5 mm. This doesn't mean “Universal is 28 g” in every training session: a lighter load can be used if it's legal, compatible with the gun and allowed by the shooting ground. For the beginner, the useful task is to recognise a central output, notice whether it opens out or crosses the centre line, and keep the movement going through the second attempt.

Full shot point notebook in PDF. The image above is one of the 25 pages in the round. You can download the full notebook for the FITASC Universal 1 scheme and print it to work on your composition before heading to the ground: a cover page with the table of all 25 clays and a top-down view, followed by one page per clay. If you'd prefer different settings, the simulator's “Generate notebook” button produces the same document with your own.PDF · A4 landscape · 28 pages · generated with a 400 m/s cartridge, flat rib, no wind and neutral hold. Opens with a cover page explaining the document and closes with the usage notice and licence. Supporting material, not an official document.
Exercise. From station 3, call for a short round and name each flight before you shoot: right, centre or left; low, medium or high. Don't try to memorise the metres. When you finish, compare your classification with the scheme confirmed by the person in charge of the ground.
Limit of Plato. Its ten FU_SCHEMES tables reproduce the central values of the FITASC schemes and use five machines, but the trajectory is calculated with reduced gravity and a shared visual release. It's a teaching representation, not a calibration record.
11.3

Olympic Trap

Universal combines several measured trajectories that the shooter learns to recognise from five stations. Olympic Trap uses more machines and adjusts the difficulty through its ISSF tables.

Olympic Trap uses fifteen machines and a very precise distribution of outputs. This arrangement makes the event highly comparable and, at the same time, usually demands faster detection than Universal.

Regulatory parameters. The fifteen machines and their tables increase the uncertainty about the exact direction of the output and demand faster visual acquisition. The ISSF 2026 rulebook sets them at the front of the trap house and divides them into five groups of three. The machines within a group are 1 to 1.10 m apart; the centres of adjacent groups are normally 3 to 3.30 m apart. Each square station sits 15 m from the front edge, aligned with the central machine of its group. The output mark is a triangle painted on the cover, not the marker clay used in Universal.

The device distributes a round of 25 evenly: ten clays to the right, ten to the left and five to the centre. From each station, the shooter receives two from the left-hand machine, two from the right-hand one and one from the central one, in a variable random order. The ten official tables assign an angle and a height to each of the fifteen machines. They all share a regulatory distance of 76 m with a tolerance of one metre. The demand comes from that precise combination, from the fast acquisition and from the pace, not from some supposed lack of randomness.

In the ordinary round, two cartridges may be fired per clay. In finals and finals shoot-offs, only one. ISSF ammunition may not exceed 24.0 g, with a 0.5 g tolerance in the inspection procedure, and the shot must not exceed 2.6 mm. The difference against Universal's 28 g maximum is four grams, or 16.7% over 24 g. If the shot's material and diameter were the same, 28 g would contain roughly 16.7% more pellets; this doesn't by itself imply a higher velocity or a better pattern.

In 2026, ISSF published changes to the finals format and reintroduced shoot-offs to resolve qualifying ties. These details matter to the competitor, but they don't change the fifteen-machine layout or the beginner's basic task. On the ground, a clay is perceived that appears in front of the active station and quickly reaches its angular problem. In Plato, each station exclusively selects its group of three and shows an aligned marker clay. The table is official; the parabola, the marker clay and the visual representation are the model.

Full shot point notebook in PDF. The image above is one of the 15 pages in this scheme. You can download the full notebook for the ISSF Olympic 1 table, with one page for every machine and station: five stations for each of the three machines in its group. For a different ISSF table, choose it in the simulator and press “Generate notebook”.PDF · A4 landscape · 18 pages · same settings as the Universal notebook, so you can compare the two disciplines. Opens with a cover page explaining the document and closes with the usage notice and licence. Supporting material, not an official document.
Comparison. Keep station 3, wind 0 and a 400 m/s cartridge in Plato. Call for five Universal and five Olympic. Only note how long it takes you to pick up the clay and where it appears relative to your hold; leave the scoring for a second pass.
Expert reading. The ten ISSF_SCHEMES tables were copied row by row from the second 2026 ISSF printing. The checker compares them against an independent reference table. The data in the table is official. The parabola, the way it's shown on screen and the user experience remain a model. They don't amount to a certification of the ground.
11.4

Robot

Olympic isn't simply a faster Universal, because the machines, the schemes, the timings and the competition all change. Robot shows even more clearly that the same technology can be used across different disciplines.

A Robot machine can be set up for a regulated discipline or for a “local shoot”. The machine's name alone isn't enough to tell which. You need to know its settings and the rulebook being applied.

“Robot” describes a family of machines, not a universal discipline with a single rulebook. A commercial unit can swing laterally, vary elevation, change spring tension, and release clays randomly or following a sequence. Laporte offers ABT/Trap One machines with horizontal and vertical movement; Promatic sells wobble systems with oscillation motors and random interruption. Those spec sheets demonstrate mechanical capability. They don't prove that any setting made with them is FITASC, ISSF or RFEDETO.

Closest regulated reference. FITASC's Trap1 uses a single multidirectional machine. The machine changes continuously, randomly and unpredictably within its limits. The stations are at 15 m and 2.5 m apart. The 2025 rulebook sets a flight of 50 ±2 m. The height at 10 m runs from 1.70 to 2.70 m if the machine is buried, or 2 to 3 m if it sits level with the station. It also recommends extremes of 22 degrees to each side, with a tolerance of five. It allows two shots and up to 28 g.

ABT, DTL, Trap1 and a fairground “robot” don't mean the same thing. A manufacturer can sell the same mechanical base for several disciplines. However, the rulebook depends on the setting, the shooting ground, the distribution system, the officiating and the programme. Even the word random can refer to different things. It might be continuous mechanical movement, a calculated choice between several rows, or a prepared sequence the shooter doesn't know. To understand the round, you need to ask which parameter changes and what its limits are. The machine's name isn't enough.

Plato contains five Robot setups. robot_abt represents ABT CPSA 2026 with 70 ±1 m, a ±30° sector and heights of 1.5 to 3.5 m. robot_trap1 represents Trap1 FITASC 2025 with 50 ±2 m. It uses a recommended sector of ±22° and heights of 1.7 to 2.7 m for the installation represented. Both generate positions continuously within their limits. “Fairground round”, “extreme popular” and “pseudorandom” are non-regulatory models. The three controls let you test changes. The band switches to “Out of limits” when a regulated profile stops meeting its framework.

Full shot point notebook in PDF. The image above is one of the 25 pages generated. You can download the full notebook for the robot_abt preset, which runs through 25 consecutive releases of the sequence within the limits of ABT CPSA 2026. Robot's three controls change the result, so it's worth generating your own after adjusting force, height and lateral spread.PDF · A4 landscape · 28 pages · fixed-seed sequence, so the notebook is reproducible even though the real machine doesn't repeat its positions. Opens with a cover page explaining the document and closes with the usage notice and licence. Supporting material, not an official document.
Useful question. Before a Robot round, ask whoever's in charge for four things: the announced discipline, the lateral sector, the height or tilt, and the expected distance or tension. If the answer is “whatever the organiser fancies”, treat it as a local shoot, not regulation training. During the first round, note whether the machine oscillates while you wait or whether the controller picks a fixed row; that observation tells apart two sources of uncertainty that often get called the same thing.
Essential separation. ABT and Trap1 no longer share an ambiguous label. Each preset states its own standard, distance, sector, height and tolerance. A machine's commercial capacity may cover both, but the active preset and its configuration determine which reference is being studied.
11.5

Local rounds, fairs and “trillos”

An oscillating machine can follow a recognised rulebook or have an extreme local setup; calling it Robot doesn't clarify which of the two it's using. Popular shoots and “local shoots” explain the origin of many of those settings.

In this manual, “local shoot” is a colloquial way of naming certain popular shoots. It isn't an official discipline and shouldn't be confused with a homologated shooting ground.

Personal comment. I use “trillos” as a colloquial term for certain village or fair shoots with a robotic machine and participants from different backgrounds. It doesn't appear as a discipline in the international regulations I consulted, and its use can vary by region. Here it names a specific local experience, not a legal or federation category. A popular shoot can be perfectly authorised, organised and refereed; what shouldn't be assumed is that its trajectories belong to Universal Trench, Olympic Trap or Trap1 just because clays are being shot at.

A temporary set-up can introduce changes that a regulated shooting ground controls with measurements and tolerances. If the base isn't level, an equal movement to both sides can produce different heights on the right and left. If the machine sits on a mound, the relationship between the station, the output and the horizon changes. Spring tension affects the range. The vertical tilt changes the height, and the oscillation disc limits the lateral spread. Manufacturers allow these adjustments to be made. This explains the wide variety of clays, but it doesn't authorise using any combination without first checking safety and local regulations.

The perceptual context also changes. A background of trees, pale ground, low sun or a nearby safety tape can alter the moment when the clay is spotted. The order might be circular and end up being learned; it might be random; or the organiser might deliberately choose eye-catching outputs.

Personal comment. I've seen shooters capable of breaking 23 or 24 clays on their usual ground get thrown off by very high, very low or extremely wide releases. This is a situated observation, not a statistic representative of every shooter or every fair.

The answer isn't to turn irregularity into heroics. Before taking part, you need to know who's running the round, what rules apply, where the limits lie, and what loading, rotation and incident instructions govern it. The admissible gun and ammunition may differ from an ISSF or FITASC competition. The beginner shouldn't assume that a shotgun allowed at a popular shoot is admitted in another discipline. The authority of the person in charge of the ground, and real safety, take precedence over any custom remembered from somewhere else.

Arrival list. Ask about the rules, who's in charge, shooting order, number of cartridges, waiting area and the procedure for a malfunction. Watch a full round before taking part, and don't copy someone else's handling if it contradicts the instructions.
Source of information. The need to level the machine and its adjustment mechanisms are covered in regulations and manufacturer manuals. The frequency of irregular set-ups and how shooters react to them, on the other hand, come from personal comments. Both kinds of information are useful if kept clearly apart.
11.6

Extreme trajectories

Popular shoots combine sociability and skill, and some setups favour those who know the machine; even so, they must be safe and explain their conditions. Their extreme trajectories can be studied with geometry, without treating them as something mysterious.

A trajectory is only extreme relative to a specific reference. It can be trained for, but an intentional difficulty shouldn't be confused with a badly adjusted machine.

A trajectory isn't extreme on its own. It has to be compared with a discipline, a shooting ground or the shooter's experience. In Universal, the rulebook limits the sector to 90 degrees and its tables set a height at 10 m of between 1.5 and 3.5 m. In Trap1, the limits are considerably narrower and the range is 50 ±2 m. In Olympic, the tables reach angles of 45 degrees and set 76 ±1 m. You can't call “regulatory” any flight that simply falls between the lowest and the highest across all these disciplines. Each one uses a different system.

There are five main difficulties. A very high clay forces you to raise the line without hiding it under the barrels. A very low one leaves little time before it nears the ground. A very fast one shrinks the decision window. An irregular sequence stops you anticipating the output. On extreme crossers, transverse speed dominates: the clay covers more of the visual field, usually demands more lead, and can leave the comfortable swing zone sooner. Two moderate conditions, such as more speed and more lateral movement, can turn out harder than a spectacular but familiar output.

Commercial machines show why these flights are technically possible. A Laporte Gravity declares tilts of up to 70 degrees and distance adjustment between 5 and 120 m; a Promatic Super Sporter offers elevation of up to 70 degrees and long-range versions. These are manufacturer specifications for versatile equipment, not values automatically admitted in a trench competition. The organiser has to fit the machine's capability within the rulebook and the safe zone of their shooting ground.

In Plato, “Robot extreme popular” includes outputs of up to ±55 degrees, heights of 5.2 m at ten metres, and ranges of 96 m. The controls can push the model to 120 m and ±62 degrees. Everything is shown as non-regulatory. To learn, start from a reference and change a single variable; starting at the maximum mixes too many effects together.

Progression. Repeat a Robot clay with 100% force, 0 degrees of height and 100% lateral. Then use 120% lateral, go back to 100 and add +6 degrees. Only combine both at the end. Describe what changed in how you were looking before you judge the result.
Valid comparison. Height at 10 m, horizontal angle and range are geometric conditions; the simulator's initial speed is derived from them. On a real machine, they can be adjusted through related mechanisms, so they aren't always independent variables. Changing a spring can alter both flight time and the landing point at once.
11.7

Gentle grounds

A very high, low or lateral clay leaves less time and takes the meeting point into unfamiliar zones. Gentler shooting grounds let you practise the technique before adding that difficulty.

Saying a shooting ground is “gentle” means its clays come out more comfortable under certain conditions. A high score there is valid, but it doesn't guarantee the same result on another ground.

“Gentle” is a judgement used during training. It isn't an ISSF or FITASC category. It can refer to clays with a shorter range, more central angles, medium heights, an output that's easy to see, or sequences that repeat. It can also describe a regulatory shooting ground whose scheme feels comfortable to a particular shooter. Without measuring, we don't know which of these causes explains the feeling. That's why saying “they were slow today” isn't enough as a technical statement. Perception depends on the machine, the wind, the background, the light, the station and the moment of the shot.

A forgiving setup has real value. It lets a beginner practise safety, gun mount, visual acquisition and continuity of movement without devoting all their attention to rescuing the clay. Trap1, for example, offers a regulated single-machine framework with a shorter range than Olympic Trap; that doesn't make it a flawed version, but a different sporting problem. Outside competition, the instructor can repeat a specific output or reduce difficulty, always within safe operation and the ground's rules.

The risk appears when the score is interpreted as universal ability. If the background makes the orange easier to spot sooner, reaction time improves without coordination having changed. If the angles are narrow, the lateral lead is reduced. If the same pattern repeats, the brain can anticipate it. A 23/25 under those conditions is still a valid result for that round, but it doesn't promise 23/25 on another scheme, in different light, or in a competition where the machines have been checked and sealed according to the rulebook.

The fix isn't to make every session harder. It's best to alternate blocks. First an action is consolidated with low variability; then one dimension is expanded, such as height or laterality; finally a regulated or unfamiliar round is tried. In Plato, “Repeat target” creates the stable block, and changing the scheme introduces variability. Robot's percentages are model controls: 80% force doesn't mean “80% regulatory”, nor does it correspond to a universal manufacturer setting.

Log. Alongside the score, write down the discipline, the scheme if known, the shooting ground, the light, the wind and the technical goal. After three sessions, look for the result that holds steady when the background changes; that stability tells you more than your best isolated round. For example, compare a 23 against a clean background with two rounds of 20 against different backgrounds: if in all three you acquired the clay early and kept swinging through, the process may be transferring even though the score drops.
Diagnosis. To claim that a shooting ground is out of adjustment, you need measurements of angle, height and distance taken with the applicable procedure. The shooter's perception can prompt a query to the person in charge, but it doesn't replace calibration.
11.8

Demanding grounds

A gentle shooting ground doesn't hand you the hits, but it offers clearer cues for steadying your eyes and your movement. Difficult grounds add changes that only help once a good foundation already exists.

A shooting ground can be difficult and still fully comply with the rulebook. Telling it apart from a badly adjusted facility or an off day for the shooter takes observation and measurement.

A demanding shooting ground can fully comply with the rulebook. A scheme with several high, lateral or hard-to-spot clays is still valid if every machine meets its table and its tolerances. In Olympic, the balanced distribution stops a shooter receiving only central, comfortable clays. In Universal, the five trajectories from each station appear in random order. Standardisation doesn't remove the difficulty. It ensures the challenge is defined, can be checked, and is comparable within the competition.

There are also causes unrelated to the table. A crosswind alters the flight; the rulebook provides for checks and decisions by the jury, but it doesn't turn the air into a static laboratory. The background can hide the output, the light can change during the round, and the pace of a fast squad reduces mental recovery time. A tired shooter tends to spot late, lift their head, or stop their hand. A drop in score alone doesn't prove the machine is “out of adjustment”.

A technical suspicion must be raised with data. In Universal, range and height are checked, the settings are locked, and the round is stopped if a machine has moved. In ISSF, the fifteen machines on a ground must be the same make and model, and the tables fix direction, height and 76 ±1 m. Only authorised staff should intervene. A competitor can report an observed irregularity to the referee, but must not enter the trap house, touch a machine, or ask for a trajectory to be softened to their liking.

Hard training works best when a simple task is kept. Rather than trying to recover the usual score straight away, you can pick a single goal: “see the clay before you move”, “don't stop the swing” or “open the gun without turning round”. Plato helps separate the visual reading from other problems. First the result is hidden, slow motion is used, and it's repeated. Then the full scheme is introduced. An extreme setup isn't the same as a championship ground. Competition requires the standard to be met. The simulator's extreme mode is designed to break your usual references.

Personal protocol. After five misses, don't change your cartridge, choke and hold all at once. Check your safety, breathing and acquisition point; ask your coach for a single observation. If several shooters notice an unusual release, report it to the referee. Record the station and the type of release: five scattered misses suggest a different problem from five misses concentrated on the same flight.
Criterion. “Close to competition” means measured against the specific rulebook, not simply fast. A clay outside tolerance can be harder and, precisely because of that, stop being a good specific stimulus. It's worth noting the time, wind and station too, before comparing two rounds.
11.9

Training on the competition ground

The difficulty depends on speed, angle, visibility and sequence, not on a single machine setting. Training on the competition ground reduces some of the uncertainty of the environment.

Visiting the venue lets you get to know the background, the light, the sound and the organisation. However, a prior session doesn't guarantee that the wind, the scheme or the conditions will be the same on competition day.

Training at the venue helps because your eyes get used to a specific place. It lets you discover where the clay appears against the background and what landmarks lie behind the trap house. It also helps you learn how the light comes in, how audible the sono-pull is, and what pace the referees and squads keep. You can also rehearse the practical organisation: access, paperwork, the waiting area, moving between grounds, and warm-up time. None of these things appears in a table of angles, but all of them can occupy part of your attention on competition day.

Official practice has limits. FITASC states that, for its Universal championship, the facility opens on the three days before, and the technical committee checks the settings the day before; during the competition there's no practice on the grounds being used. The training schemes may be the same as those used in the event, but they don't have to be. ISSF regulates pre-event training within the championship programme and equipment inspection. So “having shot there before” doesn't mean knowing every output in advance.

The visit should produce small decisions. It's worth choosing a hold point for each station, identifying the area where the orange is picked up best, and checking whether your glasses give enough contrast. It isn't the time to rebuild the stock or try out three new cartridges. If the light changes between morning and afternoon, note it down; if the wind alters the trajectories, avoid memorising a rigid visual distance. The useful reference is a reading process, not an exact mental photograph.

You always need to check the competition programme. There's no guarantee that the venue has already set the final scheme, or that the organisers allow free practice. Plato can help you prepare for switching between disciplines and stations. However, it doesn't reproduce each club's backgrounds, the real sound, the sun, or a wind with turbulence. What can be carried over to the ground is the general way of reading the visual problem.

Personal comment. I recommend attending the week of the competition when possible and allowed, because some regular practice grounds can feel gentler. It's practical experience, not a guarantee about how the venue is set up.
Venue log. Divide a sheet into five stations. For each one, note the acquisition background, the starting hold and a single cue. Close the session with that sheet; don't try to keep twenty different verbal corrections in your head. A useful example would be “station 4: acquire against the light hillside, hold below its edge, follow through”; it's an observable instruction that can be checked the next day without trying to guess the flight path.
Training design. Knowing the environment can improve the result even though the ballistics stay the same. To know whether the improvement comes from the scheme or the location, you'd need to study both factors separately. A single score doesn't let you identify the cause. It's worth always recording the time slot observed.
11.10

Cartridge change

Knowing the background, the light, the stations and the machines helps, but it doesn't replace the ability to read each output. Changing cartridge also alters the timing, the energy and the feel of the shot on the same ground.

Between 24 and 28 g, the total mass of pellets changes, and their number can change too. Velocity is a separate characteristic. That's why it's not a good idea to explain all the differences using velocity alone.

Load affects the possible pellet count, pattern density and felt recoil. Velocity changes flight time. None of these characteristics describes a complete cartridge on its own. Rule before habit. ISSF limits load to 24.0 g plus 0.5 g of tolerance in its competitions. FITASC allows up to 28 g in Universal, plus the stated tolerance. These figures are maximums. They don't require using exactly 28 g in Universal, and they don't allow using 28 g in ISSF Olympic Trap either. The event programme, the ground's rules, compatibility with the gun and the applicable regulations determine which cartridge can be used.

Four grams may not seem like much, but 28 g contains 16.7% more mass than 24 g. If the pellets and material are the same, the count increases by roughly that same proportion. If the diameter changes, the sums change a great deal, because the mass of a sphere grows with the cube of its diameter. More pellets can raise the pattern's potential density, but the actual distribution also depends on choke, wad, deformation, barrel, distance and batch. The only real way to check is to pattern safely and under control, wherever you're authorised to do so.

Load alone doesn't determine velocity either. The Fiocchi 2024 catalogue, for example, states that within the same Official Rossa family, 24 g gives 415 m/s and 28 g gives 400 m/s, measured at the muzzle with a 700 mm barrel and full choke. That's one manufacturer's example, not a universal law. A brand can formulate different products. Figures shouldn't be compared either without reading the test method: temperature, barrel length and measurement point all affect the published number.

Calculated recoil depends, among other factors, on the mass and velocity of the load, on the gases, and on the weight of the gun. The recoil the shooter actually feels also depends on the stock, stance, clothing and anticipation. Changing cartridge just before a competition brings a different response, even if the product looks better on paper. A sensible way to adapt is to start with a few rounds, keep the same choke, and log the first and second shots. Plato only has a reference velocity control between 360 and 430 m/s. It doesn't represent grams, brand, wad, pressure or the difference in recoil between 24 and 28 g.

Ordered test. With an instructor, at an authorised shooting ground, compare two legal cartridges while keeping the gun, choke and flight path the same. Record acquisition, recovery for the second shot, comfort and pattern; don't decide based on a single spectacular break. Repeat the order on another day, or alternate which one is tested first, because fatigue can make the cartridge used last look worse.
Expert reading. Treating grams as velocity in the simulator would be incorrect. The load affects total mass and pellet count; velocity is a separate specification. A future comparison should model both and document the manufacturer's conditions.
11.11

How to represent the disciplines in the simulator

The cartridge should be chosen for its compatibility, the rules, and its proven performance, not as an attempt to correct a late read. The simulator represents each discipline with clear parameters and always keeps its provenance visible.

An official configuration must keep the data that gives it its meaning. Users can create modified exercises, but the screen must make it clear when the original reference is no longer in use.

A ready-made configuration, or preset, is more than a list of numbers. It must state the discipline, the source, the edition, the units, the tolerances and any simplifications. In a normative reference, the number of machines, the stations, the trajectory tables, the distribution and the ammunition form a set that must be protected. If a user widens an angle or changes the range, the result can still be a good exercise. However, it must appear as “modified” or “pedagogical”. Keeping the official label after the change would give a confidence the model can't justify.

Plato separates three disciplines in the interface. Universal offers ten tables of five rows; Olympic, ten tables of fifteen; Robot, two regulated profiles and three popular single-machine models. Station, wind, slow motion, cartridge and aids all stay adjustable. Only Robot exposes force, height and laterality. There's no general reset button: switching discipline repopulates the schemes, but doesn't necessarily return wind, cartridge, chokes, gain or Robot controls to their starting values. “Reset” restarts the round, not the configuration.

The rows for Universal and Olympic are checked against independent regulatory reference tables. ABT CPSA and Trap1 FITASC keep their own limits. The other three Robot configurations appear as “Non-normative model”. When force, height or laterality is modified, the regulated profile is recalculated. The screen then shows “Within limits” or “Outside limits”. This distinguishes the reference, the modified exercise and popular practice, without claiming that the application has measured a real facility.

A future version could make checking even easier. It would keep the original reference locked and create a copy the moment the first change is made. The visible card might show “ISSF 2026, table 1, unmodified” or “pedagogical derivative: lateral 120%”. A reset button would return all controls to their starting value. A side-by-side comparison would keep the station, the moment of the shot and the scale. Until that feature exists, users must note down the values and reload the page to fully recover the initial state.

Review exercise. Open Plato, choose Fairground robot, change the force, the height, the laterality, the wind and the cartridge, and press “Reset”. Watch which values stay the same. Then reload the page and note the difference between resetting a round and restoring the whole laboratory. Take a screenshot of the values before you change them. If someone else can't reconstruct the exercise from that information, you're still missing data to repeat it.
Documentary control. The regulated data is already separated by edition and discipline. The pending improvement is to show the edition and the source link right on the visible card, and to record when a control turns the reference into a modified exercise.

What we have learned

Universal, Olympic and Robot are not three levels of the same scale. Machines, distribution, heights, ranges, ammunition, pace and references all change. The regulation defines a reproducible problem; the manufacturer describes what its machine can do; the club decides on a configuration within its own framework; and the shooter's experience explains how it feels, without replacing any measurement.

Plato is most valuable when it makes that separation visible. Universal and Olympic keep their regulatory tables; ABT CPSA and Trap1 FITASC keep their own limits; and the three popular models allow variables to be exaggerated without presenting themselves as the norm. “Meets official table”, “Within limits”, “Outside limits” and “Non-normative model” answer different questions and shouldn't be read as interchangeable labels, nor as certificates for a real facility.

Chapter 12

How to use the simulator to train

The simulator teaches more when it answers a specific question. Repeat a release, change a single variable, watch the result and write it down.

Exercises progress from visible aids to varied situations. The real test comes afterwards, on an authorised shooting ground, under supervision, without crediting the simulator with what only practice can teach.

12.1

The simulator as a mental tool

All disciplines require intercepting a target, but they don't use the same machines, tables or limits. Now we'll put this comparison into practice with specific exercises in the simulator.

How can we stop moving the mouse and hitting by chance, and start making a decision we can explain and repeat?

Plato's mental value doesn't lie in memorising a distance measured in pixels. It lies in consciously following six steps. Before calling for the clay, we think about what we expect to see. When it appears, we watch its direction and height. Then we choose a meeting zone, move the sight, review the correction, and repeat to check whether the decision holds up. This cycle helps us tell what we expected apart from what actually happened.

The yellow aid shows the solution calculated by the model. The blue cloud represents the probable centre of the pattern in the area we're aiming at. At first, you can leave both visible. Later on, it's worth hiding the yellow aid, trying to guess where it would appear, and showing it again to check. That way we stop simply following a cue and start reading the clay's flight. The orange trajectory can also be hidden once you recognise the movement without needing a line.

The code confirms two important functions. The application saves the corrections for the first and second shots. It also lets you repeat exactly the last clay without adding another clay or changing station. Using these functions as a mental cycle is a training suggestion. It is not an ISSF or FITASC rule.

Personal comment. I find repeating a clay especially useful for helping a beginner understand that they're not shooting at its current position. It's a suggestion born from field experience, not a universal rule.

The first exercise has six attempts. In the first two, you look at the aid before moving the sight. In the next two, you try to guess the solution and then check it. In the last two, the aid stays hidden. After each attempt, say a single decision out loud: “I expected a crossing shot to the right, so I chose this zone.” The result isn't measured by clays broken, but by how many times the prediction landed on the same side and at the same height as the solution. If you start responding without thinking, or your explanations lose clarity, it's time to end the session.

Transfer. Bring your instructor an observable statement, for example: “on right-hand crossers I start the decision late.” Don't bring a mechanical instruction invented by the screen.
Expert reading. The cycle links mental preparation, execution and feedback on the result. Deliberate practice needs a specific task, attention, an explanation of the error and further attempts. Racking up many hours on the simulator doesn't, on its own, produce sporting experience.
12.2

What you can train

The simulator helps you think about trajectories and decisions, but it doesn't fully reproduce the shooter's body on the ground. With that limit clear, we can separate out what can genuinely be trained on screen.

What can reasonably be improved with a screen, and what data is worth recording to check it?

Plato lets you practise several specific abilities. You can watch a trajectory, separate its lateral movement from its vertical movement, picture a future meeting zone, and compare how these relationships change when you move to another station or adjust a control. You can also practise the moment you stop thinking about the clay's current position and start thinking about its future position. These are perceptual and understanding skills. They don't yet make up the complete movement of a real shot.

To measure progress, it's not enough to note down “hit” or “miss”. The correction panel separates lead or lag from height. When the aid is visible, the top indicator shows the calculated lead. Across six repetitions, you can note whether the error fell behind, ahead, low or high. You can also watch whether those errors start clustering. Visual memory is checked by repeating without the aid and, better still, by then trying a similar trajectory. It isn't proven by reciting a number.

You can also compare relative velocities. The “Cartridge” control changes the model's pellet flight time. The Robot settings and controls change the clay's release. The station moves the virtual eye and changes the crossing angle we see. These relationships exist within the program. However, the figures don't certify a real cartridge, machine or perception. The application uses known simplifications and projects the scene onto a flat screen.

One study compared expert and novice clay target shooters. It didn't find extraordinary basic visual abilities in the experts. It also warned that training vision generally, beyond normal levels, doesn't necessarily improve shooting. This limits what we can promise. Plato doesn't “improve your eyesight” in general. It trains specific reading and matching tasks within its own image. Whether what's learned transfers to another task has to be checked afterwards. It isn't enough for the exercise to start feeling easy.

To test it, choose a family of crossing clays. Make four attempts with the aid and four without it. Note whether the prediction fell behind or ahead, and high or low. Rest, then repeat the next day with a similar release. The goal is to keep the decision pointing in the right direction without relying on the aid. An immediate streak of hits can simply be down to having just repeated the same exercise.

On the ground. Ask your instructor to watch a single transfer, such as starting the movement in the right direction. Body position and safety stay under their control.
Expert reading. Three things need to be told apart: performing better during the exercise, keeping the improvement after a break, and applying it to a new situation. Only the last two allow us to talk cautiously about learning.
12.3

What can't be fully trained

Reading, comparing and anticipating can be practised with repeatable scenes and visible results. Gun mount, recoil, gun handling and real pressure either don't appear at all, or are only partly represented.

What skills go untrained, even when someone gets good results with the mouse?

A good result in Plato doesn't prove that someone knows how to hold a shotgun, mount the stock, keep their face in place, turn with their feet and torso, or follow through after firing. The mouse only controls two angles from a fixed virtual eye. It doesn't convey weight, weight distribution, stock length, recoil, fatigue or pain. That's why it doesn't teach gun mount on its own, and it doesn't build the muscle memory of the real movement.

Nor does it reproduce the sound or the social atmosphere of a shooting ground. There, you wear ear protection, hear shots close by, follow a group's pace, wait your turn, and light and pressure over the result both change. The application can run at 1.00x and present unexpected releases. Even so, making the program harder isn't the same as feeling the pressure of a competition. Any resemblance should be treated as a possibility to be checked, not as a proven equivalence.

The most important limit is safety. Plato doesn't teach you to check the chambers, load at the right moment, keep the gun pointed in a safe direction, break it open after firing, or deal with a malfunction. Real practice depends on the Guardia Civil, the regulations, the sporting rulebook, the facility and the person in charge of the ground. An icon or a warning is no substitute for an in-person correction. A beginner should never treat the simulator as permission to handle a real gun.

If there's pain, loss of control or any safety doubt, stop practising and consult a competent person. No observation made on the ground sets a universal number of series, nor allows you to diagnose an injury.

Personal comment. I've seen that fatigue, insufficient support and an incorrect gun mount can come with bruising or discomfort in the face and shoulder. I mention it to reinforce the need for supervision, not as a diagnosis.

To remember these limits, set up two columns. Under “screen”, write trajectory, meeting point, comparison and decision. Under “ground”, write open gun, safe direction, gun mount, balance, recoil and getting along with others. Before taking an exercise onto the ground, the instructor decides which part can be checked and which part must be taught from scratch. If a conclusion depends on the body or the real gun, it isn't confirmed by moving another control in the program.

Rule of thumb. The simulator can help you put into words “I think I'm falling low.” Only a real, safe and supervised setting can separate reading, gun mount, stance and adjustment.
Expert reading. The physical difference is huge. The simulator takes a two-dimensional movement and offers no resistance. The real body moves many joints and takes the recoil. So only part of the movement learning can carry over.
12.4

One-variable method

Recognising what's missing stops you from assuming that everything learned on screen carries straight over to the ground. To make good use of the simulator, we'll organise each session using the change-one-variable method.

How can we tell which control caused a difference, when the application offers so many options?

If we change discipline, station, cartridge, choke, wind and gain all at once, the result can have too many causes. The one-variable method avoids that confusion. First, fix a starting situation and save the last clay. Then press “Repeat target”, change a single control, watch the difference, and go back to the starting value. Your notes should state what was changed and what was kept the same.

Not every variable is studied the same way. Cartridge, choke, gain, wind and the aids are recalculated when you repeat, and let you compare the exact same clay. The Robot controls for force, height and laterality, on the other hand, only take effect when a new clay is created. “Repeat target” recovers the position and velocity that were already saved. To study those three controls, you have to go back to the starting situation, change one, create several new clays and compare families of releases. You can't claim to have repeated exactly the same release.

The full method has seven steps. First, fix the baseline: Universal, station 3, wind 0, camera 0.35x, cartridge 400 m/s and aids visible. Second, repeat the same release. Third, change a single thing: cartridge to 430 m/s. Fourth, watch how the yellow solution moves. Fifth, describe the direction and approximate size of the change, without converting pixels into real metres. Sixth, note the conclusion: “with a higher velocity in the model, the time was shorter and the lead moved closer to the clay.” Seventh, go back to 400 m/s.

You need to repeat several times, because the pattern changes and a single hit can be misleading. To study the geometry, look first at the recommended centre, not just whether a pellet breaks the clay. To study the distribution, try to keep the direction and the moment of the shot as similar as possible. The conclusion should stay small: “in this scenario, within the model, X changed.” We can't claim: “this real cartridge always needs exactly Y.”

At the very least, prepare a table with the starting situation, the changed value, three observations, and the result on returning to the start. If the expected pattern doesn't reappear, check whether the moment of the shot, the station, an aid, the second shot's choke or the release also changed. Going back to the starting value keeps the experiment under control.

On the ground. Changing one variable also means not adjusting the stock, the cartridge and the hold point on the same day. The instructor decides which real comparison is safe and relevant.
Expert reading. This method compares conditions within the same situation and controls only part of the differences. It doesn't remove human variation, and it doesn't turn a release generated by the program into a certified laboratory trial.
12.5

Exercise 1: understanding lead

Changing a single variable lets you understand the cause of the result, and makes every attempt useful. The first exercise applies this method to lead.

How can a beginner see that, if they shoot at the clay's current position, the pattern can arrive behind it?

While the pellets are travelling, the clay keeps moving. That's why, if you aim at where it is right now, the pattern usually arrives behind it. Start with a clay that crosses clearly, and use slow motion. There's no general “moderate crossing clay” button. Choose Universal or ABT CPSA and call for clays until you get a sideways release that's easy to read. The application saves the last clay so “Repeat target” can reconstruct it. There's no visible history or a selector for previous attempts. Keep wind, station, cartridge, chokes and gain unchanged.

On the first attempt, deliberately shoot at the clay's current position. The point isn't to practise a miss so you can repeat it later on the ground. The goal is to see, just once and without a real gun, what happens as a matter of geometry. Then check the panel. If the centre falls behind, the initial idea is confirmed within the model. On the following repetitions, move the sight a little further ahead each time. Leave the yellow aid visible for three attempts, and hide it for the last three.

The meeting point doesn't always sit the same distance from the clay. It changes with the moment, the direction, the distance and the calculated flight time. That's why you don't learn it by placing your fingers on the screen or memorising centimetres. The useful reference links three ideas: the direction of flight, the future position and the continuity of the decision. Don't change the zoom either. Zoom only changes the image, and it would make the on-screen gaps stop being comparable.

Record six attempts in three columns: side of the error, height of the error, and aid visible or hidden. The main measure is the central lead error over the last three attempts, not your best shot. If you land ahead some times and behind others, you don't yet have a stable reference. Show the aid again, slow the speed down, and do fewer repetitions with more attention.

If you take this idea onto the ground, phrase it as a question for the instructor: “on crossers in this direction, am I making the decision too late?” Don't carry over metres or on-screen references.

Stopping criterion. Stop after six attempts, or sooner if you start chasing a hit without being able to describe where the centre of the shot ended up.
Expert reading. The yellow solution repeatedly calculates the point where the clay and the pellets will meet. It uses the same physics as the pattern-centre diagnosis. The result belongs to the model, and it changes while the clay is in flight.
12.6

Exercise 2: understanding height

The lead exercise links direction, velocity and flight time to a gap we can actually see. The second exercise works on height alone, so as not to mix the two axes.

How can we tell a height error apart from a lead error, without turning rib gain into a recipe for adjusting the shotgun?

The lateral error shows up in the direction the clay is moving. The vertical error falls above or below its flight line. Choose a rising clay that doesn't cross too far to one side. Keep station, cartridge, choke, wind, zoom and the approximate moment of the shot unchanged. Repeat the release and start with “Rib gain” at 0%. Then compare 50% and 100%. The red dot is still the sight. As gain increases, the physical shot centre appears higher up.

Try to keep the lateral gap stable. On each repetition, place the sight a similar lateral distance away, and watch mainly the panel's vertical axis. Note low, centred or high. If the moment of the shot also changes, the clay will be at a different distance and the comparison will be less reliable. Slow motion helps you pick a similar instant, though it can't guarantee it's exactly the same one.

The code confirms that this control is a simplification. It converts the percentage into a maximum elevation of 0.96 degrees and adds it to the shot direction without moving the sight.
