It doesn't calculate stock length, drop or cast. Nor does it include cheek pressure, the rib, or the shooter's anatomy. The “Flat” and “High” buttons quickly set two values, but they don't indicate what adjustment a real shooter needs.

So the exercise answers this question: “what visual effect does raising the line of the shot produce?” It doesn't answer “how much should I raise my stock?” Breaking a clay with gain at 100% doesn't prove that a real shotgun needs raising. In a real test, gun mount and the point of impact measured with a specific gun and ammunition both matter. Any change should be decided by a competent instructor and, where needed, a gunsmith.

Make three attempts at each value and note whether the centre falls low, centred or high. Don't just count the pellets that touch the clay. The expected conclusion is simple: within the model, a higher gain raises the shot centre. If it doesn't, check whether you fired at different moments or the second shot's choke changed. When you finish, go back to 0% and note down which values you used.

Transfer. Bring the observation “my pattern falls low on rising clays” and ask your instructor to separate out gaze, head position, gun mount and point of impact before touching the gun.
Expert reading. The exercise changes the angle of the point of impact. It doesn't reproduce a stock's whole geometry. Nor is there a calibrated equivalence between the program's percentage and a number of millimetres at a real distance.
12.7

Exercise 3: cartridge and flight time

Working on height separately uncovers gun-mount and vertical-reading faults that the words “ahead” or “behind” don't explain. The third exercise adds cartridge velocity and its flight time.

Why does a higher velocity change the meeting point? And why doesn't this comparison let you decide between commercial cartridges?

A higher starting velocity reduces the time needed to cover the same distance. If everything else stays the same, it also reduces the lead needed. Use “Repeat target” to keep exactly the same clay, and try to fire at a similar moment. Start with 360 m/s, move on to 400, and finish at 430. The clay's trajectory doesn't change. The control only affects the model's pellets. Look at the yellow aid before firing and note whether it moves closer to the clay as velocity increases.

The internal workings have been checked. The program multiplies the figure by 0.78 to get the effective starting velocity. It then applies a loss that increases with distance. The arrival time is used to calculate the meeting point. That's why, if nothing else changes, a higher velocity usually calls for less displacement into the future. The visible difference depends on the crossing angle and the distance. On a clay flying almost straight away, it can be very small.

This exercise doesn't compare 24 g and 28 g loads, or ammunition brands. Plato keeps 306 simulated particles, a fixed mass for each pellet, and the same air-resistance model. It doesn't include the wad, pressure, batch, temperature, real diameter or the manufacturer's testing. “430 m/s” is a setting within the application. It isn't a certified reproduction of a commercial cartridge advertised at that velocity.

Make four repetitions at each value. Alternate 360–430–360–430 to reduce the effect of getting used to the order. Keep the choke and the pattern variation fixed. The main measure is how much the recommended solution changes. As a second measure, note the side of the error when you use a constant sight reference. If you only count broken clays, the pattern's random variation can hide the relationship with time. Note the order too, so the test can be repeated.

The conclusion must stay limited to the model: “on this crosser, raising the control reduced the recommended lead.” Before changing real ammunition, check the rulebook, compatibility, the manufacturer's data, recoil and the coach's opinion. The simulator can't tell you whether a cartridge will be comfortable, what pressure it will have, or how it will perform in competition.

On the ground. Don't change cartridge during a test without your instructor's agreement. If you do compare, record the real product, the batch and the conditions, and keep everything else constant.
Expert reading. The aid and the impact calculation use the same relationship between distance, time and velocity loss. This consistency lets you compare scenarios within Plato, but it doesn't prove that the coefficient matches a chronograph measurement.
12.8

Exercise 4: chokes and distance

Comparing cartridges shows that a small difference in time moves the meeting point and changes the energy that remains. The fourth exercise studies how chokes change the margin for error with distance.

Why does a more open cloud cover a bigger area and, at the same time, leave fewer pellets near the clay?

Opening the pattern increases the area covered. At the same time, it spreads the pellets out more and can reduce their concentration on the clay. The exercise uses the same clay and two moments: an early one and a later one, when the clay is further away. First set “Pattern shot 1” to Full, then to open. Keep cartridge, gain, wind, variation and sight direction the same. The goal is to watch the size and the gaps in the pattern. A single break doesn't decide which choke “wins”.

In Plato, the radius grows with distance and with the control's value. The labels are educational ranges: Full, 3/4, 1/2, 1/4 and open. The cloud keeps 306 simulated pellets. If they spread over a wider cone, they cover more area, but gaps and lower-density zones can appear. A tight pattern concentrates the pellets, though it demands placing its centre more accurately. No choke setting corrects a shot that's clearly landed behind or low.

Make five shots with each combination: Full-early, open-early, Full-late and open-late. Keep the same “Pattern variation” value. Note the apparent size, the centre's position and the number of breaks. Also draw a simple circle around the cloud and mark whether you see gaps near the clay. The sample is still small. What's more, the application doesn't calculate the regulation percentage of hits within a circle. So the reading can only be indicative. A drawn cloud is no substitute for an actual pattern board.

The model simplifies many factors. It doesn't know the constriction in millimetres, the barrel's inner diameter, the wad, deformation, or the real gun or cartridge. The control's labels don't certify that the result matches an installed choke. Nor does it represent the fact that two barrels or two loads with the same name can produce different patterns. The exercise teaches the relationship between coverage and density. To choose a real choke, you need to check compatibility, the rulebook, and the result of that specific combination in a controlled test.

On the field, don't change choke over a single miss. Ask whether the real pattern keeps enough density at the second shot's distance, and check it under supervision with a proper test.

Safety. Any handling or change of choke is done with the gun unloaded, following the manufacturer's manual and away from the moment of firing, under supervision if you're a beginner.
Expert reading. patternRadius() controls the envelope, and pelletDirections() the occupancy. There's no published calibration between the 0–100 scale and real pattern percentages.
12.9

Exercise 5: stations

Choke changes density and spread, but it doesn't correct a badly placed shot centre. The fifth exercise changes station to watch the same release from another perspective.

How does the way we see a release change when we move to another station? And how do we avoid attributing to the station a difference that actually belongs to a different clay?

Changing station changes how the eye sees a family of trajectories, even though the ground itself stays the same. Start in Universal mode and pick station 1. Call for several clays until you recognise a clear family, for example a release to the right. Don't try to keep that flight using “Repeat target” after changing station. The button also recovers the original station. The code confirms this limitation, so you can't make an exact comparison between stations. You have to look for clays from the same family, without claiming they're exactly the same one.

Then compare stations 3 and 5. Keep playback speed, cartridge, chokes, gain, wind and aids fixed. At each station, watch five releases of the chosen family without firing. Classify what you see as crossing in, crossing out, or almost frontal. Also note whether the apparent angle is small, medium or large. Finally, make three attempts at each station and record the panel's error: ahead or behind, high or low.

When you change station, the line of sight changes. Plato can't match identical releases between stations, and it doesn't reproduce stance, turning or peripheral vision either. The comparison is approximate.

To get a useful data point, work out, at each station, how many lateral errors appear in each direction. If four out of five attempts land behind at station 1, and only one does at station 5, you've found a possible reading difficulty within the simulator. You haven't discovered a law about those stations. Repeat on another day in the order 5–3–1. Changing the order lets you check whether the improvement was simply because you already knew the exercise.

The official rules define different positions and procedures. On the ground, the coach chooses a comparable release, explains the permitted zone, and decides whether to shoot or just observe.

Transfer. Take a single question to the ground: “at which station do I read this crosser late?” Your instructor can offer a few controlled repetitions and check whether the pattern from your log shows up again.
Expert reading. “Repeat target” also saves the station. To compare stations, we can only classify families of releases and look at several samples. We aren't comparing an exactly identical experimental condition.
12.10

Exercise 6: Universal Trench vs Olympic Trap

Rotating through the stations forces you to recalculate the apparent direction without artificially moving the ground. The sixth exercise compares Universal and Olympic with their own schemes.

What can we learn by switching mode? And what conclusions shouldn't we draw about two disciplines with their own rules?

The comparison watches four things: where the clay is released from, what trajectories can appear, how they're distributed, and how fast the eye has to locate it. Choose station 3, playback at 0.35×, cartridge at 400 m/s, and aids visible. In Universal, watch ten clays without firing. Note whether each one is released to the left, through the centre or to the right, and whether it goes low, medium or high. Then switch to Olympic and repeat. Don't use “Repeat target” to switch mode, because that function belongs to the clay that's already been created. Select the new mode and generate a fresh sample.

The application organises each mode with internal schemes. Universal uses five machines. Olympic chooses between fifteen groups, shown as M1 to M15. Each scheme's height and range affect the calculated starting velocity. The code confirms this behaviour. What we can't claim is that ten examples represent every frequency in a round. Nor does the on-screen label prove that a real facility meets its official measurements.

On a second round, fire at five clays from each mode. Always use the same rule: start acting when the clay crosses a visual reference you've chosen. Note the recommended yellow lead, the side of the error, and whether your decision came before or after that reference. Don't just compare broken clays. Random selection can present different levels of difficulty. Compare the variety of releases, the time available to decide, and how stable your routine stays.

Differences in the rules should be checked against official sources. FITASC publishes the Universal Trench rulebook, and ISSF the Olympic Trap one. These documents set the number of machines, the procedures, the velocities, the angles, the ammunition and how the competition unfolds. Plato simplifies these elements to show trajectories and physical relationships. It doesn't certify facilities, it doesn't referee, and it isn't an official federation training system.

A valid conclusion would be: “in this sample of Olympic mode, I had to choose between more release groups, and my behind-the-clay errors increased.” It would not be valid to claim: “Olympic is 20% harder.” The exercise doesn't control for experience, the distribution of clays, or an equivalent level of difficulty. Repeat the comparison on another day starting with the other mode, and check whether the same pattern appears.

On the ground. Practise each discipline with a coach who knows its rulebook. Don't automatically carry over a visual reference or a calling routine without checking the real conditions.
Expert reading. Two of the program's generators are compared with the same user. To demonstrate learning, you'd need equivalent samples, a change in presentation order between sessions, and a later test under regulated conditions.
12.11

Exercise 7: extreme robot

Comparing disciplines helps you recognise their limits and rhythms before blaming the shooter for all the difficulty. The seventh exercise uses extreme Robot to practise a wider range of problems.

How can we explore extreme situations without presenting a made-up setting as if it were a real discipline?

Extreme values deliberately increase the angle, the height or the range. They're there to train reading, but they don't turn the exercise into an official discipline. Select Robot and start with a moderate reference: ABT CPSA, force 100, height 0 and lateral 100. Watch three clays and check that “Within limits” appears. Then change a single variable. Try force 70, 100 and 135, with three clays at each value. Go back to 100 and compare heights of −12, 0 and +18 degrees. Finish with lateral values of 50, 100 and 140. At the extremes, “Outside limits” usually appears. That's an expected part of the exercise, not a fault in the interface.

The interface confirms these ranges. “Force” changes the range the model calculates. “Height” changes the vertical movement. “Lateral” increases or reduces the horizontal angle. Robot mode includes ABT CPSA and Trap1 FITASC as rule-based profiles. It also offers circular fairground, extreme popular and pseudo-random as unofficial models. Changes only affect clays created afterwards. If you press “Repeat target”, the saved clay will come back, and you won't be able to check the new setting.

Start by watching without firing. Note how long you can see the clay, roughly where it reaches its highest point, and which direction it's released in. Then make two attempts at each extreme, with the yellow, orange, blue and red aids visible. Repeat, hiding only the yellow aid. What matters is whether you correctly anticipate the direction and whether the error changes side. Breaking an extreme clay once can simply be down to pattern spread. It doesn't prove your reading is stable.

Robot is the name of the single-machine laboratory. It isn't a single discipline. It includes two regulated references and three popular models. Its numbers are simulator settings, not certified measurements from a commercial machine. The extreme controls can even push a regulated profile outside its own limits. That exaggeration lets you clearly see how range, height, lateral direction, distance and meeting time relate to each other.

Only combine force 135, height +18 and lateral 140 once you understand each control on its own. Use it as a test to detect difficulties, not as a performance target. If it's hard to follow the clay, go back to 0.15× and change a single variable. The exercise is working well when you can explain which control caused each change, not when you can just endure the flashiest combination.

Safety and transfer. Don't ask for an extreme presentation by imitation at a real shooting ground. The instructor and the person in charge of the ground decide which clays are regulation, available and safe.
Expert note. In random settings, three clays are too small a sample. Extend the log and separate the generator's own variation from the effect caused by the control you changed.
12.12

Beginner plan

Extreme Robot trains reading and adaptation, as long as we don't mistake its values for official parameters. Once the exercises are defined, we can arrange them into a plan that doesn't overload a beginner.

How do we organise the first sessions to learn gradually, without getting tired or thinking you can already practise without any aids?

The plan proposes four sessions of ten to fifteen minutes, and should stop earlier if attention drops. It's an educational guide, not a federation prescription or permission to use a gun.

In the first session, use Universal, station 3 and 0.35×. Leave all the aids visible. Call for six clays. For the first three, don't fire: state the direction, the height and the possible meeting zone. For the next three, compare the yellow prediction with the orange trajectory and the blue shot centre. The goal is to correctly explain at least two relationships. For example: “if I wait, the distance increases” or “the physical shot centre may not match the sight”.

In the second session, repeat a single clay. Watch it twice and fire four times. Note whether you land ahead or behind, high or low. Only change how much lead you deliberately give the sight. On the last attempt, hide the yellow aid to make a prediction, then show it again afterwards. No number of hits is required. What matters is that the correction responds to the previous error.

In the third session, choose one of exercises 12.6, 12.7 or 12.8. Compare three values of a single variable and complete this sentence: “when I changed X, I observed Y, and I kept Z the same.” In the fourth session, mix six simple clays at a slow speed. Before each attempt, the student explains where they expect to meet the clay. Then they check the panel and note down an adjustment. Finish with a single question for the coach, not a list of technical changes.

Repeating reduces difficulty; hiding an aid forces you to remember; and showing it again lets you correct yourself. Safety, stance, gun mount and recoil still belong to in-person teaching.

Progression criterion. Move to the intermediate plan when the person explains their mistakes without guessing, changes only one variable and keeps to a short routine. Don't progress on the strength of a lucky streak of breaks.
Expert note. The duration and number of attempts are cautious suggestions. They should be adapted to age, tiredness, access needs and the instructor's observations. They are not quantities validated for everyone.
12.13

Intermediate plan

The initial plan starts with safety, using the interface, and simple trajectories before combining variables. The intermediate level adds comparisons and second shots, with more independence.

How do we move from understanding a single variable to choosing a response when we no longer know what release is coming?

The intermediate plan has five blocks of fifteen to twenty minutes, spread across several days. It isn't compulsory. Each block starts with a written idea and ends with a simple measurement. Keep 0.35× if you still need to watch the sequence calmly. Move to 1.00× only once you can explain the correction, rather than simply chasing the animation. If visual fatigue sets in or attention drops, stop.

Block one: repeat one clay and make eight attempts. Keep the yellow aid for the first four. For the last four, hide it, respond, and show it again. Count the errors behind and ahead. Block two: compare stations 1, 3 and 5 with similar families, as explained in exercise 12.9. Watch whether the main side of the error changes with the station. Don't call clays “identical” when the program can't actually match them.

Block three: practise both shots on the same clay. Plato allows a second shot and offers separate chokes and panels for each shot. It doesn't generate a two-clay double. Choose a reasonable choke for each shot, purely as a test of the model. Note the approximate distance and the correction for both shots. If the first breaks the clay, reset so you can observe the second under similar conditions.

Block four: alternate two values of the simulated cartridge or the choke. Use one value per short round. The comparison still belongs to the program: don't turn it into real brands or real-world equivalences. Block five: prepare twelve attempts mixing mode, station or family. Between one attempt and the next, don't change more than one aspect. Before each release, predict the direction and the meeting zone. Then classify the error. The final result is a table of patterns, not a competition score.

Repeating the same task helps you discover a rule. Mixing tasks forces you to recall it in a changing situation, and can help retain the learning, even though it feels harder at first. This is a general finding from motor-learning studies. It doesn't, on its own, guarantee an improvement in clay target shooting, and it doesn't justify introducing too much difficulty too soon. The important test is coming back two days later and checking, without aids at first, whether the decision still holds.

Transfer. Choose the most repeated pattern from the training log and ask the instructor for a short real task that puts it to the test. Keep the correction only if it also shows up there.
Expert note. Change the order of conditions between sessions and note what had been practised before. Improving during a block and keeping the improvement after a break are different outcomes. Fast progress during a session doesn't prove better learning.
12.14

Advanced plan

In the intermediate plan, the user proposes an explanation and checks it against the correction panels. The advanced plan also requires noting down the presets, the limits, and what you want to test afterwards on the ground.

How can we add pressure and variety without mistaking a difficult screen for real competition preparation?

An advanced shooter doesn't need to push every control to the maximum. They need to set better tests. Organise the week into three sessions of up to twenty minutes, kept separate from real practice. The first sets the reference, the second adds variety, and the third checks whether the learning is retained. This is a working suggestion, not an official protocol or a recipe that suits everyone.

In the first session, choose one mode and two families that matter to you. Work at 1.00× and leave the aids visible only for two demonstrations. Then hide the yellow aid and keep the correction panels. Decide beforehand what you'll measure: the percentage of behind-clay errors, the variation in your chosen moment, or the match between your prediction and the solution shown. Eight to twelve attempts are enough for a specific observation. Don't turn such a small sample into a sporting ranking.

In the second session, alternate Universal, Olympic and a moderate Robot setting. Pseudo-random Robot increases variety, but doesn't represent a regulation round. Keep cartridge, chokes and gain the same. That way, the main change will be reading the release. Include two exact repetitions at moments you won't know in advance. They'll let you check whether you remember a correction when the same clay comes round again.

The third session starts with no visual aids. Note your prediction before firing. Then show the aid and the panel purely to assess the decision. The next day, repeat four conditions with no specific warm-up. If the same error comes back, return to a single-variable exercise. If you improve, take a single focus cue out onto the ground. It can help to concentrate on the relationship between the trajectory and the meeting zone, but the coach must adapt it to the person and the exercise.

1.00× mode doesn't reproduce a final, and it doesn't guarantee a regulation time. A virtual test can suggest an idea, but readiness to compete is assessed through qualified observation and real results.

Control criterion. If variability worsens the explanation of the error, reduce conditions. Advanced difficulty must stay diagnosable, not a display of speed.
Expert note. Decide in advance which outcome you will measure, for how long, and how you will check that it holds. Separate the initial adjustment, improvement during practice, and transfer to another situation. That way you avoid picking, after the fact, whichever measure gave the most favourable result.
12.15

Training log

Advanced training isn't about pushing every control up, but about asking questions that can be repeated, and accepting uncertainty. A training log turns those sessions into useful information for the future.

What should we write down so a session ends in a useful decision, rather than a list of feelings?

Dedicate one page to each session. Write the date, the duration and the goal in one sentence. Then note the minimum data needed to repeat it: mode, station, scheme or visible setting, playback speed, simulated cartridge, shot choke, gain, wind, pattern variation and the state of the aids. Not all of these fields change every time. But if you forget the one that changed, the comparison stops being clear.

Before each attempt, write down a testable idea: “raising the cartridge from 360 to 430 will make the aid call for less lead on this crosser.” Then note the result and how you got there separately. The result can be clay broken or not broken. How you got there includes ahead or behind, high or low, the moment chosen, and how well it matched the prediction. Also add a confidence rating from one to five. Breaking the clay with low confidence and a badly placed centre doesn't mean the decision has been properly learned.

One example might read: “2-8-2026; Universal; station 3; 0.35×; same clay; 360 m/s; Full; aid visible. Idea: I'll need more lead. Four attempts: three behind, one centred. Correction: increase the lateral gap. At 430 m/s: two centred, two ahead. Next test: alternate values and keep the same moment.” This example describes the model. It isn't recommending a cartridge, and four hits aren't enough of a test.

When you finish, sum up a single relationship you observed, one doubt, and the next test. Label each sentence as “simulator”, “rulebook/source” or “ground”. These labels stop you mixing up an official rule, a personal observation and a program simplification. When you practise on the ground, add who supervised you, the authorised condition, the result, and whether the idea held up, changed, or stayed unresolved.

Review the training log once a week and look for errors that repeat. Don't just go by your best percentage. A more reliable improvement shows up on different days, needs less aid, and can then be observed on the ground. The training log doesn't replace the coach's judgement, and it shouldn't hold unnecessary personal data. Its job is to give shape to conscious practice: a specific goal, a visible response, information about the error, and a new, dated task adapted to you.

Editorial rule. Write “I observed” for data, “I think” for hypotheses, and “the rule states” only when you can link to the rule in force. That verbal discipline improves training discipline.
Expert note. If you compare sessions, note the app version and the order of conditions. Without that data, a change in the program or simply the order of the exercises can look like an improvement in learning.
12.16

From simulator to ground

Recording the settings, the decision and the result lets you tell real progress apart from a mere impression, and lets you share doubts with your coach. The last step is to carry a single, testable idea from the simulator to the ground.

How can we test, on a shooting ground, an idea born in the simulator, without forgetting safety, supervision and real conditions?

The simulator should end in a small, testable question. Pick a pattern that repeats, for example: “on right-to-left crossers, I start behind.” Take the record to a qualified instructor. Don't turn up with orders to change your stock, choke, cartridge or technique. The screen hasn't seen your gun mount, and it hasn't measured your gun. It has only raised a possible explanation within a virtual image.

Before practising, the person in charge must confirm that the facility, the discipline, the exercise and the equipment are legal and suitable. The beginner receives in-person training on the ground's rules, handling, protection, the gun's condition and range commands. The simulator doesn't grant access, and it doesn't replace this teaching. If an on-site instruction contradicts the prepared exercise, the instruction is obeyed and the test is abandoned.

The coach chooses a few controlled releases and decides what to observe. It might be the side of the error, the starting moment, or the stability of a visual reference. The shooter follows their usual routine and doesn't handle the gun between attempts. After a short round, the two of them compare the real result with the training log. If the same error appears, they try a simple verbal correction. If it doesn't, they note the difference. Depth, apparent speed, stance, recoil, light or how the clay is presented may all have changed the situation.

A safe way to carry what you've learned onto the ground is to test a visual reference, not to copy a setting. Under the coach's direction, you can try the idea of “keeping your eye on the trajectory and seeing the meeting zone.” You shouldn't “raise the stock by 70% because it worked in Plato.” That 70% only reaches 0.96 degrees within the model, and has no calibrated equivalence with a real stock. The same applies to chokes and cartridge velocities.

After practice, keep the idea if it comes up again, limit it if it only holds under certain conditions, or discard it if it depended on the model. The goal isn't for screen and ground to feel the same, but to make clearer decisions without reducing safety.

Guiding principle. No virtual success justifies real practice without competent supervision. When in doubt about safety, legality, equipment or procedure, stop the activity and let the person in charge decide.
Expert note. You need to check whether the learning works in another situation, not whether both experiences look similar. Note the task, the context, the time elapsed and how you decide success. Look for the same effect several times before claiming the simulator caused it.

What we have learned

  • The simulator lets you practise observation, prediction, comparison and explanation. It doesn't reproduce the full physical experience, and it doesn't prove that someone is competent.
  • An easy-to-interpret test keeps the same clay whenever possible, changes a single variable, and decides beforehand what will be measured.
  • The cartridge, choke, gain and Robot controls are educational models. Their numbers shouldn't be copied directly as settings for real equipment.
  • Learning progresses from visible aids to variety and testing. More difficulty doesn't always mean more learning.
  • The training log keeps facts about the program, the current regulations, personal observations, and ideas still to be tested, separate from each other.
  • The real test is carried out at an authorised facility, under supervision and in safety.

Expert judgement. Plato's value doesn't lie in copying every real condition. It lies in allowing controlled visual tests. Afterwards, you can check whether the idea holds up over time, and whether it proves useful in supervised practice.

Chapter 13

Limitations and responsible use of the model

A simulator is useful when it explains its own limits. Plato lets you observe trajectories and meeting points, but a figure from the program isn't a laboratory measurement, and a virtual hit doesn't prove that someone knows how to handle a gun.

This chapter keeps rules, external evidence, personal comments and simplifications separate. That way, an educational comparison isn't mistaken for a certification.

13.1

Why state the limitations

Virtual training gains value when we later test an idea in a real, safe and supervised practice session. So that we don't expect more from the simulator than it can offer, we'll now look at its limits.

Why start with what Plato can't promise? Because a simulation is only useful when we know what it was built for, what data it has been compared against, and what its limits are.

A limitation isn't an apology tacked on at the end. It's part of the model itself. NASA's guidance on models and simulations calls for explaining the objective, the consequences of a possible error, the constraints and the intended use. NIST adds other aspects: the quality of the physical model, the checks carried out, the uncertainty and the preparedness of whoever interprets the result. Put simply, before trusting a screen we need to know what question it's trying to answer and against what reference it has been checked.

Plato answers questions about its own workings well. For example, it lets you see what changes when you increase the cartridge speed, move to another station, open the pattern spread, or repeat a clay. However, it hasn't been calibrated through a published campaign using chronographs, 3D cameras, pattern boards and breakage tests. That's why it can show metres, seconds or joules, but those figures aren't certified measurements. In technical terms, they have no metrological traceability. The relationship can be internally consistent within the programme and still not accurately describe a real gun, cartridge or machine.

Speaking plainly avoids two mistakes. The first is educational: thinking that mastering the mouse is the same as mastering gun mount, recoil, vision and safety. The second is technical: reading “meets the official chart” as if the computer had measured a real trap machine. The interface checks the launch it has generated itself. What's more, the tests compare the code's charts against regulatory data prepared independently. These are valuable checks on the programme, but they don't measure a real machine or certify a shooting ground.

Knowing the limits also helps improve the project. If something doesn't match up, it's no longer enough to argue about whether it “looks real”. We can ask where the problem lies: in the rule used, in the input data, in a formula, in how the scene is drawn, in the interface, or in our own expectations. A personal impression can lead to a good question and a test. It doesn't replace measuring the angle, height, distance or speed through the proper procedure.

Personal note. I have felt that a ground can feel gentler or more demanding, and that a local shoot can break learned references. That impression is useful for asking questions, not for settling a measurement.
Specific case. A user selects Olympic and sees “meets the regulation”. They should interpret the machine, angle, height and range as data from the setting chosen in the program. They can then check the current rules. If they suspect a real ground doesn't comply, they should tell the referee. The screen isn't there to accuse a facility of being outside the permitted limits.
Expert note. Plato checks part of how it works using formulas. However, it doesn't offer a complete uncertainty calculation or external validation of all its results. Its credibility must be explained for each piece of data and for each intended use. It isn't enough to say, in general terms, that the simulator is “realistic”.
13.2

Clay speed

Explaining the limits doesn't weaken the simulator; it clarifies which questions it can answer and which need external testing. Clay speed is the first example of the difference between a regulated value and a real flight.

Does the speed shown in Plato describe a real machine? Not exactly. It's the initial speed the programme's calculated trajectory needs. It's useful for comparing scenarios, but it isn't a certified measurement.

On a shooting ground, the rules aren't based on the impression that a clay is fast or slow. For Universal Trench, FITASC defines the angles, the height at ten metres and the range of each scheme. It also sets out how they're adjusted and what margin of error is allowed. ISSF organises Trap trajectories through charts and a regulation distance. To know whether the standard is met, you have to check a clay launched by a real machine using the corresponding sporting procedure. An animation's visual likeness isn't enough.

Plato starts from two figures: height and range. The solveClayLaunch function calculates a parabola that satisfies both values. To do this, it uses a vertical acceleration of 9.81 × 0.42. It then calculates the initial speed in each direction and displays the total speed in km/h. That 42% of gravity is a simple way of imitating the mix of lift and fall on screen. It doesn't calculate the full aerodynamics of a disc. So the speed shown fits the parabola the programme has created, but it doesn't come from a radar, a chronograph or a calibrated video.

This explains a possible doubt. A setting can exactly meet the height and range set out within Plato and still move differently from a real clay between those two points. A real clay spins, tilts, generates lift and drag, loses speed and responds to wind. The DLR study tracks the flight with two cameras and analyses it in three dimensions. Plato simplifies that whole system so it's easy to observe. What's more, there's no overall control for changing clay speed. In Universal and Olympic, the speed comes from each row of the scheme. Only Robot mode indirectly changes the range and speed through the “force” control.

Slow motion introduces another change in how you see it. At 0.35x, every real second advances the simulation by 0.35 seconds. The model's trajectory doesn't change; it's just played back more slowly. Zoom doesn't alter the physics either, only the size of the scene on screen. A wider screen can show more space to the sides. What's more, a clay crossing sharply can look faster than another that actually has a higher linear speed. That's why “how fast it looks” and the figure in km/h aren't the same measurement.

Exercise. Keep wind at zero, camera at 1.00x and zoom at 1.00x. Repeat a clay and record the marker's speed. Then use 0.35x and zoom 1.40x. The internal physical figure doesn't change, but the visual experience does; that difference shows why a screen isn't a speedometer for the ground.
Distinction. The rule sets the limits and the procedure for checking a real launch. The model calculates the initial speed of a parabola with reduced gravity. The shooter perceives how fast the clay moves before their eyes. The three ideas are related, but they don't mean the same thing.
13.3

Pellet speed

A correct initial speed alone doesn't reproduce all the effects of air, machine and environment. The flight of the pellets is also calculated with some simplifications.

What does choosing 400 m/s mean if the pellets lose speed during flight? In Plato it's the initial value of the cartridge control. The programme transforms it and applies a single braking curve. In real ammunition, the figure and the behaviour depend on each product and on how it has been measured.

A cartridge's advertised speed only makes sense if we know how it was measured. SAAMI's voluntary standard for shotgun ammunition defines test barrels, measurement distances and methods for measuring pressure and velocity. It also explains that different systems can detect different parts of the shot string, that is, the elongated group the pellets form during flight. That's why a catalogue figure must be read alongside the brand, the load, the pellet size and material, the barrel length, the measuring point and the method used.

In Plato, the control runs from 360 to 430 m/s and shows that same figure. However, the shot doesn't actually start at that speed. The code multiplies it by 0.78. So 400 m/s becomes 312 m/s effective for the centre of the cloud. Pellets at the edges can receive small additional reductions depending on the chosen variation. The programme then calculates the distance with a logarithmic formula and makes the remaining speed drop exponentially with k = 0.014 m⁻¹. This means there is a loss from air resistance, although the same coefficient is used for all cartridges and for the whole cloud.

Real external ballistics is more complex. Pellets are small, light spheres with a lot of surface area relative to their mass. Air doesn't slow them down in the same way every time: the effect changes with speed and with how the air flows around them. Specialist studies use aerodynamic drag curves. Some recent papers also calculate how the pellets spread apart and how they can affect one another. Plato doesn't include diameter, air density, temperature, material, deformation, or a curve that changes with the speed of sound. In the revised version, wind doesn't move the pellets either; it only shifts the clay sideways.

That's why the comparison should be read within the programme. As you raise the control, the calculated flight time drops and the meeting point tends to sit closer to the clay's current position. This doesn't mean a commercial 430 m/s cartridge will break clays better than a 400 m/s one. Pattern density, recoil, consistency, the energy left on arrival and compatibility with the gun can all vary in different ways.

Specific case. Repeat a crosser at 360, 400 and 430 m/s without changing sight or choke. Use the result to formulate “in this model, more speed calls for less lead”. To choose real ammunition, check the manufacturer's data sheet, the discipline's rules and a safe trial; don't carry the slider figure straight into a purchase.
Expert note. The values 0.78 and 0.014 m⁻¹ are design decisions made in the program. They don't come from a published experimental fit. The energy calculation uses a fixed mass of 0.000083 kg and a minimum of 0.55 J. The result can be checked within the code, but it isn't a ballistics table for any specific diameter or alloy.
13.4

The pattern

Pellets lose speed, and their energy depends on mass, air braking and distance, not just muzzle speed. The pattern also adds a three-dimensional spread and differences between cartridges.

Does the blue ellipse predict the pattern of a specific shotgun? No. It represents a simplified three-dimensional cloud. Its spread depends on educational controls, and its shape varies slightly with each shot.

A real pattern isn't a fully filled circle, nor is it always distributed the same way. It's the result of combining a particular shotgun, barrel, choke, cartridge and distance. Browning publishes average percentages for each choke, but it also recommends checking the point of impact and the pattern with the tubes and distances you're actually going to use. The word “average” matters. Even within the same product family there are wide differences, and a choke's name doesn't guarantee an exact figure.

Research confirms that many factors are at play. The model by Davidson, Thomson and Birkbeck shows how the choke's constriction compresses the load from the sides and changes the collisions between pellets. Other studies note that distance and choke can have a big influence on pattern size. Under certain conditions, cartridge and barrel temperature can also change speed and spread. These studies don't create a rule that holds for all sporting ammunition. What they do make clear is that a “half choke” doesn't always produce a cloud of a fixed diameter.

Plato creates 306 outgoing directions. It arranges them in rings using a formula based on the golden angle. Each shot also uses a seed, that is, a number that introduces small clusters, gaps, speed changes and delays. The radius grows with distance and with the spread value. The controls show names like Full, 3/4, 1/2, 1/4 and Open. In the simulator, those names correspond to internal ranges. They don't represent the choke constriction in millimetres, and they don't account for the wad, pellet hardness and deformation, the barrel's forcing cone, the batch, or temperature.

The blue ellipse is also a perspective image. The programme calculates impact with all the pellets, even though it only draws some of them on screen. What's more, a circular three-dimensional cloud can appear as an ellipse. A clay can break from an edge pellet that hasn't been drawn. Likewise, a visible gap doesn't have to match a gap in a real pattern. The “Pattern variation” control changes the virtual cloud, but it doesn't represent a measured percentage of irregularity.

Personal note. Every gun can feel different, but the impression isn't enough. At an authorised ground and following the person in charge, a suitable target, a single combination of gun, barrel, choke and cartridge, and several recorded shots let the paper provide evidence about that combination.
Impact limit. Plato treats the clay as broken when a pellet lands within a simplified radius of 5.5 cm and keeps more than 0.55 J of energy. It doesn't require a minimum number of hits, nor does it distinguish the area struck, the disc's orientation or the material's strength. The diagram is there to study the centre, spread and variation of the pattern. It doesn't calculate a real breakage percentage.
13.5

The clay

The simulated pattern helps you understand density and possible contact, but it doesn't replace a real test with a specific gun and ammunition. The virtual clay also simplifies its material, position and breakage.

Does the orange disc fully reproduce a real clay? No. It serves as a visual reference and has a contact zone for detecting hits, but it doesn't calculate its tilt, spin, flight, strength or way of breaking in detail.

For its clays, ISSF sets a diameter of 110 ±1 mm, an overall height of 25 to 26 mm, and a mass of 105 ±5 g. It also defines the rim dimensions and certain conditions for visibility and breakage. The dome must favour a stable flight. At the same time, the clay must withstand being launched and break under a normal hit within the sporting distance. This shows that size, material, spin and fragility all work together.

Manufacturers take care over several details to achieve that balance. Laporte describes stepped edges that withstand leaving the machine, a roughened rim that helps transmit spin, and different resin blends. This is manufacturer information. It explains how the product has been designed, but it doesn't demonstrate how each clay will behave at any given temperature, humidity, machine setting or impact. What's more, not every discipline uses exactly the geometry ISSF defines.

The DLR study tracks clays with two cameras because a real disc doesn't follow a purely ballistic parabola. Its speed, spin and tilt, together with lift, air resistance and wind, change the flight. Plato doesn't calculate all of those movements. It uses a reduced, constant vertical acceleration. It also applies a sideways acceleration based on the wind control. Spin is drawn, but it doesn't change the forces. There are no gusts, turbulence, changes in air density, or variations in orientation. That's why a wind of +4 m/s is a test within the model, not an exact prediction of real-world drift.

To detect impact, the programme treats the clay as a sphere with a 5.5 cm radius around its centre. That measurement matches the outer radius, but it doesn't represent the disc's thin, tilted shape. A single pellet with enough energy generates 36 graphical fragments. The programme doesn't calculate cracks, stronger zones, conservation of mass, or the size of each piece according to where it was struck. The fragments clearly show that the clay has broken, but they don't reproduce a real fracture.

Ground case. If a clay drifts in the wind, the shooter watches the release and follows the referee's decisions. They don't enter the estimated wind into Plato to decide that the clay should have been declared a no target. The experience may prompt a question; the rule and the person in charge settle the call.
Auditable finding. The diagram uses approximate visual dimensions, but the contact zone is spherical and ends up larger than the clay's thin shape. A future validation should separate the disc's shape, its orientation and the breakage rule. It should also compare the trajectories with data measured in three dimensions.
13.6

The virtual shooter

A contact zone and a minimum energy threshold allow breakage to be calculated, though they don't reproduce everything that happens to a real disc. The virtual shooter leaves out even more aspects of the body and of perception.

What can the body learn if the virtual shooter is reduced to an eye and two angles? It can practise a visual relationship and a decision. It doesn't fully train stance, strength, recoil, eye dominance, fatigue, cheek pressure, or safe handling.

In the code, the shooter is an eye positioned at a height of 1.70 m. Its sideways position changes with the station. Aim is controlled by two angles: yaw, the horizontal turn, and pitch, the vertical turn. The mouse moves those angles, and the click fires. The model doesn't include the shotgun's weight, arm length, foot placement, torso rotation, cheek pressure, shoulder force, or the maximum speed of human movement. That's why moving the cursor doesn't reproduce the swing or the inertia of a shotgun.

There is no recoil. The programme doesn't apply an impulse to the gun or the body, doesn't change the second shot for recovery, and doesn't produce noise. Nor can it detect pain, anticipation, blinking, or fatigue. These are factors an instructor must assess in the person, not parameters the simulator has measured.

Personal note. At the start, too many rounds can worsen how the stock sits against the shoulder and cause discomfort; also, the pressure of a round can throw off a technique that seemed stable. These are observations from the ground, not measurements from the simulator.

Eye dominance isn't taken into account either. Plato draws the scene from a camera linked to the right eye and assumes the line of sight is stable. It doesn't ask which eye is dominant, whether both stay open, whether one is covered, whether double vision appears, or whether head position changes. Rib gain adds a fixed angle to the shot. It can't diagnose a stock's fit, nor tell where the real pupil sits relative to the rib.

Studies on virtual sports training show that some transfer to the real sport can exist, but they call for checking both the physical and the mental likeness. The review by Michalski and colleagues found few studies measuring that transfer and noted very different methods. Plato is less immersive than many of the systems analysed. The 2D screen and mouse retain information about the trajectory, but they bear little resemblance to the relationship between perception and movement involved in handling a shotgun.

Combined use. At home, work out whether a clay needs a lateral or vertical correction. At the ground, bring just that question to an instructor and let them watch your feet, gun mount, gaze, follow-through and safety. If pain or loss of control appears, stop the practice; a virtual success doesn't authorise you to continue.
Transfer. The reasonable goal is to transfer a closely related mental skill: recognising trajectories and understanding the future point. The project offers no evidence that it improves scores, real reaction time or body technique. Demonstrating that would require comparing groups and measuring results at the ground.
13.7

Environment and perception

The screen represents the direction of the shot and the user's decision, but not the weight, recoil, eye dominance, or fear of missing. Perception is also altered by screen size, contrast and viewing distance.

Why does the same clay look different on a laptop and on a large monitor? Because resolution, screen size, viewing distance, field of view, smoothness, latency and the device we use to aim all change.

Plato calculates a three-dimensional scene, but the user sees it projected onto a flat surface. The camera uses an initial vertical field of view of 48 degrees and calculates the focal distance from the height of the canvas, the area where the application is drawn. A wider screen shows more space to the sides. Zoom increases the focal distance and brings the image closer. However, the programme doesn't know the screen's physical size or the distance between the eyes and the monitor. Two people with the same number of pixels can see the clay at a different apparent size.

Depth isn't perceived the same way as on the shooting ground either. An ordinary screen shows perspective, changes in size and objects that occlude one another. It doesn't give each eye a separate image corresponding to a clay tens of metres away. The trap, the clay and the cloud all sit on the same physical plane: the monitor. The user can learn the order and direction of the cues. They shouldn't convert a gap in pixels into centimetres of lead to use directly out on the ground.

The animation uses requestAnimationFrame. According to MDN, this function usually follows the screen's refresh rate, which varies from one machine to another, and normally stops when the tab is hidden. Plato calculates advancement from elapsed time and caps each step at 50 ms. This avoids huge jumps, although the simulated clock can fall behind after a freeze. Total latency involves the mouse, the browser, the rendering, the graphics card, the screen's refresh rate, and the person's reaction. The programme doesn't measure or correct all of that latency.

The mouse doesn't equate to a shotgun either. In absolute movement mode, each pointer position converts into an angle. With the pointer locked, the programme adds up relative movements at a fixed sensitivity. System acceleration, surface, resolution, or device type all change the task. The keyboard arrows offer a third form of movement. That's why results shouldn't be compared between machines without noting the setup. Visual ability, device and computer performance would all get mixed together. Even on the same machine, a heavily loaded tab or power-saving mode can reduce smoothness.

Home check. Run the same clay at 1.00x on two screens or at two seating distances. Don't change the physics. Describe apparent size, smoothness and how easy it is to follow. The goal is to recognise perceptual variability, not to pick one universally “correct” distance.
Scope. Comparing users would require physical size, resolution, system scale, refresh rate, browser, eye distance, zoom, pointer method and measured latency. The code keeps the virtual geometry; it doesn't normalise the physical viewing angle of each setup.
13.8

Preserving useful relationships

The screen, the distance we view it from, and the window size all change the appearance, even though the model's coordinates stay the same. What matters is keeping relationships that allow situations to be compared.

If the exact figures are simplified, what use remains? We can study relationships that hold within the programme. They're useful for reasoning, as long as we don't present them as proven truths outside the model.

The first relationship concerns time. If distance and air resistance stay the same, a higher effective speed reduces flight time. Since the clay keeps moving, this normally means less separation is needed to the meeting point. The second relationship concerns distance: if pellet speed doesn't change, a greater distance demands more time. We say “normally” because the clay can also change its speed and apparent direction. Not every situation depends on a single variable.

The third relationship is sideways crossing. A clay's sideways speed determines how far it moves while the pellets arrive. A clay heading almost straight away can be very fast and need little sideways correction. A slower one crossing from the side can sweep through a wider angle in front of our eyes. Plato lets you repeat a trajectory and observe this effect. It doesn't offer a universal lead measurement in metres, and it doesn't replace the instructor's visual method.

The fourth relationship is vertical. A rising clay changes height during the pellets' flight. That's why the future point also needs a vertical correction. The programme's rib gain modifies the shot's angle relative to the sight. This lets us separate “where I see the red dot” from “where the centre of the shot travels”. It doesn't indicate how much a real comb would need raising.

The fifth relationship is spread. In Plato's formula, a wider opening produces a larger radius at any distance above zero. Manufacturers and studies support the general relationship between choke and density. Even so, two real chokes can produce overlapping results depending on the cartridge, the barrel and shot-to-shot variation. The sixth relationship is perspective. Changing station changes the angle from which we see the launch, even though the trajectory on the ground is the same.

These relationships are best understood by changing only one variable at a time. “Repeat target” keeps the clay's position and initial speed, but it doesn't repeat the exact shape of the pattern. To study the geometry, it's best to look at the centre and make several attempts. If we change the wind, the zoom, the station and the cartridge all at once, we'll no longer know what caused the result.

Six-test series. Do two repetitions per pair: 360/430 m/s; near/late shot; central/side release; flat/high rib; Full/open; station 1/5. Write one observation per pair and label it “confirmed in Plato”, not “law of my shotgun”.
Property-based validation. These relationships can be checked automatically. Some always keep the same direction in the formula, such as the radius increasing as the pattern opens. Others depend on the situation, such as the lead seen on screen. The tests must state under which conditions each relationship holds and keep the cases that contradict it. They must not impose a rule broader than the data allow.
13.9

Not a certification tool

Relative time, direction, spread and the meeting point can all help you learn even within a simplified model. That usefulness doesn't turn the programme into a certification tool.

Which decisions are still out of scope even when the screen shows precise numbers? All those that need an inspection, a real test, a professional diagnosis, an official reference, or the accreditation of a person or a facility.

Plato can't certify a shooting ground. In Spain, the Reglamento de Armas (the Weapons Regulation) sets out the conditions and authorisations for grounds, galleries and ranges. Federations also set the geometry and sporting checks. The application doesn't inspect the terrain, the safety zones, the machines, the measurements or the documents. The badge reviews the launch the programme itself has generated. The tests also compare its charts against regulatory data prepared independently. None of these operations carries administrative, official measurement, or federative standing.

It doesn't validate a cartridge or replace an official ballistics chart. It doesn't know the batch, pressure, wad, diameter, hardness or real mass of each pellet, nor does it carry out the SAAMI or manufacturer's method. The 360 to 430 m/s control feeds a single common formula. Nor does it turn the result from 306 simulated particles into a certificate of density, energy, or conformity with a discipline.

It doesn't diagnose a stock either. Rib gain adds up to 0.96 degrees to the shot's axis. It doesn't measure length, drop, cast, cheek pressure, eye dominance, or the real point of impact. A gunsmith examines the gun and its fit. An instructor watches the gun mount and the movement. A pattern test provides data. Altering a stock based on what seems to appear on screen can turn a visual error into a mechanical problem.

Plato doesn't certify that a gun is safe, either. The programme doesn't know its wear, possible obstructions, ammunition compatibility, the condition of the locks, the chokes, or the chambers. It doesn't replace an instructor. It doesn't see where the barrel is actually pointing, it can't stop dangerous conduct, and it doesn't demonstrate that the user knows how to handle the gun. NASA's guidance distinguishes between verifying a model and approving it for a specific use. Plato hasn't been through an accreditation process for making decisions about safety, purchase, fit, or competition.

The useful question is: “who can settle each doubt?” The organisation and its technicians check the ground. The manufacturer and a laboratory document the ammunition. The current regulations define the discipline. The gunsmith examines the gun. The instructor works with the shooter. The competent authority grants or refuses authorisations. Plato can help put the data in order before talking to each of them, but it can't take their place.

Specific decision. After several low misses, the user sees that a high rib improves the virtual result. The responsible course is to bring the observation to the instructor, check the gun mount and test the pattern before touching the stock. If there's a mechanical doubt, a gunsmith steps in; if there's a rule doubt, the referee or the federation.
Precision without authority. Showing two decimal places makes the figure easier to read within the program. It doesn't mean the data is calibrated, that its margin of error is known, or that it has legal standing. The interface must use “internally verified” only to talk about the calculation, and avoid words that sound like an external approval.
13.10

Safe and ethical use

Plato can check its own workings through rules and tests, but it can't certify a shooting ground, a cartridge, or a gun's fit. We also need to communicate its results safely and honestly.

How do you use a shooting tool responsibly? As educational and sporting material. It should steer real practice towards proper training, the necessary authorisations, a suitable facility, and competent supervision.

Plato has a sporting purpose: explaining how a moving clay and a cloud of pellets can meet. This removes none of the responsibility. Spain's Reglamento de Armas (the Weapons Regulation) requires that anyone using a gun can control it and acts carefully around other people. It also restricts its use to permitted places and activities. Grounds and facilities need authorisation and safety measures. An HTML page doesn't grant a licence, a permit, insurance, or access to a shooting ground.

Practical safety is learned with the actual gun, at an authorised place, and under the rules of whoever is responsible there. Plato must not be used to rehearse at home with a real gun, to decide it's unloaded, to practise turns, to clear a jam, or to check a choke. The absence of an accident on screen isn't evidence of control. ISSF's rules, for example, turn safety into visible states and referee commands; the simulator observes neither.

Ethical use also demands honesty about results. A screenshot isn't published as proof of ability, a beginner isn't mocked for their virtual score, and modifying someone else's gun based on a preset isn't recommended. If a Robot scenario is marked as popular or extreme, it isn't presented as certified training. If a rule changes, the source is dated and the manual is corrected. If a claim comes from a personal comment, it's identified as such and left open to challenge.

To protect the user, what's learned has to be carried across step by step. It's fine to learn that a crossing clay keeps moving while the pellets are in flight. It isn't fine to turn that idea into instructions for using a gun outside an authorised facility. The programme can help prepare a question for the instructor, but it doesn't replace their observation. Minors, people without a licence, and those starting from scratch should go to a club or a federation, meet the current requirements, and receive in-person training.

Responsible scenario. Someone with no experience uses Plato to understand why you aim at where the clay will be. They end the session without handling any firearm, contact an authorised club, explain that they're starting from scratch, and follow whatever procedure they're given. The app has done its conceptual job without simulating any kind of authorisation.
Ethical design. The interface shouldn't show badges that look like they certify skill, “certified” messages, or automatic equipment recommendations. It's worth placing notices near the extreme controls and keeping links to current regulations. How a result is presented can also lead to a mistaken interpretation.
13.11

How to report a discrepancy

The simulation doesn't authorise real practice, nor does it replace the diagnoses and advice of responsible professionals. If a discrepancy shows up, it's better to record it properly than to hide it or exaggerate it.

What information turns “this is wrong” into a problem someone else can reproduce? You need to give the version, the device, the discipline, the settings, the steps, a screenshot, and the expected and observed results. It also helps to clarify whether you're talking about a rule, a personal experience, or a programme bug.

First you need to classify the discrepancy. “The clay comes out to the right when the chart says left” can point to a sign error or a misread visual reference. “At my club it feels faster” is a personal experience that needs measuring before it's compared. “The rules set a different distance” can flag a problem with the standard and should include the edition, the page, or the link. “It doesn't fire on the second click” describes a possible interface bug. Separating these categories stops the code being changed to imitate a local habit, or a regulatory figure being dismissed as a mere impression.

The minimum report starts with the environment. It should include the date, the URL or file used, the browser and its version, the operating system, and the window size. If the problem affects smoothness or scale, it also helps to note the resolution, the refresh rate, the zoom, and the approximate distance to the monitor. After that, note the discipline, the scheme, the station, the hold point, slow motion, the delay, the cartridge, both patterns, the variation, the wind, the rib, and the Robot controls. Saying “default values” isn't always enough, because the page can keep changes, and the Reset button may only reset the round.

The steps should be numbered and short: reload, choose the mode, set the controls, call for the clay, move the sight, and fire. The screenshot should show the top badge, the indicators, and the panel. The expected result needs a reference: “according to FITASC 2025, scheme X...” or “according to the patternRadius function, opening the pattern shouldn't reduce its radius”. The observed result should state facts, not assumed causes: what text appeared, in what position, what the console showed, where it froze, or what difference was measured.

GitHub Issues is the traceable channel when it's enabled. GitHub's documentation recommends a descriptive title, a full enough body, and checking for duplicates. Plato's public repository has an Issues tab. Don't include personal data, licence details, gun serial numbers, or third-party images without permission. For a possible physical safety issue, don't wait for the software to respond: stop practice and notify whoever is responsible for the ground.

There's currently a limit to reproducing a test exactly. “Repeat target” keeps the clay, but each shot gets a different pattern seed, and the general draw uses Math.random(). The code internally stores shotSeed, the number defining that cloud, within the correction signal. However, it doesn't display it or let you enter it again. If the problem depends on a pellet sitting at the edge, a screenshot and several attempts can help, though they may not reproduce the same cloud.

Brief template. Title: “Olympic, station 4: the badge shows X”. Environment and version. Full parameters. Five steps. Expected, with a link. Observed. Screenshot. How often it happens. Safety affected: yes or no. With that, the maintainer can start investigating.
Priority improvement. A “Copy diagnostics” button could gather the version, the parameters, the state, the Robot seed and shotSeed, without including any personal data. Showing the simulator's version would also help distinguish between the local file, GitHub Pages and the project's main branch.

What we have learned

Plato preserves useful relationships, but it simplifies trajectory, pattern, impact, perception and the shooter. A figure with a unit isn't necessarily a measurement; an internal test doesn't certify a shooting ground; and a virtual break doesn't validate a cartridge or a technique.

Ask a question, change one variable, and check the conclusion with whoever is responsible: referee, manufacturer, gunsmith, instructor, or authority. Documenting a discrepancy makes it possible to verify it and improve the model.

Chapter 14

The cost of training and its economics

Cost doesn't end with cartridges and clays. It also includes fees, travel, training, maintenance and equipment. Since prices change, this chapter teaches you how to prepare a personal budget with a source and a date.

The examples can be recalculated with your own prices. They're not offers and don't promise savings: the simulator helps prepare questions, but training and verification still happen on the shooting ground.

14.1

Cost components

Just as a discrepancy needs data that can be checked, a cost needs clear concepts, units, sources and dates. With this foundation, we can look at how much it costs to train and what savings simulation might bring.

What expenses should we include so a season that looked cheap doesn't end up full of unexpected payments?

A beginner's first question is usually “how much does a round cost?” To organise things properly, it's worth asking another: “how much will it cost me to keep training this way for a year?” Access may require a federation licence, sports insurance, a joining or membership fee and, depending on the facility, a different price for non-members. You shouldn't always add the insurance and the licence together. Some federations include cover within their licence; in other cases you need to check the policy or the regional documentation. What's more, a federation licence doesn't replace a firearms licence, nor does it make someone a member of a club.

Next come the practice expenses: the price of the ground or of each round, the cartridges used, and certain services. These can include equipment hire, the coach, an exclusive booking, or repeating a trajectory. You also have to count the full return journey, tolls, parking, and, when necessary, accommodation. Order HFP/793/2023 sets 0.26 euros per kilometre for certain official travel by the Spanish administration. It's an administrative reference, not the real cost of every car. For a personal budget, it can be used as a starting point, or you can work out fuel, tyres, maintenance, depreciation and insurance with your own figures.

Equipment is calculated differently. Glasses, ear protection, a vest, a cartridge bag, or a shotgun aren't used up in a single visit. If we load their full price onto the first month, that month will look horrendously expensive and the following ones almost free. It's clearer to separate the initial purchase from an annual reserve for future replacements. The same goes for cleaning, choke checks, repairs, and the gunsmith's work. Some years there will be barely any expense, and others will include a major job. Training should also have its own line. If it's hidden under “extras”, it'll be easier to cut precisely when a beginner needs it most.

Published rates show why there's no single national price. The Federación de Tiro Olímpico de Castilla-La Mancha lists 62 euros for its clay shooting licence. It also states that it covers the calendar year and isn't split by month. The Federación de Castilla y León published a 145 euros licence for 2026, with a different amount for those over 70. These are examples from two regions, not prices that can be swapped for one another. A proper budget needs to note who charges each item, what period it covers, and whether it's compulsory for the activity you want to do.

Practical case. Before your first visit, set up five columns: access, ground and ammunition, travel, equipment and training. Ask the club to mark which items are paid there, which go to the federation, and which only appear if you compete. Don't buy equipment just to settle a figure that's still uncertain.
Expert reading. Separate the annual fixed costs F, the costs per visit V, the costs per round S and the durable purchases that are spread across several years A. That way, you can compare different ways of training. A fixed fee weighs heavily if you make just one visit, and very little if you make a hundred. Cartridges and rounds, on the other hand, increase at almost the same rate as the number of training sessions.
14.2

Cost per round

The real cost includes the ground, the clays, the ammunition, the travel, the licences, the equipment and the time; leaving out one part gives a false picture. To start with, we'll work out the cost of a round.

Does a round of 25 clays necessarily use up a box of 25 cartridges?

A round is a sporting unit. A box is a purchasing unit. In Universal Trench, the FITASC rules define a round of 25 clays and allow two cartridges to be loaded for each clay. The basic calculation is simple. If you fire once at each clay, you use 25 cartridges. If you always fire twice, you use 50. The normal figure will sit between the two: some clays break with the first shot, others get a second attempt, and during training there may be agreed repeats. In Olympic Trap you need to check the rules and format of each event. It's not a good idea to simply copy Universal's consumption.

To work out the cost of the ammunition used, there's no need to round up to full boxes. If Ps is the price of the round, Pc the price of a box of 25, and N the number of cartridges fired, the direct cost is Ps + N × Pc / 25. Let's use an example: 6 euros for the round and 8 euros per box. With 25 shots, the cost is 14 euros. With 35 shots, 17.20 euros. With 50, 22 euros. The 6 euros matches a published rate from the Venta Las Tablas ground. The 8 euros for the box is just an example and should be replaced with each reader's current price.

The money paid on the day can differ from the cost of what's actually used. If someone arrives without ammunition and can only buy full boxes, 35 shots will force them to buy two boxes. They'll have 15 cartridges left over for another day. In that case, the cost of the visit would be Ps + ceiling(N/25) × Pc. To work out the cost of a season, what matters is how much has been used. To know how much money to take to the ground, what matters is how much will have to be paid. If the two ideas get mixed up, the first visit will look too expensive and the next one too cheap.

Being a beginner doesn't determine how many cartridges you'll use. A student may work with a single shot because that's what the instructor asks for. An experienced shooter may practise the second shot deliberately. What matters isn't the level, but what's actually done in each round. Over three rounds, it's enough to note the clays, the first shots and the second shots. Dividing N by 25 gives the average number of cartridges per clay. That personal figure helps prepare the budget for the next session.

Ground exercise. Take two boxes and a sheet of paper. Mark a line for every second shot, without changing your technique to save cartridges. At the end, add 25 to those lines. That number is worth more for your budget than an average taken from someone else.
Expert reading. It's worth keeping two figures. The additional cost of a new round helps decide whether to add another one. The total cost of the visit also includes travel and services. The first figure shouldn't count expenses that have already been paid. The second should show all the money spent during the day.
14.3

Beginners and the second shot

We've already seen that a round doesn't always equal a box of cartridges, because the second shot changes consumption a great deal. Now we'll look at when that second cartridge helps you learn and when it just adds to the expense.

Is it worth giving up the second shot to spend less while you're still hitting few clays?

The second cartridge can double a round's consumption, but it doesn't have to be a waste. It's useful for learning to keep your cheek on the stock, continue the swing, and correct when the clay is already further away. The problem appears when you fire out of reflex without having understood the first mistake. Then more ammunition is spent, and two attempts get mixed together that the beginner can't tell apart. Saving money doesn't mean banning the second shot. It means knowing what it's for.

A session can be split into three blocks. In the first, one cartridge is used to steady the gun mount, the gaze and the continuity of the swing. In the second, the second shot is only allowed when the instructor asks for a specific correction. In the third, the shift from the first shot to the second is practised deliberately. This way, consumption stops being a vague figure of “up to 50” and becomes planned instead. With 25 clays and a second shot on 40% of them, 35 cartridges are used. If the box costs 8 euros, that's 11.20 euros worth of ammunition, against 8 euros with a single shot. The difference is 3.20 euros. That doesn't seem enough to drop a useful exercise, but it doesn't justify repeating it without a goal either.

Repeating also costs differently on the ground and in Plato. In the app, “Repeat target” rebuilds the last clay exactly from the same station, allows up to two shots, and doesn't advance the counter of 25. That virtual repeat uses up no real clay or cartridge. At a facility, repeating a trajectory depends on the system, on whoever's in charge, on how the group is organised, and on how the practice is charged; the simulator's button should never be assumed to describe a rule of the ground.

Before travelling, it's worth setting out a specific idea: “my second shot falls behind because I stop my hand” or “I lose sight of the clay when I block it out”. In Plato, you can repeat the clay, first bring on a mistake that falls behind, and then use the second shot to watch the correction. Slow motion and the panels help show the difference. You then take a single question to the ground and check it with the instructor. The simulator cuts down on the trial-and-error needed to grasp the concept, but it doesn't train recoil, the body's recovery, or safe handling. Those parts need real practice.

Practical case. Budget for 35 cartridges per round for a beginner's session, and take two boxes. Ask the instructor to decide beforehand on which ten clays the second shot will be practised. At the end, record whether those attempts produced an observable correction.
Expert reading. If q is the proportion of clays with a second shot, the expected consumption is 25(1 + q). The parameter runs from 0 to 1; turning it into an explicit input avoids wrongly linking it to the hit rate or the shooter's level.
14.4

Occasional training scenario
