Using more cartridges can be necessary, but the second shot only adds value if it's aimed at correcting something and doesn't just repeat the first mistake. An example of occasional practice will show how to include it in an annual budget.

What annual budget does someone need if they visit the ground once a month, without hiding initial purchases or inventing universal prices?

An example is only useful if we can check its figures. Let's think of someone who already has their own or supervised equipment. They go to the ground once a month, shoot two rounds of 25 clays, and use 35 cartridges per round. On each visit they cover 50 kilometres there and back. For the calculation we use four values that can be changed: 6 euros per round, 8 euros per box of 25, 0.26 euros per kilometre, and 62 euros for the annual federation licence. Only the round price and the licence come from published examples. The box price is an assumption. The cost per kilometre is an administrative reference, not that person's car's proven cost.

The cost of each visit breaks down as follows: 12 euros for the ground, 22.40 euros for the 70 cartridges used, and 13 euros for travel. The variable total is 47.40 euros. Twelve visits add up to 568.80 euros. Adding the 62 euros annual fee brings the total to 630.80 euros. This figure doesn't include the membership fee, training, hire, maintenance, food, parking, or buying equipment. That's why it should be called a subtotal calculated with these figures, not “the annual cost of clay shooting”.

It's also worth setting aside an amount for expenses that don't come up every month. If this person puts aside 10 euros a month for protection, cleaning, or a future check-up, the budget rises to 750.80 euros a year. If they book two training sessions, they should add their real price on a separate line. It's better to show a complete total than to try to reach a low figure. An incomplete budget can lead to training or maintenance being cancelled when an unexpected expense turns up.

Training only now and then also has a drawback. Feel and routine can be forgotten between one visit and the next. Shooting many rounds on the same day once fatigue has set in doesn't always make up for that gap. Plato can cover part of the time between sessions with short exercises on reading and the meeting point. It doesn't preserve gun mount or reproduce recoil. A sensible plan is to prepare a single variable at home, work on it over two rounds with breaks, and note the result. If the second round loses quality, it may be better to stop early even if there's still money left to spend.

Specific decision. Replace the example's four values with the club's receipt, your actual ammunition price, your round-trip kilometres and your licence. Add an annual reserve and two training sessions before deciding whether one or two monthly visits are sustainable.
Expert reading. This example assumes twelve equal months and doesn't remove months with no activity. If we want to know when we'll need the money, the licence has to be placed on its payment date and the boxes on their purchase date. If we want to know the annual cost, expenses can be spread out according to consumption and the equipment's useful life.
14.5

Frequent training scenario

Monthly training needs to balance continuity, fatigue and travel, not just add up rounds. Training more often increases the opportunities, but also the small repeated expenses.

What changes when you train two days a week and small amounts of spending turn into thousands of euros?

When practising two days a week, the annual fee loses weight within the total. The budget depends mainly on the rounds, the cartridges and the travel. Let's use a cautious example: eight visits a month, three rounds per visit, 35 cartridges per round, and 25 kilometres there and back. We'll keep the earlier assumptions: 6 euros per round, 8 euros per box, and 0.26 euros per kilometre. Each visit costs 18 euros for the ground, 33.60 euros for the ammunition used, and 6.50 euros for travel. The total is 58.10 euros.

Eight visits cost 464.80 euros a month and 5,577.60 euros a year. Adding 62 euros for the licence and an annual reserve of 300 euros for maintenance, protection and replacements, the total reaches 5,939.60 euros. This doesn't include the gun, the club joining fee, the instructor, or competitions. Nor does it mean that everyone who trains frequently uses 35 cartridges per round. In this example, if you fire only once per clay, annual ammunition drops from 3,225.60 to 2304 euros. If you always fire twice, it rises to 4,608 euros. How you train changes the total far more than a small difference in the annual fee.

Training more also changes how you buy and manage supplies. Buying several boxes at once can cut down trips to the gun shop or change the unit price. This doesn't permit storing or transporting ammunition without following the rules, nor does it recommend choosing it purely on price. The budget records the cartridges fired and the price paid. Safety, compatibility and the discipline's rules are checked separately. A maintenance reserve doesn't authorise DIY repairs either. It only means there'll be money available for cleaning, consumables, and a professional check when needed.

There's another risk: confusing quantity with quality. If fatigue means the third round bakes in a bad gun mount, buying cheaper ammunition won't fix the problem. It's worth noting the hits, the second shots, the day's goal, and the moment attention starts to drop. Cutting from three rounds per visit to two, while keeping eight visits, would cut 96 euros of ground fees a month plus the corresponding ammunition. That's only an improvement if the training still covers the necessary work. The instructor can help decide where that limit lies.

Practical case. During a frequent month, note the actual number of cartridges and score each round's quality from one to five. If the last round is consistently worse, try another month with two focused rounds and compare learning, not just spending.
Expert reading. Check how the total changes when you alter three variables: visits, rounds and cartridges per round. The three multiply together. Cutting the box price by 10% can save less than dropping repetitions with no clear goal, if those make up a bigger share of consumption.
14.6

Competitive scenario

When you train more, noting quality and the moment fatigue sets in matters just as much as chasing good prices. Competition adds entry fees, travel, accommodation and contingencies that don't exist in an ordinary round.

Why isn't the cost of competing worked out by simply multiplying a training round by the number of clays?

A competition adds expenses that don't usually turn up on a normal training morning. You have to count the entry fee, specific preparation, a longer journey, possible accommodation, meals away from home, practice and competition cartridges, spare parts, and technical support. What's more, not every event has the same number of clays. The ISSF Rule Book 2026 sets 125 clays for the Trap qualification. FITASC runs its international Universal Trench championships over 200 clays. A regional or social round might have 50 or 75. You can't budget for “a competition” without first reading its programme.

Published prices also vary a great deal. Aragón's 2026 Compak guidelines set 30 euros for 50 clays, 40 euros for 75, and 50 euros for the next level given in the document. The Venalmazán 2026 fair announced 10 euros for the local entry and 20 for the open one. These prices can't be applied to a national championship. What's more, the entry fee may not include cartridges, official practice, the national licence, or some of the ground's services.

Let's look at a worked example, not a prediction. Imagine six competition trips a year. Each one costs 60 euros in entry fees, uses 175 cartridges at 8 euros a box, covers 300 kilometres at 0.26 euros, and costs 90 euros in accommodation and 35 euros in meals. Each event costs 319 euros, and the six together come to 1,914. If we add, before each event, two rounds at 6 euros and 70 practice cartridges, that adds a further 206.40 euros. The additional cost on top of regular training is 2,120.40 euros. All the prices are examples. They should be replaced with the actual programme, bookings and receipts.

It's worth adding an amount for contingencies and showing it separately. 10% on top of travel and accommodation can cover reasonable changes, but it doesn't replace the real prices. Spare parts should be permitted, compatible material that stops a minor fault costing you an event. They don't justify working on a gun without the necessary training. If a coach or trainer takes part, count their fee, their travel, and the specific sessions. If a federation offers grants, only subtract them from the budget once they've actually been awarded.

Specific decision. Before you enter, open the programme and fill in a sheet for each event: clays, entry fee, official practice, expected cartridges, kilometres, tolls, nights, meals, spare parts and support. Then compare that total with the annual budget, not just with the entry fee.
Expert reading. The cost of competing is additional. It should only include expenses that wouldn't exist without the competition, so as not to count regular training twice. It's worth preparing three scenarios for travel and accommodation: low, normal and high. These are the prices most likely to change over time.
14.7

Cost calculator

Competing requires budgeting per event and per season, with a margin for contingencies, and counting grants only once they're confirmed. An editable calculator lets you change the figures without redoing the whole calculation.

What formula lets you change any price without rewriting the chapter or relying on an outdated figure?

The calculator brings the budget together in seven figures you can change. “Cost per round” is the price of 25 clays. “Boxes per round” converts the cartridges used into boxes of 25. “Travel per visit” adds up the journey, tolls and parking once the reader has worked out that amount. This keeps the form easy to handle. Anyone who wants to separate the kilometres and the cost per kilometre can do that multiplication first and then enter the total travel figure.

The formula for one visit is Cv = S × Ps + S × B × Pc + D. Here, S is the number of rounds per visit, Ps their price, B the boxes used per round, Pc the price of each box, and D the total travel cost. If V is the number of monthly visits and F the annual fixed costs, the equivalent monthly cost is M = V × Cv + F/12. The annual cost is A = 12 × V × Cv + F. Splitting the fixed costs across twelve months helps you compare scenarios, but it doesn't change the date on which a licence or a fee is actually paid.

Editable training calculator

Per visit47.40 €
Monthly equivalent52.57 €
Annual estimate630.80 €

With the initial values, each visit includes two rounds at 6 euros. Each round uses 1.4 boxes at 8 euros, and travel costs 13 euros. The result is 47.40 euros per visit. Splitting the fixed 62 euros across twelve months gives an equivalent monthly cost of 52.57 euros and an annual cost of 630.80 euros. Changing any field updates all three results in the browser. The word “equivalent” matters. The fee may be paid in full in January rather than bit by bit each month. The calculator shows an average cost. To know when the money actually goes out, you need a payment calendar.

This calculator is part of the book, not the main panel of app.html. The initial values are a dated educational example. They don't represent a national rate, and they don't promise any savings. All the figures stay visible so the reader can replace them with their own receipts, their club's rates, and their own distances. How to keep them up to date is explained in 14.10, Prices and updates.

Exercise. Work out three columns: your normal month, a month with one less visit, and a month with one less round per visit. Don't automatically pick the lowest figure; write alongside it which learning goal you'd lose or keep.
Expert reading. Don't round the annual cartridge consumption up to whole boxes. Use a separate output to work out the money that will need to be paid on each purchase, and apply ceil only there. Also keep the currency, the date, the territory and the unit. Without that data, two budgets can't be compared properly.
14.8

How much the simulator can save you

The formula uses each area's prices and personal habits to build a clear, dated estimate that can be reviewed. With that figure, we can weigh up which expenses the simulator can avoid or better prepare for.

When can a session in Plato avoid a real expense, and when does it just add more time in front of the screen?

A virtual shot doesn't replace a real cartridge. The possible saving appears when preparation avoids aimless trial and error and lets you arrive at the ground with a specific question. It doesn't remove the need for training.

The app offers several tools for preparation. “Repeat target” keeps the last clay and station without advancing the round. Slow motion lets you go from 0.15x up to normal speed. The yellow and blue aids show the recommended point and the likely centre of the pattern. The panels store the correction for both shots. To look for a possible saving, you need to decide before travelling which variable you're going to study and how many rounds you'll spend checking it. It's not about racking up virtual hits.

We can calculate a possible saving if we spell out what situation we're comparing it against. In technical terms, this is called a counterfactual. Suppose preparation avoids one trial-and-error round a month, but the visit to the ground still goes ahead. With 6 euros for the ground, 35 cartridges per round, and an example box at 8 euros, that would avoid 17.20 euros a month and 206.40 euros a year. We don't add travel, because the person still goes to the ground. If a whole visit were avoided, its kilometres and other costs could be added too. On the other hand, if that round was necessary for learning the movement, the saving would be zero, and learning quality could even suffer.

Scientific studies advise caution. A 2019 review of the transfer from virtual environments to real sporting skills found interesting early results. However, only four papers met the basic criteria for measuring that transfer. Plato isn't an immersive system either, and it hasn't published a trial on improvement in real clay shooting. That's why any saving should be recorded as an experience or a hypothesis of the user's own. It's worth logging the planned sessions, the rounds completed, the cartridges used, and the result on the ground before and after.

Practical case. Spend ten minutes preparing a crossing clay, change only the cartridge speed, and write down a prediction. On the ground, dedicate one round to testing that single idea. It only counts as a saving if another trial round stops being necessary without reducing safety or quality.
Expert reading. Work out the additional saving by comparing the cost with preparation against a similar situation without it. Without a comparison group or a stable track record, you can't prove that the simulator caused the saving. All you'll have is a useful personal estimate, not a promise that holds for everyone.
14.9

Non-financial value

Plato can avoid poorly framed trials, but it doesn't replace the sessions needed to learn the movement, safety, and the step onto the ground. It also brings benefits that aren't measured in money.

What can improve even when the number of euros spent doesn't change?

The budget doesn't capture everything that matters. Arriving at the ground with a specific question can reduce frustration, sharpen focus, and make the conversation with the instructor easier. Instead of saying “I always miss”, the student can explain “on the second shot I lose the clay when I stop my swing” or “I want to check whether my reference point is too low”. Talking this way doesn't guarantee a hit, but it turns a confused feeling into something that can be observed.

Plato offers a shared vocabulary for talking about trajectory, meeting point, pattern, lead and height. Its value shows up when that reading ends in a simple decision for the ground.

Research on mental and virtual training offers some support, but no specific guarantee. A review published in 2026 studied movement imagery, action observation, and the combination of both. Eight of the nine studies included found improvements across various sporting aspects. Even so, the review noted that no single optimal method has yet been proven. Another paper on virtual environments warned that very few studies measured the transfer to the real sport. Plato doesn't show a real athlete or offer full immersion. Its most likely contribution is mental: preparing the reading and the decision.

Recognising the limits in advance also adds value. The screen doesn't reproduce weight, recoil, noise, stock pressure, eye dominance, fatigue, or handling rules. Knowing this stops you mistaking a bodily fault for a geometry problem. It also stops a virtual break being treated as a certification. To assess this non-financial benefit, you can answer four questions: what doubt did I bring? what variable did I observe? what could I ask the instructor? and what happened next on the ground? If the answers get clearer over time, there's an improvement even if spending doesn't change.

Exercise. Before each visit, write a sentence of no more than fifteen words stating your goal. Then ask the instructor for feedback on that sentence and record some observable evidence. Avoid using the number of clays broken as your only measure.
Expert reading. Separate intermediate results, such as understanding and the quality of the question, from final results, such as stable performance on the ground. The first can be measured through self-recording; the second requires repetition, comparison and control of many variables.
14.10

Prices and updates

Arriving at the ground with a clear question, reducing frustration, and speaking more precisely are advantages even when the cost doesn't change. For the figures to stay reliable, the prices and assumptions need to be kept up to date.

How do you publish a useful figure today without it turning into false advice tomorrow?

Every price needs six pieces of information attached to it: what is being paid for, how much it costs, what unit it's expressed in, which territory or supplier offers it, what the source is, and when it was checked. Saying “the licence costs 62 euros” isn't enough. It's better to write “Castilla-La Mancha clay shooting federation licence, 62 euros per calendar year, according to the federation's page, checked on 2 August 2026”. That way it can be verified and replaced. The same applies to a round: you need to state whether it includes 25 clays, whether the price changes for members and non-members, and whether it's for practice or competition.

The figures behind this review change so much that publishing a national average wouldn't be right. Castilla-La Mancha reports 62 euros for its clay shooting licence. Castilla y León published 145 euros for 2026, with a special price for people over 70. Venta Las Tablas advertises 6 euros for 25 clays and 5 euros for members. Aragón's 2026 Compak guidelines publish prices for 50 and 75 clays. One specific municipal round distinguishes between local and open entry. We shouldn't average these figures, because each price pays for something different.

An easy table to maintain keeps the published price and adds a box for the reader to write in their own. If there's no direct, recent and comparable source for cartridges, equipment, accommodation or a coach, it's better to leave the suggestion blank. The reader can add their own invoice or quote. Sporting documents define the clays and the conditions. Clubs and organisers set the fees. Federations set the licences for their territory. Shops price a product at a given moment. None of these sources replaces the others.

Licences and fees can be reviewed once a year. Ammunition is worth reviewing every month or before you buy. Entry fees, travel and accommodation need updating for each event. If a link stops working, the figure is marked “pending”. A figure is never copied from another source without saying so. The interactive calculator in 14.7 starts with a clear example and lets you change it without touching the code. Its values are editable data, not physical constants or recommended prices. That way, the method stays useful even if every amount changes.

When you update the calculator, you also need to check the units. A box can hold 25 cartridges, a round 25 clays, and a visit several rounds. If a supplier uses a different presentation, you first need to convert it into the unit the form asks for. The “travel” field expects euros per visit. The “fixed costs” field expects euros per year. If you enter kilometres where euros are expected, or a monthly fee where an annual one is expected, the result can look correct and be completely wrong.

Document control. On the first day of each season, check the licence, the club membership fee and the round price. Before each block of training, update the box price and travel cost. Before competing, replace all the values with the event's programme and bookings.
Expert reading. Model each price as a record, not as a single number: {importe, moneda, unidad, territorio, fuente, consultado, vigenteHasta, estado}. A calculation can keep working with manual entries even if one source is still pending.

What we have learned

  • The real cost separates fixed payments, consumption per round, travel, equipment, maintenance, training and competition.
  • A round of 25 clays can use between 25 and 50 cartridges in Universal Trench; observed behaviour matters more than the “beginner” label.
  • Every scenario must keep its assumptions and distinguish cost incurred, outlay and published price.
  • Plato can help prepare a hypothesis and cut down on conceptual guesswork, but it doesn't replace practice, safety, a coach or validation on the ground.
  • A price is only maintainable when it includes a unit, territory, source and date.

Expert reading. The budget is there to check how the cost changes when we alter each variable; it isn't a price list. Its value lies in showing which factors multiply spending and in allowing decisions that can be reviewed. It doesn't turn a coincidence between using the simulator and lower spending into a promise of savings.

Chapter 15

Installation, licence, attribution and reuse

Plato can be used, copied, modified and published, but authorship doesn't disappear. A copy must keep the necessary files and the MIT licence notice.

LICENSE contains the legal text in English, and LICENCIA_ES.md is an informative translation. MIT requires the copyright and permission notices to be kept in copies or substantial portions. In Plato these identify Roberto Canales Mora and the original URL. Documenting changes is recommended, but it doesn't add conditions to MIT.

15.1

Nature of the project

A budget needs a source, a date and a review, and an application needs a clear version, licence and origin. Having looked at costs, we'll now see how to use, copy and maintain the project legally.

What's behind Plato? Does it need services installed, or is it enough to open a set of files from the browser?

Plato is a static web application that runs in the user's browser. “Static” doesn't mean it doesn't move. It means the server doesn't build a different response for each person, and it doesn't calculate the physics either. It only delivers HTML, CSS and JavaScript files, plus the images already stored. The browser then draws the simulator inside a canvas element. It also picks up mouse and keyboard input and calculates the trajectories, pattern and impacts on the user's own computer. In the reviewed code there are no fetch calls, no API queries, no user accounts, no database, no telemetry and no sessions stored on a server.

The two main entry points serve different purposes. index.html contains the web book. This file loads the stylesheet docs/manual-libro/assets/css/manual.css, the navigation and search script docs/manual-libro/assets/js/manual.js, and the manual's images. app.html contains the interactive lab. Its styles and JavaScript sit inside the same file, and it doesn't declare any external libraries. That's why it can look like a standalone page. Even so, its links to the manual and the project need the repository structure or an internet connection.

GitHub Pages fits well with this structure because it publishes static files from a branch or through an Actions workflow. It doesn't run PHP, Python or Ruby on the server, but Plato doesn't need those languages there. The public address shows index.html as the front page and app.html as the simulator. If accounts, shared results or a database were ever added, this structure would no longer be enough. It would need a data server, a privacy policy and new security measures.

Not having a data server has practical effects. The session doesn't sync across devices, and reloading the page brings back the initial state. In exchange, how it works can be checked easily. You can open app.html, find its tables and formulas, and run the scheme check included in scripts/verify-schemes.js. This architecture also makes hosting simpler. It doesn't remove the need for maintenance, though: browsers, the manual, sporting rules and sources keep changing.

Specific case. A club wants to show Plato on a laptop without creating user accounts. It's enough to serve the project folder on the local network and open app.html. If it also wants to save several people's scores in a shared database, that feature doesn't exist: an extra system would need to be designed, along with an explanation of what data it collects.
Expert reading. Execution happens in the browser. GitHub Pages delivers the resources over HTTP. After that, requestAnimationFrame, the input events and Canvas 2D run on the user's own machine. “No external dependencies” describes the reviewed version, but it doesn't guarantee that future versions will keep that feature.
15.2

Using the original page

Plato is a static application that can be checked and run with just a few files, but it still needs versions, tests and maintenance. The original publication is the simplest way to access it and recognise where it comes from.

What's the fastest way to try Plato, and what compatibility has actually been tested, without promising more than has been checked?

Plato's public entry point is https://rcanalescoder.github.io/Plato/. That address opens the book and offers a link to app.html, which is the simulator. Using these addresses avoids confusing the page that shows the code on GitHub with the application you can actually run. The first time you open the manual, the browser downloads the stylesheet and the navigation JavaScript. Lazily loaded images download when they're needed. The simulator keeps its code and styles inside the HTML itself, so its main startup doesn't depend on an external content network or CDN.

The reviewed version uses technologies that are standard in today's browsers: HTML, CSS, Canvas 2D, form controls, mouse, keyboard and requestAnimationFrame. That's why a current version of Firefox, Chrome, Edge or Safari on a computer can be recommended. However, the project doesn't publish an automated test across every version of every browser. The word “compatible” expresses a technical expectation and a practical check, not a certification. On an older browser, things like 100dvh or some input API might not work properly and could affect the layout or the controls.

The basic walkthrough starts with Call for clay, continues by moving the red dot, and uses Fire. Repeat target keeps the previous outcome; Reset restarts the round; the station buttons change the position; and the controls below adjust discipline, scheme, slow motion, delay, cartridge, pattern, wind, zoom and Robot. Quick help opens a section inside app.html; Manual and Technical documentation open parts of index.html.

Plato doesn't have its own fullscreen button and doesn't use the Fullscreen API. To fill the whole screen you need to use the browser's own function, which varies by operating system. The interface rearranges its panels once the width reaches 1040 or 560 pixels. However, aiming on the canvas uses mousedown and mousemove, which are mouse events. It doesn't yet use touch or general pointer events. Because of that, a phone can display the page and let you tap buttons, but it shouldn't be presented as a full touch experience until aiming is implemented and tested for touch.

Acceptance test. On a laptop, open the root URL, go into the simulator, call for a clay with Enter, aim with the mouse, fire with the space bar, and go back to the manual. If it will be used in a class, repeat the same journey on the specific browser and screen beforehand.
Compatibility limit. The HTML controls can be used with the keyboard. However, the canvas shows visual information that has no full text alternative. A design that adapts to screen size doesn't, on its own, prove that touch handling works or that WCAG accessibility standards are met.
15.3

Local installation

Using the original publication makes it easy to know where it comes from and how it's been reviewed, even though how it behaves depends on the browser and the input device. A local installation lets you control and keep a specific copy.

How do you get a copy that someone else can reproduce, and why is it better to open it through localhost rather than double-clicking the HTML?

There are two useful ways to get a copy. Cloning the project with Git keeps its history and makes it easy to receive updates. Downloading via Code → Download ZIP creates a snapshot copy, but without history. The ZIP for a branch can change as that branch moves on. To keep exactly one version, you need to note down the commit identifier or download the file for that commit. If a release is published in future — that is, a version prepared for distribution — its files could be a more convenient reference. In the current review there's no installer and no standalone executable package.

git clone https://github.com/rcanalescoder/Plato.git
cd Plato
python3 -m http.server --bind 127.0.0.1 8000

You then open http://127.0.0.1:8000/ to read the book, or http://127.0.0.1:8000/app.html to use the simulator. On Windows you can use py -m http.server 8000 if the Python launcher is installed. The Python module serves the files in the current folder and recognises index.html as the entry page. It's good for a local test. Python's own documentation warns that it shouldn't be used as a public production server.

Double-clicking can open app.html, because the current version keeps its runtime resources there. Even so, it isn't a reliable way to check the whole project. file:// addresses can be treated differently for security and can block local resources, fonts, requests or storage. Even if one particular version seems to work, serving the folder over HTTP is closer to how GitHub Pages behaves. It also handles relative paths better and avoids depending on a particular browser's security choices.

The folder shouldn't be broken apart. To read the manual with its images you need to keep index.html and the docs/ tree; to run the simulator you need app.html. LICENSE must go with any copy or substantial part, because of the MIT condition. Before using the copy for an activity, it's worth checking the front page, the simulator, an image, the help, the links and the browser console.

Updating doesn't mean replacing the folder without checking anything. With a cloned copy you can check the history first and compare the changes. If you're using a ZIP, it's worth keeping the previous copy until you've checked the new one. Local adaptations need to be noted down before bringing in a new revision. That way they aren't lost, and a problem created by mixing versions doesn't get blamed on the original project. To stop the local server, press Ctrl+C.

Local exercise. Download the ZIP, unzip it into a new folder, start the server, and open both URLs. Disconnect from the internet and reload: the simulator will keep running, but the external links and linked fonts won't be available.
Reproducibility. Note down the commit, the date and the command used. A ZIP of main changes along with the branch; a commit identifies a stable version. Limiting the server to 127.0.0.1 stops it being needlessly available to the whole local network.
15.4

Hosting your own copy

Opening the copy through localhost avoids some of the limitations of local files and lets you repeat the test without setting up a complex service. If you publish it again, you need to take care with the paths, the notices and the version.

What changes when you publish a copy on GitHub Pages, your own server, an intranet, or inside another page?

A fork is a GitHub copy that keeps a link to the original repository. It lets you modify the project and propose changes without writing directly to Roberto's branch. To publish it with GitHub Pages, the fork's owner chooses a branch and a folder under Settings → Pages. They can also set up an Actions workflow. Since index.html is in the root, publishing the root of the chosen branch keeps the current structure. The new address will belong to the fork's account and shouldn't be confused with the official page.

To publish on your own server or on an intranet, you need to copy the whole tree and keep its relative paths. Then index.html is set up as the entry page. The server needs to serve the HTML, CSS, JavaScript and PNG files correctly. An intranet lets you use Plato without the internet, but it can also keep an old edition running for years. Whoever administers it needs to record the commit that was published and decide how they'll bring in regulatory, physics or editorial corrections.

The safest way to embed Plato in another website is usually a link. That keeps the navigation, the sizing and the context intact, and the user knows which site they're going to. An iframe, which shows one page inside another, can work in a virtual classroom or an internal portal if there's enough space. It needs a descriptive title attribute, enough height and an alternative link. If you want fullscreen to work, the container needs to allow it. The current version of Plato doesn't request it in code.

The iframe also has limits. If it's small, it squeezes an interface that carries a lot of information. If the page belongs to a different origin, the site containing it can't inspect or control it, because of the browser's security policy. On top of that, an overly strict sandbox attribute can block the scripts or the navigation. It's not a good idea to strip out protections just to make it work without understanding the risk. The publication must keep the MIT notice and mustn't imply that Roberto approves a modified version.

A public release needs to use HTTPS. It also needs to check the cache and test the paths with their correct upper and lower case, because a link that works on one computer can fail on the server. GitHub Pages makes the published content available on the internet. An intranet can limit who has access, but it doesn't by itself fix the permissions, the notices, or any sensitive data that whoever maintains the copy has added.

Integration decision. For a club's website, put an “Open Plato” link first. Only use iframe if it's been tested at the actual width, and offer “Open in a new tab” below it. Label the copy as the club's version and link to the original project.
Minimal example. <iframe src="https://rcanalescoder.github.io/Plato/app.html" title="Plato simulator" allow="fullscreen"></iframe>. It's a starting point, not a guarantee: you need to check headers, content policy, focus, keyboard access and size on the final server.
15.5

Files you must keep

A publication of your own needs to state its changes, keep the licence, authorship and provenance visible, and test the hosting. To do it properly, we need to know which files always have to travel with the copy.

This section explains what a copy needs to include: which notice must go with the software, and which documents are worth adding. How to visibly credit the author is explained in 15.6. The final review of the obligations appears in 15.8.

MIT doesn't require a particular folder structure or a set file name. The condition concerns the content: the copyright notice and the permission notice must be included in all copies or substantial portions of the Software. In Plato, the clearest way to comply is to distribute the complete LICENSE file. The notice includes Copyright (c) 2026 Roberto Canales Mora and Original project: https://robertocanales.com/proyectos#plato. Both pieces of information are part of the original notice and must be kept.

LICENCIA_ES.md doesn't replace LICENSE. It's an informative Spanish translation, and it states that the English version is the canonical one. It can be kept to make reading easier, but handing over only the translation creates doubt and doesn't literally reproduce the permission that applies. If the interface shows a summary, the canonical file still needs to be included in the distributed copy.

Other people's materials are governed by their own licences. A font, a photograph, an icon, a library or a snippet added to Plato may require credit, legal text or a different licence. Those notices mustn't be deleted. Nor should they be blended in a way that makes it look like Roberto created those resources. Likewise, copying an external resource into the repository doesn't automatically turn it into MIT material. Before redistributing, you need to list the materials that were added and keep proof of where they came from.

It's well worth keeping a changelog with date, person responsible, commit, files and reason. This record helps distinguish the results of the original project from those produced by a modified version. However, describing changes isn't a condition written into MIT, and it shouldn't be presented as an additional restriction. MIT also doesn't require you to publish the source code, use GitHub, link to the author from every screen, or keep LICENCIA_ES.md. The minimum legal obligation remains keeping the copyright notice and the permission notice. The changelog adds clarity and quality.

If a substantial part of Plato is included inside another package, the notice can be placed in the licences folder or in the product's legal documentation. It genuinely needs to travel with the copy and be easy to find. Leaving it only behind an external URL is more fragile, because that address could disappear. This section explains what content needs to go with the delivery. It doesn't yet decide how to show it to the reader, and it doesn't replace the legal check in 15.8.

Delivery checklist. When you copy a version for a course, include the complete LICENSE file in the root, keep the notice naming Roberto and the URL, add notices for any new resources, and keep a CHANGELOG if you've modified the application. Label that last file as documentation practice, not as an MIT condition.
Substantial portions. The MIT text doesn't set an automatic percentage. If you're unsure whether an extracted part is substantial, keeping the full notice is the simplest practical decision. This chapter informs you about the project, but it doesn't replace legal advice in a specific dispute.
15.6

Authorship and attribution

A distribution needs to keep the licence text; keeping the README, the tests and the sources as well helps you follow the project's history. Attribution lets you credit the author and flag any later changes.

After the file list in 15.5, this section explains how to communicate provenance. The goal is to credit the original author, show the changes, and not attribute to him a version he hasn't reviewed.

To meet the minimum attribution when redistributing a substantial part, you need to keep the notice included in LICENSE. That notice identifies the original author as Roberto Canales Mora and includes the page robertocanales.com/proyectos#plato. It shouldn't be shortened to the point of losing the name or the address. Nor should it be replaced by the brand of whoever publishes the copy. MIT allows the project to be modified and redistributed, but it doesn't allow its history to be hidden.

Besides the legal file, a credits block helps people understand what they're using: “Plato, original project by Roberto Canales Mora; official page; original repository; MIT licence.” If there are changes, you can add “Adapted version by…” along with the date or the commit. Identifying the modifications avoids confusion and makes it easier to reproduce the results. It's good practice, even though MIT doesn't require it as an extra condition.

Crediting the authorship doesn't mean the author approves of a modified version. A derivative work can change the physics, the text or the parameters and still use the licence. However, it mustn't suggest that Roberto has checked those decisions. Nor should it hide MIT's disclaimer of warranty. The name credits the origin. It can't be used to shift responsibility for other people's changes onto the author, or to present a derivative as an official edition.

The repository and the official page play different roles. The author's page provides context and identity; github.com/rcanalescoder/Plato lets you review the files and history. The canonical notice only carries the URL of the project's page, so that's the address that needs to be kept inside the notice. Adding the repository is useful, but it doesn't replace or alter the original text.

Where the credit sits can adapt to the format. In a folder, it's clearest to put LICENSE in the root. A packaged application can have a “Licences” screen that shows the text. In a dossier, the figures can be accompanied by a credits page. What matters is that the notice travels with the substantial copy. A visible link helps people find the information, but it shouldn't replace the file when someone needs to keep the full permission offline.

If other people contribute later, each of them keeps the rights to their own contribution, unless there's some other agreement. Listing their names among contributors doesn't allow Roberto's notice to be removed. Nor does it require them to be included in his copyright line. The project's origin and later contributions can be shown separately, without creating false authorship. The goal is for anyone to know who did each part. After that, 15.8 will check whether the package keeps to MIT's literal condition.

Reusable block. “Plato. Original project by Roberto Canales Mora: https://robertocanales.com/proyectos#plato. Original repository: https://github.com/rcanalescoder/Plato. MIT licence; see LICENSE. Adapted by [name], reviewed on [date].” The last sentence is only filled in if changes were actually made.
Provenance separation. Keep the details of the original project, known as upstream, separate from those of the derived version. The legal notice must stay unchanged. The version record identifies the original commit, the modified commit and the person responsible. That way, every screenshot or result can be linked to the correct implementation.
15.7

What the licence allows

The original author and the contributors can appear separately, with their responsibilities and provenance clearly marked. From this distinction we can now understand what the MIT licence permits.

Can Plato be used at home, in a club, in a business, or inside a derivative work, even if it's modified or charged for as part of a service?

Plato's MIT permission is deliberately broad. It allows you to use, copy, modify, merge, publish, distribute, sublicense and sell copies of the Software. It also allows you to authorise other people to do the same. The text itself defines “Software” as the program and its associated documentation files. That's why Plato can be installed on a computer, studied in a class, brought into an intranet, adapted to another interface, or distributed inside a larger project.

The licence doesn't limit use to personal, educational or non-commercial purposes. A club can use Plato in its courses. A business can charge for a service that includes it. Anyone can publish a fork or combine parts with another application. The licence also doesn't require a derivative version to use MIT, or its code to be public. However, even if the whole thing uses a different licence, the MIT notice protecting the part that comes from Plato must be kept.

The word “sublicense” doesn't allow earlier rights to be erased or someone else's work to be claimed as your own. It means the Software can form part of later agreements, as long as the original condition is respected. Selling a copy doesn't turn Roberto's work into your own, either. The copy or substantial portion that someone else receives still needs to include the copyright notice and the permission notice.

The licence governs the copyright of the material it covers. It doesn't guarantee that any technically possible action is sensible, legal or compliant in every case. Publishing a version to teach shooting doesn't authorise the use of firearms, the opening of a shooting ground, or presenting the simulator as officially approved. Those matters depend on other laws, regulations and responsibilities. Freedom to use the software doesn't turn a physics hypothesis into a certified fact.

The permission doesn't change depending on the medium used. Sending a folder by email, hosting it in the cloud, projecting it in class, or building it into a product are all different ways of exercising the same rights. What matters is whether a copy or a substantial part is being handed over. If so, the notice needs to travel with the material. A plain link, by itself, doesn't copy Plato's files.

Specific case. An instructor adapts the colours and starting values for a class and distributes the folder to their students. They can do this, and they can charge for the training. They must keep the original MIT notice in the copy; it's advisable to note that the presets are their own, so they aren't attributed to Roberto.
Licence compatibility. When you combine Plato with another component, both licences need to be checked. MIT usually integrates easily because it imposes a brief condition, but that ease doesn't override a more restrictive licence on the other component, or grant rights its holder hasn't given.
15.8

What obligations must be kept

MIT allows the project to be used, modified and distributed, even for commercial purposes. These permissions carry specific obligations that need to be kept in the copies.

This section checks compliance. It applies MIT's exact legal condition to the package put together in 15.5 and to the attribution explained in 15.6. It also separates that obligation from good editorial practice.

MIT's condition can be summed up in one sentence: all copies or substantial portions of the Software must include the copyright notice and the permission notice. In Plato, keeping the complete LICENSE file preserves Roberto Canales Mora's name and the original URL. It also preserves the grant of rights, the condition and the disclaimer of warranty. Writing “MIT” in a page footer isn't enough if the distributed copy doesn't contain the full notices.

The LICENCIA_ES.md translation makes reading easier, but it doesn't replace the English text and it doesn't create a second licence either. MIT doesn't require every change to be listed, the source code to be published, modifications to be sent back to the original repository, Roberto's name to be used in advertising, or the author to be linked from every screen. The project can recommend visible attribution and a changelog because they help users and maintainers. Turning those recommendations into conditions of use would add restrictions that the MIT text doesn't include.

The original site's address needs a precise explanation. It isn't a general requirement invented outside MIT. The URL is written inside Plato's original copyright notice. The condition requires keeping “the above copyright notice”. So removing just that line would change the notice that was received. A faithful copy keeps the year, the author and the project's address together.

External materials are reviewed separately. If a version adds a Creative Commons photo, an SIL OFL font, or Apache-2.0 code, it needs to keep the notices those licences require. That obligation comes from the added resource, not from MIT. Before publishing, it's worth drawing up a list by file or component. SPDX and REUSE offer tidy ways of expressing provenance, although Plato doesn't require those systems to be used.

It's also worth not oversimplifying things. Replacing LICENSE with a badge, a short phrase, or the MIT identifier can help classify the project, but it doesn't include the full notice required when redistributing. The identifier and the text can appear together, because they serve different purposes. The whole path runs like this: 15.5 lists what's handed over, 15.6 explains the provenance, and 15.8 checks that the presentation hasn't removed or altered the mandatory notice.

Copy check. Search the final package for “Roberto Canales Mora”, the original URL, “Permission is hereby granted” and the “AS IS” disclaimer. If any part is missing, restore LICENSE. Then check the notices for anything you've added separately.
No added restrictions. A project note can ask for changes to be described, but it mustn't claim that MIT bans commercial use, requires derivatives to be published, or imposes prior approval. None of those conditions appears in the canonical text applied to Plato.
15.9

What the licence doesn't cover

The main obligation is to keep the copyright notice and the licence text in copies or important portions. We shouldn't confuse this requirement with other good practices of courtesy and tracking that don't change the licence.

Does being able to reuse a work mean the author guarantees its accuracy, takes on liability for any damage, or grants rights over all related materials?

MIT provides the Software “as is”. This means it offers no express or implied warranties about its merchantability, its fitness for a particular purpose, or freedom from infringement. To the extent the applicable law allows, it also excludes the liability of authors and copyright holders for claims or damages related to the Software. This clause isn't a negative opinion about Plato. It simply marks the line between what the licence grants and what each user has to check for themselves.

So the licence doesn't certify that a trajectory is physically accurate. Nor does it approve a discipline, confirm that a setup will work for tuning a shotgun, or guarantee better results. The simulator states its approximations and works as an educational lab. Whoever publishes a derivative version with new values needs to stand behind their own claims. They can't shift that responsibility onto Roberto just by citing him.

MIT doesn't grant a trademark licence, either. The fact that Plato is the project's name doesn't let anyone else present their version as the official product, use someone else's logos, or imply sponsorship. In the same way, the permission over Plato's files doesn't grant rights over photographs, trademarks, manufacturers' manuals or external images. Those materials need their own authorisation.

The absence of a warranty doesn't remove any legal obligations that a country or territory doesn't allow to be excluded. Nor does it protect misleading conduct. Checking the software, explaining its limits and fixing errors is still possible and still necessary. In fact, those tasks are what let an open project be used responsibly. The practical idea is simple: there's permission to work with the code, the notice has to be kept, and decisions about physics, regulation, commerce or other rights need to be checked separately.

The phrase “as is” refers to the Software as delivered. It shouldn't automatically be applied to any later service. A person or business offering support, installation, training or their own commercial warranty can take on commitments through a contract. Those commitments belong to whoever offers them. They don't change Roberto's permission, and they can't be presented as a guarantee from the original author.

Edge-case scenario. A company changes the physics and sells access to its version. MIT allows this, but it can't advertise its product as certified by Roberto or attribute the new results to him. It must keep the notice and take responsibility for reviewing its own changes.
Layers of rights. Copyright in the code, trademarks, image rights, resource licences and sporting regulations are separate layers. Settling one through MIT doesn't automatically grant permissions in the others.

Faced with a risky decision, consult the source or a qualified professional.

15.10

Licence for the infographics and documentation

The licence grants permissions, but it doesn't guarantee accuracy, usefulness, official approval, or rights over other people's materials. The infographics and the documentation also need to clearly state where they come from.

Are the manual's pages and their PNGs covered by the same permission, and what happens if an image contains material from somewhere else?

The LICENSE of Plato defines “Software” as the program and its associated documentation files. In addition, this review's README explains that MIT covers the source code, the original documentation, the simulator screenshots and the PNG infographics created for Plato, unless a file states a different origin or licence. Linked external sources are treated differently. They're cited as references, keep their own rights, and aren't distributed as if they were Plato's own work. No clause such as “educational use only”, “modification prohibited” or “non-commercial” should be added, because it would go against the permission granted.

MIT requires the copyright notice and the permission notice that go with the Software to be included. A general URL doesn't replace them on its own. In this review, the canonical notice in LICENSE contains Roberto Canales Mora's name and the address of the original project. It needs to be kept in full. An infographic can be cropped, translated, annotated or included in a presentation. If a substantial copy is distributed, the notice needs to be kept. Adding “modified by…” and linking to the source helps identify the version, but it isn't an extra MIT condition.

The available format also creates a practical limit. This book's pieces are distributed as PNGs. It hasn't been confirmed that equivalent vector or editable files exist. MIT allows the images to be modified, but it doesn't require the author to hand over another source format. An adaptation can edit the pixels or create a new image. If it changes labels or scientific relationships, it shouldn't be presented as “unchanged from the original”.

The licence only covers the rights the project is able to grant. If a future infographic includes a photograph, a font, a logo or a figure by someone else, that element keeps its own licence or permission. Before reusing an image, you need to check whether it was created for Plato, whether it's authorised external material, or whether it only appears as a linked reference. If the author decides in future to use different licences for the code and the content, it will need to be stated clearly, by folder or by file. Until then, the repository shouldn't be credited with a separation it doesn't declare.

A future split in licensing shouldn't be assumed to apply retroactively, either. Each copy is interpreted according to the notices that came with the version obtained. That's why it's worth keeping the commit and LICENSE together. That way you can tell later whether an image belonged to an earlier edition or to a new policy.

Editorial case. A club translates an infographic into Galician and puts it in a dossier. They can do this under MIT; they keep Plato's notice in the dossier and note that it's a translation. If they add a stock photo, they also need permission for that photo.
Recommended inventory. Record, for each asset: path, author or provenance, licence, available source format and modifications. REUSE proposes per-file information; using it would be a maintenance improvement, not a new obligation for whoever receives the current version.
15.11

Contributions

The project's own materials can use its licence, but third-party materials keep their own terms and need to be recorded separately. Future contributions also need to include evidence and clear authorship.

What does it take to turn an observation from the ground or a screen glitch into an improvement that someone else can review and reproduce?

The public repository allows Issues and Pull requests to be created, although no CONTRIBUTING.md file with its own instructions has been found. An issue is used to record a bug, a question or an improvement before changing the code. It needs a clear title, the URL or commit, the browser, the screen size, the discipline, the parameters, the steps followed, the observed result, the expected result and how often the problem happens. A screenshot is useful, but it doesn't replace steps that someone else can reproduce.

Physical or regulatory differences need more information. You need to cite the body, the edition, the page, the date it was checked and the measurement affected. “On my ground it comes out higher” is a useful observation, but it isn't a general rule yet. First you need to measure the machine, the discipline and the conditions. If the change affects a scheme, you run node scripts/verify-schemes.js and attach the result. If it changes the manual, you check the links, the date, the consistency and the matching infographic.

A pull request proposes merging the changes from one branch into another, and lets you comment on specific lines. It should ideally solve one limited issue, explain why the behaviour changes, include tests and link to the issue. It shouldn't mix a mathematical fix with a complete rewrite of the styles. On Canvas, before-and-after screenshots are very useful. Even so, they need to be accompanied by the values needed to reproduce the scene.

Plato's style distinguishes between facts, experiences and approximations. The main text explains before it lists. The technical note develops the model. The specific case sets out a decision. Every claim that could change carries a source and a date. A contribution can go against the maintainer's intuition, but it needs to bring evidence. If there's a physical safety problem, a response on GitHub isn't what's expected. Actual practice is stopped, and whoever is responsible for the ground is told.

Before opening an issue, you check for duplicates and remove personal information. ID documents, licence numbers, firearm details, or images of other people shouldn't be posted without permission. If two reports look the same but use different commits, that difference is kept: it might point to a regression that was already fixed, or one that's just been introduced.

Minimal report. “ABT CPSA 2026, commit X, Firefox Y, 1440 × 900. Steps 1-5. Expected 70 m according to the source and observed 92 m on the band. Happens 5/5. Attaching a screenshot, the controls and the output of verify-schemes.” A report like that lets you investigate without guessing.
Review criterion. A contribution is ready when it keeps the behaviour that wasn't meant to change, tests the new property, explains its assumptions, and doesn't turn a local experience into a rule. Reviewing the text and reviewing the calculations are two different checks.
15.12

Project README

A useful contribution can be reproduced, protects personal data, and separates what was observed, what's proposed, and what's already been checked. The README brings together the basic information needed to identify and try out the project.

What does the README need to explain so someone can identify, try, review and reuse Plato without reading the whole book first?

GitHub shows the README on the repository's main page. That's why it works as an entry map. It needs to quickly explain what Plato is, who created it, where it can be tried, and how far it goes. The reviewed version opens with the visual lab, names Roberto Canales Mora, and links to the original page, the licence, the book and the simulator. It also shows a screenshot. After that it explains the controls, the geometry, the disciplines, the physics, the pattern, the corrections, the limits and the installation.

The README shouldn't compete with index.html. Its job is to summarise and offer links. The book takes care of the detailed explanation. An up-to-date list of features stops a screenshot from promising controls that no longer exist. A limits section stops “meets the regulations” being confused with full official approval. The installation section distinguishes between the public page, the ZIP, cloning and the local server. In the licence part, LICENSE appears as the canonical text. The informative translation can also be linked, but without presenting it as a substitute.

A version's status needs a reference that can be checked. A date isn't enough if main changes several times in the same day. To share an edition, you need to record the release tag or the commit identifier, the date, the test that was run and the matching documentation. If there's no numbered release, the README needs to say so, instead of inventing a “v1.0”. Git lets you reconstruct the changes, but the user shouldn't have to guess which commit produced a screenshot.

It's also worth checking the links and duplicated content. The current version confirms there are no other parallel manuals called docs.html or manual.html. index.html is the documentation that's kept up to date. This decision stops two manuals ending up saying different things. When a feature is added, the same change should update the controls, the limits, the screenshot if the interface changes, and the version status.

The main screenshot needs to show the actual product. Its relative path needs to work both on GitHub and in the local copy. If it changes, the alt text and the resolution need checking. A README with an old image can work technically and still mislead the reader. It can make them think that buttons which have been removed, or earlier results, are still available.

Review before publishing. Someone who doesn't know Plato should be able to open the README and, in under two minutes, find the demo, the manual, installation, the licence, the author and the limitations. Then check that the screenshot matches the interface of the commit stated.
Documentary test. Extract the control and mode IDs from app.html and compare them with the README. Check the links with an automated tool, and confirm that every important physical figure points to the manual or a source. Documentation can have regressions too.
15.13

Help page and GitHub Pages

The README needs to serve as an entry point without repeating the book: the version, the access, the tests, the licence and the screenshot need to match the actual state. GitHub Pages and the help then connect this short presentation to the full documentation.

How does the quick help connect to the full book, and what limits still exist on mobile and in accessibility?

The quick help sits inside app.html, in the #ayuda section. It explains the disciplines, the controls, the on-screen information, the speed, the pattern and the limits without leaving the simulator. From that panel you can close the help, open the full manual, go to the technical documentation, or visit Plato's page. There's no docs.html file. All the documentation is gathered in index.html, and the technical part opens through index.html#ingenieria.

GitHub Pages offers a stable address for the repository's structure, even though the content of main can change. The relative links between index.html and app.html let you publish the folder on another domain without rewriting them. To cite a version that can be reproduced, you need to add the commit or the release. For everyday use, the public URL is enough. The book's header and links let you go back to the simulator.

The adaptation to mobile is partial. The viewport tag and the CSS rules stack the controls one below another on narrow screens. However, a long interface on a page without scrolling, and aiming that's designed for a mouse, both need specific testing. The canvas doesn't currently use pointerdown, pointermove or touch events. That's why the documentation should talk about “responsive design” and not “full mobile compatibility”. To claim the second, you'd need to check that you can aim, fire, call for the clay and use every control on several devices.

Accessibility shows both progress and work still to do. There are areas with aria-label, standard HTML buttons, form labels, aria-pressed states and a dynamic notice using aria-live. The Enter and space keys let you carry out the essential actions. However, the Canvas scene doesn't offer a text description of its whole state. The aiming point depends on the view, the arrow keys move it without announcing the coordinates, and the panels with a lot of information need their focus and contrast checked. WCAG requires that functions can be used with a keyboard, and that components communicate their name, role and value. Meeting some of these criteria isn't the same as a full audit.

The help needs to stay brief and link to the full explanation instead of repeating it. If a control changes, its label, the help, the README and the corresponding part of the book all need updating at the same time. Otherwise, the address will keep opening, but the instructions will point to a different interface.

Walkthrough test. Without a mouse, open the help, go back, call for the clay and fire, change a control and open the manual. Then repeat it with a screen reader and on a real mobile device. Record not just whether it “loads”, but which tasks can be completed and what state gets lost.
Priority improvement. A visible “Full documentation” link could point to index.html; adding Pointer events would unify mouse, pen and touch. Canvas needs a text output for its state and result, not a permanent generic description.

What we have learned

  • Plato is a static web application: the server delivers files, and the browser runs the simulator without a backend.
  • The original publication is the fastest route; for a reproducible local copy, it's best to serve the folder through localhost.
  • MIT permits using, copying, modifying, publishing, distributing, sublicensing and selling, including in commercial contexts.
  • MIT's condition is to keep the copyright notice and the permission notice in copies or substantial portions.
  • Plato's notice keeps the attribution to Roberto Canales Mora and the original URL; the English LICENSE is canonical, and LICENCIA_ES.md is informative only.
  • Documenting changes, the commit and external resources is a traceability practice, not an extra restriction invented on top of MIT.
  • The help and the responsive design exist, but built-in fullscreen, touch aiming and full accessibility remain limits that can be checked.

Chapter 16

Sources, glossary, frequently asked questions and document control

A technical claim needs to be something you can check. This chapter brings together the sources, their version, the simulator's decisions and the questions still open.

The links were checked on 2 August 2026. That date doesn't guarantee a rule is still in force: before applying it, check its current edition.

16.1

Types of sources

The application, the manual, the help and the licence form a single publication and must keep their authorship and their version together. The last technical chapter explains how to record sources, revisions and the necessary warnings.

Why do the BOE, a sporting regulation, a ballistic test, a manufacturer's manual and Plato's code each answer different questions?

The first question shouldn't just be “is this source reliable?”. It's better to ask first, “can this source answer this question?”. The BOE publishes Spain's legal rules. The Guardia Civil explains the procedures and actions of the Intervención de Armas. These are primary sources for knowing which authorisation applies or which document must accompany a firearm. A club can explain how it organises its daily activity, but it cannot amend the Weapons Regulation.

Something similar happens in sport. The ISSF runs its international Olympic shooting events. FITASC publishes the rules for Universal Trench. The RFETO and the regional federations apply that framework to the calendars, licences and competitions within their remit. The regulation for one discipline doesn't automatically apply to another. A custom at one shooting ground doesn't become a universal rule just because it's common practice.

To study the physics, academic papers, tests with measurements and technical manuals are used. A paper on the aerodynamic flight of the clay helps to understand the forces involved and calculate the trajectory. Another on the pellet cloud allows the air braking and spread to be studied. A manufacturer is the main source for the features and maintenance of its model. However, its marketing claims don't on their own demonstrate a general advantage. A specialist manual can connect concepts if it cites measurements and explains how far its conclusions reach.

Two more important sources remain. The author's personal comments contribute questions, everyday phrases and observed situations. They're presented as experience, not as regulation or controlled testing. The source code shows what the reviewed version actually does: its initial values, formulae, controls and limits. The code doesn't prove that the model reproduces reality exactly. It does let you know what the program calculates.

Specific case. If someone asks how many grams Universal Trench allows, you open the current FITASC edition. If they ask why Plato draws 306 pellets, you inspect pelletCount(). If they ask whether their shotgun patterns evenly, neither source is enough: they need to follow an authorised procedure specific to their gun, choke and cartridge.
Expert reading. Every claim needs to state its field: legal, sporting, physical, product features, ground observation or implementation. Cross-checking sources means gathering the right documents for the same question. It doesn't mean averaging texts that talk about different things.
16.2

Trust hierarchy

Each source answers a different question: a rule prescribes, a test measures, a manufacturer describes and the code implements. Trust should depend on that function, not on an automatic classification.

Is there a universal classification of sources, or does trust depend on the question, the edition and whether the data can be reproduced?

Plato doesn't invent confidence percentages. Saying a source is “95% reliable” wouldn't make sense without a formal assessment. Instead, it uses a classification that's explained and easy to follow. For a legal obligation, the officially published rule and its amendments take priority. The BOE's consolidated text makes reading easier, although the BOE itself warns that this consolidation is for information only. The Guardia Civil's procedures page helps you carry out the process and should be read together with the rule it's based on.

In competition, the current edition published by the body governing the discipline takes priority. It ranks above a summary, a news article or a shooter's recollection. A later circular can change how an article is applied. That's why you need to check the rules, their amendments and their interpretation. The national or regional federation decides on its own events and licences. However, it cannot on its own change Spanish law or international rules outside its remit.

In physics, the evidence matters more than the institution's name. What counts is the measurements, the explanation of method, the control of variables, academic review and whether the results can be checked. A study carried out with one cartridge, one choke and specific distances doesn't let you fix a universal figure. Manufacturer documentation is a good source for a product's dimensions, use and maintenance. It's weaker for comparing overall performance if it doesn't include a test that can be repeated.

The author's personal comments and other shooters' observations help identify real problems. For example, losing sight of the clay when it's blocked from view, or getting tired after several rounds. These experiences are recorded within their context. A forum post or a video can suggest an idea or show a difference, but it doesn't on its own prove a conclusion. The next step is to look for the primary source, repeat the scene, or keep the claim clearly labelled as experience.

Practical decision. A fellow shooter says “in Olympic you can always use 28 g”. This isn't settled by seniority or reputation: you check the ammunition chapter of the applicable ISSF edition. The comment stays as a reason to check, not as the answer.
Decision matrix. For each claim, note the source's authority, how close it is to the fact, the edition, the method, its independence, whether the data can be reproduced, and whether it's still in force. A source can be primary and still be outdated, apply only to a limited case, or contain an error corrected later.
16.3

Source log

To trust a source, we need to check who publishes it, which edition it is, what it covers and whether its data can be reproduced. A source register keeps this information so it can be reviewed over time.

What details need to be recorded so someone else can check a claim months later and find out whether the source has changed?

The minimum record includes the title, the body or authors, and the date or edition given by the source itself. It also keeps a stable URL, the date it was accessed, the section that uses it, the claim it supports, the type of evidence and the reason to review it again. If a DOI exists — a permanent identifier for a publication — it's kept. If the web page changes frequently, the downloaded edition or a copy that can be checked is kept instead. A missing date must never be replaced by the access date.

FuenteEdition or date checkedUse in PlatoStatus and next review
Weapons Regulation, BOE-A-1993-6202Última actualización publicada: 04/04/2025Licences, documentation, custody and transportFuente legal primaria; revisar ante nueva modificación
ISSF Rule Book 2026Second Print 07/2026, valid from 01/07/2026Olympic Trap, ammunition, shooting ground and refereeing decisionsOfficial sports regulations; check rules and amendments
FITASC Fosse Universelle-UT01/01/2025Universal Trench, 25 clays, machines, flight paths and safetyOfficial regulations linked by FITASC; check for a new edition
Plato's app.htmlCommit 14a8bf6…, reviewed on 02/08/2026; SHA-256 d1af33c6…686858Controls, formulas, tables and limits implementedVersion-controlled code source; review with every functional change

The “confidence level” shouldn't just say high, medium or low. The reason has to be explained. Some examples are “primary legal source”, “official regulation for the discipline”, “experimental paper with limited conditions” or “ground observation that can't be generalised”. It also states whether the document sets a rule, describes a fact, or explains an implementation. This avoids citing a ballistics study as if it were a sporting obligation.

The trail must reach right down to the fragment used. For an important figure, the chapter, article, table or function is kept on record. If a URL disappears, the title, authors, DOI, edition and passage make it possible to find another legitimate copy. If the source changes, the record isn't replaced without notice. A review is opened, the texts are compared, and the affected claims and infographics are noted.

The register also distinguishes between a “checked link” and “confirmed content”. A page still opening doesn't prove it shows the same edition. The review must open the document, locate the passage used and note any change of address or replacement.

Audit exercise. Take the claim “a Universal Trench round consists of 25 clays”. Record rule 7.01, edition 01/01/2025, the URL, the access date 02/08/2026, and the sections where it appears. If FITASC publishes another edition, the search immediately returns every place that needs checking.
Provenance model. The source is an entity; extraction and review are activities; author, body and reviewer are agents. The derived claim must keep its relationship to those elements. W3C PROV offers a formal vocabulary, although this manual uses a readable table instead.
16.4

Regulatory sources

Recording the title, version, date, fragment and use makes it possible to detect changes even when the link still works. For statutes, you also need to state the territory where they apply and whether they're still in force.

How do you look up a rule on licences, safekeeping, transport, cartridges or authorised grounds without mistaking this manual for legal advice?

In Spain, the starting point is the Weapons Regulation. Its Article 96 requires the relevant authorisation to carry or possess firearms. It also places the E licence among the categories that include the usual shotguns. Article 89 governs the ownership document and states that it must accompany the firearm during its use, storage and transport. Article 144 requires it to be kept in a secure place and for measures to be taken against loss, theft or misappropriation. These are general legal references. Each personal situation should be confirmed with the Intervención de Armas.

The Guardia Civil publishes procedure pages for applying for or renewing the E licence and for taking the first-issue tests. These pages are useful because they set out the requirements, forms, fees and ways to submit the application. They don't replace the rule itself, and an old screenshot may be out of date. The record should link to the current page, note the date it was accessed and keep the related legal article on file.

The word “transport” also needs precision. Carrying your own documented shotgun to a shooting ground isn't the same as organising a commercial shipment of dozens of firearms. The Regulation has different rules for movement, ownership documents and consignments. In addition, security measures for storage during professional transport were published in 2025. This manual doesn't automatically apply those obligations to every private journey. It recommends checking each real case.

Cartridges, shooting grounds and federation requirements fall under related but different rules. The Pyrotechnic Articles and Cartridges Regulation governs the purchase, storage and transport of cartridges. Grounds and ranges need their own administrative authorisations. The federation licence belongs to the sporting sphere. Each ground also adds its own internal rules. None of these layers replaces the others.

Specific case. Someone moves house and asks whether their previous way of storing their firearm is still sufficient.
